✅ Study Best Practices

📖 Read Every Explanation
Even for correct answers — explanations contain deeper policy insights, anihpj context, and command examples you'll need on the exam.
🔁 Re-Answer Wrong Ones
Track incorrect answers. Re-answer them 48 hours later. If still wrong, re-read the entire domain section — there's a concept gap.
⚡ Prioritize Domain 5
Writing Policies is 32% of the exam. If you only master ONE domain, make it this one. Focus on validate, mutate, generate, and verifyImages rules.

Block non-compliant

Auto-provision

🔍 Anatomy of Every Question — What Makes This Guide Different

🧱 STRUCTURE OF EVERY MCQ
1. Domain Pill — Color-coded badge (6 domains)
2. Question — Scenario-based, exam-style
3. 4 Options — A/B/C/D with code examples
4. Toggle Answer — Interactive reveal
5. Explanation — Plain-English with emphasis
6. ASCII Diagram — Visual illustration
7. anihpj/jobpost — Real-world application context
🎯 WHY THIS APPROACH WORKS
Active Recall: Toggle answers force you to THINK before seeing the solution — proven to improve retention by 50%+.

Visual Learning: ASCII diagrams map abstract policy concepts to concrete visual patterns you can recall during the exam.

Context Anchoring: Every answer ties back to anihpj/jobpost — a real Django app — so you learn HOW policies work, not just WHAT they are.

⚡ Essential Commands You'll Use Throughout This Guide

Top 10 Kyverno Commands — Quick Reference
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # │ COMMAND                              │ WHAT IT DOES             │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  1 │ kubectl apply -f policy.yaml         │ Deploy a policy          │
  │  2 │ kubectl get clusterpolicy            │ List all ClusterPolicies │
  │  3 │ kubectl get policyreport -n anihpj   │ Check compliance         │
  │  4 │ kubectl describe cpol <name>         │ Debug policy status      │
  │  5 │ kyverno apply policy.yaml -r pod.yaml│ Test policy locally      │
  │  6 │ kyverno test ./tests/                │ Run test suite           │
  │  7 │ kyverno jp -q "expr" resource.yaml   │ Test JMESPath            │
  │  8 │ helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno │ Install Kyverno          │
  │  9 │ kubectl get polr -A -o json | jq     │ Aggregate all reports    │
  │ 10 │ cosign sign --key cosign.key image   │ Sign container image     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

🎯 What This Document Is — And How To Use It

This is your complete Kyverno (KCA) exam preparation system. It's not just a study guide — it's a policy-as-code training simulator built around a real Django application (anihpj/jobpost) running on your 6-cluster lab. Every section is designed to build policy muscle memory through progressive difficulty: theory → policy diagnostics → hands-on hardening.

📝 Part 1: 200 Multiple Choice Questions

Purpose: Build foundational Kyverno knowledge across all 6 official KCA domains. Every question has a detailed explanation with real-world policy context from anihpj/jobpost.

Domains covered: Fundamentals (Q1-Q36), Installation & Configuration (Q37-Q72), Kyverno CLI (Q73-Q96), Applying Policies (Q97-Q116), Writing Policies (Q117-Q180), Policy Management (Q181-Q200).

How to use: Answer 20-30 questions per study session. Read the explanation even for correct answers — they contain deeper policy insights. Target: 85%+ correct before exam day.

✅ Daily Study Checklist
  • Morning (30 min): Answer 20-30 MCQs from Part 1. Read ALL explanations — even for correct answers.
  • Afternoon (45 min): Deep-dive one domain. Practice kyverno apply/test on your cluster with real policies.
  • Evening (30 min): Review wrong answers. Re-read the weakest domain's questions.
  • Weekend (2-3 hours): Full 50-question timed practice exam. Review all mistakes. Focus on Writing Policies (32% of exam).
  • Day before exam: Review top 20 Kyverno commands from memory. Re-read Category 5 (Writing Policies) and Category 6 (Policy Management).
  • Exam day morning: Set up environment: alias k=kubectl, verify kyverno version. Quick scan of anchor reference card (Q119). Stay calm — you've practiced 200 questions.

🏆 Skills You'll Master — Before vs After This Guide

❌ BEFORE — Common Beginner Pitfalls

  • ❌ "Anyone can deploy a privileged container?" — No policy enforcement in place
  • ❌ "The policy passed kyverno apply but fails in cluster" — Don't know about Audit vs Enforce
  • ❌ "My mutation isn't working" — Didn't know mutate runs before validate
  • ❌ "Policies work but reports show nothing" — Background scan not configured
  • ❌ "Image verification keeps failing" — Cosign keys not properly configured
  • ❌ "My generate rule created duplicate resources" — synchronize not set correctly
  • ❌ "Kyverno is slowing down my cluster" — No resource limits or webhook tuning

✅ AFTER — Skills This Guide Builds

  • ✅ Write validate, mutate, generate, and verifyImages policies from scratch
  • ✅ Debug policy failures using kyverno apply, PolicyReports, and events
  • ✅ Enforce least-privilege: non-root, read-only root FS, drop ALL capabilities
  • ✅ Implement supply chain security: Cosign signing + Kyverno image verification
  • ✅ Auto-generate NetworkPolicies, ResourceQuotas, and PDBs with generate rules
  • ✅ Use JMESPath for complex conditional logic and API call data extraction
  • ✅ Build compliance dashboards from PolicyReports for audit evidence

🚨 Critical Do's & Don'ts During The Exam
  • ✅ DO: Use kubectl create ... --dry-run=client -o yaml to generate YAML in seconds — never type manifests from scratch.
  • ✅ DO: Run kubectl config use-context <given-context> as the FIRST command for every task. Wrong context = 0 points.
  • ✅ DO: Use kubectl explain <resource> --recursive when you forget a field name. It's faster than searching docs.
  • ✅ DO: Create a debug pod: kubectl run test --rm -it --image=busybox -- sh to test DNS, connectivity, and curl from inside the cluster.
  • ❌ DON'T: Spend more than 7 minutes on any single task. Flag it, move on, return with fresh eyes.
  • ❌ DON'T: Forget to set the namespace. Many tasks require -n flag. Use kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=<ns> to save typing.
  • ❌ DON'T: Leave any task blank. Even partial work (creating the namespace, writing partial YAML) may earn partial credit.
  • ❌ DON'T: Use kubectl delete pod on a static pod — you must delete the manifest file from /etc/kubernetes/manifests/.

📊 Exam Overview & StrategySTRATEGY

KCA Exam Format & Preparation Strategy

Exam: Kyverno Certified Associate (KCA) | Duration: 2 hours | Format: Multiple choice + hands-on policy authoring | Passing Score: 75% | Cost: $250 | Certification Validity: 3 years | Prerequisite: None (Kubernetes basics helpful but not required)

6 Official Domains: Fundamentals of Kyverno (18%), Installation & Configuration (18%), Kyverno CLI (12%), Applying Policies (10%), Writing Policies (32%), Policy Management (10%). Writing Policies at 32% is the HEAVIEST — prioritize validate, mutate, generate, and verifyImages rules.

Exam Strategy: Read all questions first (5 min) → Solve easy policy questions first → Flag and return to complex JMESPath/CEL questions → Use kyverno apply and kyverno jp to test locally → Always verify policies with kubectl get policyreport. The exam environment provides full access to kyverno.io documentation.

Your 6-cluster lab (below) mirrors real exam conditions. Kyverno is installed on each cluster. Use ksw to switch between clusters.

📚 Pre-Learning: Kyverno & Policy-as-Code FoundationsF.1–F.5

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Building Your Policy Mental Model

F.1 What Is Policy-as-Code & Why Does Kubernetes Need It? — The "Wild West" problem: Kubernetes gives you ANYTHING you ask for. What IS a policy? (If X, then Y). The admission control chain. Why Kyverno? (vs OPA/Gatekeeper vs Pod Security Admission). The "Shift Left" philosophy.

F.2 Kyverno Architecture — How It Works Under the Hood — Dynamic Admission Controller, webhook registration. Kyverno components: Admission Controller, Background Controller, Reports Controller, Cleanup Controller. How kubectl apply flows through Kyverno (step-by-step). ValidatingWebhookConfiguration vs MutatingWebhookConfiguration.

F.3 The Kyverno Policy Structure — Your First Policy Deconstructed — Policy vs ClusterPolicy. The anatomy: apiVersion, spec.rules, match/exclude, validate/mutate/generate/verifyImages. validationFailureAction: Audit vs Enforce. background: true vs false. Your first policy: "require labels on all pods" annotated line by line.

F.4 The anihpj/jobpost Application — Your Policy Playground — Django job board (web + API + PostgreSQL + Redis). Security requirements per tier. Complete file structure: k8s/ manifests + kyverno/ policies directory (13 policies covering labels, resource limits, non-root, read-only root FS, capabilities, PVC limits, hostPath blocking, NetworkPolicy generation, image verification, mutation, PDB generation, cleanup). Dockerfile: multi-stage build, distroless, non-root user.

F.5 Kyverno vs Other Policy Tools — Making the Right Choice — Kyverno vs OPA/Gatekeeper (YAML vs Rego), vs Pod Security Admission, vs custom webhooks. Comparison matrix. Kyverno's unique advantages: Generate rules, Cleanup, JMESPath-native, no new language. The CNCF landscape — Kyverno's place in cloud-native security.

If you're new to policy-as-code: Read through F.1–F.5 before diving into Part 1. These foundations will give you the mental models needed to understand Kyverno policies, troubleshooting, and lab scenarios. The entire guide uses the anihpj/jobpost Django application as its running example with a complete kyverno/ policies directory.

📝 Part 1: 200 Multiple Choice Questions

Complete KCA MCQ Bank — Organized by the 6 official Linux Foundation KCA exam domains. Each question includes a detailed explanation, diagrams, and real-world policy context using anihpj/jobpost.

200Questions
6Categories
200Explanations

📦 Category 1: Fundamentals of Kyverno (18%)Q1-Q36

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Understanding What Kyverno Is & Why It Exists

This category is about WHAT Kyverno is and WHY Kubernetes needs policy enforcement. Think of Kyverno like a building inspector — before any resource enters your cluster, Kyverno checks it against your policies (building codes). Without it, anyone can deploy anything — privileged containers, hostPath mounts, images from untrusted registries. For anihpj/jobpost, Kyverno ensures every pod runs as non-root, has resource limits, and uses only signed container images.

Q1-Q36 36 Questions 🟢 Beginner

1.1 Kyverno Policies & Rules — The Big PictureQ1-Q8

Fundamentals Q1 of 200
What is "policy-as-code" in Kubernetes, and what problem does Kyverno solve?
A) Policy-as-code means writing Kubernetes YAML manifests in a specific programming language like Go or Python to enforce security rules
B) Policy-as-code is the practice of defining, versioning, and automating security and operational rules as declarative code (YAML policies) that are automatically enforced — Kyverno acts as a Kubernetes-native policy engine that validates, mutates, generates, and verifies resources BEFORE they enter the cluster
C) Policy-as-code is a feature built into kubectl that automatically checks YAML syntax before applying resources to the cluster
D) Policy-as-code means using cloud provider IAM roles to control which users can run kubectl commands on a cluster
✅ Correct Answer: B

Policy-as-code means defining your security and operational rules as declarative YAML policies stored in version control (just like application code). Kyverno is the Kubernetes-native engine that enforces these policies through the admission control chain — intercepting every kubectl apply or API request and checking it against your policies before the resource is persisted to etcd.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Without Kyverno vs With Kyverno — The "Wild West" Problem
  WITHOUT KYVERNO (No Policy Enforcement):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                       │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌───────────┐    ┌──────┐                       │
  │  │ API      │───▶│ AuthN/Z   │───▶│ etcd │  ✅ Resource SAVED!   │
  │  │ Server   │    │ (RBAC)    │    └──────┘                       │
  │  └──────────┘    └───────────┘                                    │
  │                                                                    │
  │  ⚠️ ANYTHING passes if you have RBAC permissions:                 │
  │     • privileged: true container?  ✅ Allowed!                    │
  │     • hostPath mounting /etc?      ✅ Allowed!                    │
  │     • run as root with ALL caps?   ✅ Allowed!                    │
  │     • image from untrusted registry? ✅ Allowed!                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WITH KYVERNO (Policy-as-Code Enforcement):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                       │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌───────────┐    ┌────────────┐    ┌──────┐     │
  │  │ API      │───▶│ AuthN/Z   │───▶│  KYVERNO   │───▶│ etcd │     │
  │  │ Server   │    │ (RBAC)    │    │ (Admission) │    └──────┘     │
  │  └──────────┘    └───────────┘    └─────┬──────┘                  │
  │                                         │                         │
  │                              ┌──────────┴──────────┐              │
  │                              │  MUTATE first       │              │
  │                              │  (auto-fix issues)  │              │
  │                              ├─────────────────────┤              │
  │                              │  VALIDATE second    │              │
  │                              │  (enforce rules)    │              │
  │                              └──────────┬──────────┘              │
  │                                         │                         │
  │                           ✅ PASS → etcd    ❌ FAIL → 403 DENIED  │
  │                                                                    │
  │  🔒 Policies enforce:                                              │
  │     • "No privileged containers"          → BLOCKED if privileged  │
  │     • "Must run as non-root user"         → BLOCKED if runAsRoot  │
  │     • "Only images from anihpj-registry"  → BLOCKED if untrusted  │
  │     • "Drop ALL capabilities"             → MUTATE to add drop    │
  │     • "Require resource limits"           → BLOCKED if missing    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Kyverno solves the "Wild West" problem of Kubernetes — by default, if you have RBAC permissions to create a pod, you can create ANY pod (privileged, hostNetwork, root user, untrusted images, no resource limits). Kyverno adds a policy enforcement layer between authentication and etcd, ensuring EVERY resource complies with your organization's security and operational standards.

Why YAML and not a new language? Kyverno policies are written in Kubernetes-native YAML — the same YAML you already know. Unlike OPA/Gatekeeper which requires learning Rego (a custom language), Kyverno uses familiar Kubernetes resource structures with match, exclude, validate, mutate, generate, and verifyImages rules. This means: zero new language to learn, version-controlled policies, GitOps-friendly, and CI/CD integrable.

For the anihpj/jobpost application, Kyverno policies ensure every pod in the anihpj namespace runs as non-root, has resource limits, drops all capabilities, uses read-only root filesystems, and only runs images signed by anihpj's Cosign key. Without Kyverno, a developer could accidentally deploy a privileged database container that compromises the entire job board.

Fundamentals Q2 of 200
Why does Kubernetes NEED policy enforcement like Kyverno? What is the "Wild West" problem?
A) Kubernetes has a built-in security scanner that blocks all dangerous configurations by default — Kyverno is just an optional enhancement
B) Kubernetes only needs policy enforcement for multi-tenant clusters; single-tenant clusters are automatically secure
C) Kubernetes by default has NO guardrails beyond RBAC — anyone with permission to create a pod can deploy privileged containers, mount host filesystems, run as root with full capabilities, and pull images from untrusted registries. Kyverno fills this gap by adding a policy layer that validates, mutates, and blocks dangerous configurations BEFORE they hit the cluster
D) Policy enforcement is only needed for compliance audits — it has no effect on actual cluster security during runtime
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kubernetes is intentionally unopinionated — it trusts you to know what you're doing. If you have RBAC permission to create pods, Kubernetes will accept ANY valid pod YAML you submit. This includes dangerously insecure configurations that no production environment should ever allow.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Real Breach Scenario — What Happens WITHOUT Kyverno
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  SCENARIO: A developer accidentally deploys a dangerous pod     ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  STEP 1 — Developer deploys:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: v1                                                │
  │  kind: Pod                                                     │
  │  metadata: {name: test-pod, namespace: anihpj}                │
  │  spec:                                                         │
  │    containers:                                                  │
  │    - name: app                                                  │
  │      image: unknown-registry.com/malicious:latest  ← UNTRUSTED │
  │      securityContext:                                           │
  │        privileged: true              ← DANGER! Full host access │
  │        runAsUser: 0                  ← DANGER! Running as root  │
  │      volumeMounts:                                              │
  │      - name: host-root                                          │
  │        mountPath: /host                ← MOUNTS NODE FILESYSTEM │
  │    volumes:                                                     │
  │    - name: host-root                                            │
  │      hostPath: {path: /}               ← ENTIRE NODE EXPOSED!   │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  STEP 2 — Kubernetes accepts it (RBAC check only):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl apply -f dangerous-pod.yaml                         │
  │  pod/test-pod created   ✅ NO QUESTIONS ASKED!                  │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  STEP 3 — The attacker now has:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Inside the pod:                                                │
  │  $ ls /host/etc/kubernetes/pki/                                 │
  │  ca.crt   ca.key   apiserver.crt   apiserver.key   ← STOLEN!   │
  │  $ cat /host/etc/kubernetes/admin.conf  ← FULL CLUSTER ADMIN!  │
  │  $ curl http://anihpj-db.anihpj:5432    ← DATABASE ACCESS!     │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  THE FIX: Kyverno blocks this pod BEFORE it reaches the node    ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

This is not theoretical — it's the default behavior of every Kubernetes cluster. RBAC controls WHO can do things, not WHAT they can do. A developer with legitimate pod-creation permissions can accidentally (or maliciously) deploy a container that:

🔴 Reads all Secrets in the namespace (they're mounted as files)
🔴 Accesses the host filesystem via hostPath mounts
🔴 Steals Kubernetes certificates from /etc/kubernetes/pki/
🔴 Runs cryptominers with unlimited CPU (no resource limits)
🔴 Exfiltrates the database by connecting to internal services

Kyverno solves this by adding a policy enforcement layer between RBAC and etcd. Even if RBAC says "this user can create pods," Kyverno says "but not THAT kind of pod." For the anihpj/jobpost application, Kyverno policies block all of the above — requiring non-root users, dropping all capabilities, blocking hostPath, enforcing resource limits, and verifying image signatures.

Fundamentals Q3 of 200
What exactly IS a Kyverno policy? How would you describe it to someone who has never used policy-as-code before?
A) A Kyverno policy is a Kubernetes Custom Resource (CR) written in YAML that contains one or more rules — each rule can validate (check compliance), mutate (auto-fix), generate (auto-create), or verifyImages (authenticate) resources. Think of it like a multi-tool: validate = inspection mirror (check), mutate = auto-wrench (fix), generate = 3D printer (create), verifyImages = ID scanner (authenticate)
B) A Kyverno policy is a shell script that runs on each node and checks container runtime behavior at the Linux kernel level
C) A Kyverno policy is a Go program compiled into a binary that runs as a sidecar container alongside every pod
D) A Kyverno policy is a configuration file stored in etcd that kubectl reads before allowing any command to execute
✅ Correct Answer: A

A Kyverno policy is a Kubernetes Custom Resource Definition (CRD) — just like a Deployment or a Service, but instead of defining a workload, it defines rules. Each rule has a specific job: validate (check if something is compliant), mutate (automatically fix misconfigurations), generate (auto-create companion resources), or verifyImages (cryptographically verify container image signatures).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Anatomy of a Kyverno Policy — The Multi-Tool Analogy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  K Y V E R N O   P O L I C Y   —   T H E   M U L T I - T O O L │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                   │
  │  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1        ← "I am a Kyverno resource"    │
  │  kind: ClusterPolicy              ← "I apply cluster-wide"        │
  │  metadata:                                                        │
  │    name: require-non-root-pods    ← "My name"                     │
  │  spec:                                                            │
  │    validationFailureAction: Enforce  ← "Block if violated"        │
  │    background: true               ← "Also check existing pods"    │
  │                                                                   │
  │    rules:                         ← THE MULTI-TOOL KIT            │
  │    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │    │  Rule 1: validate (🔍 INSPECTION MIRROR)                 │    │
  │    │  "I CHECK that every pod has securityContext.runAsNonRoot"│   │
  │    │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌─────────────────┐ │    │
  │    │  │ kubectl  │───▶│ match: Pods  │───▶│ validate:       │ │    │
  │    │  │ apply    │    │ in anihpj ns │    │ runAsNonRoot    │ │    │
  │    │  └──────────┘    └──────────────┘    │ == true?        │ │    │
  │    │                                      │ ✅ PASS / ❌ DENY│ │    │
  │    │                                      └─────────────────┘ │    │
  │    └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                   │
  │    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │    │  Rule 2: mutate (🔧 AUTO-WRENCH)                         │    │
  │    │  "I FIX pods by automatically adding resource limits"     │    │
  │    │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌─────────────────┐ │    │
  │    │  │ kubectl  │───▶│ match: Pods  │───▶│ mutate: add     │ │    │
  │    │  │ apply    │    │ without limits│    │ resources:      │ │    │
  │    │  └──────────┘    └──────────────┘    │ {cpu:100m,      │ │    │
  │    │                                      │  mem:128Mi}     │ │    │
  │    │                                      └─────────────────┘ │    │
  │    └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                   │
  │    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │    │  Rule 3: generate (🖨️ 3D PRINTER)                        │    │
  │    │  "I CREATE a NetworkPolicy when a new namespace appears"  │    │
  │    │  ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐ │    │
  │    │  │ New namespace│───▶│ match: ns    │─▶│ generate:    │ │    │
  │    │  │ created      │    │ with label   │  │ NetworkPolicy│ │    │
  │    │  └──────────────┘    │ env=prod     │  │ (default-deny)│ │    │
  │    │                      └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘ │    │
  │    └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                   │
  │    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │    │  Rule 4: verifyImages (🪪 ID SCANNER)                     │    │
  │    │  "I AUTHENTICATE that images are signed by anihpj's key"  │    │
  │    │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌─────────────────┐ │    │
  │    │  │ kubectl  │───▶│ match: Pods  │───▶│ verifyImages:   │ │    │
  │    │  │ apply    │    │ with image:  │    │ cosign key      │ │    │
  │    │  └──────────┘    │ anihpj-api:* │    │ signature valid?│ │    │
  │    │                  └──────────────┘    │ ✅ / ❌ DENY     │ │    │
  │    │                                      └─────────────────┘ │    │
  │    └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Key insight: You don't need to learn a new programming language. Kyverno policies are plain YAML — the same format you already use for Deployments, Services, and ConfigMaps. This means they can be stored in Git, reviewed in pull requests, deployed through CI/CD pipelines, and managed with the same kubectl apply command you use for everything else in Kubernetes.

For anihpj/jobpost, we maintain a kyverno/ directory with 13 policy files — each one protecting a different aspect of the application. When a new team member joins, they don't need to memorize security rules; the policies automatically enforce them.

Fundamentals Q4 of 200
What is the difference between a Policy and a ClusterPolicy in Kyverno, and when should you use each?
A) Policy applies to pods only; ClusterPolicy applies to all other resource types like Deployments and Services
B) Policy runs in Audit mode only; ClusterPolicy runs in Enforce mode only
C) Policy is NAMESPACED — it only applies to resources within the namespace where it's created (good for tenant-specific rules). ClusterPolicy is CLUSTER-WIDE — it applies to resources in ALL namespaces (good for organization-wide security standards). Use Policy when each team manages their own namespace rules; use ClusterPolicy for global requirements like "all pods must be non-root"
D) Policy is the older deprecated format; ClusterPolicy is the newer replacement and Policy will be removed in future versions
✅ Correct Answer: C

Policy and ClusterPolicy are the two "kinds" of Kyverno resources. The only difference is scope: Policy is namespaced, ClusterPolicy is cluster-wide. Everything else — the rule types, the YAML structure, the behavior — is identical.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Policy vs ClusterPolicy — Scope Comparison Table
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FEATURE              POLICY              CLUSTERPOLICY          │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Scope                1 Namespace         ALL Namespaces          │
  │  API Kind             Policy              ClusterPolicy           │
  │  Creation             kubectl apply       kubectl apply           │
  │                       -n <namespace>      (no -n needed)         │
  │  kubectl get          -n <namespace>      (no -n needed)         │
  │  Visibility           Only in that NS     Cluster-wide view       │
  │  Who can create?      NS admin            Cluster admin           │
  │  Rule types           All 4 supported     All 4 supported         │
  │  Match resources      Only in same NS     Any namespace           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  BEST FOR:                                                    │
  │  Policy:         Tenant-specific rules (Team A's namespace)    │
  │                  "anihpj pods must have app=jobpost label"      │
  │  ClusterPolicy:  Organization-wide security standards           │
  │                  "ALL pods must run as non-root"                │
  │                  "NO pods may use hostPath anywhere"            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  REAL-WORLD ANALOGY:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🏢 OFFICE BUILDING                                              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ClusterPolicy = BUILDING RULES (apply everywhere):              │
  │    • "No smoking anywhere in the building"                       │
  │    • "Fire exits must remain unlocked during business hours"     │
  │    • "All visitors must sign in at reception"                    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Policy = FLOOR/ROOM RULES (apply to specific area):            │
  │    • "Quiet hours on Floor 3 (Engineering) after 2 PM"          │
  │    • "No food in Server Room B"                                  │
  │    • "HR department files must be double-locked"                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use both: ClusterPolicies for global security standards (all pods non-root, no hostPath, require resource limits across every namespace) and Policies for app-specific rules (anihpj-web pods must use a specific image registry, anihpj-db PVC must not exceed 50Gi).

Exam tip: If a question asks you to "apply this rule to all namespaces" → use ClusterPolicy. If it says "apply to namespace X only" → use Policy. The YAML is identical except for the kind field.

Fundamentals Q5 of 200
What are the four types of rules you can write in a Kyverno policy, and what does each one do?
A) allow, deny, log, alert — these control what happens when a policy matches a resource
B) match, exclude, precondition, condition — these control which resources a policy applies to
C) pattern, overlay, anchor, wildcard — these control how validation patterns are structured
D) validate (check resources for compliance and block non-compliant ones), mutate (automatically modify resources to fix issues before they're saved), generate (auto-create new resources like NetworkPolicies when triggers occur), and verifyImages (cryptographically verify container image signatures using Cosign/Sigstore)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Every Kyverno rule has exactly ONE type — validate, mutate, generate, or verifyImages. A single policy can contain multiple rules of different types, but each individual rule does one job.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Rule Types — Order of Execution in the Admission Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                       │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗ │
  │  ║              K Y V E R N O   A D M I S S I O N              ║ │
  │  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣ │
  │  ║                                                              ║ │
  │  ║  STEP 1: verifyImages  🪪  "Is this image signed?"          ║ │
  │  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║ │
  │  ║  │ Checks Cosign/Sigstore signatures on container images│    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ❌ FAIL = 403 Forbidden (image untrusted)             │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ✅ PASS = continue to next step                       │    ║ │
  │  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║ │
  │  ║                              │                               ║ │
  │  ║                              ▼                               ║ │
  │  ║  STEP 2: mutate  🔧  "Auto-fix what's broken"               ║ │
  │  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║ │
  │  ║  │ Automatically modifies the resource:                  │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Add default resource limits if missing              │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Add standard labels (app, tier, team)              │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Replace image tags with digests                     │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Inject sidecar containers                           │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ⚠️ Mutation failure = resource blocked                │    ║ │
  │  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║ │
  │  ║                              │                               ║ │
  │  ║                              ▼                               ║ │
  │  ║  STEP 3: validate  🔍  "Check for compliance"               ║ │
  │  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║ │
  │  ║  │ Checks the (potentially mutated) resource:            │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Is runAsNonRoot: true?                              │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Are resource limits set?                            │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Is readOnlyRootFilesystem: true?                   │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Are required labels present?                        │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ❌ FAIL = resource blocked                            │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ✅ PASS = resource saved to etcd                      │    ║ │
  │  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║ │
  │  ║                                                              ║ │
  │  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝ │
  │                                                                   │
  │  STEP 4: generate  🖨️  "Auto-create companion resources"        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Separate from admission flow — triggers on events:       │    │
  │  │ • New namespace created → generate NetworkPolicy          │    │
  │  │ • New deployment created → generate PDB                   │    │
  │  │ • ConfigMap created → clone to another namespace          │    │
  │  │ ⚠️ Runs asynchronously, not during kubectl apply         │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Critical order: verifyImages → mutate → validate. This order matters! The mutate step can fix issues BEFORE validation checks them. For example, a mutate rule could add runAsNonRoot: true to every pod, and then the validate rule checks that runAsNonRoot is indeed set — the pod passes because mutate already fixed it.

Generate is different: It doesn't run during kubectl apply. Generate rules listen for events (like "a new namespace was created") and then create resources in response. Think of it like an auto-reply rule in email — when X happens, automatically do Y.

For anihpj/jobpost, all four rule types are used: verifyImages ensures only Cosign-signed images run, mutate adds default resource limits and image registry prefixes, validate checks non-root and read-only root FS requirements, and generate auto-creates NetworkPolicies and PDBs for new deployments.

Fundamentals Q6 of 200
What is validationFailureAction in Kyverno, and what is the difference between Audit and Enforce modes?
A) Audit mode deletes non-compliant resources; Enforce mode quarantines them for manual review
B) Audit mode LOGS policy violations (in PolicyReports) but ALLOWS the resource to be created — useful for testing policies safely. Enforce mode BLOCKS non-compliant resources with a 403 Forbidden error — used for production policy enforcement. Best practice: start with Audit, review the PolicyReports, then switch to Enforce once you're confident the policy is correct
C) Audit mode applies only to new resources; Enforce mode applies to both new and existing resources
D) Audit mode sends violation emails to administrators; Enforce mode sends violations to a SIEM system
✅ Correct Answer: B

validationFailureAction is the most important setting in any Kyverno policy — it controls what happens when a resource violates the policy. Think of it like a security guard: Audit mode writes down the violation in a logbook but lets you pass; Enforce mode physically blocks the door.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Audit vs Enforce — Side-by-Side Behavior Comparison
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  POLICY: "All pods must run as non-root (runAsNonRoot: true)"   ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  AUDIT MODE (validationFailureAction: Audit)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml (runAsUser: 0)                      │
  │       │                                                        │
  │       ▼                                                        │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌───────────┐    ┌────────────┐               │
  │  │ API      │───▶│ AuthN/Z   │───▶│  KYVERNO   │               │
  │  │ Server   │    │ (RBAC)    │    │  (Audit)   │               │
  │  └──────────┘    └───────────┘    └─────┬──────┘               │
  │                                         │                       │
  │                    ┌────────────────────┴──────────────┐        │
  │                    │  Policy VIOLATED!                   │        │
  │                    │  → Write to PolicyReport: FAIL      │        │
  │                    │  → Log event in kyverno logs        │        │
  │                    │  → BUT... ALLOW resource creation!  │        │
  │                    └────────────────────┬──────────────┘        │
  │                                         │                       │
  │                                         ▼                       │
  │                                    ┌──────┐                    │
  │                                    │ etcd │  ✅ POD CREATED!   │
  │                                    └──────┘                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Result: Pod runs as root ⚠️ but you can SEE the violation     │
  │  in PolicyReports and fix the policy before switching modes.    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ENFORCE MODE (validationFailureAction: Enforce)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml (runAsUser: 0)                      │
  │       │                                                        │
  │       ▼                                                        │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌───────────┐    ┌────────────┐               │
  │  │ API      │───▶│ AuthN/Z   │───▶│  KYVERNO   │               │
  │  │ Server   │    │ (RBAC)    │    │ (Enforce)  │               │
  │  └──────────┘    └───────────┘    └─────┬──────┘               │
  │                                         │                       │
  │                    ┌────────────────────┴──────────────┐        │
  │                    │  Policy VIOLATED!                   │        │
  │                    │  → Write to PolicyReport: FAIL      │        │
  │                    │  → Return 403 FORBIDDEN to user    │        │
  │                    │  → Resource is BLOCKED!            │        │
  │                    └────────────────────────────────────┘        │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                    │
  │  Error from server: admission webhook "validate.kyverno.svc"    │
  │  denied the request: policy require-non-root/validate-non-root  │
  │  validation error: Running as root is not allowed.              │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Result: Pod is BLOCKED 🛡️ — cannot run as root, period.       │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Best practice: Always deploy new policies with validationFailureAction: Audit first. Let them run for a few days while you monitor PolicyReports. Once you've confirmed the policy is catching the right violations (and not blocking legitimate workloads), switch to Enforce. This prevents accidentally breaking production with a misconfigured policy.

For anihpj/jobpost, we started all 13 Kyverno policies in Audit mode for one week. After reviewing PolicyReports and confirming zero false positives, we switched critical policies (non-root, no hostPath, image verification) to Enforce mode while keeping less critical ones (label requirements) in Audit mode for ongoing monitoring.

Fundamentals Q7 of 200
What does the background: true setting do in a Kyverno policy, and when should you set it to false?
A) background: true runs the policy as a background process with lower CPU priority to avoid impacting cluster performance
B) background: true means the policy only runs at night during maintenance windows; false means it runs continuously
C) background: true (default) means Kyverno scans ALL EXISTING resources in the cluster, not just new ones — if an existing pod violates the policy, it shows up in PolicyReports. Set to false when your policy uses request.userInfo (background scans have no user context) or when you only want to check new/updated resources going forward
D) background: true enables a dark theme for the Kyverno dashboard; false uses the light theme
✅ Correct Answer: C

The background field controls whether Kyverno retroactively scans resources that already exist in the cluster. With background: true (the default), Kyverno periodically scans every existing resource and reports violations in PolicyReports — even resources created before the policy was installed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
background: true vs background: false — What Gets Scanned?
  TIMELINE: Policy "require-non-root" created at 2:00 PM
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  background: true (DEFAULT — Scan everything)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                 │
  │  10:00 AM               2:00 PM                3:00 PM          │
  │  ─────────              ───────                ───────          │
  │  Pod A created          POLICY                 Pod B created    │
  │  (runAsUser: 0)         INSTALLED              (runAsUser: 1000)│
  │      │                      │                       │           │
  │      │         ┌────────────┴───────────┐           │           │
  │      │         │  BACKGROUND SCAN runs  │           │           │
  │      │         │  Checks Pod A → FAIL   │           │           │
  │      │         │  (appears in PolR)     │           │           │
  │      │         └────────────────────────┘           │           │
  │      │                                              │           │
  │      │                           ┌──────────────────┴──────┐    │
  │      │                           │ ADMISSION check:         │    │
  │      │                           │ Pod B → runAsUser=1000 ✅│    │
  │      │                           └─────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ✅ PolicyReport shows:                                         │
  │     Pod A = FAIL (existing resource caught by background scan)  │
  │     Pod B = PASS (new resource checked at admission)            │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  background: false (Only check new/updated resources)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                 │
  │  10:00 AM               2:00 PM                3:00 PM          │
  │  ─────────              ───────                ───────          │
  │  Pod A created          POLICY                 Pod B created    │
  │  (runAsUser: 0)         INSTALLED              (runAsUser: 1000)│
  │      │                      │                       │           │
  │      │         ┌────────────┴───────────┐           │           │
  │      │         │  NO background scan    │           │           │
  │      │         │  Pod A is IGNORED      │           │           │
  │      │         │  (NOT in PolicyReport) │           │           │
  │      │         └────────────────────────┘           │           │
  │      │                                              │           │
  │      │                           ┌──────────────────┴──────┐    │
  │      │                           │ ADMISSION check:         │    │
  │      │                           │ Pod B → runAsUser=1000 ✅│    │
  │      │                           └─────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️  PolicyReport: Pod A is invisible — you have a root pod    │
  │     running and the policy doesn't know about it!              │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

When to use background: false: If your policy uses request.userInfo (checking who submitted the request), background scans will fail because there is no "user" during a background scan — set background: false to avoid spurious errors. Also use it for policies that only need to check resources going forward, not historical ones.

For anihpj/jobpost, most policies use background: true to ensure complete coverage. The only exception is a policy that checks who created each resource — that one uses background: false because background scans have no user identity.

Fundamentals Q8 of 200
How does Policy-as-Code (Kyverno) compare to traditional Manual Governance — what are the key advantages?
A) Manual governance is always more secure because humans can apply context and judgment that automated policies cannot replicate
B) Policy-as-code and manual governance are identical — the only difference is that policies are stored in YAML files instead of Word documents
C) Policy-as-code is only useful for small startups; large enterprises should always use manual governance with dedicated security review boards
D) Policy-as-Code (Kyverno) provides AUTOMATION (policies enforced 24/7 without human intervention), CONSISTENCY (every resource is checked against the same rules — no "I forgot to check this one"), VERSION CONTROL (policies stored in Git with full change history), and AUDIT TRAIL (PolicyReports provide verifiable evidence of compliance). Manual governance relies on human review, which is slow, inconsistent, error-prone, and produces no automated audit evidence
✅ Correct Answer: D

Policy-as-Code transforms security from a manual, human-dependent process (checklists, email approvals, periodic audits) into an automated, machine-enforced system that never forgets, never gets tired, and leaves a complete audit trail.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Manual Governance vs Policy-as-Code — Comparison
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FACTOR            MANUAL GOVERNANCE       POLICY-AS-CODE (KYVERNO)│
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Enforcement       Human reviews YAML      Kyverno checks EVERY    │
  │                    before approving         resource automatically  │
  │                    (maybe once per week)    (milliseconds per check)│
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Consistency       "Bob checks pods,        100% consistent —      │
  │                    Alice checks Deployments, same rules for every  │
  │                    they have different       resource, every time   │
  │                    standards"                                       │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Speed             Hours/days for review    Milliseconds — policy  │
  │                    committee to meet         blocks before etcd     │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Version Control   Word doc on SharePoint   Git repository with    │
  │                    "security_policy_v3_      full history, PR       │
  │                     final_FINAL.docx"        reviews, rollbacks     │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Audit Evidence    "Trust me, we reviewed    PolicyReports =        │
  │                    it" — no proof            timestamped, verifiable│
  │                                              compliance records    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Scalability       1 cluster = manageable    100 clusters = same   │
  │                    100 clusters = impossible  policies via GitOps   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Human Error       "Oops, missed the          Zero human error —   │
  │                    securityContext section"    machine enforcement  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Shift-Left        Security checked AFTER    Security checked      │
  │                    deployment (reactive)      BEFORE deployment     │
  │                                              (kyverno apply in CI) │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  REAL-WORLD EXAMPLE — anihpj/jobpost:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BEFORE KYVERNO (Manual):                                        │
  │  • Team lead reviews YAML files once a week                      │
  │  • Developer deploys privileged pod on Wednesday                 │
  │  • Review happens Friday — pod has been running 2 days!          │
  │  • Audit asks: "Are all pods non-root?" → "We have a policy..." │
  │    Auditor: "Show me proof." → "Uh..."                           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  AFTER KYVERNO (Policy-as-Code):                                 │
  │  • Policy enforced at admission — privileged pod BLOCKED         │
  │  • PolicyReports generated in real-time                           │
  │  • Auditor: "Show me proof." → `kubectl get polr -A`            │
  │    Output: 847 PASS, 0 FAIL, 0 WARN — here's your proof ✅       │
  │  • Policies stored in GitHub with full change history            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The bottom line: Manual governance doesn't scale. As clusters, teams, and deployment frequency grow, human review becomes the bottleneck and the weakest link. Policy-as-Code flips the model — security is part of the deployment pipeline, not a separate gate that slows everything down.

For anihpj/jobpost, switching from manual security reviews to Kyverno policies reduced deployment-to-production time from 3 days (waiting for review) to 30 minutes (automated policy check + deploy). Compliance audits are now a single kubectl command instead of a week-long evidence-gathering exercise.

1.2 YAML Manifests — The Language of KyvernoQ9-Q16

Fundamentals Q9 of 200
What is the complete anatomy of a Kyverno policy YAML manifest — what are its required sections and what does each one do?
A) A Kyverno policy has 4 required top-level sections: apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1 (tells Kubernetes this is a Kyverno resource), kind (Policy or ClusterPolicy — determines scope), metadata (name, labels, annotations for identification and organization), and spec (contains rules[], validationFailureAction, background, and failurePolicy — this is where the actual policy logic lives)
B) A Kyverno policy only needs two sections: a name and a list of commands to run — everything else is optional
C) A Kyverno policy requires 7 sections: apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec, status, rules, and conditions — all are mandatory
D) A Kyverno policy is defined entirely in a single-line JSON string with no YAML structure required
✅ Correct Answer: A

Every Kyverno policy follows the standard Kubernetes resource structure — the same 4 sections you find in a Deployment, Service, or ConfigMap: apiVersion, kind, metadata, and spec. If you know how to write a Deployment YAML, you already know the skeleton of a Kyverno policy.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Complete Kyverno Policy Anatomy — Annotated YAML Walkthrough
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1                                     │
  │     └─ "I am a Kyverno resource — use Kyverno's API schema"      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ② kind: ClusterPolicy                                           │
  │     └─ "I apply to ALL namespaces (use Policy for one namespace)" │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ③ metadata:                                                     │
  │       name: require-non-root-pods   ← "My unique identifier"     │
  │       annotations:                                                │
  │         policies.kyverno.io/title: Require Non-Root Pods         │
  │         policies.kyverno.io/category: Security                   │
  │         policies.kyverno.io/severity: high                        │
  │         policies.kyverno.io/subject: Pod                          │
  │         policies.kyverno.io/description: >-                        │
  │           All pods must run as non-root user for security         │
  │     └─ "My label, my description, my metadata"                   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ④ spec:                                                         │
  │       validationFailureAction: Enforce  ← "Block if violated"    │
  │       background: true                  ← "Scan existing pods"   │
  │       failurePolicy: Fail               ← "Deny if I error out" │
  │                                                                   │
  │       rules:                          ← THE RULES ARRAY           │
  │       ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │       │  - name: validate-runAsNonRoot    ← "This rule's name"│   │
  │       │    match:                         ← "WHICH resources?" │   │
  │       │      any:                                              │   │
  │       │      - resources:                                      │   │
  │       │          kinds:                                         │   │
  │       │          - Pod                ← "Target Pods"          │   │
  │       │    validate:                    ← "WHAT to check?"     │   │
  │       │      message: "Running as root is not allowed"         │   │
  │       │      pattern:                                           │   │
  │       │        spec:                                            │   │
  │       │          containers:                                    │   │
  │       │          - =(securityContext):                          │   │
  │       │              =(runAsNonRoot): true  ← "Must be true"   │   │
  │       └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KEY INSIGHT:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion + kind = WHAT type of resource this is               │
  │  metadata           = WHO this policy is (name, labels, desc)    │
  │  spec               = WHAT this policy DOES (rules, settings)    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Memory trick: Think of the 4 sections like a driver's license — apiVersion = the issuing authority (Kyverno), kind = the type of license (Policy vs ClusterPolicy), metadata = your photo and name (identification), spec = the actual permissions this license grants (what the policy enforces).

For anihpj/jobpost, every policy in the kyverno/ directory follows this exact structure. This consistency means any team member who understands one policy can read and modify any other policy — there's no special format to learn for different rule types.

Fundamentals Q10 of 200
What does apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1 mean, and why is it required at the top of every Kyverno policy?
A) kyverno.io/v1 is the URL where Kyverno policies are downloaded from — it tells kubectl where to fetch the policy template
B) apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1 tells the Kubernetes API server: "This resource belongs to the Kyverno API group (kyverno.io), and it uses version v1 of that API." This is how Kubernetes routes the resource to the correct controller — Kyverno's controller watches for resources with this apiVersion and processes them as policies. Without it, Kubernetes would reject the resource as an unknown type
C) kyverno.io/v1 specifies the minimum Kubernetes version required to run this policy — policies won't work on older clusters
D) apiVersion is optional in Kyverno policies — it's only included for documentation purposes and has no functional effect
✅ Correct Answer: B

The apiVersion field is Kubernetes' routing mechanism — it ensures your YAML reaches the right controller. Every custom resource in Kubernetes (including Kyverno policies) is identified by its API group and version.

📝 Detailed Explanation
How apiVersion Routes Resources to the Right Controller
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f policy.yaml                                    │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  API SERVER reads apiVersion + kind from the YAML:        │    │
  │  │                                                           │    │
  │  │  "apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1, kind: ClusterPolicy"        │    │
  │  │                                                           │    │
  │  │  → Routes to: Kyverno admission controller               │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  COMPARISON — Same apiVersion pattern across K8s resources:      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  RESOURCE          APIVERSION           KIND              │    │
  │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
  │  │  Pod               v1                   Pod               │    │
  │  │  Deployment        apps/v1              Deployment        │    │
  │  │  Ingress           networking.k8s.io/v1 Ingress           │    │
  │  │  Kyverno Policy    kyverno.io/v1        Policy            │    │
  │  │  Kyverno CLPolicy  kyverno.io/v1        ClusterPolicy     │    │
  │  │  Kyverno PolR      wgpolicyk8s.io/v1alpha2 PolicyReport   │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  BREAKING DOWN "kyverno.io/v1":                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  kyverno.io  ← API GROUP (the organization/domain)       │    │
  │  │  /v1         ← API VERSION (v1 = stable, v1beta1 = beta) │    │
  │  │                                                           │    │
  │  │  This is how Kyverno REGISTERS itself with Kubernetes:    │    │
  │  │  $ kubectl api-resources | grep kyverno                   │    │
  │  │  policies                      kyverno.io/v1     true     │    │
  │  │  clusterpolicies               kyverno.io/v1     false    │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why versions matter: The v1 means this is the stable API version. Kyverno previously used v1beta1 during development. As the API matured and became stable, it graduated to v1. Older policies using v1beta1 still work but should be migrated to v1. The version also determines which fields are available — newer versions may add features that older versions don't support.

For anihpj/jobpost, all 13 policies use apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1 — the stable, production-ready API version. When we first set up Kyverno, we validated this by running kubectl api-resources | grep kyverno to confirm the CRDs were registered correctly.

Fundamentals Q11 of 200
In a Kyverno policy YAML, the kind field can be Policy or ClusterPolicy. Which one is namespaced and which is cluster-wide?
A) Both are cluster-wide — the kind field is just cosmetic and doesn't affect scope
B) kind: Policy is NAMESPACED — it must be created in a specific namespace and only applies to resources within that namespace. kind: ClusterPolicy is CLUSTER-WIDE — it applies to resources in ALL namespaces and is not tied to any single namespace
C) kind: Policy is cluster-wide and kind: ClusterPolicy is namespaced — the naming is counterintuitive
D) kind: Policy applies only to Pod resources; kind: ClusterPolicy applies to all resource types including Deployments and Services
✅ Correct Answer: B

The kind field is the simplest but most consequential choice in a Kyverno policy — it determines where the policy lives and what it can reach. Policy is namespaced (like a Deployment), ClusterPolicy is cluster-scoped (like a Node or PersistentVolume).

📝 Detailed Explanation
kind: Policy vs kind: ClusterPolicy — YAML Declaration
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kind: Policy (NAMESPACED)                                       │
  │  ─────────────────────────                                       │
  │  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1                                       │
  │  kind: Policy                  ← Namespaced scope                │
  │  metadata:                                                       │
  │    name: require-anihpj-labels                                   │
  │    namespace: anihpj          ← REQUIRED — which namespace?      │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    rules:                                                        │
  │    - name: check-labels                                          │
  │      match:                                                      │
  │        any:                                                      │
  │        - resources:                                              │
  │            kinds: [Pod]                                          │
  │            namespaces: [anihpj]  ← Can ONLY target "anihpj" ns   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  $ kubectl apply -f policy.yaml -n anihpj  ← Must specify -n     │
  │  $ kubectl get policy -n anihpj           ← Must specify -n     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  USE CASE: Team-specific rules, tenant isolation                 │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  kind: ClusterPolicy (CLUSTER-WIDE)                              │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────                           │
  │  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1                                       │
  │  kind: ClusterPolicy           ← Cluster-wide scope              │
  │  metadata:                                                       │
  │    name: require-non-root-all ← NO namespace in metadata!        │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    rules:                                                        │
  │    - name: check-runAsNonRoot                                    │
  │      match:                                                      │
  │        any:                                                      │
  │        - resources:                                              │
  │            kinds: [Pod]                                          │
  │            # NO namespaces: field — applies to ALL namespaces    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  $ kubectl apply -f cluster-policy.yaml  ← NO -n flag            │
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicy             ← NO -n flag            │
  │                                                                   │
  │  USE CASE: Organization-wide security standards                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Memory trick: Policy = 6 letters, needs a namespace (like "I need a home"). ClusterPolicy = 14 letters, is "too big for one namespace" — it lives everywhere. Also: kubectl get policy -n anihpj (needs -n) vs kubectl get clusterpolicy (no -n).

For anihpj/jobpost, we use ClusterPolicy for global rules (all pods non-root, no hostPath anywhere) and Policy for app-specific rules that only need to apply within the anihpj namespace.

Fundamentals Q12 of 200
What is the metadata section in a Kyverno policy, and what are the key fields — name, annotations, and labels — used for?
A) metadata is optional — Kyverno policies work fine without it, it's just for human readability
B) metadata only stores the policy version number and last-modified timestamp — nothing else is supported
C) metadata is REQUIRED and contains: name (unique identifier for the policy — used in kubectl get/describe), annotations (key-value metadata for tools and UIs — e.g., policies.kyverno.io/title, policies.kyverno.io/category, policies.kyverno.io/severity), and labels (organizational tags for grouping, filtering, and selection — e.g., app: kyverno, team: platform). Annotations drive the Kyverno dashboard, policy reports, and CLI output
D) metadata in Kyverno policies is completely different from standard Kubernetes metadata and uses a custom schema unique to Kyverno
✅ Correct Answer: C

The metadata section follows the exact same Kubernetes conventions as every other resource — Deployments, Services, Pods all use the same metadata structure. Kyverno adds special annotations that tools and dashboards consume.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Policy Metadata — Annotated Breakdown
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  metadata:                                                       │
  │    # ═══ REQUIRED ═══                                           │
  │    name: require-non-root-pods    ← UNIQUE identifier            │
  │    │  • Used in: kubectl get cpol, kubectl describe cpol         │
  │    │  • Must be lowercase, use hyphens (not underscores)         │
  │    │  • Maximum 63 characters                                    │
  │    │                                                             │
  │    # ═══ OPTIONAL BUT RECOMMENDED ═══                            │
  │    annotations:                     ← Key-value metadata          │
  │      policies.kyverno.io/title: "Require Non-Root Pods"          │
  │      │  → Display name in Kyverno dashboard & CLI               │
  │      │                                                           │
  │      policies.kyverno.io/category: "Security"                    │
  │      │  → Groups policies: Security, Pod Security, Best Practices│
  │      │                                                           │
  │      policies.kyverno.io/severity: "high"                        │
  │      │  → high, medium, low — used for filtering & alerting     │
  │      │                                                           │
  │      policies.kyverno.io/subject: "Pod"                          │
  │      │  → Which resource type this policy targets               │
  │      │                                                           │
  │      policies.kyverno.io/description: >-                          │
  │        All pods must run as non-root user. This prevents          │
  │        container escape and host compromise.                      │
  │      │  → Human-readable policy purpose                          │
  │      │                                                           │
  │      pod-policies.kyverno.io/autogen-controllers: "none"         │
  │      │  → Controls autogen behavior for Pod policies             │
  │      │                                                           │
  │    labels:                           ← Organizational tags       │
  │      app: kyverno                                                │
  │      team: platform-security                                     │
  │      environment: production                                     │
  │    │  → Standard K8s labels for filtering:                       │
  │    │  kubectl get cpol -l team=platform-security                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why annotations matter: The Kyverno dashboard, CLI output, and PolicyReports all use these annotations to enrich the display. Without them, a policy is just a name; with them, it has a human-readable title, a severity level, and a category — making it much easier to manage dozens or hundreds of policies across a cluster.

For anihpj/jobpost, every policy in our kyverno/ directory includes all recommended annotations. This means kubectl get cpol shows clean, organized output, and the Kyverno dashboard groups our policies by category (Security, Best Practices, Supply Chain) for easy navigation.

Fundamentals Q13 of 200
What is the spec section in a Kyverno policy, and what are its key sub-fields: rules, validationFailureAction, background, and failurePolicy?
A) spec is THE MOST IMPORTANT section — it defines what the policy actually DOES. It contains: rules[] (the array of validate/mutate/generate/verifyImages rules — this is where the logic lives), validationFailureAction (Audit vs Enforce), background (scan existing resources?), and failurePolicy (Fail vs Ignore — what happens if the policy itself errors out during evaluation)
B) spec is optional — Kyverno uses default rules if you omit it, and validationFailureAction always defaults to Enforce
C) spec only contains the policy version number and the Kubernetes version it's compatible with
D) spec is where you define which cluster nodes the policy runs on — similar to nodeSelector in a Pod spec
✅ Correct Answer: A

spec is the engine room of a Kyverno policy — it's where you define what rules to enforce, how strictly to enforce them, and what to do when things go wrong. Every other section (apiVersion, kind, metadata) identifies the policy; spec makes it DO something.

📝 Detailed Explanation
spec Section — The 4 Key Fields
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════    │
  │    ① validationFailureAction: Enforce   ← "HOW strict?"         │
  │       • Audit  = Log violation, allow resource (test mode)       │
  │       • Enforce = Block non-compliant resources (production)     │
  │    ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════    │
  │    ② background: true                   ← "Check OLD resources?" │
  │       • true  = Scan existing resources + new ones               │
  │       • false = Only check new/updated resources                 │
  │    ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════    │
  │    ③ failurePolicy: Fail                ← "Policy errors?"       │
  │       • Fail   = If policy evaluation errors, BLOCK the request  │
  │       • Ignore = If policy errors, ALLOW the request (risky!)    │
  │    ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════    │
  │    ④ rules:                             ← "WHAT to enforce?"     │
  │       - name: validate-non-root                                  │
  │         match:                                                   │
  │           any:                                                   │
  │           - resources:                                           │
  │               kinds: [Pod]                                       │
  │         validate:                                                │
  │           message: "Root user not allowed"                       │
  │           pattern:                                               │
  │             spec:                                                │
  │               containers:                                        │
  │               - securityContext:                                 │
  │                   runAsNonRoot: true    ← THE ACTUAL CHECK       │
  │                                                                   │
  │       - name: mutate-add-labels                                  │
  │         match: { ... }                                           │
  │         mutate: { ... }                                          │
  │                                                                   │
  │       - name: generate-networkpolicy                             │
  │         match: { ... }                                           │
  │         generate: { ... }                                        │
  │                                                                   │
  │       A policy can have MULTIPLE rules — each with ONE type      │
  │       (validate, mutate, generate, or verifyImages)              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Default values: If you omit validationFailureAction, it defaults to Audit (safe for testing). If you omit background, it defaults to true (scans everything). If you omit failurePolicy, it defaults to Fail (secure by default — errors block the request). Always explicitly set these for clarity.

For anihpj/jobpost, every policy explicitly declares all 4 spec fields. This makes the policy's behavior immediately obvious to anyone reading the YAML — no guessing about defaults.

Fundamentals Q14 of 200
Kyverno policies are written in YAML. What are the most common YAML indentation mistakes beginners make, and how do you avoid them?
A) YAML indentation doesn't matter in Kyverno policies — the parser ignores whitespace entirely
B) The only mistake is using tabs instead of spaces — as long as you use spaces, any number of spaces works
C) YAML mistakes are automatically fixed by kubectl when you apply the policy, so you don't need to worry about them
D) Common mistakes include: (1) mixing tabs and spaces — YAML requires spaces only, (2) inconsistent indentation within the same level — siblings must have identical indentation, (3) incorrect nesting of list items — items under a hyphen must be indented further than their parent, (4) misplacing fields at the wrong depth — e.g., putting match at the spec level instead of inside a rule. Always test with kyverno apply policy.yaml -r resource.yaml before applying to the cluster, and use a YAML linter or VS Code's YAML extension with Kyverno schema support
✅ Correct Answer: D

YAML is whitespace-sensitive — a single extra space can change the meaning of a policy or cause kubectl to reject it entirely. Kyverno policies are deeply nested (spec → rules → match → resources → kinds), making indentation errors especially common.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Common YAML Indentation Mistakes in Kyverno Policies
  ❌ MISTAKE 1: Tabs instead of spaces
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  rules:                                          │
  │  → name: my-rule    ← TAB character — REJECTED! │
  │    match:                                        │
  │  Fix: Use SPACES only. Configure your editor     │
  │  to replace tabs with 2 or 4 spaces.            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ❌ MISTAKE 2: Inconsistent indentation (siblings)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                           │
  │    validationFailureAction: Enforce   ← 4 spaces │
  │      background: true                ← 6 spaces — WRONG! │
  │  Fix: All sibling fields must start at the       │
  │  same column. Both should be at 4 spaces.        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ❌ MISTAKE 3: List item nesting
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  rules:                                          │
  │  - name: my-rule                                 │
  │    match:               ← 4 spaces (correct)     │
  │      any:               ← 6 spaces (correct)     │
  │      - resources:       ← 6 spaces — WRONG!      │
  │  Fix: Items under a list (-) must be indented    │
  │  more than the hyphen: 8 spaces after "any:"      │
  │    Correct:                                       │
  │      any:                                         │
  │      - resources:       ← Item under any: list  │
  │          kinds:          ← Further indented      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ❌ MISTAKE 4: Wrong depth (field at wrong level)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                           │
  │    match:               ← WRONG! match goes      │
  │      any:                  INSIDE a rule, not    │
  │        resources:          at spec level         │
  │  Fix:                                             │
  │  spec:                                           │
  │    rules:                 ← match lives here     │
  │    - name: my-rule                               │
  │      match:                                      │
  │        any:                                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ✅ BEST PRACTICE: Test locally before applying
  $ kyverno apply policy.yaml --resource test-pod.yaml
  • Catches indentation errors instantly
  • Shows which resources pass/fail
  • No risk to the cluster

VS Code tip: Install the "YAML" extension by Red Hat and add the Kyverno JSON schema. This gives you autocomplete, validation, and red squiggly lines for indentation errors — catching mistakes before you even save the file. Also: kyverno apply is your best friend — it validates both policy syntax AND logic against test resources.

For anihpj/jobpost, all 13 policies use consistent 2-space indentation and are validated through CI/CD with kyverno apply before merging to the main branch.

Fundamentals Q15 of 200
What are Kyverno annotations like policies.kyverno.io/scored, policies.kyverno.io/category, and policies.kyverno.io/severity used for?
A) These annotations control the policy's runtime behavior — e.g., scored=true makes the policy Enforce mode, severity=high blocks more resources
