B) These are METADATA annotations for tooling and dashboards — they don't affect policy enforcement. scored (true/false) indicates whether violations count toward compliance scores, category groups policies by topic (Security, Best Practices, etc.), and severity (high/medium/low) helps prioritize remediation. The Kyverno dashboard, PolicyReports, and CLI use these to organize and display policy results
C) These annotations are required by the Linux Foundation for KCA certification — policies without them won't be accepted in the exam
D) These annotations are Kubernetes built-in fields that every resource type uses — they're not specific to Kyverno
✅ Correct Answer: B

Kyverno annotations are purely informational — they enrich the display but never change how a policy behaves. Think of them like tags on a blog post: they help organize and filter, but the post content is what matters.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Annotations — How They Appear in Tools
  POLICY YAML:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  metadata:                                                       │
  │    annotations:                                                   │
  │      policies.kyverno.io/scored: "true"                          │
  │      policies.kyverno.io/category: "Pod Security"                │
  │      policies.kyverno.io/severity: "high"                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KYVERNO CLI OUTPUT (kyverno apply):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Policy : require-non-root                                       │
  │  Category : Pod Security           ← FROM annotation            │
  │  Severity : high                   ← FROM annotation            │
  │  Scored   : true                   ← FROM annotation            │
  │  Rule     : validate-runAsNonRoot  │ PASS (1/1)                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  POLICY REPORT (kubectl get polr):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NAME                    KIND      CATEGORY       SEVERITY  RESULT│
  │  require-non-root        validate  Pod Security   high      pass  │
  │  require-resource-limits validate  Best Practices medium    fail  │
  │  block-hostpath          validate  Security       high      pass  │
  │  verify-api-image        image     Supply Chain   high      skip  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  COMMON ANNOTATION VALUES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  policies.kyverno.io/scored:                                      │
  │    true  → Counts toward compliance score (default)              │
  │    false → Informational only, doesn't affect score              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  policies.kyverno.io/category:                                    │
  │    "Security"           "Pod Security"                           │
  │    "Best Practices"     "Supply Chain"                            │
  │    "Multi-Tenancy"      "Operations"                             │
  │                                                                   │
  │  policies.kyverno.io/severity:                                    │
  │    high   → Must fix immediately                                 │
  │    medium → Should fix in next sprint                             │
  │    low    → Nice to have, not urgent                             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Key insight: Changing scored: false to scored: true doesn't make the policy stricter — it just means violations now count toward compliance scores. The actual enforcement is controlled by validationFailureAction (Audit vs Enforce), not by annotations.

For anihpj/jobpost, we categorize all 13 policies and set severity based on risk: high for non-root and image verification, medium for resource limits and hostPath blocking, low for label requirements.

Fundamentals Q16 of 200
What are the best practices for naming Kyverno policies and their rules?
A) Policy names should be randomly generated UUIDs to avoid naming conflicts — human-readable names are discouraged
B) Policy names can be anything — there are no conventions or best practices because kubectl handles name conflicts automatically
C) Best practices: (1) Use descriptive, lowercase-hyphenated names that describe what the policy DOES — e.g., require-non-root-pods, block-hostpath-volumes, add-default-resource-limits. (2) Prefix with the action: require- for validation, block- for denial, add- for mutation, generate- for generation, verify- for image verification. (3) Rule names should be unique within a policy and describe the specific check — e.g., validate-runAsNonRoot, mutate-add-labels. (4) Keep names under 63 characters (Kubernetes limit)
D) Policy names should always include the team name and date created — e.g., platform-team-2024-03-15-policy-7
✅ Correct Answer: C

Good naming is self-documenting code. A well-named policy tells you what it does without reading the YAML. The verb-first convention (require-, block-, add-, generate-, verify-) makes it instantly clear whether a policy validates, mutates, generates, or verifies.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Naming Conventions — Anihpj Example
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  POLICY NAMING PATTERN: [verb]-[what]-[on-what]                  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  VERBS (maps to rule type):                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ require-     │ validate     │ "This MUST be true"         │   │
  │  │ block-       │ validate/deny│ "This must NOT happen"      │   │
  │  │ add-         │ mutate       │ "Auto-add this if missing"  │   │
  │  │ generate-    │ generate     │ "Auto-create this resource" │   │
  │  │ verify-      │ verifyImages │ "Check image signature"     │   │
  │  │ restrict-    │ validate     │ "Limit this to..."          │   │
  │  │ enforce-     │ validate     │ "Enforce this standard"     │   │
  │  │ cleanup-     │ cleanup      │ "Delete old resources"      │   │
  │  └──────────────┴──────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ANIHJP/JOBPOST POLICY NAMES (kyverno/ directory):               │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  01-require-labels.yaml          ← "Pods MUST have labels" │  │
  │  │  02-require-resource-limits.yaml ← "MUST have CPU/mem"    │  │
  │  │  03-block-privileged.yaml        ← "NO privileged pods"   │  │
  │  │  04-enforce-non-root.yaml        ← "MUST run as non-root" │  │
  │  │  05-readonly-rootfs.yaml         ← "Root FS must be RO"  │  │
  │  │  06-drop-capabilities.yaml       ← "Drop ALL caps"        │  │
  │  │  07-restrict-pvc-size.yaml       ← "PVC max 50Gi"         │  │
  │  │  08-block-hostpath.yaml          ← "NO hostPath volumes"  │  │
  │  │  09-add-networkpolicy.yaml       ← "Auto-create NP"       │  │
  │  │  10-verify-api-image.yaml        ← "Verify image sig"     │  │
  │  │  11-add-image-registry.yaml      ← "Add registry prefix"  │  │
  │  │  12-generate-pdb.yaml            ← "Auto-create PDB"      │  │
  │  │  13-cleanup-old-reports.yaml     ← "Delete old reports"   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  RULE NAMING WITHIN A POLICY:                                     │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Policy: require-non-root-pods                              │  │
  │  │    rule: validate-runAsNonRoot    ← "validate" + what       │  │
  │  │    rule: validate-runAsUser       ← specific check          │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │  Policy: add-default-resources                               │  │
  │  │    rule: mutate-add-cpu-limit     ← "mutate" + what         │  │
  │  │    rule: mutate-add-memory-limit  ← each distinct mutation  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

DON'T: Use generic names like policy1, security-rule, or test — you'll regret it when you have 50 policies and can't tell which is which. DO: Use the verb-first convention consistently across your entire team.

For anihpj/jobpost, the numbered prefix (01-, 02-, etc.) indicates the recommended application order — foundational policies first, then additive, then cleanup. This numbering is in the filename only, not in the metadata.name field.

1.3 Admission Controllers — How Kyverno Intercepts RequestsQ17-Q28

Fundamentals Q17 of 200
What is a Kubernetes Admission Controller, and where does it sit in the API request lifecycle?
A) An Admission Controller is a firewall that sits in front of the Kubernetes API server and filters network traffic based on IP addresses
B) An Admission Controller is a plugin that intercepts API requests AFTER authentication and authorization, but BEFORE the resource is persisted to etcd. It can MODIFY (mutating admission) or REJECT (validating admission) resources. Kyverno registers itself as both a Mutating and Validating admission webhook
C) An Admission Controller is a Grafana dashboard that monitors which users are logging into the cluster
D) An Admission Controller is a command-line tool that replaces kubectl for managing cluster resources
✅ Correct Answer: B

Admission controllers are Kubernetes' "last line of defense" — they run after authentication (who are you?) and authorization (are you allowed to do this?), giving them the final say on whether a resource is accepted or rejected. Kyverno is a dynamic admission controller that registers via webhooks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes API Request Lifecycle — Where Admission Controllers Fit
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                       │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗ │
  │  ║              K U B E R N E T E S   A P I   S E R V E R      ║ │
  │  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣ │
  │  ║                                                              ║ │
  │  ║  ① AUTHENTICATION (AuthN)                                    ║ │
  │  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║ │
  │  ║  │ "WHO are you?" — Check certificates, tokens, OIDC    │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ✅ User: alice@anihpj.com (valid certificate)        │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ❌ FAIL here → 401 Unauthorized                      │    ║ │
  │  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║ │
  │  ║                              │                               ║ │
  │  ║                              ▼                               ║ │
  │  ║  ② AUTHORIZATION (AuthZ — RBAC)                              ║ │
  │  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║ │
  │  ║  │ "Are you ALLOWED to do this?" — Check RBAC policies  │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ✅ Alice can create pods in namespace anihpj         │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ❌ FAIL here → 403 Forbidden                         │    ║ │
  │  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║ │
  │  ║                              │                               ║ │
  │  ║                              ▼                               ║ │
  │  ║  ③ MUTATING ADMISSION (Modify the request)                   ║ │
  │  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║ │
  │  ║  │ "Can we FIX anything before it's saved?"             │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ 🔧 Kyverno mutate rules run HERE                     │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Add default resource limits                        │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Inject sidecar containers                          │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Add standard labels                                │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ❌ Mutation failure → request blocked                │    ║ │
  │  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║ │
  │  ║                              │                               ║ │
  │  ║                              ▼                               ║ │
  │  ║  ④ VALIDATING ADMISSION (Accept or Reject)                   ║ │
  │  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║ │
  │  ║  │ "Does this resource COMPLY with policies?"           │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ 🔍 Kyverno validate rules run HERE                   │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Is runAsNonRoot: true?                             │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Are resource limits set?                           │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ • Are required labels present?                       │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ✅ PASS → resource saved to etcd                     │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ❌ FAIL → 403 Forbidden with policy message          │    ║ │
  │  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║ │
  │  ║                              │                               ║ │
  │  ║                              ▼                               ║ │
  │  ║                          ┌──────┐                            ║ │
  │  ║                          │ etcd │  ✅ RESOURCE PERSISTED!    ║ │
  │  ║                          └──────┘                            ║ │
  │  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why this order matters: Mutating admission runs FIRST so that any fixes (like adding default resource limits) are applied BEFORE validation checks them. If validate ran first, it would reject resources that the mutating controller could have fixed.

For anihpj/jobpost, Kyverno registers as BOTH a mutating and validating webhook. This means every pod creation passes through Kyverno twice: once to fix issues (add labels, set defaults) and once to verify compliance (check non-root, verify image signatures).

Fundamentals Q18 of 200
What is Dynamic Admission Control, and how do admission webhooks work in Kubernetes?
A) Dynamic Admission Control uses HTTP webhooks (HTTPS callbacks) — when a resource is submitted, the API server sends an AdmissionReview request (JSON) to the registered webhook URL. The webhook (like Kyverno) evaluates the resource against policies and responds with an AdmissionReview response (allowed: true/false). This is "dynamic" because webhooks can be added/removed without recompiling or restarting the API server
B) Dynamic Admission Control means the Kubernetes API server automatically adjusts its admission rules based on cluster load and resource usage patterns
C) Dynamic Admission Control uses gRPC streaming to continuously monitor all cluster events in real-time and dynamically create firewall rules
D) Dynamic Admission Control is a feature that allows end users to vote on whether a resource should be allowed into the cluster via a web interface
✅ Correct Answer: A

Dynamic Admission Control is how Kyverno integrates with Kubernetes — through webhook registration. The API server calls Kyverno's HTTP endpoint for every matching request, sends the resource as JSON, and waits for a yes/no response. No API server restart needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Admission Webhook Flow — Kyverno's Integration
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STEP 1: Kyverno registers webhooks at startup                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  $ kubectl get validatingwebhookconfiguration              │  │
  │  │  NAME                          WEBHOOKS   AGE               │  │
  │  │  kyverno-resource-validating   1          5d               │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │  $ kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration                │  │
  │  │  NAME                          WEBHOOKS   AGE               │  │
  │  │  kyverno-resource-mutating     1          5d               │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  STEP 2: kubectl apply triggers the flow                         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                  │  │
  │  │       │                                                     │  │
  │  │       ▼                                                     │  │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌───────────┐                              │  │
  │  │  │ API      │───▶│ AuthN/Z   │  ✅ Authenticated            │  │
  │  │  │ Server   │    └───────────┘                              │  │
  │  │  └────┬─────┘                                                │  │
  │  │       │                                                     │  │
  │  │       │ ① POST /mutate?timeout=10s                          │  │
  │  │       ▼                                                     │  │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │  │
  │  │  │              KYVERNO (Mutating Webhook)               │   │  │
  │  │  │                                                       │   │  │
  │  │  │  Receives: AdmissionReview (JSON)                     │   │  │
  │  │  │  {                                                    │   │  │
  │  │  │    "request": {                                       │   │  │
  │  │  │      "uid": "abc-123",                                │   │  │
  │  │  │      "kind": {"group":"", "kind":"Pod"},              │   │  │
  │  │  │      "object": { /* the pod YAML as JSON */ },        │   │  │
  │  │  │      "userInfo": {"username": "alice"}                │   │  │
  │  │  │    }                                                  │   │  │
  │  │  │  }                                                    │   │  │
  │  │  │                                                       │   │  │
  │  │  │  Evaluates: match → mutate → returns JSON patch       │   │  │
  │  │  │                                                       │   │  │
  │  │  │  Responds: AdmissionResponse                          │   │  │
  │  │  │  {                                                    │   │  │
  │  │  │    "response": {                                      │   │  │
  │  │  │      "uid": "abc-123",                                │   │  │
  │  │  │      "allowed": true,                                 │   │  │
  │  │  │      "patch": "[{op:add, path:..., value:...}]"       │   │  │
  │  │  │    }                                                  │   │  │
  │  │  │  }                                                    │   │  │
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │  │
  │  │       │                                                     │  │
  │  │       │ ② POST /validate?timeout=10s                       │  │
  │  │       ▼                                                     │  │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │  │
  │  │  │             KYVERNO (Validating Webhook)              │   │  │
  │  │  │                                                       │   │  │
  │  │  │  Evaluates patched resource against validate rules    │   │  │
  │  │  │                                                       │   │  │
  │  │  │  Responds: {"response": {"allowed": true/false}}      │   │  │
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │  │
  │  │       │                                                     │  │
  │  │       ▼                                                     │  │
  │  │  ┌──────┐                                                   │  │
  │  │  │ etcd │  ✅ (if allowed: true)                            │  │
  │  │  └──────┘                                                   │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

"Dynamic" vs "Static": Static admission controllers are compiled into the API server binary and require restart to change (e.g., NamespaceLifecycle, LimitRanger). Dynamic admission controllers (like Kyverno) register via webhooks at runtime — install, upgrade, or remove them without touching the API server. This is why Kyverno can be installed with a simple helm install command.

For anihpj/jobpost, Kyverno was installed on all 6 clusters via Helm in under 5 minutes per cluster — no API server downtime, no node restarts, no service interruption.

Fundamentals Q19 of 200
Where exactly does Kyverno sit in the Kubernetes admission flow, and why does it register as BOTH a Mutating and Validating webhook?
A) Kyverno only registers as a Validating webhook — it doesn't support mutation at the admission level
B) Kyverno registers as a single webhook that handles both mutation and validation simultaneously in one call
C) Kyverno registers as TWO separate webhooks: a MUTATING webhook (runs FIRST — auto-fixes resources with mutate rules) and a VALIDATING webhook (runs SECOND — checks the now-fixed resource with validate rules). This order is critical: mutate fixes issues before validate checks for them, so resources that would fail validation can be automatically repaired by mutation
D) Kyverno sits before authentication — it can block requests before the user's identity is even verified
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kyverno's dual-webhook architecture is one of its most powerful design decisions. By registering as BOTH a mutating and validating webhook, Kyverno can fix AND verify resources in a single admission pipeline — no other admission controller needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Why Two Webhooks? — The Mutate-Then-Validate Pipeline
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  EXAMPLE: Pod submitted WITHOUT resource limits                   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                       │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────┐              │
  │  │ apiVersion: v1                                  │              │
  │  │ kind: Pod                                       │              │
  │  │ metadata: {name: anihpj-api}                   │              │
  │  │ spec:                                           │              │
  │  │   containers:                                   │              │
  │  │   - name: api                                   │              │
  │  │     image: anihpj-api:latest                    │              │
  │  │     # ❌ NO resources section!                  │              │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────┘              │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗ │
  │  ║  WEBHOOK 1: MUTATING (mutate rules run)                     ║ │
  │  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║ │
  │  ║  │ Policy: add-default-resource-limits                  │    ║ │
  │  ║  │   Rule: mutate-add-resources                         │    ║ │
  │  ║  │                                                       │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ "Pod has no resources section → Let me add defaults" │    ║ │
  │  ║  │                                                       │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ JSON Patch applied:                                   │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ [{op: "add",                                          │    ║ │
  │  ║  │   path: "/spec/containers/0/resources",               │    ║ │
  │  ║  │   value: {requests: {cpu: "100m", memory: "128Mi"},   │    ║ │
  │  ║  │           limits: {cpu: "500m", memory: "256Mi"}}]    │    ║ │
  │  ║  │                                                       │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ✅ Pod NOW has resource limits (mutated in memory)    │    ║ │
  │  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║ │
  │  ║                              │                               ║ │
  │  ║                              ▼ (mutated pod passed through)  ║ │
  │  ║  WEBHOOK 2: VALIDATING (validate rules run)                  ║ │
  │  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║ │
  │  ║  │ Policy: require-resource-limits                      │    ║ │
  │  ║  │   Rule: validate-resources-exist                      │    ║ │
  │  ║  │                                                       │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ "Does this pod have resource limits?"                │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ✓ Yes! (Thanks to the mutate rule above)             │    ║ │
  │  ║  │                                                       │    ║ │
  │  ║  │ ✅ PASS — pod is compliant!                           │    ║ │
  │  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║ │
  │  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝ │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌──────┐                                                       │
  │  │ etcd │  ✅ Pod saved WITH default resource limits!           │
  │  └──────┘                                                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  IF VALIDATE RAN FIRST (wrong order):
  • validate: "No resource limits! → ❌ REJECTED!"
  • Developer frustrated: "But I wanted Kyverno to add them!"
  • mutate never gets a chance to fix the resource

The mutate-then-validate pipeline is what makes Kyverno so powerful — it can automatically fix common misconfigurations AND verify the final result, all in one admission call. Without this dual registration, you'd need one admission controller to fix and another to verify.

For anihpj/jobpost, our mutate rules (add default limits, add image registry prefix, add standard labels) run BEFORE our validate rules (check non-root, check read-only root FS). This means developers can deploy simple pod YAMLs and Kyverno automatically adds production-safe defaults.

Fundamentals Q20 of 200
What is a ValidatingWebhookConfiguration, and how does Kyverno use it to register with the Kubernetes API server?
A) ValidatingWebhookConfiguration is a Kyverno-specific CRD that only Kyverno understands — other admission controllers use different mechanisms
B) ValidatingWebhookConfiguration is a configuration file stored on each node that tells kubelet which admission controllers to load
C) ValidatingWebhookConfiguration is a command-line flag passed to the API server at startup to enable admission control plugins
D) ValidatingWebhookConfiguration is a standard Kubernetes resource that tells the API server: "For resources matching these rules, send an AdmissionReview HTTPS POST to this URL (Kyverno's service) and wait for an allowed/denied response." It specifies: which resources trigger the webhook (pods, deployments, etc.), the webhook service URL (kyverno-svc.kyverno.svc:443), the CA bundle for TLS verification, and the failure policy (Fail vs Ignore)
✅ Correct Answer: D

ValidatingWebhookConfiguration is the registration form Kyverno submits to the API server. It says: "Dear API Server, whenever anyone tries to create, update, or delete these resource types, please call me at this URL and I'll tell you if it's okay."

📝 Detailed Explanation
ValidatingWebhookConfiguration — Anatomy of Kyverno's Registration
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: admissionregistration.k8s.io/v1                     │
  │  kind: ValidatingWebhookConfiguration                             │
  │  metadata:                                                        │
  │    name: kyverno-resource-validating                              │
  │  webhooks:                                                        │
  │  - name: validate.kyverno.svc                  ← Unique webhook ID│
  │                                                                   │
  │    clientConfig:                           ← HOW to call Kyverno  │
  │      service:                                                      │
  │        name: kyverno-svc                    ← Kyverno's Service   │
  │        namespace: kyverno                   ← Kyverno's namespace  │
  │        path: /validate                      ← API endpoint        │
  │      caBundle: LS0tLS...                   ← TLS CA certificate  │
  │                                                                   │
  │    rules:                                   ← WHEN to call Kyverno│
  │    - apiGroups:   [""]                                             │
  │      apiVersions: ["v1"]                                           │
  │      operations:  ["CREATE", "UPDATE"]    ← On these actions      │
  │      resources:   ["pods", "services", ...]  ← For these resources│
  │      scope:       "Namespaced"                                   │
  │                                                                   │
  │    failurePolicy: Fail                      ← IF Kyverno is down  │
  │    │  • Fail   = BLOCK all matching requests (secure)            │
  │    │  • Ignore = ALLOW all matching requests (risky!)           │
  │    │                                                              │
  │    timeoutSeconds: 10                       ← Max wait for response│
  │                                                                   │
  │    admissionReviewVersions: ["v1"]          ← API version to use  │
  │                                                                   │
  │    sideEffects: None                        ← No external effects │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  HOW TO INSPECT:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get validatingwebhookconfiguration                    │
  │  NAME                          WEBHOOKS   AGE                     │
  │  kyverno-resource-validating   1          5d                     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  $ kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration                      │
  │  NAME                          WEBHOOKS   AGE                     │
  │  kyverno-resource-mutating     1          5d                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Failure policy is critical: failurePolicy: Fail means if Kyverno is down or unreachable, ALL matching requests are BLOCKED — your cluster can't create pods until Kyverno recovers. This is the secure default. failurePolicy: Ignore would allow all requests during an outage, which defeats the purpose. For production, use Fail with high-availability Kyverno (3 replicas) to minimize outage risk.

For anihpj/jobpost, Kyverno runs with failurePolicy: Fail and 3 replicas across our 6-cluster lab. The ValidatingWebhookConfiguration was auto-created by the Helm install — we didn't need to write it manually.

Fundamentals Q21 of 200
What is a MutatingWebhookConfiguration, and how does Kyverno use it differently from ValidatingWebhookConfiguration?
A) MutatingWebhookConfiguration registers Kyverno's MUTATE rules — it tells the API server to send resources to Kyverno FIRST (before validating webhooks) so Kyverno can apply JSON patches to modify the resource (add labels, set defaults, inject sidecars). ValidatingWebhookConfiguration runs SECOND and only returns allowed/denied — it cannot modify resources. Mutating runs first so fixes are in place before validation checks them
B) MutatingWebhookConfiguration is an older, deprecated version of ValidatingWebhookConfiguration — they do the same thing but Mutating is being phased out
C) MutatingWebhookConfiguration handles pod mutations only; ValidatingWebhookConfiguration handles all other resource types
D) MutatingWebhookConfiguration is used only by OPA/Gatekeeper — Kyverno exclusively uses ValidatingWebhookConfiguration
✅ Correct Answer: A

The key difference is that mutating webhooks return a JSON patch (instructions to modify the resource), while validating webhooks return a simple allowed: true/false. Mutating always runs first so that any fixes are already applied when validation checks the resource.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Mutating vs Validating Webhook — Response Comparison
  VALIDATING WEBHOOK RESPONSE (simple yes/no):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  {                                                    │
  │    "response": {                                      │
  │      "uid": "abc-123",                               │
  │      "allowed": true,        ← or false              │
  │      "status": {                                      │
  │        "message": "Running as root is not allowed"   │
  │      }                                                │
  │    }                                                  │
  │  }                                                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  MUTATING WEBHOOK RESPONSE (modifications + yes/no):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  {                                                    │
  │    "response": {                                      │
  │      "uid": "abc-123",                               │
  │      "allowed": true,                                 │
  │      "patchType": "JSONPatch",                       │
  │      "patch": "W3sib3AiOiJhZGQiLCJwYXRoIjoiLi4uIn1d",│
  │      │                                               │
  │      │  Decoded:                                     │
  │      │  [                                            │
  │      │    {"op": "add",                               │
  │      │     "path": "/spec/containers/0/resources",   │
  │      │     "value": {"requests": {"cpu":"100m"}}}    │
  │      │  ]                                            │
  │    }                                                  │
  │  }                                                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  EXECUTION ORDER (API Server enforces this):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply                                        │
  │       │                                               │
  │       ▼                                               │
  │  ① ALL MutatingWebhooks run (alphabetically by name) │
  │     kyverno-resource-mutating: adds defaults, labels  │
  │       │  (sends JSON patch back to API server)        │
  │       ▼                                               │
  │  ② Object schema validation (is it valid YAML?)      │
  │       │                                               │
  │       ▼                                               │
  │  ③ ALL ValidatingWebhooks run (alphabetically)       │
  │     kyverno-resource-validating: checks compliance    │
  │       │  (returns allowed: true/false)                │
  │       ▼                                               │
  │  ✅ etcd (if all passed)                              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Multiple mutating webhooks: If you have multiple mutating webhooks (Kyverno + a custom one), they run in alphabetical order by name. Each one sees the output of the previous one. This means Kyverno's mutations can be further modified by downstream mutating webhooks.

For anihpj/jobpost, Kyverno's MutatingWebhookConfiguration is configured to intercept CREATE and UPDATE operations on Pods, Deployments, StatefulSets, DaemonSets, Jobs, and CronJobs — covering all workload types.

Fundamentals Q22 of 200
Walk through the complete API request lifecycle through Kyverno — what happens step by step from kubectl apply to etcd?
A) kubectl apply → Kyverno validates → etcd — Kyverno is the only step between the user and storage
B) kubectl apply → API Server receives request → Authentication (who?) → Authorization/RBAC (allowed?) → Mutating Admission (Kyverno mutate rules apply JSON patches) → Object Schema Validation → Validating Admission (Kyverno validate rules check compliance) → etcd (persist if all passed). This is the full Kubernetes admission chain — Kyverno sits at steps 4 and 6
C) kubectl apply → Kyverno pre-processes → API Server → etcd — Kyverno runs before the API server even sees the request
D) kubectl apply → etcd → Kyverno scans asynchronously → PolicyReport — Kyverno runs after the resource is already saved
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Kubernetes API server processes every request through a strict 7-step pipeline. Kyverno participates in steps 4 and 6 — but only for resources that match its webhook rules. Understanding this flow is critical for debugging "why didn't my policy fire?" questions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Complete kyverno Admission Flow — 7 Steps
  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml
       │
       ▼
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  ① AUTHENTICATION (AuthN)                                       ║
  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║
  ║  │ API Server checks: certificate, bearer token, OIDC       │    ║
  ║  │ → "User alice@anihpj.com" (identity established)         │    ║
  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║
  ║                              │                                   ║
  ║                              ▼                                   ║
  ║  ② AUTHORIZATION (AuthZ — RBAC)                                 ║
  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║
  ║  │ API Server checks: can alice CREATE pods in ns anihpj?    │    ║
  ║  │ → "Yes, RoleBinding allows it"                            │    ║
  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║
  ║                              │                                   ║
  ║                              ▼                                   ║
  ║  ③ MUTATING ADMISSION (runs FIRST)                              ║
  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║
  ║  │ API → POST /mutate → Kyverno mutating webhook             │    ║
  ║  │ Kyverno: match → preconditions → mutate → JSON patch      │    ║
  ║  │ Returns: {"allowed":true, "patch":[{add resource limits}]}│    ║
  ║  │ API Server applies JSON patch to the resource in memory   │    ║
  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║
  ║                              │                                   ║
  ║                              ▼                                   ║
  ║  ④ OBJECT SCHEMA VALIDATION                                     ║
  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║
  ║  │ API Server checks: is the (now-mutated) resource valid?   │    ║
  ║  │ Are required fields present? Types correct?               │    ║
  ║  │ This catches YAML that would fail even without policies   │    ║
  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║
  ║                              │                                   ║
  ║                              ▼                                   ║
  ║  ⑤ VALIDATING ADMISSION (runs SECOND — after mutations)         ║
  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║
  ║  │ API → POST /validate → Kyverno validating webhook         │    ║
  ║  │ Kyverno: match → preconditions → validate                 │    ║
  ║  │ Returns: {"allowed":true} or {"allowed":false,"msg":"..."}│    ║
  ║  │ ❌ If false → 403 Forbidden returned to user              │    ║
  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║
  ║                              │                                   ║
  ║                              ▼                                   ║
  ║  ⑥ PERSIST TO etcd                                              ║
  ║  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    ║
  ║  │ Resource (with mutations applied) written to etcd          │    ║
  ║  │ → "Pod anihpj-api-7d8f9 created in namespace anihpj"     │    ║
  ║  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
       │
       ▼
  Kyverno background controller (separate, asynchronous):
  • Updates PolicyReports with the result
  • Runs background scans on existing resources
  • Generate rules create companion resources

Key insight: If Kyverno is NOT installed, steps ③ and ⑤ are simply skipped — the API server doesn't call any webhooks. The resource goes directly from RBAC → schema validation → etcd. This is why installing Kyverno doesn't break existing workflows; it only adds checks to the pipeline.

For anihpj/jobpost, this 7-step flow processes every pod, deployment, and service creation. The entire admission chain typically completes in under 100ms with Kyverno adding ~10-20ms of latency per webhook call.

Fundamentals Q23 of 200
Why does mutating admission run BEFORE validating admission in the Kubernetes API request lifecycle — and what would go wrong if the order were reversed?
A) The order is arbitrary — Kubernetes randomly selects which runs first for load balancing purposes
B) Validating runs first because it's more important to block bad resources than to fix them — mutation is secondary
C) Mutating runs FIRST so that any auto-fixes (adding default resource limits, injecting sidecars, setting labels) are APPLIED before validation checks the resource. If validating ran first, it would REJECT resources that the mutating webhook could have fixed — defeating the purpose of mutation. The mutate-then-validate order ensures resources are "repaired before inspection"
D) Mutating and validating run simultaneously in parallel threads — neither waits for the other
✅ Correct Answer: C

The mutate-then-validate order is designed into Kubernetes itself — not a Kyverno-specific choice. The API server always calls all mutating webhooks first, applies their patches, then calls all validating webhooks on the patched resource.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Why Mutate-First Matters — Anihpj Real Example
  CORRECT ORDER (Mutate → Validate):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Developer deploys a simple pod (no resource limits):            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐          │
  │  │ containers:                                        │          │
  │  │ - name: api                                        │          │
  │  │   image: anihpj-api:v2                             │          │
  │  │   # NO resources section!                          │          │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘          │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  MUTATE: Kyverno adds defaults                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐          │
  │  │ containers:                                        │          │
  │  │ - name: api                                        │          │
  │  │   image: anihpj-api:v2                             │          │
  │  │   resources:                    ← AUTO-ADDED!      │          │
  │  │     requests: {cpu: 100m, mem: 128Mi}              │          │
  │  │     limits:   {cpu: 500m, mem: 256Mi}              │          │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘          │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  VALIDATE: Checks the NOW-FIXED resource                         │
  │  "Does this pod have resource limits?" → ✅ YES! PASS!           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Result: ✅ Pod created WITH default resource limits             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WRONG ORDER (Validate → Mutate — HYPOTHETICAL):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Developer deploys same pod (no resource limits):                │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  VALIDATE: Checks the original resource                          │
  │  "Does this pod have resource limits?" → ❌ NO! REJECTED!        │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Developer: "But I wanted Kyverno to add them!"                  │
  │  Admin: "Too late — validation already blocked it."              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Result: ❌ Pod REJECTED — mutate never got a chance             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

This is not Kyverno-specific: The mutation-first order is a Kubernetes architectural guarantee. If you have 3 mutating webhooks (Kyverno, Istio sidecar injector, Vault agent injector) and 2 validating webhooks (Kyverno, OPA), they ALL follow this order. The API server is the coordinator.

For anihpj/jobpost, this order means our mutate policies (add-default-resources, add-image-registry, add-standard-labels) always run before our validate policies (require-non-root, require-resource-limits, block-hostpath). Developers deploy simple YAML; Kyverno makes it production-ready.

Fundamentals Q24 of 200
What is the failurePolicy setting in webhook configurations, and what happens when Kyverno is down with Fail vs Ignore?
A) failurePolicy controls what happens when a specific policy rule fails — it has nothing to do with Kyverno being down
B) Fail and Ignore are identical in behavior — the names are misleading and both allow all requests during downtime
C) Ignore blocks all requests during downtime to prevent unvalidated resources from entering the cluster; Fail allows them
D) When Kyverno is unreachable (pod crash, network issue, upgrade in progress), failurePolicy: Fail BLOCKS all matching API requests — your cluster cannot create pods, deployments, or services until Kyverno recovers (secure but risky). failurePolicy: Ignore ALLOWS all matching requests — resources flow through unchecked, defeating the purpose of policy enforcement (risky but avoids cluster outage). Production best practice: use Fail with HA Kyverno (3 replicas + PDB)
✅ Correct Answer: D

failurePolicy answers the question: "If Kyverno itself is broken, should the cluster break too?" — Fail means yes (secure), Ignore means no (available but insecure). This is a fundamental trade-off between security and availability.

📝 Detailed Explanation
failurePolicy: Fail vs Ignore — During Kyverno Outage
  SCENARIO: Kyverno pod crashes (OOMKilled, node failure, upgrade)
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  failurePolicy: Fail (RECOMMENDED for production with HA)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                       │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  API Server → POST /validate → kyverno-svc                       │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       │  ⏱️ 10 seconds pass... no response                       │
  │       │  ⏱️ Timeout reached                                      │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ❌ Internal Server Error (500)                                  │
  │  "admission webhook 'validate.kyverno.svc' timed out"           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                       │
  │  Error from server: Internal error occurred: failed              │
  │  calling webhook "validate.kyverno.svc"                          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⚠️ NO new pods can be created until Kyverno recovers!          │
  │  ⚠️ NO deployments, services, configmaps — NOTHING!             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  failurePolicy: Ignore (RISKY — use only for development)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                       │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  API Server → POST /validate → kyverno-svc                       │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       │  ⏱️ Timeout reached                                      │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ✅ Request ALLOWED — webhook failure is IGNORED                 │
  │                                                                   │
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                       │
  │  pod/privileged-pod created    ✅                                │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⚠️ All policies are BYPASSED — privileged pods, hostPath,      │
  │  ⚠️ untrusted images — everything goes through!                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  PRODUCTION SOLUTION: High Availability Kyverno
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno \                          │
  │    --set replicaCount=3 \              ← 3 replicas              │
  │    --set podDisruptionBudget.minAvailable=2  ← keep 2 running   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  With 3 replicas: If one crashes, the other 2 handle requests   │
  │  without interruption. Use failurePolicy: Fail + HA Kyverno.    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Exam note: The CKA/CKS exams may test this distinction. Remember: Fail = secure (blocks if Kyverno is down), Ignore = available (allows if Kyverno is down). The Kyverno Helm chart defaults to Fail.

For anihpj/jobpost, all clusters use failurePolicy: Fail with 3 Kyverno replicas and a PDB requiring at least 2 available — ensuring that even during node drains or pod restarts, Kyverno continues enforcing policies without interruption.

Fundamentals Q25 of 200
What is webhookTimeoutSeconds in a Kyverno webhook configuration, and how should you tune it?
A) webhookTimeoutSeconds controls how long Kyverno waits before retrying a failed policy evaluation — default is 60 seconds
B) webhookTimeoutSeconds is the maximum time the API server waits for Kyverno to respond to an AdmissionReview request (default: 10 seconds for Kyverno, 30 seconds max per Kubernetes). If Kyverno takes longer (complex policies, slow API calls in context, heavy load), the API server times out and applies the failurePolicy. Tune it higher (20-25s) for complex policies; keep it low (5-10s) to prevent slow policies from impacting cluster responsiveness
C) webhookTimeoutSeconds controls how long Kyverno caches policy evaluation results — increasing it improves performance but may serve stale results
D) webhookTimeoutSeconds is the interval between Kyverno background scans — default is 3600 seconds (1 hour)
✅ Correct Answer: B

webhookTimeoutSeconds is your admission latency budget — how long the API server is willing to wait for Kyverno to say yes/no before giving up. Set it too low and legitimate policies time out; set it too high and slow policies delay every kubectl command.

📝 Detailed Explanation
webhookTimeoutSeconds — Tuning Guide
  DEFAULT: 10 seconds (Kyverno Helm chart)
  ─────────────────────────────────────────

  SCENARIO 1: Timeout too LOW (5 seconds)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Complex policy with API call in context:            │
  │  context:                                            │
  │  - name: registry-data                              │
  │    apiCall:                                          │
  │      urlPath: "https://registry.anihpj.com/v2/..."  │
  │                                                      │
  │  API call takes 3 seconds + policy evaluation 3s     │
  │  = 6 seconds total → EXCEEDS 5s timeout!            │
  │                                                      │
  │  Result: ❌ Timeout → failurePolicy applied          │
  │  Even though the policy WOULD have passed!           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  SCENARIO 2: Timeout too HIGH (25 seconds)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Every kubectl apply waits up to 25 seconds          │
  │  • Deploying 10 resources = 250 seconds!             │
  │  • CI/CD pipeline crawls                             │
  │  • Developers complain: "kubectl is so slow"        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RECOMMENDED VALUES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Simple policies (no API calls):   5-10 seconds      │
  │  Complex policies (API calls):     15-20 seconds     │
  │  Image verification (Cosign):      20-25 seconds     │
  │                                                      │
  │  Kubernetes maximum: 30 seconds                      │
  │  Kyverno default (Helm): 10 seconds                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  HOW TO CONFIGURE (Helm values.yaml):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kyverno:                                            │
  │    webhookTimeout: 15    ← seconds                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Monitor before tuning: Use kyverno_admission_review_duration_seconds Prometheus metric to see actual policy evaluation times before adjusting the timeout. If your P99 latency is 200ms, you don't need a 20-second timeout.

For anihpj/jobpost, we use the default 10-second timeout. Our 13 policies are simple enough that evaluation completes in under 50ms. For image verification (Cosign), which requires network calls to the registry and Rekor transparency log, we increased it to 20 seconds on the production cluster.

Fundamentals Q26 of 200
Why should you configure exclusion namespaces in Kyverno, and which namespaces should typically be excluded?
A) Exclusion namespaces prevent Kyverno from processing resources in critical system namespaces (kube-system, kyverno, kube-public, kube-node-lease) where policies could interfere with cluster operations. Without exclusions, a policy could accidentally block the CoreDNS pod, the kube-proxy DaemonSet, or even Kyverno itself — causing a cluster-wide outage. Exclusions are configured in the Kyverno ConfigMap or via the Helm values
B) Exclusion namespaces are purely for performance optimization — they have no security implications and skipping them only saves CPU cycles
C) Kyverno automatically excludes all system namespaces — no manual configuration is ever needed
D) Exclusion namespaces are configured per-policy in the match/exclude block, not globally — there is no global namespace exclusion mechanism in Kyverno
✅ Correct Answer: A

Exclusion namespaces are a critical safety mechanism — without them, a Kyverno policy could block Kubernetes from updating its own system components, causing cascading failures that are extremely difficult to recover from.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Exclusion Namespaces — Why They're Critical
  DISASTER SCENARIO (without exclusions):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Policy: "All pods must have resource limits"                    │
  │  (ClusterPolicy, applies to ALL namespaces)                      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ① CoreDNS pod in kube-system is restarted                       │
  │  ② New CoreDNS pod has NO resource limits                        │
  │  ③ Kyverno BLOCKS the CoreDNS pod!                              │
  │  ④ DNS resolution stops working cluster-wide                     │
  │  ⑤ Service discovery breaks — pods can't find each other         │
  │  ⑥ kubectl commands fail — API server can't resolve service URLs │
  │  ⑦ Cluster is effectively DEAD                                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WITH EXCLUSIONS (kyverno ConfigMap):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: v1                                                   │
  │  kind: ConfigMap                                                  │
  │  metadata:                                                        │
  │    name: kyverno                                                  │
  │    namespace: kyverno                                             │
  │  data:                                                            │
  │    resourceFilters: |                                             │
  │      [                                                           │
  │        {"kind":"*","namespace":"kube-system","action":"skip"},   │
  │        {"kind":"*","namespace":"kyverno","action":"skip"},       │
  │        {"kind":"*","namespace":"kube-public","action":"skip"},   │
  │        {"kind":"*","namespace":"kube-node-lease","action":"skip"}│
  │      ]                                                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  NAMESPACES TO ALWAYS EXCLUDE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kube-system        ← CoreDNS, kube-proxy, CNI, metrics-server  │
  │  kyverno            ← Kyverno itself (don't block yourself!)    │
  │  kube-public        ← Cluster-info ConfigMap                    │
  │  kube-node-lease    ← Node heartbeat leases                     │
  │  local-path-storage ← Storage provisioner (if used)             │
  │  cert-manager       ← TLS certificate automation               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Helm configuration: The Kyverno Helm chart pre-configures sensible exclusions for kube-system and kyverno. If you install additional system components (Istio, cert-manager, monitoring stack), add their namespaces to the exclusion list.

For anihpj/jobpost, we exclude kube-system, kyverno, and local-path-storage on all 6 clusters. Our application policies only target the anihpj namespace, so system namespaces are never affected — but the exclusion is still in place as a safety net.

Fundamentals Q27 of 200
What is the structure of an AdmissionReview request — the JSON payload the Kubernetes API server sends to Kyverno's webhook?
A) The AdmissionReview is a simple text string containing the kubectl command that was executed — Kyverno parses the command to determine what to do
B) The AdmissionReview is a binary protobuf message — it cannot be inspected as text and requires special tools to decode
C) The AdmissionReview is a JSON object with: kind: AdmissionReview, request.uid (unique ID), request.kind (Pod, Deployment, etc.), request.operation (CREATE/UPDATE/DELETE), request.object (the FULL resource YAML as JSON — this is what Kyverno's patterns match against), request.oldObject (previous version for UPDATEs), request.userInfo (who made the request), and request.namespace. This is the same payload for ALL admission webhooks — not Kyverno-specific
D) The AdmissionReview only contains the resource name and namespace — Kyverno must call back to the API server to get the full resource definition
✅ Correct Answer: C

The AdmissionReview is a standard Kubernetes API object — not Kyverno-specific. Every admission webhook (Kyverno, OPA, Istio, custom) receives the same JSON structure. Understanding it helps debug "why didn't my policy match?" questions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AdmissionReview — Full JSON Payload Anatomy
  POST /validate HTTP/1.1
  Content-Type: application/json

  {
    "kind": "AdmissionReview",
    "apiVersion": "admission.k8s.io/v1",
    "request": {

      "uid": "a1b2c3d4-e5f6-7890-abcd-ef1234567890",
      │  ← Unique ID for this admission request

      "kind": {"group": "", "version": "v1", "kind": "Pod"},
      │  ← WHAT type of resource (Pod, Deployment, Service...)

      "resource": {"group": "", "version": "v1", "resource": "pods"},
      │  ← Resource plural name

      "operation": "CREATE",
      │  ← CREATE, UPDATE, DELETE, or CONNECT

      "namespace": "anihpj",
      │  ← WHERE the resource is being created

      "userInfo": {
        "username": "system:serviceaccount:anihpj:api-sa",
        "groups": ["system:serviceaccounts", "system:authenticated"]
      },
      │  ← WHO made the request (for request.userInfo in policies)

      "object": {
        "apiVersion": "v1",
        "kind": "Pod",
        "metadata": {
          "name": "anihpj-api-7d8f9-xk2lm",
          "namespace": "anihpj",
          "labels": {"app": "anihpj-api"}
        },
        "spec": {
          "containers": [{
            "name": "api",
            "image": "anihpj-api:v2",
            "securityContext": {"runAsNonRoot": false}
            ← THIS IS WHAT KYVERNO PATTERNS MATCH AGAINST!
          }]
        }
      },
      │  ← The FULL resource as JSON (request.object in Kyverno)

      "oldObject": null
      │  ← Previous version (only populated for UPDATE operations)

    }
  }

Why this matters: When you write request.object.spec.containers[].securityContext.runAsNonRoot in a Kyverno policy, you're navigating this exact JSON structure. The request.object variable IS the "object" field in the AdmissionReview. Understanding the JSON structure makes writing complex JMESPath expressions much easier.

For anihpj/jobpost, our more advanced policies use request.userInfo to check which ServiceAccount created a resource, and request.oldObject for UPDATE operations that need to compare old vs new values.

Fundamentals Q28 of 200
How does Kyverno compare to other admission controllers like OPA/Gatekeeper and custom admission webhooks?
A) Kyverno, OPA/Gatekeeper, and custom webhooks are identical — they all use Rego for policy definitions and are interchangeable
B) Kyverno replaces the need for custom webhooks entirely — you cannot run Kyverno alongside other admission controllers
C) OPA/Gatekeeper is always the better choice because it supports more policy types than Kyverno
D) Kyverno uses Kubernetes-native YAML (no new language), supports mutate + generate + verifyImages (OPA is primarily validate-only), and is designed specifically for Kubernetes. OPA/Gatekeeper uses Rego (a custom policy language — more flexible but steeper learning curve), excels at complex conditional logic, and works beyond Kubernetes. Custom webhooks give maximum flexibility but require writing, deploying, and maintaining your own admission server. They can all run together — Kyverno for K8s-native policies, OPA for complex logic, custom webhooks for unique needs
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kyverno, OPA/Gatekeeper, and custom webhooks are complementary, not competing. Each has strengths: Kyverno for Kubernetes-native simplicity, OPA for cross-platform complex logic, custom webhooks for unique requirements. Many organizations run two or all three together.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Admission Controller Comparison Matrix
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FEATURE           KYVERNO        OPA/GATEKEEPER   CUSTOM WEBHOOK│
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Policy Language   YAML (native)  Rego (custom)    Any (Go, Py) │
  │  Learning Curve    🟢 Easy         🔴 Steep          🟡 Medium   │
  │  Validate          ✅              ✅                ✅           │
  │  Mutate            ✅              ✅ (limited)      ✅           │
  │  Generate          ✅              ❌                ❌           │
  │  VerifyImages      ✅              ❌                ❌           │
  │  Cleanup           ✅              ❌                ❌           │
  │  CLI Testing       ✅ (kyverno)    ✅ (opa test)     Manual      │
  │  K8s-Native UX     ✅              🟡 (Constraint)   N/A         │
  │  Beyond K8s        ❌              ✅ (Terraform,    ✅           │
  │                                    CI/CD, APIs)                  │
  │  Maintenance       🟢 Helm         🟡 Gatekeeper     🔴 Custom   │
  │  Written By        YAML DevOps     Rego Engineers   Developers   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  BEST FOR:                                                       │
  │  Kyverno:         Kubernetes-native policies, mutate/generate,   │
  │                   teams already comfortable with K8s YAML        │
  │  OPA/Gatekeeper:  Complex conditional logic, multi-platform      │
  │                   policy (K8s + Terraform + APIs), Rego experts  │
  │  Custom Webhook:  Unique requirements no tool supports,          │
  │                   integration with external systems              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  DEFENSE IN DEPTH (running Kyverno + OPA together):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                       │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  Kyverno mutate:    "Add default resource limits"               │
  │  Kyverno validate:  "Is runAsNonRoot: true?"                    │
  │  OPA validate:      "Does this user's department allow this?"   │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ✅ etcd                                                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Kyverno's unique advantages: No new language (YAML you already know), generate rules (auto-create NetworkPolicies, ResourceQuotas), verifyImages (Cosign/Sigstore native integration), cleanup policies (automated TTL-based resource deletion), and a built-in CLI for local testing.

For anihpj/jobpost, we chose Kyverno exclusively because our team is YAML-native, we needed mutate + generate + verifyImages, and we wanted zero learning curve for new team members. For organizations with existing Rego investments or multi-platform policy needs, OPA/Gatekeeper may be the better fit.

1.4 OCI Images — Understanding Container Images in PoliciesQ29-Q36

Fundamentals Q29 of 200
What is an OCI (Open Container Initiative) image, and what are its key components?
A) An OCI image is simply a tar.gz file containing application binaries — it has no standardized structure
B) An OCI image is a standardized package format consisting of: layers (filesystem changes — each RUN/COPY/ADD in a Dockerfile creates a layer), a manifest (metadata listing all layers + their digests), a config (runtime settings — entrypoint, env vars, exposed ports), and a digest (SHA256 hash of the manifest — the immutable identifier). Kyverno's verifyImages rule checks the image digest against Cosign signatures stored in the registry
C) An OCI image is a virtual machine disk image that contains a full operating system — it boots like a VM
D) An OCI image is Kubernetes-specific and can only be used with container runtimes that support the Kubernetes CRI
✅ Correct Answer: B

Understanding OCI images is essential for Kyverno's verifyImages rules — you're cryptographically verifying the image manifest's digest against a signature. If you don't understand what a digest is, you can't understand how image verification works.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OCI Image Anatomy — What Kyverno Verifies
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OCI IMAGE: registry.anihpj.com/anihpj-api:v2                    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  MANIFEST (metadata about the image)                       │   │
  │  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │   │
  │  │  │ {                                                   │    │   │
  │  │  │   "schemaVersion": 2,                              │    │   │
  │  │  │   "mediaType": "application/vnd.oci.image...",     │    │   │
  │  │  │   "config": {                                       │    │   │
  │  │  │     "digest": "sha256:abc123...",  ← Config digest │    │   │
  │  │  │     "size": 1500                                    │    │   │
  │  │  │   },                                                │    │   │
  │  │  │   "layers": [                                        │    │   │
  │  │  │     {"digest": "sha256:def456...", "size": 75MB},   │    │   │
  │  │  │     {"digest": "sha256:ghi789...", "size": 20MB},   │    │   │
  │  │  │     {"digest": "sha256:jkl012...", "size": 10MB}    │    │   │
  │  │  │   ]                                                 │    │   │
  │  │  │ }                                                   │    │   │
  │  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │   │
  │  │                                                             │   │
  │  │  IMAGE DIGEST = SHA256(entire manifest JSON)               │   │
  │  │  sha256:7a8b9c0d1e2f3a4b5c6d7e8f9a0b1c2d3e4f5a6b7c8d9e0f │   │
  │  │  ↑ THIS IS WHAT COSIGN SIGNS!                              │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  CONFIG (runtime settings)                                 │   │
  │  │  {                                                         │   │
  │  │    "Entrypoint": ["/app/entrypoint.sh"],                   │   │
  │  │    "Env": ["DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE=anihpj.settings"],     │   │
  │  │    "ExposedPorts": {"8000/tcp": {}},                       │   │
  │  │    "User": "1001"                                          │   │
  │  │  }                                                         │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  LAYERS (filesystem changes, stacked in order)             │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────┐                                      │   │
  │  │  │ Layer 4: Django   │ ← COPY . /app                       │   │
  │  │  ├──────────────────┤                                      │   │
  │  │  │ Layer 3: pip pkgs │ ← RUN pip install -r reqs.txt       │   │
  │  │  ├──────────────────┤                                      │   │
  │  │  │ Layer 2: Python   │ ← FROM python:3.12-slim              │   │
  │  │  ├──────────────────┤                                      │   │
  │  │  │ Layer 1: Debian   │ ← Base OS layer                      │   │
  │  │  └──────────────────┘                                      │   │
  │  │  Layers are content-addressed: digest = SHA256(content)    │   │
  │  │  If any byte changes → new digest → new manifest → new img │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why digests matter for Kyverno: The verifyImages rule checks Cosign signatures that are attached to the image DIGEST. If you reference an image by tag (anihpj-api:v2), the tag could point to a different digest tomorrow. Always use digests (anihpj-api@sha256:abc...) for immutable references.

For anihpj/jobpost, our CI/CD pipeline builds the Docker image, pushes it to the registry, signs it with Cosign, and Kyverno verifies the signature at admission time — ensuring only images built by our pipeline can run in production.

Fundamentals Q30 of 200
What is the difference between referencing a container image by tag (nginx:latest) vs by digest (nginx@sha256:abc123...), and why does this matter for Kyverno policies?
A) Tags are MUTABLE — nginx:latest can point to a completely different image tomorrow (the tag is a movable pointer). Digests are IMMUTABLE — nginx@sha256:abc... forever refers to the exact same image content. For Kyverno: image verification and supply chain security REQUIRE digests — you can't verify a signature on a tag that changes. Mutate rules can automatically replace tags with digests for immutability
B) Tags and digests are functionally identical — the only difference is that digests are longer strings
C) Tags are more secure than digests because they allow rolling back to previous versions; digests permanently lock you to one image
D) Digests are deprecated in favor of tags in newer versions of the OCI specification
✅ Correct Answer: A

Tags are convenient but dangerous — they're human-friendly labels that can silently change what code runs in production. Digests are inconvenient but secure — they cryptographically guarantee that you're running exactly what you think you're running.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Tags vs Digests — Why Kyverno Needs Digests
  TAGS (mutable — the "latest" trap):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Day 1:                                                          │
  │  $ docker push anihpj-api:v2                                     │
  │  v2 → sha256:abc123...   (safe, tested, secure)                 │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Day 30:                                                         │
  │  $ docker push anihpj-api:v2   ← SAME TAG, DIFFERENT IMAGE!     │
  │  v2 → sha256:xyz789...   (new code, possibly untested)          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⚠️ Pod with image: anihpj-api:v2 is now running DIFFERENT code │
  │  ⚠️ Cosign signature from Day 1 no longer matches!              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  DIGESTS (immutable — cryptographic guarantee):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Day 1:                                                          │
  │  $ docker push anihpj-api:v2                                     │
  │  sha256:abc123def456789... ← This hash FOREVER means this image │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Day 30:                                                         │
  │  $ docker push anihpj-api:v2                                     │
  │  sha256:xyz789ghi012345... ← NEW hash for the new image         │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ✅ image: anihpj-api@sha256:abc123... ← ALWAYS the Day 1 image │
  │  ✅ Cosign signature verified against this specific digest       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KYVERNO MUTATE RULE — Auto-replace tags with digests:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Policy: mutate-tag-to-digest                                    │
  │  Rule: For every pod using an image tag, replace with digest     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  BEFORE: image: anihpj-api:v2                                    │
  │  AFTER:  image: anihpj-api@sha256:abc123def456...               │
  │                                                                   │
  │  This is a BEST PRACTICE for supply chain security!             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  COMPARISON TABLE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FEATURE              TAG (v2, latest)    DIGEST (sha256:...)   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Mutability           Mutable (changes)   Immutable (permanent)  │
  │  Human readability    ✅ Easy              ❌ Hard (long hash)   │
  │  Supply chain security ❌ Unsafe             ✅ Cryptographically │
  │  Cosign verification  ❌ Can't verify       ✅ Verifiable        │
  │  Rollback             Tag points to old   Need old digest string │
  │  Caching              Same tag → cache hit New digest → re-pull │
  │  Kyverno recommended  ❌ Avoid in prod      ✅ Use in production │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Kyverno best practice: Use a mutate rule to automatically replace all image tags with digests at admission time. This gives you the convenience of using tags in your Deployment YAML while getting the security of digests at runtime. Combine with a verifyImages rule that checks Cosign signatures on the digest.

For anihpj/jobpost, our CI/CD pipeline tags images with the git commit SHA (anihpj-api:abc123), pushes to the registry, and signs with Cosign. Kyverno's mutate rule replaces the tag with the digest, and the verifyImages rule checks the Cosign signature — end-to-end supply chain security.

Fundamentals Q31 of 200
Why are container image tags mutable while digests are immutable, and what are the security implications?
A) Both tags and digests are immutable — the distinction is a myth and has no practical impact on Kubernetes
B) Tags are immutable because they're stored in the registry manifest; digests are mutable because they're computed locally
C) Tags are mutable because they are just pointers — nginx:latest is a label that the registry can reassign to any image digest at any time (when a new build is pushed). Digests are immutable because they are cryptographic hashes of the image content — sha256:abc... is mathematically bound to the exact bytes of the manifest. Security implication: deploying by tag means you might run different code tomorrow without changing your YAML; deploying by digest guarantees you always run the exact same image
D) Tags are only mutable in public registries like Docker Hub; private registries enforce tag immutability
✅ Correct Answer: C

Tags and digests serve fundamentally different purposes: tags are for humans (readable labels), digests are for machines (cryptographic verification). Confusing them is the #1 supply chain security mistake in Kubernetes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Tag Mutability — The "latest" Time Bomb
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WHAT HAPPENS WHEN YOU PUSH TO THE SAME TAG:                     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  $ docker build -t anihpj-api:latest .                            │
  │  $ docker push anihpj-api:latest                                  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Registry stores:                                                 │
