  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  TAG: latest                                              │    │
  │  │    ↓                                                      │    │
  │  │  MANIFEST DIGEST: sha256:aaa111...  ← Day 1 image        │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  NEXT DAY — new build pushed to SAME TAG:                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  TAG: latest                    ← SAME LABEL!             │    │
  │  │    ↓                                                      │    │
  │  │  MANIFEST DIGEST: sha256:bbb222...  ← COMPLETELY DIFFERENT│    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  YOUR DEPLOYMENT STILL SAYS:                                      │
  │  image: anihpj-api:latest                                         │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⚠️ When the pod restarts, it pulls a DIFFERENT image!           │
  │  ⚠️ No code change in Git, no audit trail, no rollback plan      │
  │  ⚠️ Cosign signature from Day 1 no longer matches!              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  THE DIGEST GUARANTEE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Digest = SHA256(entire manifest JSON)                            │
  │                                                                   │
  │  • Same content → ALWAYS produces same digest                     │
  │  • Different content → ALWAYS produces different digest           │
  │  • One bit changed → completely different hash (avalanche effect) │
  │  • Mathematically impossible to forge (preimage resistance)       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why does Kyverno care? When you write a verifyImages rule, Kyverno checks the Cosign signature attached to a specific DIGEST. If you reference an image by tag, the tag could resolve to a different digest tomorrow — and the new digest might not be signed, or might be signed by a different key. Always use digests for policy enforcement.

For anihpj/jobpost, our GitOps pipeline never uses :latest — every deployment references images by git commit SHA tag (anihpj-api:abc123def), and Kyverno's mutate rule replaces even those with digests. Zero tag mutability risk.

Fundamentals Q32 of 200
How does Kyverno reference container images in policies using imageReferences and match.images?
A) Kyverno cannot reference images in policies — images are only checked at the container runtime level by the kubelet
B) Kyverno can match images in validate rules using match.images with patterns like registry.anihpj.com/*, and in verifyImages rules using imageReferences to specify which images need signature verification. The {{images}} variable gives access to all container images in the resource, including initContainers and ephemeral containers
C) Images are referenced by their Dockerfile path — Kyverno traces the image back to its build source
D) Kyverno uses the image's SHA256 digest exclusively — human-readable image names like "nginx:latest" are not supported
✅ Correct Answer: B

Kyverno provides multiple ways to target images in policies — from simple wildcard patterns to exact digest matching. The right approach depends on whether you're validating (pattern-based) or verifying (digest-based).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Image Referencing in Kyverno Policies
  1. VALIDATE RULES — match.images (pattern matching):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  - name: block-untrusted-registries                               │
  │    match:                                                         │
  │      any:                                                         │
  │      - resources:                                                 │
  │          kinds: [Pod]                                             │
  │    validate:                                                      │
  │      message: "Images must come from approved registries"        │
  │      pattern:                                                     │
  │        spec:                                                      │
  │          =(containers):                                           │
  │          - =(image): "registry.anihpj.com/*"   ← Must match      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  2. VERIFY IMAGES RULES — imageReferences (exact or wildcard):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  - name: verify-anihpj-images                                     │
  │    match:                                                         │
  │      any:                                                         │
  │      - resources:                                                 │
  │          kinds: [Pod]                                             │
  │    verifyImages:                                                  │
  │    - imageReferences:                                             │
  │      - "registry.anihpj.com/anihpj-api:*"   ← Wildcard           │
  │      - "registry.anihpj.com/anihpj-web:*"                        │
  │      attestors:                                                   │
  │      - count: 1                                                   │
  │        entries:                                                   │
  │        - keys:                                                    │
  │            publicKeys: |                                          │
  │              -----BEGIN PUBLIC KEY-----                           │
  │              MFkwEwYHKoZIzj0CAQYIKoZIzj0DAQc...                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  3. JMESPATH — {{images}} variable:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Access ALL container images in the resource:                     │
  │  {{ images.containers.*.name }}                                   │
  │  {{ images.initContainers.*.name }}                               │
  │  {{ images.ephemeralContainers.*.name }}                          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Useful for conditions:                                           │
  │  condition: "{{ contains(images.containers.*.name, 'nginx') }}"   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WILDCARD PATTERNS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  "registry.anihpj.com/*"     → Any image in this registry        │
  │  "*/anihpj-api:*"            → anihpj-api from any registry      │
  │  "registry.anihpj.com/anihpj-api:v2.1.*" → Specific version      │
  │  "*"                         → All images (rarely used)          │
  │  "!registry.anihpj.com/*"    → NOT from this registry (exclude) │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use imageReferences in our verifyImages policy to target only our application images (anihpj-api, anihpj-web) while leaving system images (postgres, redis) untouched — you typically only sign and verify your own images, not third-party ones.

Fundamentals Q33 of 200
How do image wildcards and patterns work in Kyverno — what does "registry.anihpj.com/*" match vs "*/nginx:*"?
A) Wildcards match any single character only — * is equivalent to ? in regex
B) Wildcards in Kyverno use full regex syntax — .* matches anything
C) Image patterns can only match exact strings — wildcards are not supported in Kyverno image policies
D) Kyverno uses glob-style wildcards (like shell file patterns): * matches any characters (including slashes), ? matches one character, [abc] matches one of a/b/c. "registry.anihpj.com/*" matches any image in that registry (anihpj-api:v1, anihpj-web:latest, etc.). "*/nginx:*" matches the nginx image from ANY registry with ANY tag. Prefix with ! to exclude: "!docker.io/*" blocks all Docker Hub images
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kyverno's image patterns use glob matching — the same pattern syntax you use in shell commands like ls *.yaml. This is intentionally simpler than regex to make policy authoring accessible.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Image Wildcard Pattern Reference
  GLOB PATTERNS IN KYVERNO (like shell wildcards):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PATTERN                          MATCHES                         │
  │  ───────                          ───────                         │
  │  *                                Everything                      │
  │  registry.anihpj.com/*            Any image in our registry       │
  │  */anihpj-api:*                   anihpj-api from any registry    │
  │  registry.anihpj.com/anihpj-*:*  api, web, db, redis in our reg  │
  │  */*                              Any image (registry/repo:tag)   │
  │  !docker.io/*                     Block Docker Hub images         │
  │  !*.azurecr.io/*                  Block Azure images              │
  │  registry.anihpj.com/anihpj-api:v2.1.*  Minor version wildcard   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  PRACTICAL EXAMPLE — anihpj image policy:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BLOCK everything EXCEPT anihpj registry:                        │
  │                                                                   │
  │  spec:                                                            │
  │    rules:                                                         │
  │    - name: require-anihpj-registry                                │
  │      match:                                                       │
  │        any:                                                       │
  │        - resources:                                               │
  │            kinds: [Pod]                                           │
  │      validate:                                                    │
  │        message: "Only images from registry.anihpj.com allowed"   │
  │        pattern:                                                   │
  │          spec:                                                    │
  │            containers:                                            │
  │            - image: "registry.anihpj.com/*" ← MUST match         │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ALLOW specific third-party images:                               │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Use multiple patterns with any/all logic:                        │
  │  pattern:                                                         │
  │    spec:                                                          │
  │      containers:                                                  │
  │      - image: "registry.anihpj.com/* | *postgres:* | *redis:*"   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Or add an exclude for trusted third-party images:               │
  │  exclude:                                                         │
  │    any:                                                           │
  │    - resources:                                                   │
  │        names: [anihpj-db*, anihpj-redis*]  ← DB + Redis exempt  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Exclusion with ! prefix: The ! prefix in image patterns means "NOT this". It's most useful in verifyImages to say "verify all images from my registry, but skip this one specific image that's not signed yet."

For anihpj/jobpost, we use "registry.anihpj.com/*" for our validate rule (all app images must come from our registry) and "registry.anihpj.com/anihpj-api:*" + "registry.anihpj.com/anihpj-web:*" for our verifyImages rule (only API and web images are signed with Cosign — PostgreSQL and Redis are trusted third-party images).

Fundamentals Q34 of 200
What are multi-architecture container images (manifest lists), and how does Kyverno handle them?
A) Multi-architecture images use a manifest list (also called a fat manifest or image index) that contains pointers to platform-specific images — e.g., one for linux/amd64, one for linux/arm64. When you pull nginx:latest, the registry returns the manifest list, and the container runtime picks the right image for the node's architecture. Kyverno handles these correctly — it resolves the manifest list to the platform-specific manifest before applying policies, and verifyImages checks signatures on the resolved manifest
B) Kyverno cannot process multi-architecture images — policies only work with single-architecture images
C) Multi-architecture images are automatically converted to single-architecture by Kyverno before policy evaluation
D) Multi-architecture images are only supported on ARM64 clusters — amd64 clusters must use single-architecture images
✅ Correct Answer: A

Multi-architecture images are why nginx:latest works on both your MacBook (ARM64) and your cloud VM (AMD64). The image index abstracts away the architecture — Kyverno resolves to the correct platform-specific manifest before evaluating policies.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Manifest Lists — How Kyverno Resolves Multi-Arch Images
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  IMAGE INDEX (Manifest List): nginx:latest                        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  {                                                         │   │
  │  │    "mediaType": "application/vnd.oci.image.index.v1+json", │   │
  │  │    "manifests": [                                           │   │
  │  │      {                                                      │   │
  │  │        "platform": {"os": "linux", "arch": "amd64"},       │   │
  │  │        "digest": "sha256:aaa111..."  ← AMD64 image         │   │
  │  │      },                                                     │   │
  │  │      {                                                      │   │
  │  │        "platform": {"os": "linux", "arch": "arm64"},       │   │
  │  │        "digest": "sha256:bbb222..."  ← ARM64 image         │   │
  │  │      },                                                     │   │
  │  │      {                                                      │   │
  │  │        "platform": {"os": "linux", "arch": "arm/v7"},      │   │
  │  │        "digest": "sha256:ccc333..."  ← ARM32 image         │   │
  │  │      }                                                      │   │
  │  │    ]                                                       │   │
  │  │  }                                                         │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  KYVERNO POLICY EVALUATION FLOW:                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  ① Pod specifies: image: nginx:latest                       │   │
  │  │  ② Kyverno fetches manifest list from registry              │   │
  │  │  ③ Kyverno resolves to node's architecture (e.g., amd64)   │   │
  │  │  ④ Kyverno applies policy to sha256:aaa111...               │   │
  │  │  ⑤ verifyImages checks Cosign signature on sha256:aaa111   │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  COSIGN SIGNING FOR MULTI-ARCH:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ cosign sign --key cosign.key \                                │
  │      registry.anihpj.com/anihpj-api:v2                           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Cosign signs ALL platform-specific manifests in the index.      │
  │  Each architecture gets its own signature. Kyverno verifies      │
  │  the correct one based on the node's architecture.               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Exam note: The KCA exam may ask about how Kyverno handles multi-arch images in the context of verifyImages. Key point: Kyverno resolves the manifest list FIRST, then applies policies to the resolved platform-specific manifest. You don't need to write separate policies per architecture.

For anihpj/jobpost, our images are built as multi-architecture (linux/amd64 + linux/arm64) so they run on both our Intel-based kubeadm cluster and any future ARM64 nodes. Cosign signs the entire manifest index, and Kyverno verifies per-platform.

Fundamentals Q35 of 200
How does the Kubernetes imagePullPolicy (Always, IfNotPresent, Never) interact with Kyverno policies, especially verifyImages?
A) imagePullPolicy has no effect on Kyverno — Kyverno ignores it completely during policy evaluation
B) Kyverno's admission-time checks (validate, mutate, verifyImages) run BEFORE the image is pulled — so imagePullPolicy doesn't affect admission. However, imagePullPolicy: Never combined with Kyverno verifyImages can be dangerous: the image is verified at admission, but if the policy is later updated to block that image, the cached image on the node can still run (because it's never re-pulled). Use Always or IfNotPresent with digest-based references for security
C) imagePullPolicy: Always causes Kyverno to re-verify the image signature every time the kubelet pulls it — it triggers a second admission check
D) imagePullPolicy: Never is recommended with Kyverno because it reduces registry load and Kyverno has already verified the image
✅ Correct Answer: B

imagePullPolicy governs what the kubelet does at pod start time — well AFTER Kyverno has already made its admission decision. Kyverno checks the image reference in the YAML; kubelet decides whether to pull it from the registry.

📝 Detailed Explanation
imagePullPolicy — When Kyverno Checks vs When kubelet Pulls
  TIMELINE — Pod Creation with verifyImages:
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  t=0ms    kubectl apply -f pod.yaml
  t=10ms   Kyverno ADMISSION CHECK (verifyImages):
           • Reads image reference from YAML
           • Fetches signature from registry (Cosign)
           • Verifies signature against public key
           • ✅ allowed: true → API server proceeds
  t=50ms   Resource written to etcd
  t=100ms  Scheduler assigns pod to node
  t=500ms  KUBELET on node:
           • Reads imagePullPolicy from pod spec
           ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
           │  Always:      Pull from registry every time          │
           │                ✅ Gets latest image                   │
           │                ❌ Slow (network call every start)     │
           │                                                       │
           │  IfNotPresent: Pull only if not cached on node       │
           │                ✅ Fast (uses cache)                   │
           │                ⚠️ May run stale image if tag changed │
           │                                                       │
           │  Never:        Use ONLY cached image on node         │
           │                ⚠️ Pod fails if image not cached      │
           │                ⚠️ Ignores Kyverno policy updates     │
           └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  SECURITY GAP — imagePullPolicy: Never + verifyImages:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Day 1:   Pod created with image: nginx@sha256:abc...            │
  │           Kyverno verifies signature → ✅ allowed                 │
  │           Image cached on node                                    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Day 30:  CVE discovered in nginx: that specific digest is       │
  │           now blocked by an updated Kyverno policy                │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Day 31:  Pod restarts → kubelet checks cache                    │
  │           imagePullPolicy: Never → uses CACHED image!            │
  │           Kyverno never re-checks (admission already passed)     │
  │           ⚠️ Vulnerable container is running!                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Best practice: Use imagePullPolicy: Always in development (ensures latest), IfNotPresent with digest-based references in production (fast but immutable — digest guarantees the same content). Combine with Kyverno verifyImages for defense in depth.

For anihpj/jobpost, production deployments use IfNotPresent with digest references (anihpj-api@sha256:abc...). The digest is immutable, so "if not present" is safe — the cached image is guaranteed to be the correct one.

Fundamentals Q36 of 200
What are the major container registries (Docker Hub, ECR, GCR, Harbor, GHCR) and how do they relate to Kyverno policies?
A) All container registries work identically with Kyverno — there are no differences in how policies are applied
B) Kyverno only supports Docker Hub and AWS ECR — other registries require custom adapters
C) Kyverno has built-in support only for public registries — private registries are not supported for verifyImages
D) Kyverno works with ANY OCI-compliant registry. Key differences in policy context: Docker Hub (public, no auth for pulls, most images here), AWS ECR (IAM authentication, regional endpoints — ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com), GCR/GAR (gcr.io, Google Cloud auth), Harbor (self-hosted enterprise registry with vulnerability scanning), GHCR (ghcr.io, GitHub Packages, GH Actions integration). For verifyImages, the registry must support OCI artifacts (for Cosign signatures) and tag-to-digest resolution
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kyverno is registry-agnostic — any OCI-compliant registry works. The differences between registries matter for authentication, regional availability, and advanced features like vulnerability scanning.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container Registries — Kyverno Policy Reference
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  REGISTRY        ENDPOINT              AUTH         NOTES         │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Docker Hub      docker.io             None (pulls)  Most images  │
  │                  (index.docker.io)     Token (pushes)Rate limited │
  │  AWS ECR         <acct>.dkr.ecr.      IAM roles     Regional     │
  │                  <region>.amazonaws.com             per account  │
  │  GCR/GAR         gcr.io               GCP SA        Google Cloud │
  │                  <region>-docker.pkg.dev             integration  │
  │  Harbor          your-harbor.com       Local users   Self-hosted  │
  │                                                      Vuln scanning│
  │  GHCR            ghcr.io              GITHUB_TOKEN  GH Actions   │
  │  Azure ACR       <name>.azurecr.io    Azure AD      Microsoft    │
  │  Quay            quay.io              Robot accts   Red Hat      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KYVERNO IMAGE PATTERNS BY REGISTRY:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Block Docker Hub (force using private registry):                │
  │  pattern:                                                        │
  │    spec:                                                         │
  │      containers:                                                 │
  │      - image: "!docker.io/*"  ← Reject docker.io images         │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Allow only AWS ECR:                                             │
  │  pattern:                                                        │
  │    spec:                                                         │
  │      containers:                                                 │
  │      - image: "*.dkr.ecr.*.amazonaws.com/*"                     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Allow anihpj Harbor + official postgres + official redis:       │
  │  pattern:                                                        │
  │    spec:                                                         │
  │      containers:                                                 │
  │      - image: "harbor.anihpj.com/* | *postgres:* | *redis:*"    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Private registry authentication: If your images are in a private registry, the nodes need pull secrets. Kyverno doesn't need registry credentials for basic validate/mutate rules — it only checks the image REFERENCE string. For verifyImages, Kyverno DOES need to fetch signatures from the registry, so it must be able to authenticate (via imagePullSecrets or node-level registry configuration).

For anihpj/jobpost, we use a self-hosted Harbor registry for all application images and Docker Hub for trusted third-party images (postgres, redis). Our Kyverno policies allow both sources but require Cosign signatures only on anihpj images.

⚙️ Category 2: Installation, Configuration & Upgrades (18%)Q37-Q72

Getting Kyverno Running & Keeping It Running

This category is about the practical mechanics of Kyverno — installing it via Helm, understanding its Custom Resource Definitions, configuring controller behavior with flags, setting up RBAC for policy management, running in high availability mode, and performing version upgrades. Think of it like setting up a building security system — install the cameras (Helm), configure the control room (flags), set up access cards (RBAC), ensure redundancy (HA), and periodically upgrade firmware (upgrades).

2.1 Helm-based Installation & ConfigurationQ37-Q44

Install & Config Q37 of 200
How do you add the Kyverno Helm repository and what command installs Kyverno into your cluster?
A) Kyverno is installed via kubectl using a single manifest file — no Helm repository is needed
B) Kyverno can only be installed via the Kubernetes Operator Lifecycle Manager (OLM) — Helm is not supported
C) Add the repo with helm repo add kyverno https://kyverno.github.io/kyverno/, update with helm repo update, then install with helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno -n kyverno --create-namespace. This deploys Kyverno as a Deployment in the kyverno namespace with all required CRDs, webhooks, RBAC, and service accounts automatically configured
D) Kyverno must be compiled from source and deployed as a static pod manifest on each control plane node
✅ Correct Answer: C

Helm is the official and recommended installation method for Kyverno. A single helm install command sets up everything — CRDs, deployments, services, webhooks, RBAC, and service accounts — in one atomic operation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Helm Installation — Complete Command Sequence
  # Step 1: Add the Kyverno Helm repository
  $ helm repo add kyverno https://kyverno.github.io/kyverno/
  "kyverno" has been added to your repositories

  # Step 2: Update Helm repository cache
  $ helm repo update
  Hang tight while we grab the latest from your chart repositories...
  ...Successfully got an update from the "kyverno" chart repository

  # Step 3: Search for available Kyverno charts
  $ helm search repo kyverno
  NAME                    CHART VERSION   APP VERSION   DESCRIPTION
  kyverno/kyverno         1.11.0          1.11.0        Kubernetes Native Policy Management
  kyverno/kyverno-policies 1.11.0         1.11.0        Kyverno Policies collection

  # Step 4: Install Kyverno
  $ helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno \
      --namespace kyverno \
      --create-namespace

  # Step 5: Verify installation
  $ kubectl get pods -n kyverno
  NAME                      READY   STATUS    RESTARTS   AGE
  kyverno-7d8f9b6c4-abc12   1/1     Running   0          30s

  # What was automatically created:
  $ kubectl get all -n kyverno
  NAME                           READY   STATUS    RESTARTS   AGE
  pod/kyverno-7d8f9b6c4-abc12    1/1     Running   0          30s

  NAME              TYPE        CLUSTER-IP     PORT(S)   AGE
  service/kyverno-svc ClusterIP  10.96.100.50   443/TCP   30s

  NAME                      READY   UP-TO-DATE   AVAILABLE   AGE
  deployment.apps/kyverno   1/1     1            1           30s

What Helm creates automatically: The Kyverno Helm chart creates the Deployment, Service, ValidatingWebhookConfiguration, MutatingWebhookConfiguration, CRDs (Policy, ClusterPolicy, PolicyReport, etc.), ServiceAccount, ClusterRole, ClusterRoleBinding, ConfigMap, and network policies — everything needed for a fully functional Kyverno installation.

For anihpj/jobpost, we installed Kyverno via Helm on all 6 clusters with this exact command sequence. The entire process takes under 2 minutes per cluster.

Install & Config Q38 of 200
What is the complete helm install command with all commonly used flags for a production Kyverno deployment?
A) helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno -n kyverno --create-namespace --set replicaCount=3 --set resources.requests.cpu=200m --set resources.requests.memory=256Mi --set resources.limits.cpu=1000m --set resources.limits.memory=512Mi --set podDisruptionBudget.minAvailable=2 — this deploys 3 replicas with resource limits and a PDB for high availability
B) Kyverno cannot be customized during Helm install — all configuration must be done after installation via ConfigMap updates
C) helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno — that's the only valid command; additional flags are not supported
D) Kyverno requires a separate values.yaml file — inline --set flags are not supported for security reasons
✅ Correct Answer: A

The base helm install works for development, but production needs HA configuration, resource limits, and availability guarantees. The Helm chart exposes all these through values.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Production Kyverno Helm Install — Annotated
  helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno \
    -n kyverno \                          ← Namespace for Kyverno
    --create-namespace \                  ← Create namespace if missing
    --set replicaCount=3 \                ← 3 replicas for HA
    --set resources.requests.cpu=200m \   ← Minimum CPU per pod
    --set resources.requests.memory=256Mi\← Minimum memory per pod
    --set resources.limits.cpu=1000m \    ← Max CPU per pod
    --set resources.limits.memory=512Mi \ ← Max memory per pod
    --set podDisruptionBudget.minAvailable=2  ← Keep 2/3 running during drains

  POPULAR HELM VALUES (kyverno/kyverno):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VALUE                          DEFAULT     DESCRIPTION      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  replicaCount                   1           Number of replicas│
  │  resources.requests.cpu         200m        CPU request       │
  │  resources.limits.cpu           1000m       CPU limit         │
  │  webhookTimeout                 10          Admission timeout │
  │  failurePolicy                  Fail        Webhook failure   │
  │  backgroundScan.enabled         true        Scan existing     │
  │  config.resourceFilters         []          Exclude namespaces│
  │  image.pullPolicy               IfNotPresent Image pull       │
  │  image.tag                      latest      Kyverno version   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

values.yaml alternative: For complex configurations, create a values.yaml file and use helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno -n kyverno -f values.yaml. This is the GitOps-friendly approach — your configuration is version-controlled alongside your application code.

For anihpj/jobpost, our production cluster uses 3 replicas with the resource settings shown above, while development clusters (minikube, kind) use the default single-replica configuration to conserve resources.

Install & Config Q39 of 200
How do you use a values.yaml file to customize a Kyverno Helm installation, and what are the most commonly customized values?
A) values.yaml is only used for documentation — Kyverno ignores custom values files and always uses built-in defaults
B) Create a values.yaml file, specify your customizations (replicaCount, resources, extraArgs, config.resourceFilters, features), then install with helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno -n kyverno -f values.yaml. Common customizations include: replicaCount (HA), resource limits/requests, webhook timeout, failure policy, background scan toggle, namespace exclusions, and feature flags (admission reports, policy reports, cleanup controller)
C) Kyverno Helm values can only customize the Kyverno pod name and namespace — all other settings are immutable
D) values.yaml must be generated by the kyverno CLI tool — manual creation is not supported
✅ Correct Answer: B

The values.yaml file is the GitOps-friendly way to configure Kyverno — it's version-controlled, reviewed in pull requests, and applied consistently across environments.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno values.yaml — Production Example
  # kyverno-values.yaml — Production configuration for anihpj
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # High Availability                                             │
  │  replicaCount: 3                                                 │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Resource allocation                                           │
  │  resources:                                                       │
  │    requests:                                                      │
  │      cpu: 200m                                                    │
  │      memory: 256Mi                                                │
  │    limits:                                                        │
  │      cpu: 1000m                                                   │
  │      memory: 512Mi                                                │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Availability during node drains                               │
  │  podDisruptionBudget:                                             │
  │    minAvailable: 2                                                │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Admission control settings                                    │
  │  webhookTimeout: 10                                               │
  │  failurePolicy: Fail                                              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Features                                                       │
  │  features:                                                        │
  │    backgroundScan:                                                │
  │      enabled: true                                                │
  │    admissionReports:                                              │
  │      enabled: true                                                │
  │    policyReports:                                                 │
  │      enabled: true                                                │
  │    cleanupController:                                             │
  │      enabled: false             ← disabled for now               │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Namespace exclusions                                          │
  │  config:                                                          │

  APPLY:

      -n kyverno --create-namespace \
      -f kyverno-values.yaml

      -n kyverno -f kyverno-values.yaml

Environment-specific values: Maintain separate values files for dev/staging/production: values-dev.yaml (single replica, low resources, Audit mode), values-prod.yaml (3 replicas, high resources, Enforce mode with Fail failurePolicy). Use -f values-base.yaml -f values-prod.yaml to layer them.

For anihpj/jobpost, we maintain a kyverno-values.yaml in our infrastructure Git repository. Changes go through PR review, and CI/CD applies them via helm upgrade.

Install & Config Q40 of 200
How do you enable specific Kyverno features like background scanning, PolicyReports, and the cleanup controller during Helm installation?
A) Kyverno features are hardcoded and cannot be toggled — all features are always enabled
B) Features must be enabled by manually editing the Kyverno Deployment YAML after installation — Helm values cannot control features
C) Features are enabled via Kubernetes annotations on the kyverno namespace — not through Helm values
D) Features are toggled via Helm values under the features key: --set features.backgroundScan.enabled=true, --set features.policyReports.enabled=true, --set features.admissionReports.enabled=true, --set features.cleanupController.enabled=true. Each feature flag controls whether the corresponding Kyverno controller is deployed and active
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kyverno is modular — you enable only the controllers you need. This keeps resource usage low for simple deployments while allowing full functionality for production.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Feature Flags — What Each Controller Does
  FEATURE FLAGS (Helm values under features.*):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FLAG                              DEFAULT   CONTROLLER ROLE     │
  │  ────                              ───────   ──────────────      │
  │  features.backgroundScan.enabled   true      Scans existing      │
  │                                              resources against   │
  │                                              all policies        │
  │                                                                   │
  │  features.policyReports.enabled    true      Aggregates policy   │
  │                                              results into        │
  │                                              PolicyReport CRs    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  features.admissionReports.enabled true      Reports on admission │
  │                                              decisions per req   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  features.cleanupController.enabled false    TTL-based resource   │
  │                                              deletion (Cleanup   │
  │                                              Policies)           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  features.autoUpdateWebhooks.enabled true    Auto-update webhook │
  │                                              CA bundles          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  INSTALL WITH FEATURES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Minimal install (admission only, no reporting):               │
  │  helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno \                          │
  │    -n kyverno --create-namespace \                               │
  │    --set features.backgroundScan.enabled=false \                 │
  │    --set features.policyReports.enabled=false \                  │
  │    --set features.admissionReports.enabled=false                 │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Full install (everything enabled):                            │
  │  helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno \                          │
  │    -n kyverno --create-namespace \                               │
  │    --set features.backgroundScan.enabled=true \                  │
  │    --set features.policyReports.enabled=true \                   │
  │    --set features.admissionReports.enabled=true \                │
  │    --set features.cleanupController.enabled=true                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Feature impact on resources: Each enabled feature adds a controller to the Kyverno pod. Background scanning and policy reporting add the most overhead (periodic API calls to scan all resources). In resource-constrained environments (minikube, kind), disabling non-essential features reduces Kyverno's CPU and memory footprint significantly.

For anihpj/jobpost, all 6 clusters run with all features enabled EXCEPT the cleanup controller (we haven't adopted Cleanup Policies yet). The production cluster uses 3 replicas with generous resource limits to handle the background scanning load.

Install & Config Q41 of 200
How do you install the Kyverno Policies chart (kyverno/kyverno-policies), and what does it provide compared to the main Kyverno chart?
A) The policies chart is required — Kyverno won't work without it because the main chart doesn't include any CRDs
B) The policies chart is an optional separate Helm chart that provides a curated library of ready-to-use Kyverno policies for common Kubernetes security scenarios. Install with helm install kyverno-policies kyverno/kyverno-policies -n kyverno. It includes policies like "require-non-root", "drop-capabilities", "require-resource-limits", and "disallow-host-path" — all pre-tested and maintained by the Kyverno community. The main chart provides the engine; the policies chart provides the rules
C) The policies chart replaces the main chart — you install one or the other, not both
D) The policies chart must be installed in every namespace where you want policies to apply
✅ Correct Answer: B

The main Kyverno chart provides the policy engine (controllers, webhooks, CRDs). The policies chart provides a library of pre-written policies — you can use them as-is, customize them, or write your own from scratch. They are completely independent.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Main Chart vs Policies Chart — What Each Provides
  MAIN CHART (kyverno/kyverno):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno -n kyverno                 │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Provides:                                                        │
  │  • Kyverno deployment (the policy engine)                         │
  │  • CRDs (Policy, ClusterPolicy, PolicyReport, etc.)               │
  │  • Webhook configurations (Mutating + Validating)                 │
  │  • ServiceAccount, ClusterRole, ClusterRoleBinding                │
  │  • ConfigMap for Kyverno configuration                            │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Does NOT include: Any actual policies — you write those!         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  POLICIES CHART (kyverno/kyverno-policies) — OPTIONAL:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  helm install kyverno-policies kyverno/kyverno-policies \        │
  │    -n kyverno                                                     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Provides (curated ClusterPolicies):                              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Pod Security:                                              │  │
  │  │  • require-non-root-pods        (runAsNonRoot: true)        │  │
  │  │  • drop-capabilities            (drop ALL, no add)          │  │
  │  │  • disallow-host-path           (no hostPath volumes)       │  │
  │  │  • disallow-privileged-containers                           │  │
  │  │  • require-read-only-root-filesystem                        │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │  Best Practices:                                             │  │
  │  │  • require-resource-limits      (CPU/memory must be set)    │  │
  │  │  • require-pod-probes           (liveness/readiness req)   │  │
  │  │  • disallow-latest-tag          (no :latest images)         │  │
  │  │  • require-labels               (app, team labels required) │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Start with policies chart, then customize: The policies chart gives you a solid security baseline in minutes. As your needs evolve, you can disable specific policies with Helm values and add your own custom policies. It's the fastest way to go from zero to production-grade security.

For anihpj/jobpost, we started with the policies chart but quickly outgrew it — we needed custom policies for our image registry, Cosign verification, and NetworkPolicy generation. We kept a few community policies (require-non-root, drop-capabilities) and wrote the rest ourselves.

Install & Config Q42 of 200
After installing Kyverno via Helm, how do you verify that the installation is successful and Kyverno is fully functional?
A) If the Helm install command returns without errors, Kyverno is guaranteed to be working — no further verification is needed
B) Kyverno can only be verified by deploying a test pod and checking if it gets blocked by the default policies
C) Verify with: helm list -n kyverno (chart deployed), kubectl get pods -n kyverno (all Running 1/1), kubectl get validatingwebhookconfiguration (kyverno-resource-validating present), kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration (kyverno-resource-mutating present), kubectl get crd | grep kyverno (CRDs registered), and kubectl get clusterpolicy (no errors). A quick functional test: kubectl apply -f test-policy.yaml with a simple Audit-mode policy to confirm Kyverno processes it
D) Verification is done by checking the Kyverno dashboard at https://kyverno:8443 — the UI shows a green checkmark if everything is healthy
✅ Correct Answer: C

Helm install succeeding means the resources were created — it doesn't guarantee they're healthy. You need to verify the full stack: pods, webhooks, CRDs, and functional behavior.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Installation Verification Checklist
  # 1. VERIFY HELM DEPLOYMENT
  $ helm list -n kyverno
  NAME     NAMESPACE  REVISION  STATUS    CHART           APP VERSION
  kyverno  kyverno    1         deployed  kyverno-1.11.0  1.11.0

  # 2. VERIFY PODS ARE RUNNING

  kyverno-7d8f9b6c4-abc12    1/1     Running   0          2m

Common failure modes: The most common issue is the webhook not registering because the CA bundle hasn't propagated. If kubectl get validatingwebhookconfiguration shows the webhook but it never becomes ready, check Kyverno pod logs with kubectl logs -n kyverno deploy/kyverno.

For anihpj/jobpost, we added this verification checklist to our post-install CI/CD pipeline. If any step fails, the pipeline alerts the team and rolls back the installation.

Install & Config Q43 of 200
How do you install Kyverno in an air-gapped (offline/restricted network) environment where the cluster cannot reach the internet?
A) Kyverno cannot be installed in air-gapped environments — it requires internet access to validate policies against the kyverno.io schema
B) Air-gapped installation is identical to online installation — Helm handles the registry mirroring automatically
C) You must compile Kyverno from source on the air-gapped machine — pre-built Helm charts don't work offline
D) Air-gapped installation requires: (1) Download the Helm chart and container images on an internet-connected machine: helm pull kyverno/kyverno + docker pull ghcr.io/kyverno/kyverno:v1.11.0. (2) Transfer the chart .tgz and images to the air-gapped environment. (3) Push images to your private registry: docker tag + docker push. (4) Install with helm install kyverno ./kyverno-1.11.0.tgz -n kyverno --set image.repository=private-registry.com/kyverno/kyverno
✅ Correct Answer: D

Air-gapped installation is the same as online, plus image relocating — you download everything on an internet-connected machine, transfer it to the isolated network, and point Helm at your private registry.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Air-Gapped Kyverno Installation — Step by Step
  INTERNET-CONNECTED MACHINE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Step 1: Pull the Helm chart                                    │
  │  helm repo add kyverno https://kyverno.github.io/kyverno/        │
  │  helm pull kyverno/kyverno --version 1.11.0                      │
  │  # Produces: kyverno-1.11.0.tgz                                  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Step 2: Pull container images                                  │
  │  docker pull ghcr.io/kyverno/kyverno:v1.11.0                     │
  │  docker pull ghcr.io/kyverno/kyvernopre:v1.11.0                  │
  │  docker pull ghcr.io/kyverno/background-controller:v1.11.0       │
  │  docker pull ghcr.io/kyverno/cleanup-controller:v1.11.0          │
  │  docker pull ghcr.io/kyverno/reports-controller:v1.11.0          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Step 3: Save images as tar files                              │
  │  docker save -o kyverno-images.tar \                             │
  │    ghcr.io/kyverno/kyverno:v1.11.0 \                             │
  │    ghcr.io/kyverno/kyvernopre:v1.11.0 \                          │
  │    ghcr.io/kyverno/background-controller:v1.11.0 \               │
  │    ghcr.io/kyverno/cleanup-controller:v1.11.0 \                  │
  │    ghcr.io/kyverno/reports-controller:v1.11.0                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  TRANSFER TO AIR-GAPPED ENVIRONMENT:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Step 4: Load images and push to private registry              │
  │  docker load -i kyverno-images.tar                               │
  │                                                                   │
  │  docker tag ghcr.io/kyverno/kyverno:v1.11.0 \                   │
  │    harbor.anihpj.internal/kyverno/kyverno:v1.11.0               │
  │  docker push harbor.anihpj.internal/kyverno/kyverno:v1.11.0     │
  │  # Repeat for all images...                                      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Step 5: Install from local chart + private registry           │
  │  helm install kyverno ./kyverno-1.11.0.tgz \                    │
  │    -n kyverno --create-namespace \                               │
  │    --set image.repository=harbor.anihpj.internal/kyverno/kyverno│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Helm values for private registry: You need to set image.repository to your private registry path. If your registry requires authentication, you also need to configure imagePullSecrets in the Helm values or at the namespace level.

For anihpj/jobpost, our staging and production environments are fully air-gapped behind a corporate firewall. We use Harbor as our private registry and mirror all Kyverno images there. Our CI/CD pipeline automates the image sync and Helm chart deployment.

Install & Config Q44 of 200
Why does Kyverno need its own dedicated namespace (kyverno), and what are the best practices for namespace configuration?
A) Kyverno needs its own namespace for security isolation and operational clarity: (1) RBAC — Kyverno's ClusterRoles are scoped to its namespace, preventing privilege escalation. (2) Exclusion — Kyverno policies should exclude their own namespace (kyverno) to avoid interfering with themselves. (3) Resource organization — all Kyverno components (deployments, services, configmaps, secrets) are grouped together. (4) Network policies — you can restrict traffic to/from the kyverno namespace. Best practice: always use the default kyverno namespace and exclude it from all policies
B) Kyverno can run in any namespace — the namespace name has no functional impact and is purely cosmetic
C) Kyverno must run in kube-system alongside other system components — using a custom namespace will cause webhook failures
D) Kyverno runs as a DaemonSet on every node and doesn't use namespaces at all
✅ Correct Answer: A

The kyverno namespace is more than a label — it's a security boundary. By isolating Kyverno in its own namespace, you prevent policy enforcement from accidentally affecting itself (Kyverno pods must be exempt from your own policies) and make RBAC management cleaner.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Why Kyverno Needs Its Own Namespace
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NAMESPACE: kyverno                                              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Deployment/kyverno           ← Policy engine               │  │
  │  │  Service/kyverno-svc          ← Webhook endpoint            │  │
  │  │  ConfigMap/kyverno            ← Configuration               │  │
  │  │  Secret/kyverno-tls           ← TLS certificates            │  │
  │  │  ServiceAccount/kyverno       ← RBAC identity               │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  CRITICAL: EXCLUDE kyverno namespace from ALL policies!          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  If you have a ClusterPolicy "require-non-root" and         │  │
  │  │  Kyverno's own pod violates it:                             │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │  → Kyverno pod restarts                                     │  │
  │  │  → Policy blocks the NEW Kyverno pod!                       │  │
  │  │  → Kyverno is deadlocked — can't start because it blocks    │  │
  │  │    itself from starting                                     │  │
  │  │                                                              │  │
  │  │  SOLUTION: Always exclude kyverno namespace!                │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KYVERNO CONFIGMAP — resourceFilters (exclude kyverno namespace):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: v1                                                   │
  │  kind: ConfigMap                                                  │
  │  metadata:                                                        │
  │    name: kyverno                                                  │
  │    namespace: kyverno                                             │
  │  data:                                                            │
  │    resourceFilters: '[{"kind":"*","namespace":"kyverno",         │
  │                        "action":"skip"}]'                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Can you use a different namespace? Technically yes, but it's strongly discouraged. The Helm chart, documentation, and community all assume kyverno. Using a different name means you must update every reference — webhook service URLs, RBAC bindings, ConfigMap references, and policy exclusions. Stick with kyverno.

For anihpj/jobpost, all 6 clusters use the default kyverno namespace with the standard resource filter excluding it from policy evaluation. All our ClusterPolicies also explicitly exclude the kyverno namespace in their match blocks as a secondary safety measure.

2.2 Kyverno Custom Resource Definitions (CRDs)Q45-Q50

Install & Config Q45 of 200
What are Kubernetes Custom Resource Definitions (CRDs), and how does Kyverno use them to extend the Kubernetes API?
A) CRDs are configuration files for kubectl plugins — they extend the CLI, not the API server
B) CRDs allow you to define NEW resource types in Kubernetes — extending the API beyond built-in types like Pod and Deployment. Kyverno registers CRDs for Policy, ClusterPolicy, PolicyReport, and others. After registration, you can kubectl get policy just like kubectl get pod — they become first-class Kubernetes resources with full API support (create, read, update, delete, watch, list)
C) CRDs are only used by the Kubernetes control plane internally — external tools like Kyverno cannot create CRDs
D) CRDs are an alternative to Helm charts — you use one or the other for installation
✅ Correct Answer: B

CRDs are Kubernetes' extension mechanism — they let you teach Kubernetes about new types of objects. Without CRDs, kubectl get policy would return "the server doesn't have a resource type 'policy'". With CRDs registered, Policy becomes as native as Pod.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CRDs — How Kyverno Extends the Kubernetes API
  WITHOUT CRDs (vanilla Kubernetes):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get policy                                            │
  │  error: the server doesn't have a resource type "policy"        │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Kubernetes API only knows built-in types:                       │
  │  Pod, Service, Deployment, ConfigMap, Secret, Node, Namespace... │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WITH KYVERNO CRDs INSTALLED:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl api-resources | grep kyverno                          │
  │  NAME                  SHORTNAMES   APIVERSION       NAMESPACED │
  │  clusterpolicies       cpol         kyverno.io/v1    false      │
  │  policies              pol          kyverno.io/v1    true       │
  │  policyreports         polr         wgpolicyk8s.io   true       │
  │  clusterpolicyreports  cpolr        wgpolicyk8s.io   false      │
  │  updaterequests        ur           kyverno.io/v1    true       │
  │  cleanuppolicies       cleanpol     kyverno.io/v1    true       │
  │                                                                   │
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicy                                     │
  │  NAME                    ADMISSION   BACKGROUND   READY   AGE    │
  │  require-non-root-pods   Enforce     true         True    5d    │
  │  drop-capabilities       Enforce     true         True    5d    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  HOW CRDs WORK:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Kyverno Helm chart creates CRD resources in the cluster       │
  │  ② API server registers the new types in its schema              │
  │  ③ kubectl can now interact with these types                     │
  │  ④ When you kubectl apply a ClusterPolicy:                       │
  │     → API server validates against the CRD's OpenAPI schema      │
  │     → Kyverno controller watches for changes to its CRDs         │
  │     → Controller processes the policy and updates webhooks       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Without CRDs, there is no Kyverno: Every Kyverno resource — policies, reports, update requests, cleanup policies — is a CRD. The Helm chart creates these automatically. If you ever see "no matches for kind 'ClusterPolicy'", it means the CRDs aren't installed.

For anihpj/jobpost, we can query all our policies with standard kubectl commands — kubectl get cpol, kubectl describe pol -n anihpj — because Kyverno's CRDs make policies first-class Kubernetes citizens.

Install & Config Q46 of 200
What are the four core Kyverno CRDs — Policy, ClusterPolicy, PolicyReport, and ClusterPolicyReport — and what does each one represent?
A) Policy and ClusterPolicy are the same CRD with different names — they are functionally identical
B) PolicyReport and ClusterPolicyReport are internal objects — users never interact with them directly
C) Policy = namespaced rules (apply to one namespace). ClusterPolicy = cluster-wide rules (apply to all namespaces). PolicyReport = namespaced compliance report — shows which resources in a namespace pass/fail each policy (like a report card per namespace). ClusterPolicyReport = cluster-wide compliance report — aggregates results across all namespaces for ClusterPolicies. Reports are auto-generated by Kyverno and are the primary way to see policy compliance
D) PolicyReport replaces Policy in newer versions of Kyverno — Policy is deprecated
✅ Correct Answer: C

These 4 CRDs form the policy + evidence system: Policies/ClusterPolicies define the rules, PolicyReports/ClusterPolicyReports show the results.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Core CRDs — Policies Define, Reports Show
  POLICIES (define the rules):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get policy -n anihpj                                  │
  │  NAME                      BACKGROUND   READY   AGE              │
  │  require-anihpj-labels     true         True    5d              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicy                                     │
  │  NAME                      ADMISSION   BACKGROUND   READY   AGE  │
  │  require-non-root-pods     Enforce     true         True    5d  │
  │  block-hostpath            Enforce     true         True    5d  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  REPORTS (show the results — auto-generated):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get policyreport -n anihpj                            │
  │  NAME                                  PASS   FAIL   WARN   AGE │
  │  cpol-require-non-root-pods            12     3      0      1h │
  │  cpol-block-hostpath                   15     0      0      1h │
  │  pol-require-anihpj-labels             15     0      0      1h │
  │                                                                   │
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicyreport                               │
  │  NAME                                  PASS   FAIL   WARN   AGE │
  │  cpol-require-non-root-pods            47     5      2      1h │
  │  cpol-block-hostpath                   52     0      0      1h │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RELATIONSHIP:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ClusterPolicy "require-non-root"                                 │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       │  checks pods in ALL namespaces                           │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ├──▶ PolicyReport in ns:anihpj → "12 pass, 3 fail"        │
  │       ├──▶ PolicyReport in ns:default → "15 pass, 0 fail"       │
  │       └──▶ PolicyReport in ns:staging → "20 pass, 2 fail"       │
  │                    │                                              │
  │                    └──▶ ClusterPolicyReport → "47 pass, 5 fail"  │
  │                         (aggregated across ALL namespaces)       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Reports are evidence: When an auditor asks "prove all pods run as non-root," you run kubectl get clusterpolicyreport and show the pass/fail counts. No manual evidence gathering needed. Reports are updated in real-time by Kyverno's background and admission controllers.

For anihpj/jobpost, we display PolicyReport summaries on our Grafana dashboard — green for 100% pass, yellow for warnings, red for failures. This gives the team instant visibility into policy compliance across all 6 clusters.

Install & Config Q47 of 200
What are Kyverno's additional CRDs — AdmissionReport, BackgroundScanReport, UpdateRequest, and CleanupPolicy — and when are they used?
A) These are deprecated CRDs from Kyverno v1.5 — they have been removed in newer versions
B) These are internal-only CRDs that users and administrators should never interact with or query
C) AdmissionReport and BackgroundScanReport are alternative names for PolicyReport — they all serve the same purpose
D) AdmissionReport = per-request admission decision log (which specific request passed/failed). BackgroundScanReport = results of scanning existing resources (not new admissions). UpdateRequest = internal CRD used by Kyverno's generate and mutate controllers to track pending changes. CleanupPolicy = TTL-based resource deletion rules (auto-delete resources older than X days). These are more specialized than the core CRDs and are typically queried for debugging or advanced use cases
✅ Correct Answer: D

While Policy and ClusterPolicy are the CRDs you'll use daily, these additional CRDs handle specialized tasks — debugging individual admission decisions, tracking background scan results, managing generated resources, and automating cleanup.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Additional Kyverno CRDs — When to Use Each
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CRD                    PURPOSE              USE CASE            │
  │  ───                    ───────              ────────            │
  │  AdmissionReport        Per-request log      "Why was THIS       │
  │                         of admission         specific request    │
  │                         decision             blocked?"           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  BackgroundScanReport   Results of scanning  "Does this existing │
  │                         EXISTING resources   pod violate any     │
  │                         (not new admissions) policies?"          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  UpdateRequest          Internal tracker     "Is my generate     │
  │                         for pending mutate/  rule still          │
  │                         generate operations  processing?"        │
  │                                                                   │
  │  CleanupPolicy          TTL-based auto-      "Delete PolicyReports│
  │                         deletion rules       older than 30 days" │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ADMISSION REPORT EXAMPLE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get admissionreport -n anihpj                         │
  │  NAME                           POLICY              RESULT  AGE │
  │  require-non-root-abc123        require-non-root    fail    5m │
  │  block-hostpath-def456          block-hostpath      pass    5m │
  │                                                                   │
  │  $ kubectl describe admissionreport require-non-root-abc123     │
  │  Policy: require-non-root-pods                                   │
  │  Rule: validate-runAsNonRoot                                     │
  │  Result: fail                                                     │
  │  Message: Running as root is not allowed                        │
  │  Resource: Pod/anihpj-api-7d8f9 (namespace: anihpj)             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CLEANUP POLICY EXAMPLE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v2alpha1                                 │
  │  kind: CleanupPolicy                                             │
  │  metadata:                                                       │
  │    name: cleanup-old-reports                                     │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    match:                                                        │
  │      any:                                                        │
  │      - resources:                                                │
  │          kinds:                                                  │
  │          - PolicyReport                                          │
  │    schedule: "0 2 * * *"           ← Run daily at 2 AM          │
  │    conditions:                                                   │
  │      all:                                                        │
  │      - key: "{{ target.metadata.creationTimestamp }}"           │
  │        operator: LessThan                                        │
  │        value: "{{ subtract('720h', '0h') }}"  ← Older than 30d │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

When to query each: Use PolicyReports for daily compliance monitoring. Use AdmissionReports when debugging "why was my specific request blocked?" Use BackgroundScanReports to verify existing resources are compliant. Use UpdateRequest when generate/mutate rules seem stuck. Use CleanupPolicy to automate resource lifecycle management.

For anihpj/jobpost, we primarily use PolicyReports for compliance dashboards. We query AdmissionReports when developers report that Kyverno blocked their deployment and they need to understand exactly which policy and rule was triggered.

Install & Config Q48 of 200
How do you inspect Kyverno CRDs using kubectl explain and kubectl get crd to understand their structure and available fields?
A) kubectl get crd | grep kyverno lists all Kyverno-registered CRDs with their API groups. kubectl explain clusterpolicy shows the top-level structure (apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec). kubectl explain clusterpolicy.spec drills into sub-fields. kubectl explain clusterpolicy.spec.rules --recursive shows the COMPLETE tree of all available fields. This works exactly like kubectl explain pod.spec — Kyverno CRDs become part of the Kubernetes API and kubectl treats them as native resources
B) Kyverno CRDs cannot be inspected with kubectl explain — you must read the Kyverno documentation to understand the schema
C) kubectl explain only works for built-in Kubernetes resources like Pod and Deployment — CRDs require a special plugin
D) CRD inspection is done through the Kyverno dashboard at kyverno.io/schema — kubectl doesn't support CRD introspection
✅ Correct Answer: A

kubectl explain is one of Kubernetes' most underrated features — and it works for CRDs too. Kyverno's CRDs include OpenAPI schemas, which means kubectl can show you every available field, its type, and its description — no external documentation needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Inspecting Kyverno CRDs with kubectl
  # LIST ALL KYVERNO CRDs
  $ kubectl get crd | grep kyverno
  clusterpolicies.kyverno.io          2024-01-15T10:00:00Z
  policies.kyverno.io                 2024-01-15T10:00:00Z
  cleanuppolicies.kyverno.io          2024-01-15T10:00:00Z
  updaterequests.kyverno.io           2024-01-15T10:00:00Z
  policyreports.wgpolicyk8s.io        2024-01-15T10:00:00Z
  clusterpolicyreports.wgpolicyk8s.io 2024-01-15T10:00:00Z

  # TOP-LEVEL STRUCTURE
  $ kubectl explain clusterpolicy
  KIND:       ClusterPolicy
  VERSION:    kyverno.io/v1

  DESCRIPTION:
       ClusterPolicy defines a cluster-wide policy.

  FIELDS:
     apiVersion    <string>
     kind          <string>
     metadata      <ObjectMeta>
     spec          <Object>       ← The policy definition
     status        <Object>       ← Runtime status

  # DRILL INTO spec
  $ kubectl explain clusterpolicy.spec
  KIND:       ClusterPolicy
  VERSION:    kyverno.io/v1

  FIELDS:
     background              <boolean>
     failurePolicy           <string>
     rules                   <[]Object>  ← Required!
     validationFailureAction <string>
     ...

  # RECURSIVE — show EVERYTHING
  $ kubectl explain clusterpolicy.spec.rules --recursive
  KIND:       ClusterPolicy
  VERSION:    kyverno.io/v1

  FIELDS:
     name       <string>          ← Rule name
     match      <Object>
        any     <[]Object>
           resources   <Object>
              kinds     <[]string>
              names     <[]string>
              namespaces <[]string>
              selector  <Object>
        all     <[]Object>
           (same structure as any)
     exclude    <Object>
        (same structure as match)
     validate   <Object>
        message  <string>
        pattern  <Object>
        deny     <Object>
     mutate     <Object>
        patchesStrategicMerge  <Object>
        patchesJson6902        <string>
     generate   <Object>
        clone    <Object>
        data     <Object>
        synchronize <boolean>
     verifyImages <[]Object>
        imageReferences   <[]string>
        attestors         <[]Object>
        ...

Why this matters for the KCA exam: During the exam, you won't have internet access to browse Kyverno documentation. You CAN use kubectl explain — it's built into every Kubernetes cluster and shows the complete CRD schema. If you forget a field name (like patchesStrategicMerge vs patchesJson6902), kubectl explain has the answer.

For anihpj/jobpost, we teach new team members to use kubectl explain clusterpolicy.spec --recursive as their first stop when writing policies — it's faster than searching documentation and shows only the fields available in the installed Kyverno version.

Install & Config Q49 of 200
What is CRD versioning in Kyverno — specifically kyverno.io/v1 vs kyverno.io/v2beta1 — and why does it matter?
A) CRD versions are interchangeable — you can use v1 or v2beta1 in your policy YAMLs without any difference in behavior
B) CRD versioning follows Kubernetes API conventions: v1 = stable (production-ready, backward-compatible, will not change). v2beta1 = beta (new features being tested, may change before becoming v2). To check what versions are available: kubectl get crd clusterpolicies.kyverno.io -o yaml | grep -A5 versions. Always use the stable v1 for production policies. Beta versions should only be used for testing new features and may be deprecated or removed in future releases
C) v2beta1 is newer than v1 and should always be preferred — it has more features and better performance
D) Kyverno only supports one API version at a time — when a new version is released, the old one is immediately removed
✅ Correct Answer: B

CRD versioning is how the Kubernetes API evolves without breaking existing users. Kyverno can serve multiple API versions simultaneously — v1 for stability, v2beta1 for early access to new features. Your existing v1 policies continue working even when v2beta1 is introduced.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CRD Versioning — How Kyverno Manages API Evolution
  KYVERNO CRD VERSION LIFECYCLE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Stage 1: v1alpha1 (alpha)                                       │
  │  └→ Experimental, may be removed, no backward compatibility      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Stage 2: v1beta1 (beta)                                         │
  │  └→ Feature complete, tested, may have minor changes             │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Stage 3: v1 (stable)                                            │
  │  └→ Production-ready, backward-compatible, long-term support     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  INSPECT AVAILABLE VERSIONS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get crd clusterpolicies.kyverno.io -o yaml | \       │
  │      grep -A10 versions:                                         │
  │                                                                   │
  │  versions:                                                        │
  │  - name: v1                         ← STABLE (use this!)         │
  │    served: true                     ← API server serves this     │
  │    storage: true                    ← Stored in etcd as v1       │
  │    schema:                                                        │
  │      openAPIV3Schema: {...}                                       │
  │  - name: v2beta1                    ← BETA (test only)           │
  │    served: true                     ← API server serves this     │
  │    storage: false                   ← NOT stored (converted)     │
  │    schema:                                                        │
  │      openAPIV3Schema: {...}                                       │
  │    deprecated: false                                              │
  │    deprecationWarning: null                                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WHICH VERSION DOES kubectl GET RETURN?
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicy require-non-root -o yaml            │
  │  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1     ← Uses the STORAGE version (v1)  │
  │  kind: ClusterPolicy                                              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Even if you created it with apiVersion: kyverno.io/v2beta1,    │
  │  it's CONVERTED to the storage version (v1) in etcd.            │
  │  When you read it back, you get the storage version.             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  API CONVERSION — How Kyverno Handles Multiple Versions:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply (v2beta1)                                         │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  Kyverno conversion webhook                                      │
  │  converts v2beta1 → v1 (storage version)                        │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  etcd stores as v1                                               │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  kubectl get (requested: v2beta1)                                │
  │  Kyverno converts v1 → v2beta1                                   │
  │  Returns v2beta1 format to user                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Practical rule: Always use apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1 in your policy YAMLs for production. Check kubectl get crd to see what versions are available. If you're testing beta features (like new CleanupPolicy syntax), use the beta version but don't deploy to production until it stabilizes.

For anihpj/jobpost, all 13 policies use apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1. When Kyverno introduces new features in beta versions, we test them in our kind development clusters first before migrating production policies.

Install & Config Q50 of 200
What happens when you delete a Kyverno CRD (e.g., kubectl delete crd clusterpolicies.kyverno.io), and what precautions should you take?
A) Deleting the CRD only removes the schema definition — all existing ClusterPolicy resources remain intact and can still be queried
B) Kubernetes prevents CRD deletion if any resources of that type exist — you must delete all policies first
C) The CRD is automatically recreated by the Kyverno controller within seconds — deletion has no practical impact
D) Deleting a CRD DELETES ALL resources of that type across the entire cluster — instantly and irreversibly. kubectl delete crd clusterpolicies.kyverno.io will delete EVERY ClusterPolicy in your cluster with no confirmation prompt.
