This is standard Kubernetes behavior for ALL CRDs, not unique to Kyverno. Precautions: (1) Never delete CRDs without backing up resources first. (2) Use Helm to uninstall Kyverno cleanly instead of manually deleting CRDs. (3) The Kyverno Helm chart pre-delete hook backs up policies before CRD removal
✅ Correct Answer: D

CRD deletion is one of the most dangerous kubectl commands you can run. It cascades to every resource of that type — all your policies, all your reports, everything — gone in milliseconds with no undo.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CRD Deletion — The Nuclear Option
  WHAT HAPPENS WHEN YOU DELETE A CRD:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl delete crd clusterpolicies.kyverno.io                 │
  │  customresourcedefinition "clusterpolicies.kyverno.io" deleted  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  IMMEDIATELY AND IRREVERSIBLY:                                   │
  │  ❌ ALL ClusterPolicy resources DELETED                           │
  │  ❌ ALL associated webhook configs become INVALID                 │
  │  ❌ ALL policy enforcement STOPS (no policies exist!)             │
  │  ❌ ALL ClusterPolicyReports DELETED (they reference policies)    │
  │  ❌ NO confirmation prompt — just gone                            │
  │                                                                   │
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicy                                     │
  │  No resources found            ← Everything is GONE              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  SAFE UNINSTALL (use Helm, not kubectl delete crd):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ helm uninstall kyverno -n kyverno                             │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Helm pre-delete hook:                                            │
  │  ① Backs up all policies to a ConfigMap or file                  │
  │  ② Removes webhook configurations first                          │
  │  ③ Deletes Kyverno deployment                                    │
  │  ④ Removes CRDs last (after resources are gone)                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  BACKUP BEFORE ANY CRD OPERATION:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Backup all ClusterPolicies                                    │
  │  kubectl get clusterpolicy -o yaml > backup-cpol.yaml           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Backup all Policies (all namespaces)                          │
  │  kubectl get policy -A -o yaml > backup-policies.yaml           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Backup all PolicyReports                                      │
  │  kubectl get policyreport -A -o yaml > backup-reports.yaml      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why CRDs cascade-delete: Resources of a CRD type have no meaning without their CRD. If the ClusterPolicy CRD is deleted, the API server can no longer validate, serve, or store ClusterPolicy objects — so it deletes them all. This is standard Kubernetes garbage collection behavior, not a Kyverno-specific issue.

For anihpj/jobpost, we maintain automated backups of all 13 policies in our Git repository (infrastructure-as-code). Even if someone accidentally deletes the CRD, we can reinstall Kyverno via Helm and reapply our policies from Git in under 5 minutes. All policies are version-controlled — the CRD is just the delivery mechanism.

2.3 Controller Configuration with FlagsQ51-Q56

Install & Config Q51 of 200
What are the key Kyverno controller flags --webhookTimeout and --failurePolicy, and how do they affect admission behavior?
A) These flags control kubectl client-side timeout settings and have no effect on Kyverno's admission behavior
B) --webhookTimeout (default: 10 seconds) sets how long the API server waits for Kyverno to respond to an AdmissionReview before timing out. If Kyverno doesn't respond in time, the --failurePolicy (Fail or Ignore) determines what happens: Fail blocks the request (secure), Ignore allows it (risky). These are configured via Helm values or as container arguments in the Kyverno deployment
C) --webhookTimeout controls how long Kyverno caches policy results; --failurePolicy controls retry behavior for failed policy evaluations
D) These flags are set per-policy in the policy YAML, not at the controller level
✅ Correct Answer: B

These two flags form Kyverno's admission safety net: --webhookTimeout defines patience (how long to wait), --failurePolicy defines the consequence of impatience (block or allow).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Controller Flags — How They Work Together
  KYVERNO DEPLOYMENT (container args):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  containers:                                                      │
  │  - name: kyverno                                                  │
  │    image: ghcr.io/kyverno/kyverno:v1.11.0                        │
  │    args:                                                          │
  │    - --webhookTimeout=10              ← 10 seconds                │
  │    - --failurePolicy=Fail             ← Block on timeout          │
  │    - --genWorkers=10                  ← Generate workers          │
  │    - --backgroundScan=true            ← Background scanning       │
  │    - --imagePullSecrets=regcred       ← Default pull secret       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  --webhookTimeout IN ACTION:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  API Server sends AdmissionReview                                 │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       │  ⏱️ Timer starts: 10 seconds                              │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ├── 2s: Kyverno evaluates policy → ✅ responds "allowed"   │
  │       │        (normal case — everything works)                  │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ├── 11s: Kyverno is slow (API call in context, overload)   │
  │       │        ⏱️ TIMEOUT REACHED!                               │
  │       │        --failurePolicy=Fail → ❌ REQUEST BLOCKED         │
  │       │        --failurePolicy=Ignore → ✅ REQUEST ALLOWED       │
  │       │        (allowed WITHOUT policy check — dangerous!)       │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       └── NEVER: Kyverno pod is DOWN                             │
  │                ⏱️ TIMEOUT REACHED!                               │
  │                Same failurePolicy logic applies                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  HELM CONFIGURATION:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno \                          │
  │    --set webhookTimeout=15 \             ← flags                 │
  │    --set failurePolicy=Fail                                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Tuning guidance: Increase --webhookTimeout if your policies make external API calls (registry lookups, context calls) that take longer than 10 seconds. Keep --failurePolicy=Fail in production — Ignore defeats the purpose of having Kyverno. If you need higher availability, add more replicas instead of using Ignore.

For anihpj/jobpost, production runs with --webhookTimeout=10 and --failurePolicy=Fail with 3 replicas. Our image verification policies occasionally need more time, so we increased the timeout to 20 seconds for the production cluster.

Install & Config Q52 of 200
What is the --genWorkers flag in Kyverno, and how does it affect generate rules?
A) --genWorkers (default: 10) controls how many CONCURRENT generate rule operations Kyverno can process at once. Each worker handles one generate request at a time. If you have many generate rules creating resources (NetworkPolicies, PDBs, ResourceQuotas), increasing genWorkers can speed up resource creation. Too many workers can overwhelm the API server with concurrent requests
B) --genWorkers controls how many Kyverno pod replicas are deployed — setting it to 3 creates 3 copies of the Kyverno deployment
C) --genWorkers controls the number of validate rules that can run simultaneously — it has nothing to do with generate rules
D) --genWorkers is a deprecated flag from Kyverno v1.5 and is no longer available
✅ Correct Answer: A

--genWorkers is the concurrency throttle for generate rules — it determines how many resources Kyverno can auto-create simultaneously. Think of it like the number of cash registers in a store: more registers = faster throughput, but too many = chaos.

📝 Detailed Explanation
genWorkers — Concurrency for Generate Rules
  --genWorkers=10 (default):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Trigger: 15 new namespaces created simultaneously               │
  │  (each needs a NetworkPolicy generated)                          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Worker Queue:                                                    │
  │  ┌────┐ ┌────┐ ┌────┐ ┌────┐ ┌────┐ ┌────┐ ┌────┐ ┌────┐ ┌────┐│
  │  │ W1 │ │ W2 │ │ W3 │ │ W4 │ │ W5 │ │ W6 │ │ W7 │ │ W8 │ │ W9 ││ W10│
  │  └──┬─┘ └──┬─┘ └──┬─┘ └──┬─┘ └──┬─┘ └──┬─┘ └──┬─┘ └──┬─┘ └──┬─┘ └──┬─┘
  │     │      │      │      │      │      │      │      │      │      │
  │     ▼      ▼      ▼      ▼      ▼      ▼      ▼      ▼      ▼      ▼
  │   ns-1   ns-2   ns-3   ns-4   ns-5   ns-6   ns-7   ns-8   ns-9  ns-10
  │   ✅     ✅     ✅     ✅     ✅     ✅     ✅     ✅     ✅     ✅
  │                                                                   │
  │   ns-11 through ns-15 WAIT in queue until a worker is free       │
  │   ⏱️ Total time: ~2-3 seconds (10 at a time × 2 batches)        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  --genWorkers=20 (increased):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  All 15 namespaces processed in ONE batch                         │
  │  ⏱️ Total time: ~1-2 seconds                                     │
  │  ⚠️ But: 20 concurrent API calls → potential API server load    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  TUNING GUIDE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Few generate rules (1-5):    genWorkers=5   (default is fine)  │
  │  Many generate rules (10+):   genWorkers=15  (moderate increase) │
  │  Burst creation (CI/CD):      genWorkers=20  (handle spikes)     │
  │  Resource-constrained:        genWorkers=3   (reduce API load)  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Separate from admission: Generate rules run asynchronously — they don't block kubectl apply. genWorkers controls the background processing speed, not the admission latency. If generate rules feel slow, check genWorkers before looking elsewhere.

For anihpj/jobpost, we use the default 10 workers. Our generate rules (auto-create NetworkPolicy and PDB) are triggered infrequently (new namespace creation), so 10 workers easily handles the load.

Install & Config Q53 of 200
How does the --backgroundScan flag work, and what's the difference between enabling and disabling background scans at the controller level vs per-policy?
A) --backgroundScan is a legacy flag that has no effect in modern Kyverno versions
B) Controller-level --backgroundScan and per-policy background:true are interchangeable — setting either one enables background scans
C) --backgroundScan (controller flag, default: true) is the MASTER SWITCH — it globally enables/disables the background scan controller. Per-policy background: true/false is the PER-POLICY switch — individual policies can opt out even when the master switch is on. Both must be true for a policy's background scan to run. Disabling the controller flag stops ALL background scanning regardless of per-policy settings
D) --backgroundScan only affects ClusterPolicies — namespaced Policies always run background scans regardless of this flag
✅ Correct Answer: C

The --backgroundScan flag and per-policy background setting form a two-layer gate: the controller flag is the master power switch, and each policy has its own toggle. BOTH must be on for background scanning to work.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Two-Layer Background Scan Control
  LAYER 1: Controller Flag (MASTER SWITCH)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  --backgroundScan=true   → Background controller RUNS            │
  │  --backgroundScan=false  → Background controller STOPS          │
  │                            (NO policy gets scanned, period)      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  LAYER 2: Per-Policy Setting (INDIVIDUAL TOGGLES)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Policy A: background: true   → WILL be scanned (if L1 is on)   │
  │  Policy B: background: true   → WILL be scanned (if L1 is on)   │
  │  Policy C: background: false  → NEVER scanned (even if L1 is on)│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  DECISION MATRIX:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  --backgroundScan   │  background:true  │  background:false      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  true               │  ✅ Scanned       │  ❌ Not scanned        │
  │  false              │  ❌ Not scanned   │  ❌ Not scanned        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WHEN TO DISABLE (controller level):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  • Resource-constrained clusters (minikube, kind)                │
  │  • Very large clusters (1000+ resources) — scan is expensive     │
  │  • Troubleshooting — isolate whether scans cause issues          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WHEN TO DISABLE (per-policy):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  • Policy uses request.userInfo (no user in background scan)     │
  │  • Policy only matters for NEW resources                         │
  │  • Policy has external API calls that fail in background mode    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The two-layer design is intentional: You might have 20 policies where 18 need background scanning and 2 don't. Keep --backgroundScan=true and set background: false on those 2 policies. Or if you're running on a resource-constrained development cluster, set --backgroundScan=false globally and selectively enable it later.

For anihpj/jobpost, production runs with --backgroundScan=true and all policies have background: true except one policy that checks request.userInfo. Development clusters (minikube, kind) disable background scanning globally to conserve resources.

Install & Config Q54 of 200
What is the --imagePullSecrets flag in Kyverno, and when would you need to configure it?
A) --imagePullSecrets configures how Kyverno pulls its own container images from the registry — it affects Kyverno's own startup
B) --imagePullSecrets is automatically set by Helm and never needs manual configuration
C) --imagePullSecrets controls which Secrets Kubernetes uses to pull application container images — it has nothing to do with Kyverno
D) --imagePullSecrets specifies default image pull secrets that Kyverno uses when IT needs to fetch container images from private registries — specifically for verifyImages rules that check Cosign signatures. If your images are in a private registry (not Docker Hub), Kyverno needs credentials to fetch the image manifest and signatures. Without this flag, verifyImages will fail with authentication errors against private registries
✅ Correct Answer: D

When Kyverno's verifyImages rule checks a signature, it needs to FETCH the image manifest and signature layers from the registry. For private registries, this requires authentication — which is what --imagePullSecrets provides.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Why Kyverno Needs Registry Credentials
  WITHOUT --imagePullSecrets (public images — Docker Hub):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  verifyImages rule: "Check Cosign signature on anihpj-api:v2"   │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  Kyverno → GET https://registry.anihpj.com/v2/anihpj-api/...    │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ❌ 401 Unauthorized — registry requires authentication!         │
  │  ❌ verifyImages FAILS (not because signature is wrong,          │
  │     but because Kyverno can't even fetch it)                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WITH --imagePullSecrets=regcred:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Step 1: Create the pull secret                                │
  │  kubectl create secret docker-registry regcred \                │
  │    --docker-server=registry.anihpj.com \                        │
  │    --docker-username=kyverno-bot \                               │
  │    --docker-password=ghp_xxxx \                                  │
  │    -n kyverno                                                     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Step 2: Configure Kyverno to use it                           │
  │  --imagePullSecrets=regcred                                      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Step 3: verifyImages now works                                │
  │  Kyverno → GET https://registry.anihpj.com/v2/anihpj-api/...    │
  │       │  (with Authorization header from regcred secret)         │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ✅ 200 OK — manifest + signature fetched!                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  HELM CONFIGURATION:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # values.yaml                                                    │
  │  extraArgs:                                                       │
  │    - --imagePullSecrets=regcred                                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Separate from pod imagePullSecrets: --imagePullSecrets is for Kyverno itself to access registries — it's different from the imagePullSecrets in your application Pod specs. Your pods need their own pull secrets; Kyverno needs its own to verify signatures.

For anihpj/jobpost, we created a dedicated "kyverno-bot" service account in our Harbor registry with read-only access. The regcred secret uses this bot's credentials, and --imagePullSecrets=regcred lets Kyverno verify signatures on all anihpj images.

Install & Config Q55 of 200
How should you tune resource requests and limits for the Kyverno pod in production?
A) Kyverno doesn't need resource limits — it uses negligible CPU and memory and the defaults are always sufficient
B) Production recommendations: requests (CPU: 200m, Memory: 256Mi) — the guaranteed minimum. limits (CPU: 1000m, Memory: 512Mi) — the maximum allowed. Start with these, then adjust based on actual usage from kubectl top pod -n kyverno. Scale UP if you see OOMKilled restarts or CPU throttling. For clusters with many policies (50+) or heavy background scanning, increase memory to 1Gi and CPU to 2000m
C) Kyverno should always run without limits to prevent OOMKilled — setting limits is dangerous for admission controllers
D) Resource tuning is done via the Kyverno ConfigMap, not through Kubernetes resource requests/limits
✅ Correct Answer: B

Kyverno is an admission controller — if it's slow or crashed, your cluster can't create pods. Resource limits are not optional; they're essential for predictable performance and preventing a runaway Kyverno from starving other workloads.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Resource Tuning Guide
  SYMPTOMS OF UNDER-PROVISIONED KYVERNO:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SYMPTOM                    LIKELY CAUSE         FIX             │
  │  ───────                    ────────────         ───             │
  │  OOMKilled restarts         Memory limit too low Increase memory │
  │  kubectl apply is slow      CPU throttling       Increase CPU    │
  │  Admission timeout errors   Kyverno overloaded   Scale replicas  │
  │  PolicyReport gaps          BG scan too slow      Increase both  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RESOURCE TUNING BY CLUSTER SIZE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CLUSTER SIZE    POLICIES   CPU REQ   MEM REQ   CPU LIM  MEM LIM│
  │  ────────────    ────────   ───────   ───────   ───────  ───────│
  │  Dev (kind)      1-10       100m      128Mi     500m      256Mi│
  │  Staging         10-30      200m      256Mi     1000m     512Mi│
  │  Production      30-50      500m      512Mi     2000m     1Gi  │
  │  Large Prod      50+        1000m     1Gi       4000m     2Gi  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  MONITOR ACTUAL USAGE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl top pod -n kyverno                                    │
  │  NAME                    CPU(cores)   MEMORY(bytes)              │
  │  kyverno-7d8f9-abc12     150m         200Mi                      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # If actual usage is near limits → INCREASE limits              │
  │  # If actual usage is far below requests → DECREASE requests     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Memory is the critical resource: Kyverno caches policy definitions, resource metadata, and context data in memory. Under-provisioning memory leads to OOMKilled — which triggers failurePolicy: Fail and blocks ALL admissions until Kyverno recovers. Always set memory limits generously.

For anihpj/jobpost, our production cluster (13 policies, moderate load) runs with CPU: 500m/2000m and Memory: 512Mi/1Gi. We monitor usage via Prometheus and alert if Kyverno memory exceeds 80% of its limit.

Install & Config Q56 of 200
How do you configure Kyverno via its ConfigMap — specifically resourceFilters and defaultRegistry?
A) The Kyverno ConfigMap (kubectl get configmap kyverno -n kyverno) controls global Kyverno behavior. resourceFilters specifies which resources to EXCLUDE from policy processing (critical: exclude kube-system and kyverno namespaces). defaultRegistry sets a default image registry prefix for mutate rules (e.g., if set to "harbor.anihpj.com", image "nginx:latest" becomes "harbor.anihpj.com/nginx:latest"). Changes to the ConfigMap require a Kyverno pod restart to take effect
B) The Kyverno ConfigMap is read-only and generated by Helm — you cannot modify it after installation
C) resourceFilters and defaultRegistry are set via kubectl flags, not in the ConfigMap
D) The ConfigMap changes take effect immediately without requiring a Kyverno restart
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Kyverno ConfigMap is the central configuration hub — it controls which namespaces are exempt from policies and how image references are resolved. It's created by Helm but can be modified post-installation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno ConfigMap — Configuration Reference
  VIEW THE CONFIGMAP:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get configmap kyverno -n kyverno -o yaml              │
  │  apiVersion: v1                                                   │
  │  kind: ConfigMap                                                  │
  │  metadata:                                                        │
  │    name: kyverno                                                  │
  │    namespace: kyverno                                             │
  │  data:                                                            │
  │    resourceFilters: |                                             │
  │      [                                                           │
  │        {"kind":"*","namespace":"kube-system","action":"skip"},   │
  │        {"kind":"*","namespace":"kyverno","action":"skip"}        │
  │      ]                                                           │
  │    defaultRegistry: "harbor.anihpj.com"                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  resourceFilters EXPLAINED:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Each filter is a JSON object:                                   │
  │  {"kind":"Pod","namespace":"anihpj","name":"special-pod"}       │
  │                                                                   │
  │  action values:                                                   │
  │    "skip"    → Kyverno completely ignores this resource           │
  │    "allow"   → Resource passes through without policy checks     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  COMMON FILTERS:                                                  │
  │  {"kind":"*","namespace":"kube-system","action":"skip"}          │
  │  {"kind":"*","namespace":"kyverno","action":"skip"}              │
  │  {"kind":"Pod","namespace":"anihpj","name":"debug-*","action":"skip"}│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  defaultRegistry EXPLAINED:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  defaultRegistry: "harbor.anihpj.com"                            │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Effect on mutate rules:                                          │
  │  image: nginx:latest → image: harbor.anihpj.com/nginx:latest    │
  │  image: postgres:15  → image: harbor.anihpj.com/postgres:15     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Already-qualified images are NOT modified:                      │
  │  image: docker.io/nginx:latest → STAYS unchanged                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Restart required: ConfigMap changes are NOT hot-reloaded — you must restart the Kyverno pod for changes to take effect: kubectl rollout restart deployment/kyverno -n kyverno. Plan ConfigMap changes during maintenance windows or use Helm upgrade which handles the restart automatically.

For anihpj/jobpost, we set defaultRegistry: "harbor.anihpj.com" and configured resourceFilters to exclude kube-system, kyverno, and local-path-storage namespaces. These settings are in our Git-managed Helm values file and applied via CI/CD.

2.4 Configuring Kyverno RBAC, Roles & PermissionsQ57-Q64

Install & Config Q57 of 200
What Kubernetes permissions does Kyverno need to function, and why does it require such broad access?
A) Kyverno only needs read access to Pods — it doesn't need any special permissions beyond a basic reader role
B) Kyverno needs minimal permissions — the Helm chart intentionally restricts access for security
C) Kyverno requires cluster-admin level access because it must: READ all resource types across all namespaces (to evaluate policies), MUTATE resources in any namespace (for mutate rules), CREATE resources in any namespace (for generate rules), WATCH resources cluster-wide (for triggers), READ secrets (for API call authentication), and MANAGE its own CRDs. This is the most privileged ServiceAccount in your cluster — which is why Kyverno must be carefully secured and its namespace must be excluded from policy enforcement
D) Kyverno's permissions are dynamically granted per-policy — each policy specifies what access it needs
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kyverno is the most privileged workload in your cluster — it can read, modify, and create any resource in any namespace. This is by design (it must enforce policies everywhere), but it also means Kyverno itself must be the most secured component.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno RBAC — Why So Much Access?
  KYVERNO ClusterRole (simplified):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                         │
  │  kind: ClusterRole                                                │
  │  metadata:                                                        │
  │    name: kyverno                                                  │
  │  rules:                                                           │
  │  - apiGroups: ["*"]                  ← ALL API groups             │
  │    resources: ["*"]                  ← ALL resource types         │
  │    verbs: ["get","list","watch",     ← READ everything            │
  │             "create","update",       ← WRITE everything           │
  │             "patch","delete"]        ← MODIFY everything          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  WHY EACH PERMISSION:                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  get/list/watch  → "What resources exist to check?"      │    │
  │  │  create/update   → "Generate rules create resources"      │    │
  │  │  patch           → "Mutate rules apply JSON patches"      │    │
  │  │  delete          → "Cleanup policies delete old resources"│    │
  │  │  * (all groups)  → "Core, apps, networking, batch, etc."  │    │
  │  │  * (all types)   → "Pods, Deployments, Services, etc."   │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  SECURITY IMPLICATIONS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ⚠️ Kyverno CAN:                                                  │
  │  • Read ALL Secrets in ALL namespaces                            │
  │  • Delete any resource in any namespace                          │
  │  • Create new resources anywhere                                  │
  │  • Modify any existing resource                                   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  🛡️ PROTECTIONS YOU MUST HAVE:                                   │
  │  • Exclude kyverno namespace from all Kyverno policies           │
  │  • Never run other workloads in the kyverno namespace            │
  │  • Restrict who can modify the kyverno deployment                │
  │  • Audit all changes to Kyverno RBAC                              │
  │  • Use network policies to restrict traffic to/from kyverno      │
  │  • Monitor Kyverno's own logs for suspicious activity            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

This is not a bug — it's a requirement: To enforce policies cluster-wide, Kyverno must see and touch everything. The alternative (fine-grained per-namespace Kyverno deployments) would be operationally complex and still require broad access. The key is securing Kyverno itself — restrict who can modify its deployment, ConfigMap, and RBAC.

For anihpj/jobpost, Kyverno's ClusterRole is managed by Helm and never manually modified. Only two platform engineers have access to the kyverno namespace. All changes to Kyverno configuration go through Git PR review.

Install & Config Q58 of 200
What ServiceAccount, ClusterRole, and ClusterRoleBinding does Kyverno use, and how are they created?
A) Kyverno runs as the default ServiceAccount in the kyverno namespace — no special RBAC resources are needed
B) Kyverno uses the built-in cluster-admin ClusterRoleBinding — it doesn't create its own RBAC resources
C) Kyverno's RBAC must be manually configured after Helm installation — the chart doesn't create any RBAC resources
D) The Helm chart AUTO-CREATES: a ServiceAccount named "kyverno" in the kyverno namespace, a ClusterRole named "kyverno" with broad read/write access to all resources, and a ClusterRoleBinding binding the two together. View with: kubectl get sa -n kyverno, kubectl get clusterrole kyverno, kubectl get clusterrolebinding kyverno
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kyverno's RBAC is fully automated by Helm — you don't need to create any RBAC resources manually. The chart creates the ServiceAccount, ClusterRole, and ClusterRoleBinding as part of a standard installation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno RBAC — Auto-Created by Helm
  # VERIFY THE RBAC CHAIN
  $ kubectl get serviceaccount -n kyverno
  NAME      SECRETS   AGE
  kyverno   1         5d

  $ kubectl get clusterrole kyverno
  NAME      CREATED AT
  kyverno   2024-01-15T10:00:00Z

  $ kubectl get clusterrolebinding kyverno
  NAME      ROLE               AGE
  kyverno   ClusterRole/kyverno 5d

  RBAC CHAIN:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ServiceAccount (kyverno)                                        │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       │  ClusterRoleBinding                                      │
  │       │  (links SA to ClusterRole)                               │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ClusterRole (kyverno)                                           │
  │  └→ get, list, watch, create, update, patch, delete             │
  │     on ALL resources (*) in ALL API groups (*)                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KYVERNO DEPLOYMENT (references the SA):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                                            │
  │    template:                                                      │
  │      spec:                                                        │
  │        serviceAccountName: kyverno    ← Uses this SA              │
  │        containers:                                                │
  │        - name: kyverno                                            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Don't modify these manually: Helm manages these resources. Manual changes will be overwritten on the next Helm upgrade. If you need different RBAC (unlikely), customize via Helm values.

For anihpj/jobpost, we've never needed to modify Kyverno's default RBAC. The auto-created ClusterRole provides exactly what Kyverno needs — no more, no less.

Install & Config Q59 of 200
How do you inspect Kyverno's RBAC configuration using kubectl?
A) RBAC inspection requires access to the Kubernetes audit logs — kubectl cannot show RBAC configurations
B) Inspect with: kubectl get sa -n kyverno (ServiceAccount), kubectl describe clusterrole kyverno (full permission list), kubectl describe clusterrolebinding kyverno (which SA gets which role), kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:kyverno:kyverno (see what Kyverno can actually do, from Kyverno's perspective)
C) RBAC is only visible through the Kubernetes dashboard — kubectl doesn't have RBAC inspection commands
D) Kyverno's RBAC is encrypted and cannot be inspected — this is a security feature
✅ Correct Answer: B

kubectl auth can-i --as= is one of the most useful debugging commands — it lets you impersonate any identity (if you have permission) and see exactly what they can do.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Inspecting Kyverno's RBAC — Complete Commands
  # 1. What ServiceAccount does Kyverno use?
  $ kubectl get deploy kyverno -n kyverno \
      -o jsonpath='{.spec.template.spec.serviceAccountName}'
  kyverno

  # 2. What permissions does the ClusterRole grant?
  $ kubectl describe clusterrole kyverno
  Name:         kyverno
  Labels:       app=kyverno
  Annotations:  meta.helm.sh/release-name: kyverno
  PolicyRule:
    Resources         Non-Resource URLs  Resource Names  Verbs
    ---------         -----------------  --------------  -----
    *.*               []                 []              [get list watch create update patch delete]
    ...

  # 3. See what Kyverno can ACTUALLY do (impersonation):
  $ kubectl auth can-i --list \
      --as=system:serviceaccount:kyverno:kyverno

  Resources    Non-Resource URLs  Resource Names  Verbs
  pods         []                 []              [get list watch create update patch delete]
  secrets      []                 []              [get list watch]
  deployments  []                 []              [get list watch create update patch delete]
  namespaces   []                 []              [get list watch]

  # 4. Check a specific permission:
  $ kubectl auth can-i delete secrets \
      --as=system:serviceaccount:kyverno:kyverno
  yes    ← Kyverno CAN delete secrets!

kubectl auth can-i is invaluable: When debugging "why isn't my generate rule creating resources?", check if Kyverno actually has permission to create that resource type. When auditing "what can Kyverno access?", use --list to see everything.

For anihpj/jobpost, our security audit process includes running kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:kyverno:kyverno quarterly to verify Kyverno's permissions haven't drifted from the expected baseline.

Install & Config Q60 of 200
Who should be allowed to create, modify, and delete Kyverno policies, and what RBAC roles should be used?
A) Policy management should follow least-privilege: kyverno:admin (can create/modify/delete policies and view reports — for platform team), kyverno:user (can view policies and reports but cannot modify — for developers and auditors), kyverno:viewer (read-only access to policies and reports — for read-only dashboards). ClusterPolicies should be restricted to cluster administrators only since they affect ALL namespaces
B) Everyone in the cluster should be able to create and modify policies — it's a collaborative security model
C) Only the cluster-admin can touch Kyverno policies — there are no intermediate RBAC roles
D) Kyverno policies are self-service — any developer can create policies for their own namespace without restrictions
✅ Correct Answer: A

Policy creation is a privileged operation — a misconfigured policy can block all pod creation cluster-wide (DoS) or allow dangerous configurations that compromise security. Access must be tightly controlled.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno RBAC Roles — Access Matrix
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ROLE              CAN CREATE    CAN MODIFY   CAN VIEW    SCOPE  │
  │  ────              ──────────    ──────────   ────────    ───── │
  │  cluster-admin     ✅ All        ✅ All       ✅ All      Global│
  │  kyverno:admin     ✅ Policy     ✅ Policy    ✅ All      NS/CL │
  │                    ❌ CLPolicy   ❌ CLPolicy                    │
  │  kyverno:user      ❌            ❌           ✅ Reports  NS    │
  │  kyverno:viewer    ❌            ❌           ✅ (read)   NS    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  EXAMPLE RBAC — kyverno:admin (can manage Policies in anihpj):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                         │
  │  kind: ClusterRole                                                │
  │  metadata:                                                        │
  │    name: kyverno-admin                                            │
  │  rules:                                                           │
  │  - apiGroups: ["kyverno.io"]                                      │
  │    resources: ["policies"]              ← Namespaced Policies    │
  │    verbs: ["*"]                                                   │
  │  - apiGroups: ["wgpolicyk8s.io"]                                 │
  │    resources: ["policyreports"]         ← Can view reports       │
  │    verbs: ["get","list","watch"]                                 │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Note: Cannot touch ClusterPolicies or ClusterPolicyReports!     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  PRINCIPLE OF LEAST PRIVILEGE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  • Platform Team → cluster-admin (manages ClusterPolicies)       │
  │  • App Teams    → kyverno:admin (manages their namespace policies)│
  │  • Developers   → kyverno:user (views reports for their apps)    │
  │  • Auditors     → kyverno:viewer (read-only compliance)          │
  │  • CI/CD bots   → kyverno:admin (automated policy deployment)   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Policies ARE infrastructure: Treat policy YAMLs like you treat Terraform or Helm charts — they should be in Git, reviewed via pull requests, and deployed through CI/CD. No one should be manually kubectl apply-ing policies directly to production.

For anihpj/jobpost, only the 2-person platform team has ClusterPolicy creation rights. Application teams can view PolicyReports for their namespaces but cannot modify policies. All policy changes go through Git PR review and automated CI/CD deployment.

Install & Config Q61 of 200
How do you restrict Kyverno policy management to specific users or teams — ensuring only the platform team can create ClusterPolicies?
A) Policy management is automatically restricted — only cluster-admin can create Kyverno policies by default
B) Create RBAC roles that grant policy CRUD permissions only to specific groups: a ClusterRole with kyverno.io API group access granted to the platform team via ClusterRoleBinding. For namespace-scoped policies, use Role and RoleBinding to let individual teams manage their own Policies without touching ClusterPolicies. Combine with Kyverno itself to AUDIT policy changes
C) Policy management restriction is configured in the Kyverno ConfigMap with a list of allowed users — not through Kubernetes RBAC
D) Kyverno policies can only be managed through the Kyverno dashboard — kubectl access is automatically blocked
✅ Correct Answer: B

Kyverno policies are just Kubernetes resources — they follow the same RBAC model as Pods, Deployments, and Services. You restrict access the same way: create roles, bind them to groups, and enforce least privilege.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Restricting Policy Management — Anihpj Example
  # ClusterRole: Only manage ClusterPolicies (for platform team)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                         │
  │  kind: ClusterRole                                                │
  │  metadata:                                                        │
  │    name: kyverno-clusterpolicy-admin                              │
  │  rules:                                                           │
  │  - apiGroups: ["kyverno.io"]                                      │
  │    resources: ["clusterpolicies"]                                 │
  │    verbs: ["get","list","watch","create","update","patch","delete"]│
  │  - apiGroups: ["wgpolicyk8s.io"]                                 │
  │    resources: ["clusterpolicyreports"]                            │
  │    verbs: ["get","list","watch"]                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  # ClusterRoleBinding: Grant to platform-team group
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl create clusterrolebinding platform-kyverno \            │
  │    --clusterrole=kyverno-clusterpolicy-admin \                   │
  │    --group=platform-team                                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  # Role: Manage Policies ONLY in anihpj namespace (for app team)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1                         │
  │  kind: Role                                                       │
  │  metadata:                                                        │
  │    name: kyverno-policy-admin                                     │
  │    namespace: anihpj                                              │
  │  rules:                                                           │
  │  - apiGroups: ["kyverno.io"]                                      │
  │    resources: ["policies"]                                        │
  │    verbs: ["*"]                                                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RESULT:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  platform-team  → Can manage ClusterPolicies (cluster-wide)     │
  │  anihpj-team    → Can manage Policies (anihpj namespace only)    │
  │  developers     → Can VIEW PolicyReports (read-only)             │
  │  everyone else  → No policy access at all                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The RBAC is the guardrail: Even if a developer writes a valid ClusterPolicy YAML, they can't apply it because they lack the ClusterRole. This prevents shadow IT policies and ensures all cluster-wide policies go through the platform team's review process.

For anihpj/jobpost, only 2 platform engineers belong to the platform-team group with ClusterPolicy management rights. App teams manage their own namespace Policies via Git PRs reviewed by the platform team before deployment.

Install & Config Q62 of 200
What RBAC permissions does Kyverno itself need to read and watch resources across all namespaces?
A) Kyverno only needs to watch Pods — other resource types are not relevant to policy enforcement
B) Kyverno uses a special Kubernetes API that bypasses RBAC entirely — it doesn't need standard permissions
C) Kyverno needs get, list, watch on ALL resource types across ALL API groups in ALL namespaces. This is because policies can reference any Kubernetes resource (Pods, Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps, Secrets, CRDs, etc.), background scanning checks existing resources, and generate rules create new resources. The default ClusterRole created by Helm grants exactly these permissions — it's broad by necessity, not by oversight
D) Kyverno only needs permissions for namespaces where policies are explicitly deployed
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kyverno's RBAC is intentionally broad because policies can reference any resource type. A validate rule on Pods, a mutate rule on Deployments, a generate rule on NetworkPolicies — Kyverno must be able to read all of them.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Why Kyverno Needs Broad Read Access
  KYVERNO MUST READ THESE FOR POLICY EVALUATION:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  RESOURCE          WHY KYVERNO NEEDS TO READ IT                  │
  │  ────────          ────────────────────────────                  │
  │  Pods              Validate/mutate pod specs, generate rules     │
  │  Deployments       Autogen expands pod policies to controllers  │
  │  Services          Validate service types, ports                 │
  │  ConfigMaps        Context variables (API calls to ConfigMaps)  │
  │  Secrets           Context variables (API calls to Secrets)     │
  │  Namespaces        Generate rules triggered by new namespaces   │
  │  CRDs (any)        Policies can target any custom resource      │
  │  NetworkPolicies   Verify policy existence, generate defaults   │
  │  RBAC resources    Policies checking service accounts, roles    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WHAT KYVERNO CANNOT DO (without explicit verbs):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  • EXEC into pods (requires pods/exec subresource)              │
  │  • Read pod logs (requires pods/log subresource)                │
  │  • Impersonate users (requires impersonate verb)                │
  │  • Escalate privileges (requires escalate verb on roles)       │
  │                                                                   │
  │  These require ADDITIONAL permissions beyond the default role.  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Security consideration: Kyverno can read Secrets. This means if a policy uses context variables from a Secret, Kyverno can access that Secret's data. Ensure your Kyverno deployment is as secured as your Secrets — restrict who can modify the Kyverno deployment and ServiceAccount.

For anihpj/jobpost, we carefully audit which Secrets Kyverno policies access via context API calls. Only non-sensitive configuration Secrets are exposed to Kyverno; database credentials are never referenced in policies.

Install & Config Q63 of 200
How can you audit who modified a Kyverno policy using Kubernetes audit logs?
A) Policy modifications cannot be audited — Kubernetes audit logs don't capture CRD changes
B) Kyverno has a built-in change log accessible via kubectl describe policy — no external audit configuration needed
C) Only Kyverno policy reports track modifications — audit logs are not involved
D) Kubernetes audit logs capture ALL API requests, including CRD modifications. Configure audit policy to log kyverno.io API group at the RequestResponse level. Then query: cat /var/log/kubernetes/audit.log | jq 'select(.objectRef.apiGroup=="kyverno.io")'. This shows WHO (username), WHAT (create/update/delete policy), WHEN (timestamp), and the FULL request/response body. Forward audit logs to a SIEM for long-term retention and alerting
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kubernetes audit logs are the source of truth for "who did what and when." They capture every API request — including CRD operations like creating or modifying a Kyverno policy.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Auditing Kyverno Policy Changes
  AUDIT POLICY (to capture Kyverno events):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: audit.k8s.io/v1                                      │
  │  kind: Policy                                                     │
  │  rules:                                                           │
  │  - level: RequestResponse                                         │
  │    verbs: ["create","update","patch","delete"]                   │
  │    resources:                                                     │
  │    - group: "kyverno.io"                                         │
  │      resources: ["clusterpolicies","policies"]                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  AUDIT LOG ENTRY (policy modification):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  {                                                                │
  │    "timestamp": "2024-01-15T14:30:00Z",                          │
  │    "user": {                                                      │
  │      "username": "alice@anihpj.com",   ← WHO modified it        │
  │      "groups": ["platform-team"]                                  │
  │    },                                                             │
  │    "verb": "patch",                     ← WHAT they did          │
  │    "objectRef": {                                                 │
  │      "apiGroup": "kyverno.io",                                   │
  │      "resource": "clusterpolicies",                              │
  │      "name": "require-non-root-pods",   ← WHICH policy           │
  │      "namespace": ""                                              │
  │    },                                                             │
  │    "requestObject": { /* the patch content */ },                 │
  │    "responseObject": { /* the resulting policy */ }              │
  │  }                                                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  QUERY EXAMPLES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # All Kyverno policy changes                                    │
  │  cat audit.log | jq 'select(.objectRef.apiGroup=="kyverno.io")' │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Policy deletions only                                         │
  │  cat audit.log | jq 'select(.verb=="delete" and                  │
  │    .objectRef.apiGroup=="kyverno.io")'                           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Changes by a specific user                                    │
  │  cat audit.log | jq 'select(.user.username=="alice@anihpj.com"  │
  │    and .objectRef.apiGroup=="kyverno.io")'                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Forward to SIEM: For production clusters, configure the audit log backend to forward to a SIEM (Splunk, Elasticsearch, Datadog). Set up alerts for unexpected policy deletions or modifications outside of business hours.

For anihpj/jobpost, we forward audit logs to our Elasticsearch cluster and have alerts configured for any delete operation on ClusterPolicies. All legitimate policy changes happen through CI/CD with a known service account — manual changes trigger an immediate security investigation.

Install & Config Q64 of 200
Does Kyverno really need cluster-admin level access, or can you implement least-privilege RBAC for Kyverno?
A) Kyverno can TECHNICALLY work with less than cluster-admin, but PRACTICALLY it needs near-cluster-admin access. You can restrict it by: (1) using separate ClusterRoles for specific controller functions, (2) limiting write access to only the resource types your policies actually modify, (3) using namespace-scoped Roles instead of ClusterRoles if all your policies are namespaced. However, most organizations use the Helm default (broad ClusterRole) because maintaining fine-grained RBAC is complex, error-prone, and breaks when new policies target new resource types
B) Kyverno requires exactly cluster-admin — any attempt to restrict it will break policy enforcement
C) Kyverno can run with read-only access — mutate, generate, and verifyImages don't need write permissions
D) Least-privilege RBAC for Kyverno is automatically configured by Helm — no manual tuning is ever needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

The pragmatic answer is yes, Kyverno needs near-cluster-admin access. The Helm default is broad for good reason — maintaining fine-grained RBAC that keeps up with evolving policies is operationally expensive and risky.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Least-Privilege Kyverno RBAC — Possible But Painful
  DEFAULT (Helm): Broad ClusterRole — works for everything
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ Pro: Zero maintenance, never breaks                           │
  │  ❌ Con: Kyverno can read/modify anything                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  FINE-GRAINED: Separate ClusterRoles per function
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Read-only role (background scanning)                          │
  │  ClusterRole: kyverno-reader                                      │
  │  └→ get, list, watch on all resources                             │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Mutate role (JSON patch application)                          │
  │  ClusterRole: kyverno-mutator                                     │
  │  └→ patch on pods, deployments, statefulsets, daemonsets, jobs   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Generate role (resource creation)                             │
  │  ClusterRole: kyverno-generator                                   │
  │  └→ create on networkpolicies, resourcequotas, pdbs              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ❌ Problem: Every new policy that targets a new resource type   │
  │     requires updating these ClusterRoles. Forgot to update?     │
  │     Policy silently fails with authorization errors.             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  PRACTICAL RECOMMENDATION:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Use Helm defaults (broad ClusterRole) for most deployments    │
  │  ② COMPENSATE with other controls:                                │
  │     • Restrict who can modify the Kyverno deployment             │
  │     • Exclude kyverno namespace from policies                    │
  │     • Audit Kyverno's own API calls                              │
  │     • Monitor Kyverno's resource usage                           │
  │  ③ Only implement fine-grained RBAC if you have a dedicated      │
  │     security team that can maintain it                            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The real security control: The risk isn't that Kyverno HAS broad access — it's that someone could MODIFY Kyverno (its deployment, its ServiceAccount, its policies) to abuse that access. Lock down who can touch Kyverno, not what Kyverno can touch.

For anihpj/jobpost, we use the default Helm ClusterRole. Our security model focuses on controlling access TO Kyverno (who can modify it) rather than restricting Kyverno's own access (which would break policies).

2.5 High Availability InstallationsQ65-Q68

Install & Config Q65 of 200
How do you run multiple Kyverno replicas for high availability, and what happens during a replica failure?
A) Kyverno can only run as a single replica — multiple replicas cause duplicate webhook registrations
B) Multiple replicas are created by installing Kyverno multiple times — once per replica needed
C) Set replicaCount: 3 in Helm values. The API server load-balances admission requests across all healthy replicas. If one replica fails, the API server routes to the remaining replicas — no downtime. Leader election ensures only ONE replica performs background scans and generate operations, preventing duplicate work. Admission (validate/mutate) is stateless and ALL replicas handle it
D) Multiple replicas require a separate load balancer in front of the Kyverno service — Helm cannot configure this
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kyverno is designed for horizontal scaling — admission is stateless (any replica can handle any request), while background operations use leader election to avoid duplication.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno HA — How 3 Replicas Work
  HELM CONFIGURATION:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno \                          │
  │    -n kyverno --create-namespace \                               │
  │    --set replicaCount=3                                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ARCHITECTURE: 3 Replicas
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                   │
  │  API Server                                                       │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       │  AdmissionReview (POST /validate)                        │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐                     │
  │  │        kyverno-svc (ClusterIP)           │                     │
  │  │        Load-balances across replicas     │                     │
  │  └────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┘                     │
  │           │          │          │                                  │
  │           ▼          ▼          ▼                                  │
  │      ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐                            │
  │      │ REPL 1 │ │ REPL 2 │ │ REPL 3 │                            │
  │      │ (Leader)│ │        │ │        │                            │
  │      └───┬────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘                            │
  │          │                                                        │
  │          │  ADMISSION (ALL replicas):                             │
  │          │  • Validate rules → handled by any replica             │
  │          │  • Mutate rules   → handled by any replica             │
  │          │  • verifyImages   → handled by any replica             │
  │          │                                                        │
  │          │  BACKGROUND (LEADER only):                             │
  │          │  • Background scans → ONLY leader (Repl 1)             │
  │          │  • Generate rules   → ONLY leader                      │
  │          │  • Cleanup policies → ONLY leader                      │
  │          │                                                        │
  │          │  If Leader fails → Repl 2 or 3 becomes new Leader     │
  │          │  (leader election takes ~15 seconds)                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why leader election for background scans? If all 3 replicas ran background scans, you'd get 3x the API calls and 3x duplicate PolicyReports. Leader election ensures exactly one replica handles background work while all replicas handle admission.

For anihpj/jobpost, production runs 3 replicas across different nodes (via podAntiAffinity). This ensures Kyverno survives a single node failure without any interruption to policy enforcement.

Install & Config Q66 of 200
How does Kyverno's leader election work, and why is it necessary for multi-replica deployments?
A) Leader election is handled by an external etcd cluster — Kyverno doesn't manage it internally
B) Kyverno uses Kubernetes leader election via a Lease (coordination.k8s.io/v1) in the kyverno namespace. Multiple replicas compete for the lease; the one that acquires it becomes the leader and runs background operations. Others standby. If the leader's lease expires (pod crash, network partition), another replica acquires the lease within ~15 seconds. This prevents duplicate background scans, generate operations, and cleanup jobs while ensuring admission is always handled by all replicas
C) Leader election is only needed for the cleanup controller — all other Kyverno controllers are stateless
D) Kyverno uses a round-robin schedule — each replica takes turns being the leader on a fixed rotation
✅ Correct Answer: B

Leader election is a standard Kubernetes pattern — it uses the same Lease mechanism as kube-controller-manager and kube-scheduler. Kyverno stores its leader lock as a Lease resource in the kyverno namespace.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Leader Election — Lease-Based
  VIEW THE LEASE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get lease -n kyverno                                   │
  │  NAME                      HOLDER                  AGE            │
  │  kyverno-background        kyverno-7d8f9-abc12    5d            │
  │  kyverno-cleanup           kyverno-7d8f9-abc12    5d            │
  │  kyverno-reports           kyverno-7d8f9-abc12    5d            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  LEADER ELECTION FLOW:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  t=0s    All 3 replicas start                                     │
  │          Repl1, Repl2, Repl3 compete for leader lease             │
  │                                                                   │
  │  t=1s    Repl1 acquires lease → becomes LEADER                    │
  │          • Runs background scans                                  │
  │          • Runs generate rules                                    │
  │          • Runs cleanup policies                                  │
  │                                                                   │
  │          Repl2, Repl3 are STANDBY                                 │
  │          • Handle admission requests (validate, mutate, verify)   │
  │          • Watch the leader lease                                 │
  │                                                                   │
  │  t=30s   Repl1 crashes (OOMKilled)                                │
  │          Lease expires after ~15 seconds                          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  t=45s   Repl2 detects expired lease → acquires it                │
  │          • Becomes NEW LEADER                                     │
  │          • Resumes background operations                          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  t=60s   Repl1 restarts → becomes STANDBY                         │
  │          • Handles admission only                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Separate leases per controller: Kyverno uses multiple leases — one per background controller (background scan, cleanup, reports). This means if the cleanup controller fails on one replica, the background scan controller can still run on another.

For anihpj/jobpost, we monitor leader election via the kyverno_leader_election_master_status Prometheus metric. An alert fires if no replica holds the leader lease for more than 30 seconds, indicating a potential issue with background operations.

Install & Config Q67 of 200
Why do you need a PodDisruptionBudget (PDB) for Kyverno, and how do you configure it?
A) Kyverno doesn't need a PDB — it's stateless and can be safely evicted at any time
B) The PDB prevents kubectl from deleting Kyverno pods — it's a security feature, not an availability feature
C) The PDB is automatically created by the Kubernetes scheduler — no manual configuration is needed
D) A PDB prevents VOLUNTARY disruptions (node drains, cluster autoscaler, rolling updates) from taking down ALL Kyverno replicas simultaneously. If all Kyverno pods go down with failurePolicy: Fail, the cluster stops accepting new resources. Configure with --set podDisruptionBudget.minAvailable=2 for 3 replicas — this ensures at least 2 Kyverno pods are always running during maintenance operations
✅ Correct Answer: D

The PDB is your insurance policy against maintenance-induced outages. During a kubectl drain or cluster autoscaler scale-down, the PDB tells Kubernetes: "Don't evict too many Kyverno pods at once."

📝 Detailed Explanation
PDB — Preventing Kyverno Outage During Maintenance
  WITHOUT PDB (3 replicas):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl drain node-1 --ignore-daemonsets                        │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  Node-1: Kyverno Repl1 EVICTED                                   │
  │  Node-2: Kyverno Repl2 EVICTED  ← Scheduled for maintenance too │
  │  Node-3: Kyverno Repl3 EVICTED  ← Cluster autoscaler            │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⚠️ ALL 3 REPLICAS EVICTED SIMULTANEOUSLY!                      │
  │  ⚠️ No Kyverno pods running                                      │
  │  ⚠️ failurePolicy: Fail → ALL admissions BLOCKED                │
  │  ⚠️ Cluster stuck — can't create pods to restart Kyverno!       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WITH PDB (minAvailable: 2, 3 replicas):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl drain node-1 --ignore-daemonsets                        │
  │       │                                                          │
  │       ▼                                                          │
  │  Node-1: Kyverno Repl1 → PENDING EVICTION                        │
  │           PDB check: "Would this leave < 2 available?"           │
  │           Currently: 3 available → 2 after eviction              │
  │           ✅ 2 >= 2 → ALLOWED (Repl1 evicted)                   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  New Repl1 starts on Node-4 → 3 available again                  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  kubectl drain node-2                                            │
  │  Node-2: Kyverno Repl2 → PDB check                               │
  │           3 available → 2 after eviction → ✅ ALLOWED            │
  │                                                                   │
  │  RESULT: Never fewer than 2 replicas running                     │
  │  No admission outage during maintenance!                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  HELM CONFIGURATION:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno \                          │
  │    --set replicaCount=3 \                                        │
  │    --set podDisruptionBudget.minAvailable=2                      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Or in values.yaml:                                              │
  │  replicaCount: 3                                                  │
  │  podDisruptionBudget:                                             │
  │    minAvailable: 2                                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

PDB vs failurePolicy: The PDB protects against VOLUNTARY disruptions (maintenance). failurePolicy: Fail protects against INVOLUNTARY failures (crashes). Both are needed: PDB prevents you from accidentally taking Kyverno down; failurePolicy ensures the cluster is secure if it does go down.

For anihpj/jobpost, all production clusters use minAvailable: 2 with 3 replicas. This is tested quarterly by simulating node drains in our staging environment.

Install & Config Q68 of 200
How do you manage Kyverno policies consistently across multiple Kubernetes clusters?
A) Use GitOps with tools like ArgoCD or Flux: store all policies (ClusterPolicies and namespaced Policies) in a Git repository, organized by environment (base/ + overlays/ for dev/staging/prod). The GitOps tool syncs policies to each cluster automatically. Changes go through PR review → merge → auto-deploy. For policy inheritance, use Kustomize bases or Helm umbrella charts that include Kyverno + policies in a single deployment
B) Policies must be manually applied to each cluster using kubectl — there is no way to automate multi-cluster policy distribution
C) Kyverno has a built-in multi-cluster sync feature that automatically replicates policies across clusters
D) Multi-cluster policy management requires an enterprise Kyverno license — it's not available in the open-source version
✅ Correct Answer: A

Kyverno policies are just YAML files — they're infrastructure-as-code. Any GitOps tool that can apply YAML to a cluster can distribute Kyverno policies. No special multi-cluster features needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Cluster Policy Distribution — GitOps Pattern
  GIT REPOSITORY STRUCTURE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kyverno-policies/                                                │
  │  ├── base/                                                        │
  │  │   ├── require-non-root.yaml       ← Applies to ALL clusters   │
  │  │   ├── drop-capabilities.yaml                                   │
  │  │   ├── block-hostpath.yaml                                      │
  │  │   └── kustomization.yaml                                       │
  │  ├── overlays/                                                    │
  │  │   ├── dev/                                                     │
  │  │   │   ├── validationFailureAction-patch.yaml  ← Audit only    │
  │  │   │   └── kustomization.yaml                                   │
  │  │   ├── staging/                                                 │
  │  │   │   └── kustomization.yaml                                   │
  │  │   └── production/                                              │
  │  │       ├── replicaCount-patch.yaml        ← 3 replicas         │
  │  │       └── kustomization.yaml                                   │
  │  └── clusters/                                                    │
  │      ├── kubeadm/                   ← Cluster-specific policies  │
  │      ├── kind-cka-lab/                                            │
  │      └── minikube/                                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ARGOCD APPLICATION (per cluster):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1                                 │
  │  kind: Application                                                │
  │  metadata:                                                        │
  │    name: kyverno-policies-prod                                    │
  │  spec:                                                            │
  │    source:                                                        │
  │      repoURL: https://github.com/anihpj/kyverno-policies         │
  │      path: overlays/production                                    │
  │    destination:                                                   │
  │      server: https://prod-cluster-api:6443                        │
  │      namespace: kyverno                                           │
  │    syncPolicy:                                                    │
  │      automated:                                                   │
  │        prune: true                   ← Remove policies not in Git│
  │        selfHeal: true                ← Revert manual changes     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

GitOps advantages for policies: (1) Single source of truth — Git shows exactly what policies are deployed where. (2) Audit trail — every change has a PR, reviewer, and timestamp. (3) Rollback — revert a bad policy with git revert. (4) Drift detection — ArgoCD/Flux alert if someone manually changes a policy on the cluster.

For anihpj/jobpost, we use ArgoCD to sync Kyverno policies to all 6 clusters. Base policies apply everywhere; overlays customize enforcement mode (Audit for dev, Enforce for production). A PR merged to main deploys to all clusters within 3 minutes.

2.6 Upgrading KyvernoQ69-Q72

Install & Config Q69 of 200
How do you upgrade Kyverno using Helm, and what precautions should you take before upgrading?
A) Kyverno upgrades automatically via the Kubernetes operator — no manual Helm commands are needed
B) helm repo update && helm upgrade kyverno kyverno/kyverno -n kyverno. Precautions: (1) Backup all policies (kubectl get cpol,pol -A -o yaml > backup.yaml), (2) check version compatibility in release notes, (3) test in staging first, (4) upgrade during maintenance window, (5) verify CRD migrations completed (kubectl get crd | grep kyverno), (6) monitor Kyverno pod logs during and after upgrade. The Helm upgrade handles rolling updates of the Kyverno deployment with zero-downtime for admission
C) Kyverno must be uninstalled and reinstalled — in-place upgrades are not supported
D) Upgrading requires manually editing the Kyverno deployment YAML — Helm upgrade is not supported for version changes
✅ Correct Answer: B

Helm upgrade is the standard and recommended way to upgrade Kyverno. It handles the rolling update of pods, CRD migrations, and webhook configuration updates — all while maintaining admission availability.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Upgrade — Complete Process
  BEFORE UPGRADE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # 1. Check current version                                      │
  │  $ helm list -n kyverno                                          │
  │  NAME     NAMESPACE  REVISION  STATUS    CHART           APP VER │
  │  kyverno  kyverno    1         deployed  kyverno-1.10.0  1.10.0 │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # 2. Backup all policies                                        │
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicy -o yaml > backup-cpol.yaml         │
  │  $ kubectl get policy -A -o yaml > backup-policies.yaml         │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # 3. Check release notes for breaking changes                   │
  │  $ helm search repo kyverno/kyverno --versions                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  UPGRADE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # 4. Update repo and upgrade                                    │
  │  $ helm repo update kyverno                                      │
  │  $ helm upgrade kyverno kyverno/kyverno \                        │
  │      -n kyverno \                                                │
  │      --version 1.11.0 \                                          │
  │      --reuse-values \              ← Keep existing config        │
  │      --wait \                      ← Wait for pods to be ready   │
  │      --timeout 10m                                                │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Release "kyverno" has been upgraded. Happy Helming!             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  AFTER UPGRADE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # 5. Verify                                                     │
  │  $ helm list -n kyverno                                          │
  │  NAME     NAMESPACE  REVISION  STATUS    CHART           APP VER │
  │  kyverno  kyverno    2         deployed  kyverno-1.11.0  1.11.0 │
  │                                                                   │
  │  $ kubectl get pods -n kyverno                                   │
  │  NAME                       READY   STATUS    RESTARTS   AGE     │
  │  kyverno-8e9f0a-xyz12      1/1     Running   0          2m     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # 6. Test with a policy                                        │
  │  $ kubectl apply -f test-policy.yaml                             │
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicy test-policy                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Rollback: If the upgrade causes issues, helm rollback kyverno -n kyverno reverts to the previous version. This is why backups are critical — the rollback restores the Kyverno deployment but doesn't restore any policies that were modified or deleted.
