For anihpj/jobpost, we upgrade Kyverno in a rolling fashion: minikube first (dev), then kind clusters (staging), then kubeadm (production). Each upgrade is validated for 24 hours before proceeding to the next environment.

Install & Config Q70 of 200
How do you check Kyverno version compatibility with your Kubernetes cluster version?
A) All Kyverno versions work with all Kubernetes versions — there is no version compatibility matrix
B) Kyverno automatically detects the Kubernetes version and adjusts its behavior — no manual compatibility check is needed
C) Check the Kyverno release notes and documentation for the version skew policy: Kyverno follows the same N-2 support as Kubernetes (each Kyverno version supports the current K8s version and the previous two). Verify with: kubectl version --short (cluster version) and helm search repo kyverno/kyverno --versions (available Kyverno versions). The Kyverno GitHub releases page lists compatible Kubernetes versions for each release
D) Version compatibility is checked by running kyverno doctor — a dedicated compatibility tool that must be installed separately
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kyverno follows the same version skew policy as core Kubernetes components — supporting the current and previous two minor versions. This ensures you can upgrade Kubernetes without immediately needing to upgrade Kyverno.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Version Compatibility Matrix
  VERSION SKEW POLICY (N-2 support):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  KUBERNETES VERSION    SUPPORTED KYVERNO VERSIONS                │
  │  ─────────────────    ─────────────────────────                  │
  │  v1.30                 Kyverno 1.12.x (latest)                   │
  │                        Kyverno 1.11.x                             │
  │                        Kyverno 1.10.x                             │
  │                                                                   │
  │  v1.29                 Kyverno 1.12.x                            │
  │                        Kyverno 1.11.x (recommended)               │
  │                        Kyverno 1.10.x                             │
  │                                                                   │
  │  v1.28                 Kyverno 1.12.x                            │
  │                        Kyverno 1.11.x                             │
  │                        Kyverno 1.10.x (recommended)               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CHECK YOUR ENVIRONMENT:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Kubernetes version                                            │
  │  $ kubectl version --short                                       │
  │  Server Version: v1.30.0                                         │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Available Kyverno versions                                    │
  │  $ helm search repo kyverno/kyverno --versions                   │
  │  NAME             CHART VERSION   APP VERSION   DESCRIPTION      │
  │  kyverno/kyverno  1.12.0          1.12.0        Latest stable   │
  │  kyverno/kyverno  1.11.0          1.11.0        Previous stable │
  │  kyverno/kyverno  1.10.0          1.10.0        N-2 stable      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Current Kyverno version                                       │
  │  $ kubectl get deploy kyverno -n kyverno \                       │
  │      -o jsonpath='{.spec.template.spec.containers[0].image}'     │
  │  ghcr.io/kyverno/kyverno:v1.10.0                                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Upgrade order matters: Always upgrade Kubernetes FIRST, then Kyverno. New Kyverno versions may use Kubernetes API features that don't exist in older clusters. If you upgrade Kyverno before Kubernetes, you may get API errors for missing resource types.

For anihpj/jobpost, we maintain a version compatibility matrix in our infrastructure documentation. Before any Kubernetes upgrade, we verify that our current Kyverno version supports the target Kubernetes version — and upgrade Kyverno afterward if needed.

Install & Config Q71 of 200
What are CRD migrations during Kyverno upgrades, and what can break?
A) CRDs never change between Kyverno versions — migration is not a concern
B) CRD migrations are fully automatic and never cause issues — no manual intervention is needed
C) CRD migrations only affect the Kyverno dashboard UI — policies continue working through kubectl
D) CRD migrations occur when Kyverno adds, removes, or modifies fields in its CRD schemas (Policy, ClusterPolicy, etc.). What can break: (1) Deprecated fields removed — policies using them fail validation, (2) New required fields — existing policies without them become invalid, (3) Schema changes — policies that passed validation before may now fail. Always check release notes for "Breaking Changes" and "Action Required" sections before upgrading. Test with kyverno apply against a staging cluster first
✅ Correct Answer: D

CRD migrations are the riskiest part of Kyverno upgrades. When the schema changes, existing policies may suddenly become invalid — not because the policy logic is wrong, but because the YAML structure no longer matches the CRD schema.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CRD Migration — What Can Break
  MIGRATION RISK 1: Deprecated field removed
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Kyverno v1.9 policy:                                            │
  │  spec:                                                            │
  │    validationFailureAction: enforce  ← Old field name            │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Kyverno v1.10 upgrade: field renamed to Enforce                 │
  │  ❌ Old policies using "enforce" now fail CRD validation!        │
  │  Fix: Update to validationFailureAction: Enforce                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  MIGRATION RISK 2: New required field
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Kyverno v2alpha1 CleanupPolicy:                                 │
  │  spec:                                                            │
  │    schedule: "0 2 * * *"                                         │
  │    match: { ... }                                                │
  │                                                                   │
  │  v2beta1 upgrade adds required field: conditions                 │
  │  ❌ Existing CleanupPolicies without "conditions" are invalid    │
  │  Fix: Add conditions to all CleanupPolicies                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  SAFE UPGRADE WORKFLOW:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Read release notes (Breaking Changes section)                 │
  │  ② Backup all policies:                                          │
  │     kubectl get cpol,pol -A -o yaml > backup.yaml               │
  │  ③ Test in dev: upgrade → kyverno apply backup.yaml             │
  │  ④ Fix any failing policies                                      │
  │  ⑤ Upgrade staging → verify                                      │
  │  ⑥ Upgrade production → monitor PolicyReports                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Helm's CRD management: By default, Helm does NOT manage CRDs during upgrades (to prevent accidental data loss). You may need to manually apply CRD updates or use --set crds.install=true with caution. Always check the upgrade documentation for CRD-specific instructions.

For anihpj/jobpost, we maintain a pre-upgrade checklist that includes running kyverno apply all policies against a test cluster running the target version. Any policy that fails gets fixed before the production upgrade.

Install & Config Q72 of 200
What are the best practices for backing up Kyverno policies before an upgrade?
A) (1) Export all policies: kubectl get clusterpolicy -o yaml > cpol-backup.yaml and kubectl get policy -A -o yaml > pol-backup.yaml. (2) Store in Git (preferred — version-controlled, auditable). (3) Test in staging FIRST with the backup policies. (4) Verify policies still work with kyverno apply against the target version. (5) Document the rollback procedure: helm rollback kyverno -n kyverno. (6) Schedule upgrades during maintenance windows with monitoring
B) Backups are unnecessary — Kyverno automatically backs up policies to etcd snapshots
C) The only backup needed is a Helm values file — policies are automatically preserved during upgrades
D) Policy backups must be encrypted and stored offline — kubectl export is not secure enough for backup purposes
✅ Correct Answer: A

Policy backup is cheap insurance — a one-minute operation that can save hours of recovery time. The best backup is Git: your policies are already version-controlled as infrastructure-as-code.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Policy Backup — Complete Checklist
  BACKUP COMMANDS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # All ClusterPolicies                                           │
  │  kubectl get clusterpolicy -o yaml > cpol-backup-$(date +%F).yaml│
  │                                                                   │
  │  # All namespaced Policies                                       │
  │  kubectl get policy -A -o yaml > pol-backup-$(date +%F).yaml    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # All PolicyReports (compliance evidence)                       │
  │  kubectl get policyreport -A -o yaml > polr-backup-$(date +%F).yaml│
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Kyverno ConfigMap                                             │
  │  kubectl get configmap kyverno -n kyverno -o yaml > cm-backup.yaml│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  GITOPS BEST PRACTICE (policies ARE code):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kyverno-policies/          ← Git repository                     │
  │  ├── policies/                                                  │
  │  │   ├── require-non-root.yaml                                   │
  │  │   ├── drop-capabilities.yaml                                  │
  │  │   └── ...                                                     │
  │  ├── README.md                ← Upgrade history documented      │
  │  └── CHANGELOG.md             ← Breaking changes tracked        │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ✅ Git = your backup. If policies are destroyed, re-apply from Git.│
  │  ✅ Git history = your audit trail. See who changed what, when.  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ROLLBACK PROCEDURE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # If upgrade breaks policies:                                   │
  │  $ helm rollback kyverno -n kyverno                              │
  │  $ kubectl apply -f cpol-backup-2024-01-15.yaml                  │
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicy                                     │
  │  # Verify READY: True for all policies                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Git is your best backup: If you're already storing policies in Git (as recommended), an emergency kubectl get backup is a secondary safety net. The primary backup is your Git repository — it has the policies, the history, and the PR discussions explaining why each policy exists.

For anihpj/jobpost, all 13 policies are in our kyverno-policies Git repository synced via ArgoCD. Before every Kyverno upgrade, we also run a manual kubectl get backup as a belt-and-suspenders precaution.

💻 Category 3: Kyverno CLI (12%)Q73-Q96

Testing Policies Before They Hit the Cluster

This category is about the Kyverno CLI — your spell-checker for policies. Before applying a policy to a live cluster, you test it locally with kyverno apply, run structured tests with kyverno test, and debug JMESPath expressions with kyverno jp. Think of it like linting code before committing — catch errors early, before they affect production.

3.1 Installing Kyverno CLIQ73-Q76

Kyverno CLI Q73 of 200
How do you install the Kyverno CLI on macOS or Linux using Homebrew?
A) Kyverno CLI is not available on Homebrew — it must be compiled from source
B) brew install kyverno-cli — the package is named differently from the in-cluster Kyverno
C) brew install kyverno installs the latest Kyverno CLI. Verify with kyverno version. The CLI is a single binary that includes apply, test, and jp subcommands — no separate tools needed
D) Kyverno CLI is only available as a kubectl plugin — it cannot be installed as a standalone binary
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Kyverno CLI is a single static binary — kyverno. It includes all subcommands (apply, test, jp, version, completion) in one package. No plugins, no dependencies, no language runtime required.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno CLI — Installation Methods
  HOMEBREW (macOS / Linux):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ brew install kyverno                                          │
  │  ==> Downloading https://github.com/kyverno/kyverno/releases/... │
  │  ==> Installing kyverno                                          │
  │  🍺 kyverno 1.11.0 installed                                     │
  │                                                                   │
  │  $ kyverno version                                               │
  │  Version: 1.11.0                                                  │
  │  Time: 2024-01-15T10:00:00Z                                      │
  │  Git commit: abc123def456                                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CURL (any platform):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ curl -LO https://github.com/kyverno/kyverno/releases/         │
  │    download/v1.11.0/kyverno-cli_v1.11.0_linux_amd64.tar.gz      │
  │  $ tar -xzf kyverno-cli_v1.11.0_linux_amd64.tar.gz               │
  │  $ sudo mv kyverno /usr/local/bin/                               │
  │  $ kyverno version                                               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ALL INCLUDED SUBCOMMANDS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno --help                                                 │
  │  Available Commands:                                              │
  │    apply       Apply policies on resources                       │
  │    completion  Generate shell completion scripts                 │
  │    docs        Generate reference documentation                  │
  │    jp          Evaluate JMESPath expressions                     │
  │    test        Run tests against policies and resources          │
  │    version     Print version information                         │
  │    help        Help about any command                             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

No cluster connection needed: The CLI works completely offline — it evaluates policies against local YAML files. You don't need a running Kubernetes cluster to test policies. This is perfect for CI/CD pipelines and local development.

For anihpj/jobpost, every developer has kyverno installed via Homebrew. Before opening a PR with a new policy, they run kyverno apply policy.yaml --resource test-pod.yaml locally to validate it works.

Kyverno CLI Q74 of 200
How do you install the Kyverno CLI by downloading directly from GitHub releases using curl?
A) Download the platform-specific tar.gz from GitHub releases: curl -LO https://github.com/kyverno/kyverno/releases/download/v1.11.0/kyverno-cli_v1.11.0_linux_amd64.tar.gz, extract with tar -xzf, move the binary to /usr/local/bin/. The URL pattern is: kyverno-cli_v{VERSION}_{OS}_{ARCH}.tar.gz where OS can be linux/darwin and ARCH can be amd64/arm64

C) The curl URL requires authentication — you need a GitHub token to download Kyverno CLI releases

✅ Correct Answer: A

GitHub releases provide pre-compiled binaries for all major platforms. The naming convention is consistent: kyverno-cli_v{VERSION}_{OS}_{ARCH}.tar.gz. No compilation needed — just download, extract, and run.

CI/CD usage: The curl approach is ideal for CI/CD pipelines — just add a download step before running kyverno apply or kyverno test. The binary is static (no dependencies) and starts instantly.

For anihpj/jobpost, our CI/CD pipeline downloads the Kyverno CLI via curl in the "test" stage, then runs kyverno test against all our policies and sample resources. This catches policy issues before they reach the cluster.

Kyverno CLI Q75 of 200
How do you verify that the Kyverno CLI is correctly installed and working?
A) The CLI verification requires applying a test policy to a live cluster — local verification is not possible
B) Run kyverno version to check the installed version. Test functionality with a simple policy: echo 'apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1...' | kyverno apply - -r test-pod.yaml. The CLI works completely offline — no cluster connection needed to verify it's working
C) Verification is done by checking if the kyverno binary exists in PATH — running any command besides version will fail without a cluster
D) kyverno doctor is the only command that verifies CLI installation — other commands require cluster access
✅ Correct Answer: B

Verification is a two-step process: check the binary exists with kyverno version, then test actual policy evaluation with kyverno apply against a sample resource. Both work completely offline.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CLI Verification — Two Steps
  STEP 1: Check version and binary location
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno version                                               │
  │  Version: 1.11.0                                                  │
  │  Time: 2024-01-15T10:00:00Z                                      │
  │  Git commit: abc123def456                                         │
  │                                                                   │
  │  $ which kyverno                                                  │
  │  /usr/local/bin/kyverno                                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  STEP 2: Test with a simple policy (offline)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Create a test policy                                          │
  │  cat > test-policy.yaml << EOF                                   │
  │  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1                                        │
  │  kind: ClusterPolicy                                              │
  │  metadata:                                                        │
  │    name: require-ns-label                                         │
  │  spec:                                                            │
  │    validationFailureAction: Audit                                  │
  │    rules:                                                         │
  │    - name: check-ns-label                                         │
  │      match:                                                       │
  │        any:                                                       │
  │        - resources:                                               │
  │            kinds: [Namespace]                                      │
  │      validate:                                                    │
  │        message: "Namespace must have label 'team'"               │
  │        pattern:                                                   │
  │          metadata:                                                │
  │            labels:                                                │
  │              team: "?*"                                           │
  │  EOF                                                              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Create a test resource                                        │
  │  cat > test-ns.yaml << EOF                                        │
  │  apiVersion: v1                                                   │
  │  kind: Namespace                                                  │
  │  metadata:                                                        │
  │    name: test-ns                                                  │
  │    labels:                                                        │
  │      team: platform                                               │
  │  EOF                                                              │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Apply policy against resource                                  │
  │  $ kyverno apply test-policy.yaml --resource test-ns.yaml        │
  │  Applying 1 policy rule(s) to 1 resource(s)...                   │
  │  pass: 1, fail: 0, warn: 0, error: 0, skip: 0                   │
  │  ✅ CLI is working correctly!                                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The CLI is self-contained: It bundles the same policy evaluation engine that runs in-cluster. Testing locally with kyverno apply gives you the same results as applying the policy to a real cluster — minus the risk of breaking production.

For anihpj/jobpost, our developer onboarding script includes this two-step verification. Every new team member confirms their Kyverno CLI is working before they write their first policy.

Kyverno CLI Q76 of 200
Should the Kyverno CLI version match the in-cluster Kyverno version? What happens if they differ?
A) CLI and cluster versions must be identical — any difference will cause kubectl to reject policies
B) The CLI version is irrelevant — it works with any cluster version because policies are just YAML
C) The CLI version should closely match the cluster version, but minor differences (within 1-2 minor versions) are generally safe. If the CLI is OLDER than the cluster, it may not support new policy fields and features. If the CLI is NEWER, it may use fields that the cluster doesn't recognize yet. Best practice: keep CLI and cluster on the same minor version, or use the CLI version that matches your TARGET cluster version for pre-upgrade testing
D) The CLI auto-detects the cluster version and adjusts its behavior — version mismatch is automatically handled
✅ Correct Answer: C

Version alignment matters because the CLI and cluster share the same policy engine. If the CLI doesn't understand a new field, it can't validate it. If the cluster doesn't understand a field the CLI allows, the policy will be rejected when applied.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CLI vs Cluster Version — Compatibility
  VERSION MISMATCH SCENARIOS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CLI v1.10  →  Cluster v1.12                                     │
  │  └→ CLI doesn't know about new v1.12 fields (CEL expressions)    │
  │     ⚠️ Policy using CEL: CLI says "unknown field", cluster says OK│
  │     ℹ️ Safe — CLI just has fewer features                        │
  │                                                                   │
  │  CLI v1.12  →  Cluster v1.10                                     │
  │  └→ CLI allows CEL expressions, but cluster doesn't support them │
  │     ⚠️ Policy passes CLI test, fails when applied to cluster!    │
  │     🔴 Dangerous — false sense of security                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  VERSION CHECK COMMANDS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # CLI version                                                    │
  │  $ kyverno version                                               │
  │  Version: 1.11.0                                                  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Cluster version                                                │
  │  $ kubectl get deploy kyverno -n kyverno \                       │
  │      -o jsonpath='{.spec.template.spec.containers[0].image}'     │
  │  ghcr.io/kyverno/kyverno:v1.11.0                                  │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # ✅ Matching — safe to use CLI for testing                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RECOMMENDATION:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  • Development: CLI version = cluster version (match exactly)    │
  │  • CI/CD: Install CLI matching PRODUCTION cluster version        │
  │  • Pre-upgrade: Test with NEW target CLI version before upgrade  │
  │  • Multiple clusters: Match CLI to oldest cluster for safety     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Homebrew auto-update caveat: brew upgrade may update your CLI to a newer version than your cluster. Pin the version with brew pin kyverno if needed, or install a specific version via curl.

For anihpj/jobpost, we pin the Kyverno CLI version in our CI/CD pipeline to match the production cluster version. Developers are encouraged to match their local CLI to the production version as well.

3.2 kyverno apply — Testing Policies LocallyQ77-Q84

Kyverno CLI Q77 of 200
What does kyverno apply do, and how is it used to test policies against resource YAMLs?
A) kyverno apply applies policies to a live Kubernetes cluster — it's the CLI equivalent of kubectl apply
B) kyverno apply only validates policy syntax — it doesn't test policies against actual resources
C) kyverno apply is used to install Kyverno into a cluster from the command line
D) kyverno apply policy.yaml --resource pod.yaml evaluates the policy against the resource LOCALLY (no cluster needed) and reports whether the resource passes or fails each policy rule. It's like a "dry-run on steroids" — it uses the same policy engine as the in-cluster Kyverno, so results are identical to what would happen if you applied the policy to a real cluster
✅ Correct Answer: D

kyverno apply is the most-used Kyverno CLI command — it's your policy testing sandbox. Give it a policy and a resource, and it tells you whether the resource would be allowed or blocked, without touching a real cluster.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kyverno apply — The Policy Testing Sandbox
  BASIC USAGE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno apply policy.yaml --resource pod.yaml                 │
  │  Applying 1 policy rule(s) to 1 resource(s)...                   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  policy require-non-root -> resource default/Pod/nginx           │
  │    │                                                             │
  │    ├─ validate-runAsNonRoot: FAIL                                │
  │    │  message: Running as root is not allowed.                   │
  │    │  Set securityContext.runAsNonRoot to true.                  │
  │    │                                                             │
  │  Summary: pass: 0, fail: 1, warn: 0, error: 0, skip: 0         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  MULTIPLE POLICIES + MULTIPLE RESOURCES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno apply ./policies/ --resource ./resources/             │
  │  Applying 5 policy rule(s) to 3 resource(s)...                   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  policy require-non-root -> resource default/Pod/nginx           │
  │    validate-runAsNonRoot: FAIL                                    │
  │  policy drop-capabilities -> resource default/Pod/nginx          │
  │    validate-drop-capabilities: PASS                               │
  │  policy require-labels -> resource default/Pod/nginx             │
  │    validate-labels: PASS                                          │
  │  ...                                                              │
  │  Summary: pass: 12, fail: 3, warn: 0, error: 0, skip: 0         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WHAT IT TESTS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ Validate rules — does the resource comply?                   │
  │  ✅ Mutate rules — what would be changed?                        │
  │  ✅ Generate rules — what would be created?                      │
  │  ✅ verifyImages — would the signature pass? (if --registry set)│
  │  ❌ Background scans — not applicable (no existing resources)    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Identical engine, zero risk: The CLI uses the exact same policy evaluation engine as the in-cluster Kyverno admission controller. This means if a policy passes kyverno apply locally, it will pass in the cluster (assuming the same Kyverno version). Conversely, if it fails locally, it WILL fail in the cluster.

For anihpj/jobpost, kyverno apply is integrated into our CI/CD pipeline. Every PR that modifies a policy runs kyverno apply against a library of test resources. Failing policies are blocked from merging.

Kyverno CLI Q78 of 200
How do you test an entire directory of policies against a directory of resources using kyverno apply?
A) kyverno apply ./policies/ --resource ./resources/ — Kyverno recursively scans the policies directory for all .yaml and .yml files containing policies, and the resources directory for resource YAMLs. Each resource is tested against EVERY policy. Use --policy-report for structured output
B) kyverno apply can only process one file at a time — you must loop over directories manually
C) Directory scanning requires the --recursive flag — without it, only the first file in each directory is processed
D) Policies and resources must be in the SAME directory when using directory mode
✅ Correct Answer: A

Directory mode is the most practical way to use kyverno apply — it mirrors how you organize policies in a Git repository. Point it at your kyverno/ directory and your k8s/ test fixtures directory.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Directory Mode — Testing All Policies at Once
  DIRECTORY STRUCTURE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kyverno/                             ← Policies directory       │
  │  ├── require-non-root.yaml                                        │
  │  ├── drop-capabilities.yaml                                       │
  │  ├── block-hostpath.yaml                                          │
  │  └── require-resource-limits.yaml                                 │
  │                                                                   │
  │  test-resources/                      ← Test resources            │
  │  ├── good-pod.yaml                    (should PASS all policies)  │
  │  ├── bad-root-pod.yaml                (should FAIL non-root)      │
  │  └── bad-hostpath-pod.yaml            (should FAIL hostpath)     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  COMMAND:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno apply ./kyverno/ --resource ./test-resources/         │
  │  Applying 4 policy rule(s) to 3 resource(s)...                   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  policy require-non-root -> resource default/Pod/good-pod        │
  │    validate-runAsNonRoot: PASS                                    │
  │  policy require-non-root -> resource default/Pod/bad-root-pod    │
  │    validate-runAsNonRoot: FAIL                                    │
  │  policy block-hostpath -> resource default/Pod/bad-hostpath-pod  │
  │    validate-hostpath: FAIL                                        │
  │  ...                                                              │
  │  Summary: pass: 10, fail: 2, warn: 0, error: 0, skip: 0         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

CI/CD integration: Store test resources in a test-resources/ directory alongside your policies. Your CI pipeline runs kyverno apply ./kyverno/ --resource ./test-resources/ on every PR. Expected results: good-pod.yaml should have zero failures, bad-*.yaml should have specific expected failures.

For anihpj/jobpost, our kyverno/ directory contains 13 policy files and our test-resources/ directory contains ~20 sample resources (both compliant and non-compliant). CI/CD runs the full test suite on every PR.

Kyverno CLI Q79 of 200
What are the key kyverno apply flags: --policy-report, --cluster, and --namespace?
A) These flags control the visual output theme of the CLI — they don't affect policy evaluation
B) --policy-report outputs results in PolicyReport format (structured, machine-readable). --cluster tests policies against resources in a LIVE Kubernetes cluster instead of local files. --namespace <ns> scopes the --cluster check to a specific namespace (equivalent to kubectl -n <ns>)
C) --policy-report generates a PDF report; --cluster connects to a remote Kyverno dashboard; --namespace sets the output file namespace
D) These are kubectl flags, not kyverno apply flags — kyverno apply doesn't have these options
✅ Correct Answer: B

These three flags bridge the gap between local testing and cluster validation. --policy-report gives you machine-readable output; --cluster tests against live resources.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kyverno apply Flags — Reference
  --policy-report (structured output):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno apply policy.yaml -r pod.yaml --policy-report        │
  │  {                                                                │
  │    "results": [{                                                  │
  │      "policy": "require-non-root",                               │
  │      "rule": "validate-runAsNonRoot",                            │
  │      "result": "fail",                                           │
  │      "message": "Running as root is not allowed",                │
  │      "resource": "default/Pod/nginx"                             │
  │    }],                                                            │
  │    "summary": {"pass": 0, "fail": 1, "warn": 0, "error": 0}     │
  │  }                                                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  --cluster (test against LIVE cluster):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno apply ./policies/ --cluster                           │
  │  Applying 13 policy rule(s) to resources in cluster...           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ⚠️  Reads ALL matching resources from the cluster               │
  │  ⚠️  Uses current kubectl context                                 │
  │  ⚠️  Does NOT modify anything (read-only)                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  --namespace (scope cluster check):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno apply ./policies/ --cluster --namespace anihpj       │
  │  Only tests against resources in the "anihpj" namespace          │
  │  Equivalent to: kubectl get pods -n anihpj | kyverno apply      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

--cluster is for auditing: Use --cluster to check if existing resources in a live cluster comply with your policies. This is equivalent to a background scan but run on-demand. It's read-only — it never modifies resources.

For anihpj/jobpost, we use --policy-report in CI/CD to produce JSON output that our pipeline can parse and fail if unexpected violations are found. We use --cluster --namespace anihpj for ad-hoc compliance checks during incident response.

Kyverno CLI Q80 of 200
How do you interpret kyverno apply output — what do pass, fail, warn, error, and skip results mean?
A) All five result types mean the same thing — they're just different display colors
B) pass means the policy is correctly written; fail means the policy has syntax errors; skip means the policy is disabled
C) Only pass and fail matter — warn, error, and skip are debug artifacts that can be ignored
D) pass = resource complies with the policy rule. fail = resource violates the policy (would be blocked in Enforce mode). warn = policy is in Audit mode and the resource violates it (reported but not blocked). error = the policy rule itself failed to evaluate (bad JMESPath, API call timeout, etc.). skip = the policy rule doesn't apply to this resource (didn't match the selector). The summary line shows totals: pass: 10, fail: 2, warn: 1, error: 0, skip: 3
✅ Correct Answer: D

Each result type tells a different story about the relationship between your policy and the resource. Understanding them is essential for debugging "why didn't my policy catch this?" and "why is this being blocked?"

📝 Detailed Explanation
kyverno apply Output — Result Types
  SAMPLE OUTPUT:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno apply ./policies/ --resource ./test-pods/             │
  │  Applying 5 policy rule(s) to 4 resource(s)...                   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  policy require-non-root -> resource default/Pod/good-pod        │
  │    validate-runAsNonRoot: PASS ✓                                 │
  │  policy require-non-root -> resource default/Pod/bad-root-pod    │
  │    validate-runAsNonRoot: FAIL ✗                                  │
  │  policy complex-policy -> resource default/Pod/test-pod          │
  │    validate-something: ERROR ⚠                                    │
  │  policy namespaced-only -> resource default/Pod/any-pod          │
  │    validate-namespace: SKIP →                                    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Summary: pass: 8, fail: 3, warn: 0, error: 1, skip: 5          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RESULT TYPE MEANING:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  RESULT   IN AUDIT MODE        IN ENFORCE MODE    INDICATES      │
  │  ──────   ─────────────        ───────────────    ────────       │
  │  pass     No violation         No violation       ✅ Compliant   │
  │  fail     Logged to PolR       Resource BLOCKED   ❌ Violation  │
  │  warn     Logged to PolR       Resource BLOCKED   ⚠️ Violation  │
  │  error    Policy error         Policy error       🔧 Fix policy │
  │  skip     Rule doesn't apply   Rule doesn't apply → Ignored     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WHEN TO WORRY:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  fail:   Expected on bad resources, fix the RESOURCE             │
  │  error:  NEVER expected — fix the POLICY (bad JMESPath, etc.)   │
  │  skip:   Normal for resources that don't match policy selector   │
  │  pass:   Expected on good resources, this is the goal            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

error is the most dangerous: An error result means the policy itself is broken — it couldn't even evaluate the resource. In a cluster with failurePolicy: Fail, this would BLOCK the resource even though there's no actual violation. Always investigate and fix errors before deploying policies.

For anihpj/jobpost, our CI/CD pipeline parses the apply output and fails the build if error > 0 or if any test resource that should pass shows as fail.

Kyverno CLI Q81 of 200
How do you use kyverno apply against a LIVE Kubernetes cluster instead of local files?
A) kyverno apply only works with local files — it cannot connect to live clusters
B) Use --cluster flag: kyverno apply ./policies/ --cluster --namespace anihpj. This reads resources from the live cluster (using current kubectl context) and tests them against the policies. It's READ-ONLY — no resources are modified. Useful for auditing existing resources against new or updated policies
C) Live cluster testing requires the Kyverno dashboard — the CLI cannot access cluster resources
D) --cluster mode requires cluster-admin permissions and will modify non-compliant resources
✅ Correct Answer: B

The --cluster flag bridges local policy testing and live cluster auditing. It's the same engine, but instead of reading from local YAML files, it reads from the Kubernetes API.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kyverno apply --cluster — Live Cluster Auditing
  BASIC CLUSTER MODE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno apply ./policies/ --cluster                           │
  │  Applying 13 policy rule(s) to resources in cluster...           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Scoped to a namespace:                                        │
  │  $ kyverno apply ./policies/ --cluster --namespace anihpj       │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Scoped to specific resource types:                            │
  │  $ kyverno apply ./policies/ --cluster \                         │
  │      --resources "pods,deployments"                              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WHAT IT DOES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Reads policies from local directory                            │
  │  ② Connects to cluster via current kubectl context               │
  │  ③ Lists matching resources via K8s API                          │
  │  ④ Evaluates each resource against each policy                   │
  │  ⑤ Reports pass/fail/warn/error/skip                             │
  │  ⑥ NEVER modifies resources (read-only)                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  USE CASES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  • Audit: "Do existing pods comply with our new policy?"         │
  │  • Pre-upgrade: "Will anything break after upgrading Kyverno?"   │
  │  • Incident response: "Which pods are running as root right now?"│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

⚠️ Verify context first: --cluster uses your current kubectl context. Always run kubectl config current-context before using this flag to ensure you're targeting the right cluster.

For anihpj/jobpost, we run kyverno apply --cluster --namespace anihpj weekly as a compliance audit. It generates a report showing which pods violate our policies, helping us prioritize fixes.

Kyverno CLI Q82 of 200
How do you test Kyverno policies that use variables and context (API calls) with kyverno apply?
A) Variables and context cannot be tested locally — they only work when applied to a live cluster
B) Variables are automatically resolved from local environment variables — no special configuration needed
C) Use --variables flag to pass variable values, and --values-file to provide a YAML file with context data (simulating ConfigMaps or API responses). For API calls, the CLI can resolve them if the target is reachable, or use --skip-api-calls to skip them. For complex context, create a values file that mocks the API response
D) Variables in policies are automatically replaced with defaults — no testing configuration is needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

Testing dynamic policies with variables requires providing mock data — the same data the policy would receive from the cluster. The CLI provides flags to simulate this without requiring a real cluster.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Testing Dynamic Policies Locally
  POLICY WITH VARIABLES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  context:                                                         │

For anihpj/jobpost, we maintain values files for each namespace that simulate our ConfigMaps. CI/CD uses these to test policies that reference context data without connecting to a live cluster.

Kyverno CLI Q83 of 200
How do you get machine-readable output from kyverno apply using -o json or -o yaml for CI/CD integration?
A) kyverno apply policy.yaml -r pod.yaml -o json outputs structured JSON with policy name, rule name, result (pass/fail), message, and resource info. This is ideal for CI/CD pipelines that parse the output and fail the build based on results. The JSON structure matches the PolicyReport format, making it easy to integrate with compliance dashboards
B) Kyverno apply only outputs human-readable text — JSON and YAML output are not supported
C) -o json converts the policy YAML to JSON — it doesn't output evaluation results
D) Machine-readable output requires a separate kyverno-report binary — it's not built into kyverno apply
✅ Correct Answer: A

-o json or -o yaml makes kyverno apply CI/CD-friendly — structured output that tools like jq can parse to determine pass/fail status.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CI/CD Integration with kyverno apply
  JSON OUTPUT:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno apply policy.yaml -r pod.yaml -o json                 │
  │  {                                                                │
  │    "results": [{                                                  │
  │      "policy": "require-non-root",                               │
  │      "rule": "validate-runAsNonRoot",                            │
  │      "result": "fail",                                           │
  │      "message": "Running as root is not allowed",                │
  │      "resource": "default/Pod/nginx"                             │
  │    }],                                                            │
  │    "summary": {"pass": 0, "fail": 1, "warn": 0, "error": 0}     │
  │  }                                                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CI/CD PIPELINE (GitHub Actions example):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  - name: Test Kyverno policies                                   │
  │    run: |                                                         │
  │      RESULT=$(kyverno apply ./policies/ \                        │
  │        -r ./test-resources/ -o json)                              │
  │      echo "$RESULT" | jq '.summary'                              │
  │      FAIL_COUNT=$(echo "$RESULT" | jq '.summary.fail')           │
  │      if [ "$FAIL_COUNT" -gt 0 ]; then                            │
  │        echo "❌ Policy violations found!"                         │
  │        exit 1                                                     │
  │      fi                                                           │
  │      echo "✅ All policies passed!"                              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, our CI/CD pipeline runs kyverno apply -o json and uses jq to verify that exactly zero resources fail the "require-non-root" policy. Any unexpected failures block the PR from merging.

Kyverno CLI Q84 of 200
When should you use kyverno apply vs kyverno test?
A) They are the same command with different names — use whichever you prefer
B) apply is for validation; test is for mutation — they serve completely different purposes
C) apply is for local files; test is for live clusters — they target different environments
D) kyverno apply = quick ad-hoc testing ("does this policy work against this resource?"). kyverno test = structured, repeatable test suites with EXPECTED results ("this resource should PASS this policy, and this other resource should FAIL"). Use apply during policy development; use test in CI/CD for regression testing
✅ Correct Answer: D

apply and test are complementary: apply for exploration during development, test for formal verification in CI/CD.

📝 Detailed Explanation
apply vs test — When to Use Each
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FEATURE              kyverno apply       kyverno test           │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  Purpose              Ad-hoc testing      Structured test suite  │
  │  Expected results     Not specified        Explicitly declared   │
  │  Test file needed     No                   kyverno-test.yaml     │
  │  CI/CD friendly       Yes (with -o json)  ✅ Built for CI/CD    │
  │  Fail on unexpected   Manual check         Automatic             │
  │  Regression testing   Not ideal            ✅ Designed for it    │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  USE apply WHEN:                                                 │
  │  • Writing a new policy → "Does this work?"                     │
  │  • Quick validation → "Will this pod be blocked?"               │
  │  • Exploring → "What happens if I change this?"                 │
  │                                                                   │
  │  USE test WHEN:                                                  │
  │  • CI/CD pipeline → "Did anything break?"                       │
  │  • Regression testing → "Does the policy STILL work?"           │
  │  • Formal verification → "These 5 resources MUST pass"          │
  │  • Team collaboration → "Here are the expected behaviors"       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, developers use kyverno apply during policy development. Our CI/CD pipeline uses kyverno test with a comprehensive test suite that verifies all 13 policies against 20+ test resources.

3.3 kyverno test — The Kyverno Test FrameworkQ85-Q92

Kyverno CLI Q85 of 200
What is kyverno test and how does structured policy testing work?
A) kyverno test is an alias for kyverno apply — they are functionally identical
B) kyverno test runs structured tests defined in a kyverno-test.yaml file. Each test case specifies: which policies to test, which resources to test against, and the EXPECTED result for each policy-rule-resource combination. It verifies that policies produce the expected behavior and catches regressions when policies are modified
C) kyverno test deploys policies to a temporary test cluster and runs integration tests — it requires a live Kubernetes environment
D) kyverno test is used to benchmark policy evaluation performance — it measures latency rather than correctness
✅ Correct Answer: B

kyverno test is unit testing for policies — you declare expected outcomes and the CLI verifies them. If someone accidentally makes a policy too strict or too lenient, the tests catch it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kyverno test — Structured Policy Testing
  TEST DIRECTORY STRUCTURE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  my-policy/                                                       │
  │  ├── policy.yaml              ← The policy being tested          │
  │  ├── resources/                                                   │
  │  │   ├── good-pod.yaml        ← Should PASS                      │
  │  │   └── bad-pod.yaml         ← Should FAIL                      │
  │  └── kyverno-test.yaml        ← Test definition                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  TEST DEFINITION (kyverno-test.yaml):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: cli.kyverno.io/v1alpha1                              │
  │  kind: Test                                                       │
  │  metadata:                                                        │
  │    name: test-require-non-root                                    │
  │  policies:                                                        │
  │  - policy.yaml                                                    │
  │  resources:                                                       │
  │  - resources/good-pod.yaml                                        │
  │  - resources/bad-pod.yaml                                         │
  │  results:                                                         │
  │  - policy: require-non-root                                       │
  │    rule: validate-runAsNonRoot                                    │
  │    resources: [good-pod]                                          │
  │    result: pass                ← EXPECT pass                     │
  │  - policy: require-non-root                                       │
  │    rule: validate-runAsNonRoot                                    │
  │    resources: [bad-pod]                                           │
  │    result: fail                ← EXPECT fail                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, each of our 13 policies has a corresponding test directory with good/bad resources and expected results. This ensures policy modifications don't accidentally break existing compliance requirements.

Kyverno CLI Q86 of 200
How do you write a kyverno-test.yaml file with test cases that specify expected pass/fail/skip results?
A) kyverno-test.yaml is auto-generated — you cannot write it manually
B) The test file only needs to list policy and resource files — expected results are automatically inferred
C) Create a kyverno-test.yaml with: apiVersion: cli.kyverno.io/v1alpha1, kind: Test, policies (list of policy files), resources (list of resource files), and results (array of expected outcomes: policy name, rule name, resource names, and expected result — pass/fail/skip). Run with kyverno test ./my-policy/
D) Expected results are defined in the policy YAML itself — not in a separate test file
✅ Correct Answer: C

The kyverno-test.yaml is the test plan — it declares what you're testing and what should happen. The CLI executes the plan and reports any deviations from expected results.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kyverno-test.yaml — Complete Example
  apiVersion: cli.kyverno.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Test
  metadata:
    name: anihpj-policy-tests
  policies:
  - ../policies/require-non-root.yaml
  - ../policies/require-resource-limits.yaml
  resources:
  - good-pod.yaml          ← Compliant pod
  - root-pod.yaml          ← Runs as root (should fail)
  - no-limits-pod.yaml     ← No resource limits (should fail)
  results:
  # Good pod should PASS both policies
  - policy: require-non-root
    rule: validate-runAsNonRoot
    resources: [good-pod]
    result: pass
  - policy: require-resource-limits
    rule: validate-resources
    resources: [good-pod]
    result: pass

  # Root pod should FAIL non-root policy
  - policy: require-non-root
    rule: validate-runAsNonRoot
    resources: [root-pod]
    result: fail

  # No-limits pod should FAIL resource limits policy
  - policy: require-resource-limits
    rule: validate-resources
    resources: [no-limits-pod]
    result: fail

For anihpj/jobpost, our test files are committed alongside policies in Git. CI/CD runs kyverno test on every PR — any test that doesn't produce the expected result fails the build.

Kyverno CLI Q87 of 200
What is the structure of a test case in kyverno-test.yaml — what are the name, policies, resources, and results fields?
A) name: unique test identifier. policies: list of policy YAML files to test (relative paths). resources: list of resource YAML files to test against. results: array of expected outcomes, each specifying policy (name), rule (name), resources (which resources this applies to), and result (pass/fail/skip/warn). Optional variables field for passing test variables
B) The test structure uses JSON Schema, not YAML — all fields must be in JSON format
C) policies and resources fields accept only single files — lists are not supported
D) The results field is optional — if omitted, kyverno test runs without verifying expected outcomes
✅ Correct Answer: A

Each test case is a self-contained specification — which policies, which resources, and what should happen. Multiple test cases can be defined by using YAML document separators (---) in the test file.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Test Case Structure — Field Reference
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FIELD        TYPE      REQUIRED  DESCRIPTION                    │
  │  ─────        ────      ────────  ───────────                    │
  │  apiVersion   string    ✅        cli.kyverno.io/v1alpha1        │
  │  kind         string    ✅        Test                            │
  │  metadata     object    ✅        name (unique test name)         │
  │  policies     []string  ✅        Policy file paths (relative)    │
  │  resources    []string  ✅        Resource file paths             │
  │  results      []object  ✅        Expected outcomes               │
  │    └─ policy  string    ✅        Policy name to check            │
  │    └─ rule    string    ✅        Rule name to check              │
  │    └─ resources []string ✅      Which resources this applies to │
  │    └─ result  string    ✅        pass / fail / skip / warn       │
  │    └─ status  string    ❌        Additional status message      │
  │  variables    string    ❌        Path to variables file          │
  │  values       object    ❌        Inline variable values          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we organize tests by policy: each policy has its own test directory with a kyverno-test.yaml and a resources/ subdirectory containing test fixtures.

Kyverno CLI Q88 of 200
What do the expected result status values mean in kyverno test — pass, fail, skip, and how are they verified?
A) All result values are treated as strings without special meaning — any value is accepted
B) pass means the policy was applied; fail means it wasn't — they don't relate to resource compliance
C) Only pass and fail are valid — skip is not a recognized test result
D) pass = test expects the resource to COMPLY with the policy rule (no violation). fail = test expects the resource to VIOLATE the policy rule (in Enforce, it would be blocked). skip = test expects the rule to NOT APPLY to this resource (the match selector doesn't match). If actual result ≠ expected result, the test FAILS with a clear error message
✅ Correct Answer: D

Each expected result is a contract — "I assert that this resource will have this outcome." The CLI verifies the contract and reports any violations as test failures.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Expected Results — Verification Flow
  TEST CASE: good-pod should PASS require-non-root
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Kyverno evaluates: good-pod vs require-non-root                 │
  │  Actual result: pass                                              │
  │  Expected result: pass                                            │
  │  ✅ TEST PASSES (actual == expected)                             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  TEST CASE: root-pod should FAIL require-non-root
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Kyverno evaluates: root-pod vs require-non-root                 │
  │  Actual result: fail                                              │
  │  Expected result: fail                                            │
  │  ✅ TEST PASSES (actual == expected)                             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  REGRESSION: Someone weakens the policy — now root-pod passes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Kyverno evaluates: root-pod vs require-non-root (weakened)      │
  │  Actual result: pass             ← Changed!                      │
  │  Expected result: fail            ← Still expects fail           │
  │  ❌ TEST FAILS: Expected fail, got pass                          │
  │  │                                                               │
  │  └─ "The policy was weakened — it no longer catches root pods!"  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, this regression detection is invaluable. When a developer accidentally removes an anchor from a validate pattern, the tests immediately catch that previously-blocked resources are now passing.

Kyverno CLI Q89 of 200
How do you create advanced kyverno test cases with multiple resources, multiple policies, and variables?
A) Advanced test cases require the Kyverno enterprise edition — the open-source test framework only supports single-policy tests
B) List multiple policies and resources in the test file. Use --- (YAML document separator) for multiple test cases. Pass variables via the variables field or a --values-file. Each result block can reference specific resources by name. This allows testing complex scenarios like "this mutate rule should fix this resource, then the validate rule should pass"
C) Multiple-policy testing requires running kyverno test once per policy — batch testing is not supported
D) Variables in test cases must be hardcoded in the kyverno-test.yaml — external values files are not supported
✅ Correct Answer: B

The test framework supports complex multi-policy scenarios — you can test mutate-then-validate pipelines, generate rule triggers, and multi-resource interactions, all in a single test file.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Advanced Test — Mutate + Validate Pipeline
  # Test: Mutate adds defaults, then validate checks them
  policies:
  - ../policies/add-default-resources.yaml    ← Mutate rule
  - ../policies/require-resource-limits.yaml  ← Validate rule
  resources:
  - no-limits-pod.yaml     ← No resource limits initially
  results:
  # After mutation, the pod should HAVE limits
  - policy: add-default-resources
    rule: mutate-add-resources
    resources: [no-limits-pod]
    result: pass           ← Mutation succeeded
    patchedResource: patched-pod.yaml   ← Verify the mutated output
  # Then validation should pass
  - policy: require-resource-limits
    rule: validate-resources
    resources: [no-limits-pod]
    result: pass           ← Now it has limits (thanks to mutate!)

  # Use variables for dynamic values
  variables: variables.yaml
  ---
  # Second test case
  metadata:
    name: test-with-different-values
  # ... different test with different variables

For anihpj/jobpost, our most advanced test verifies the full pipeline: mutate adds default resource limits → validate checks they exist → generate creates a NetworkPolicy if a namespace has the right label. All three rule types tested in one file.

Kyverno CLI Q90 of 200
How do you test Kyverno mutate policies and verify the mutated output with kyverno test?
A) Mutate policies cannot be tested — they only work in a live cluster with the admission webhook
B) Mutate policy testing only verifies that the mutation rule itself passes — it doesn't show what the resource looks like after mutation
C) Use the patchedResource field in test results to specify a file containing the EXPECTED resource after mutation. The CLI applies the mutate rule, generates the patched resource, and compares it to your expected output. If they match, the test passes. Use result: pass with patchedResource: expected-output.yaml
D) Mutated output can only be verified by applying the policy to a live cluster and using kubectl get -o yaml to see the result
✅ Correct Answer: C

patchedResource is the key to testing mutate policies — it lets you verify not just THAT the mutation happened, but WHAT the result looks like.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Testing Mutate Policies with patchedResource
  TEST FILE (kyverno-test.yaml):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  policies:                                                        │
  │  - add-default-resources.yaml                                     │
  │  resources:                                                       │
  │  - pod-without-limits.yaml         ← Original (no resources)     │
  │  results:                                                         │
  │  - policy: add-default-resources                                  │
  │    rule: mutate-add-resources                                      │
  │    resources: [pod-without-limits]                                 │
  │    result: pass                                                   │
  │    patchedResource: pod-with-limits.yaml  ← Expected output      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ORIGINAL (pod-without-limits.yaml):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  containers:                                                      │
  │  - name: app                                                      │
  │    image: nginx:latest                                            │
  │    # NO resources section                                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  EXPECTED OUTPUT (pod-with-limits.yaml):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  containers:                                                      │
  │  - name: app                                                      │
  │    image: nginx:latest                                            │
  │    resources:                       ← ADDED by mutate rule!      │
  │      requests:                                                    │
  │        cpu: 100m                                                  │
  │        memory: 128Mi                                              │
  │      limits:                                                      │
  │        cpu: 500m                                                  │
  │        memory: 256Mi                                              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use patchedResource to verify that our mutate rules add exactly the right defaults — the right CPU/memory values, the right labels, and the right image registry prefix.

Kyverno CLI Q91 of 200
How do you run specific test cases by name instead of the entire test suite?
A) Use kyverno test ./tests/ --test-name-selector "require-non-root" to run only test cases whose name contains the selector string. This is useful during development when you're iterating on a single policy and don't want to run all tests
B) Individual test case execution requires deleting other test cases from kyverno-test.yaml — there is no selector flag
C) Test cases can only be filtered by file name, not by test case name
D) Test case filtering requires a separate kyverno-test-filter binary — it's not built into kyverno test
✅ Correct Answer: A

--test-name-selector is the development productivity flag — when you're working on one policy and making rapid changes, running all 50+ tests on every iteration is painfully slow.

Test Name Selector — Run Specific Tests
  RUN ALL TESTS (13 policies × multiple test cases):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
