  │  ✅ All tests passed                                              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, developers use --test-name-selector during local development to iterate quickly on a single policy. CI/CD always runs the full test suite without filters.

Kyverno CLI Q92 of 200
How do you interpret kyverno test output — what does a pass/fail summary look like?
A) kyverno test only outputs "OK" or "FAIL" — there is no per-test-case detail
B) Test output is only available in JSON format — human-readable output requires additional flags
C) Test results are logged to the Kyverno dashboard — the CLI doesn't display results
D) Each test case shows: name, policy, rule, resource, expected result, actual result, and PASS/FAIL status. At the end, a summary shows total tests executed and passed. A failing test clearly shows the mismatch: "Expected pass, got fail" with the violation message. Exit code 0 = all passed, exit code 1 = at least one test failed (CI/CD friendly)
✅ Correct Answer: D

The test output is designed for both humans and machines — clear per-case details for developers, and a clean exit code for CI/CD pipelines.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kyverno test Output — All Passing vs With Failures
  ALL TESTS PASSING:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno test ./tests/                                          │
  │  Executing test-require-non-root...                               │
  │    │ policy require-non-root -> resource default/Pod/good-pod     │
  │    │ Expected: pass, Got: pass ✅                                  │
  │    │ policy require-non-root -> resource default/Pod/root-pod     │
  │    │ Expected: fail, Got: fail ✅                                  │
  │  PASS: test-require-non-root                                      │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────                            │
  │  Test Summary: 8 tests passed, 0 tests failed                     │
  │  ✅ All tests passed                                              │
  │  $ echo $?                                                        │
  │  0                                                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  TEST FAILURE (regression caught):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno test ./tests/                                          │
  │  Executing test-require-non-root...                               │
  │    │ policy require-non-root -> resource default/Pod/root-pod     │
  │    │ Expected: fail, Got: pass ❌                                  │
  │    │ Policy was expected to reject this resource, but it passed   │
  │  FAIL: test-require-non-root                                      │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────                            │
  │  Test Summary: 7 tests passed, 1 tests failed                     │
  │  ❌ Some tests failed                                             │
  │  $ echo $?                                                        │
  │  1                                                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, CI/CD checks the exit code — if kyverno test returns non-zero, the pipeline fails and the PR cannot be merged.

3.4 kyverno jp — JMESPath CLI ToolQ93-Q96

Kyverno CLI Q93 of 200
What is kyverno jp and how do you use it to evaluate JMESPath expressions?
A) kyverno jp is a JSON pretty-printer — it formats JSON but doesn't evaluate expressions
B) kyverno jp -q "expression" file.yaml evaluates a JMESPath expression against a YAML/JSON file and returns the result. It's a testing sandbox for JMESPath — test expressions here before embedding them in Kyverno policies. Example: kyverno jp -q "metadata.labels.app" pod.yaml returns the value of the app label
C) kyverno jp requires a live Kubernetes cluster to function — it queries resources via the API server
D) JMESPath expressions can only be tested within a full Kyverno policy — kyverno jp doesn't support standalone evaluation
✅ Correct Answer: B

kyverno jp is the JMESPath playground — test and debug expressions in isolation before embedding them in complex policies where debugging is much harder.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kyverno jp — JMESPath Expression Testing
  BASIC USAGE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno jp -q "metadata.labels" pod.yaml                      │
  │  {                                                                │
  │    "app": "anihpj-api",                                          │
  │    "tier": "backend",                                            │
  │    "team": "platform"                                             │
  │  }                                                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  PIPE FROM STDIN:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get pod nginx -o json | kyverno jp -q "status.phase" │
  │  "Running"                                                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  COMPLEX EXPRESSIONS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Check if any container image contains "nginx"                  │
  │  $ kyverno jp -q "contains(images.containers.*.name, 'nginx')"   │
  │      pod.yaml                                                     │
  │  true                                                             │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Filter containers running as root                              │
  │  $ kyverno jp -q "spec.containers[?securityContext.runAsNonRoot   │
  │      == \`false\` || securityContext == null]" pod.yaml           │
  │  [{...}]                                                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, developers use kyverno jp before writing JMESPath-based conditions in policies. It's much faster to iterate on expressions in the CLI than to apply a policy, check PolicyReports, and debug from there.

Kyverno CLI Q94 of 200
Why should you test JMESPath expressions with kyverno jp BEFORE using them in Kyverno policies?
A) JMESPath expressions in policies are automatically validated — pre-testing is unnecessary
B) kyverno jp is only needed if you're writing the Kyverno policy in Rego instead of YAML
C) JMESPath errors in policies cause error results (not fail) — the policy evaluation breaks, and with failurePolicy: Fail, the resource is BLOCKED even though there's no actual violation. Pre-testing with kyverno jp catches syntax errors, type mismatches, and logic errors before they impact production. It's the JMESPath equivalent of a linter
D) kyverno jp modifies the policy YAML to include the correct JMESPath expression — it's an authoring tool, not a testing tool
✅ Correct Answer: C

A broken JMESPath expression is worse than no policy at all — it causes error results that can block legitimate resources. Pre-testing prevents this.

📝 Detailed Explanation
JMESPath Errors — The Hidden Danger
  WITHOUT PRETESTING (broken JMESPath → resource blocked):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Policy has this JMESPath:                                        │
  │  condition: "spec.containers[?securityContext.runAsNonRoot]"      │
  │  (Missing: == \`true\` — broken expression!)                      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  Policy applied to cluster. Developer deploys a pod.              │
  │  Kyverno evaluates condition → JMESPath error!                   │
  │  Result: error (not pass, not fail, ERROR)                        │
  │  failurePolicy: Fail → ❌ POD BLOCKED!                           │
  │  Message: "JMESPath evaluation error: invalid expression"        │
  │                                                                   │
  │  The pod was perfectly compliant but was blocked anyway!         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WITH PRETESTING (catch errors before deployment):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno jp -q "spec.containers[?securityContext.runAsNonRoot]"│
  │      pod.yaml                                                     │
  │  Error: JMESPath syntax error at position 45                     │
  │  → Fix expression BEFORE writing the policy                      │
  │  → Test again with corrected expression                           │
  │  → Only then embed in the policy                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, our policy development workflow requires: write the JMESPath expression → test with kyverno jp against sample resources → embed in policy → test with kyverno apply → test with kyverno test → deploy.

Kyverno CLI Q95 of 200
How would you use kyverno jp to check if a pod contains a specific image like nginx?
A) kyverno jp -q "contains(images.containers.*.name, 'nginx')" pod.yaml — returns true if any container uses an image named "nginx". The images variable in Kyverno contains all container/initContainer image references, and contains() is a built-in JMESPath function
B) kyverno jp cannot check image names — it only handles metadata fields like labels and annotations
C) Image checking requires kubectl describe — the CLI cannot access image information
D) Image names must be checked with kubectl get pod -o jsonpath, not with kyverno jp
✅ Correct Answer: A

JMESPath's contains() function combined with Kyverno's images variable makes image checking straightforward. Test it first with kyverno jp, then use the same expression in your policy.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kyverno jp — Image Checking Examples
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Check for specific image name                                 │
  │  $ kyverno jp -q "contains(images.containers.*.name, 'nginx')"   │
  │      pod.yaml                                                     │
  │  true                                                             │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # Check image comes from specific registry                      │
  │  $ kyverno jp -q \                                                │
  │      "images.containers.*.name[?starts_with(@, 'registry.anihpj')]"│
  │      pod.yaml                                                     │
  │  ["registry.anihpj.com/anihpj-api:v2"]                           │
  │                                                                   │
  │  # List ALL images in the pod (containers + initContainers)      │
  │  $ kyverno jp -q "images.containers.*.name" pod.yaml             │
  │  ["anihpj-api:v2", "istio-proxy:1.19"]                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use kyverno jp to test image-checking expressions that later become precondition blocks in our policies — "only enforce this rule if the image is from our registry."

Kyverno CLI Q96 of 200
How do you debug complex JMESPath expressions iteratively using kyverno jp?
A) Complex JMESPath debugging requires a visual debugger — the CLI only supports simple expressions
B) JMESPath expressions cannot be debugged — they either work or they don't, and there's no way to see intermediate results
C) Debugging JMESPath requires kubectl exec into the Kyverno pod — the CLI doesn't support it
D) Build complex expressions STEP BY STEP: start with a simple query (-q "spec"), see the output, add the next level (-q "spec.containers"), then add filters (-q "spec.containers[?image]"), then add functions (-q "contains(...)"). Each step validates that the intermediate result is correct before adding complexity. Use -q for the query, pipe input from kubectl get -o json for real cluster data
✅ Correct Answer: D

Debugging JMESPath is best done incrementally — build the expression one layer at a time, verifying each layer's output before adding the next. This is the same technique developers use for complex SQL queries or jq pipelines.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Iterative JMESPath Debugging Example
  GOAL: Check if any container runs as root
  ──────────────────────────────────────────

  Step 1: See what spec.containers looks like
  $ kyverno jp -q "spec.containers" pod.yaml
  [{"name":"nginx","image":"nginx:latest","securityContext":{"runAsUser":0}}]
  ✅ JSON structure confirmed

  Step 2: Extract security contexts
  $ kyverno jp -q "spec.containers[*].securityContext" pod.yaml
  [{"runAsUser":0}]
  ✅ Security context found

  Step 3: Filter containers where runAsNonRoot is not explicitly true
  $ kyverno jp -q "spec.containers[?securityContext.runAsNonRoot != \`true\`]"
  ...error or unexpected result
  ⚠️ Null handling needed — some containers have no securityContext!

  Step 4: Handle nulls
  $ kyverno jp -q "spec.containers[?securityContext.runAsNonRoot != \`true\` || !securityContext]"
  [{"name":"nginx",...}]
  ✅ Now catches containers without any securityContext AND those with runAsNonRoot: false

For anihpj/jobpost, our most complex policy condition (checking image registries, tags, AND user permissions) took 8 iterations of kyverno jp testing before it was correct. Without the CLI, debugging that in-cluster would have taken hours.

Deploying policies to your cluster and controlling WHICH resources they apply to. 20 Questions (Q97-Q116).

20Questions
3Sub-Categories
🟢🟡Novice/Intermediate

📋 Category 4: Applying Policies (10%)Q97-Q116

Deploying Policies and Controlling Resource Selection

This category is about getting policies live — applying them to the cluster, verifying they're active, controlling which resources they target, and configuring key settings. Think of it like deploying a security camera system: you need to install the cameras (apply policies), verify they're recording (check status), and aim them at the right areas (resource selection).

4.1 Applying Policy in ClusterQ97-Q102

Applying Policies Q97 of 200
How do you apply a Kyverno policy to your cluster?
A) Kyverno policies are applied through the Kyverno dashboard — kubectl cannot deploy policies
B) kubectl apply -f policy.yaml — exactly like any other Kubernetes resource. For namespaced Policies, use kubectl apply -f policy.yaml -n anihpj. For ClusterPolicies, just kubectl apply -f cluster-policy.yaml (no namespace). Kyverno policies are standard CRs — kubectl works the same as for Pods or Deployments
C) Policies must be deployed via Helm — kubectl apply is not supported for Kyverno CRDs
D) Policies are applied using the kyverno apply CLI command, not kubectl
✅ Correct Answer: B

Kyverno policies are just Kubernetes resources — you deploy them with the same kubectl apply you use for everything else. No special tools, no separate CLI, no dashboard required.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Applying Kyverno Policies — It's Just kubectl
  APPLY A CLUSTERPOLICY:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl apply -f require-non-root.yaml                        │
  │  clusterpolicy.kyverno.io/require-non-root-pods created          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  APPLY A NAMESPACED POLICY:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl apply -f require-labels.yaml -n anihpj                │
  │  policy.kyverno.io/require-anihpj-labels created                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  THIS MEANS POLICIES WORK WITH EVERY K8s TOOL:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ kubectl apply / delete / get / describe / edit               │
  │  ✅ GitOps (ArgoCD, Flux) — sync like any other resource         │
  │  ✅ CI/CD — kubectl apply in pipelines                           │
  │  ✅ Helm — package policies in Helm charts                       │
  │  ✅ Kustomize — overlay environment-specific settings            │
  │  ✅ kubectl diff — preview changes before applying               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, policies are applied via ArgoCD which syncs our policy Git repository. ArgoCD uses kubectl apply under the hood — there's no special Kyverno-specific deployment mechanism.

Applying Policies Q98 of 200
What is the difference between applying a Policy (namespaced) vs a ClusterPolicy (cluster-wide)?
A) Both are applied identically — the namespace flag is ignored for both types
B) Policy requires the --namespace flag; ClusterPolicy rejects it — using the wrong flag causes kubectl to error
C) kubectl apply -f policy.yaml -n anihpj for Policy (MUST specify namespace). kubectl apply -f cluster-policy.yaml for ClusterPolicy (NO -n flag — it's cluster-scoped). kubectl get policy -n anihpj vs kubectl get clusterpolicy. The -n flag is REQUIRED for Policy and INVALID for ClusterPolicy
D) Policy and ClusterPolicy are applied the same way — the kind field determines scope, not the kubectl command
✅ Correct Answer: C

The kubectl commands differ based on scope: namespaced resources need -n, cluster-scoped resources reject it. This is standard Kubernetes behavior — not Kyverno-specific.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Policy vs ClusterPolicy — kubectl Commands
  POLICY (namespaced — requires -n):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl apply -f policy.yaml -n anihpj                        │
  │  $ kubectl get policy -n anihpj                                  │
  │  $ kubectl describe policy require-labels -n anihpj             │
  │  $ kubectl delete policy require-labels -n anihpj               │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ❌ kubectl get policy (without -n) → only shows current NS     │
  │  ✅ kubectl get policy -A → shows all namespaces                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CLUSTERPOLICY (cluster-scoped — NO -n):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl apply -f cluster-policy.yaml                          │
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicy                                     │
  │  $ kubectl describe clusterpolicy require-non-root               │
  │  $ kubectl delete clusterpolicy require-non-root                 │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ❌ kubectl get clusterpolicy -n anihpj → ERROR (not namespaced) │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use ClusterPolicies for global security rules and Policies for namespace-specific configurations. Our deployment scripts check the policy kind and apply the correct kubectl command automatically.

Applying Policies Q99 of 200
What happens behind the scenes when you apply a Kyverno policy to the cluster?
A) (1) kubectl sends the YAML to the API server. (2) API server validates it against the Policy/ClusterPolicy CRD schema. (3) Resource is stored in etcd. (4) Kyverno's controller WATCHES for new policies and picks it up. (5) If background: true, Kyverno immediately starts scanning existing resources. (6) The policy's webhook rules are evaluated for matching new admission requests. (7) PolicyReports start being generated
B) The policy is compiled into WebAssembly and injected into the kubelet on each node
C) Applying a policy only registers it in etcd — no processing happens until Kyverno is restarted
D) The policy is sent to a cloud service for validation before being accepted — local-only policies are not supported
✅ Correct Answer: A

Applying a policy triggers a cascade of events — it's not just stored; Kyverno immediately starts using it. Existing resources are scanned, new admissions are checked, and reports begin generating.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Policy Application — What Happens Behind the Scenes
  TIMELINE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  t=0s    kubectl apply -f policy.yaml                             │
  │  t=0.1s  API server validates CRD schema → ✅ valid               │
  │  t=0.2s  Resource stored in etcd                                  │
  │  t=0.3s  Kyverno controller detects new policy (WATCH event)     │
  │  t=0.5s  Policy status updated: READY=True                       │
  │                                                                   │
  │  IF background: true (default):                                   │
  │  t=1s    Background scan initiates                                │
  │  t=2s    Kyverno queries all matching existing resources          │
  │  t=5s    PolicyReport generated with results                      │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ADMISSION (continuous):                                          │
  │  t=0.5s+ All new kubectl apply requests matching this policy     │
  │         are evaluated through the webhook                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we monitor PolicyReports for 15-30 minutes after deploying a new policy in Audit mode. If the report shows the expected violations (and no unexpected ones), we switch to Enforce mode.

Applying Policies Q100 of 200
How do you verify that a Kyverno policy is active and working after applying it?
A) If kubectl apply returns "created", the policy is working — no further verification is needed
B) Verification requires deploying a test pod and checking if it gets blocked — there's no status field to check
C) The Kyverno dashboard email notification confirms policy activation — CLI verification is not available
D) Check kubectl get clusterpolicy or kubectl get policy -n <ns> — the READY column should show True. Then check PolicyReports: kubectl get policyreport -A — there should be entries for this policy showing pass/fail results. For a quick functional test: kyverno apply policy.yaml --cluster --namespace anihpj to see live evaluation results
✅ Correct Answer: D

Verification is a three-step process: check the policy status (READY), check that reports are being generated, and optionally run a live test with kyverno apply --cluster.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Policy Verification Checklist
  STEP 1: Check READY status
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicy                                      │
  │  NAME                    ADMISSION   BACKGROUND   READY   AGE    │
  │  require-non-root-pods   Enforce     true         True    30s   │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ✅ READY: True = Policy is active and processing               │
  │  ❌ READY: False = Policy has an issue — check events           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  STEP 2: Check PolicyReports (after a few minutes)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get policyreport -A | grep require-non-root           │
  │  anihpj   cpol-require-non-root-pods   12   3   0   30m          │
  │                                                                   │
  │  ✅ Report exists with pass/fail counts → Policy is working      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  STEP 3: Quick functional test
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kyverno apply policy.yaml --cluster --namespace anihpj        │
  │  pass: 12, fail: 3, warn: 0, error: 0, skip: 0                  │
  │  ✅ Live evaluation confirms policy is working                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, every policy deployment is followed by this 3-step verification in our CI/CD pipeline. Only when all three checks pass does the pipeline mark the deployment as successful.

Applying Policies Q101 of 200
What do the policy status values Ready: True and Ready: False mean?
A) Ready: True means the policy has been deployed for more than 24 hours without errors
B) Ready: True = Kyverno has successfully processed the policy — webhooks are updated, background scan is scheduled, and the policy is actively enforcing rules. Ready: False = something went wrong — check kubectl describe clusterpolicy <name>
C) Ready status only reflects whether the policy YAML is valid — it doesn't indicate if the policy is in effect
D) Ready: True means the policy has passed all background scans — it's a compliance indicator
✅ Correct Answer: B

The READY column is your first health check — always verify it after applying a policy. Think of it like a service health indicator: green means running, red means investigate. A NotReady policy EXISTS in etcd but Kyverno cannot use it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Policy Status — Ready vs NotReady Deep Dive
  HEALTHY POLICY (Ready: True):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicy                              │
  │  NAME                    ADMISSION   BACKGROUND   READY   │
  │  require-non-root-pods   Enforce     true         True    │
  │                                                           │
  │  $ kubectl describe clusterpolicy require-non-root-pods   │
  │  Status:                                                  │
  │    Conditions:                                            │
  │      Last Transition Time: 2024-01-15T10:30:00Z          │
  │      Message: Ready                                       │
  │      Reason:  Succeeded                                   │
  │      Status:  True                                        │
  │      Type:    Ready                                       │
  │  → Webhooks registered ✅  Background scan scheduled ✅   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  UNHEALTHY POLICY (Ready: False):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicy                              │
  │  NAME                    ADMISSION   BACKGROUND   READY   │
  │  bad-policy              Audit       true         False   │
  │                                                           │
  │  $ kubectl describe clusterpolicy bad-policy | tail -8   │
  │  Status:                                                  │
  │    Conditions:                                            │
  │      Message:  failed to register webhook                 │
  │      Reason:   InternalError                              │
  │      Status:   False                                      │
  │      Type:     Ready                                      │
  │  → CAUSES: Invalid YAML, missing CRDs, RBAC denied,      │
  │    webhook conflict, resource quota exceeded              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, our CI/CD pipeline waits for READY=True before marking a policy deployment successful. If False persists for >60s, the pipeline fails and rolls back. This prevents broken policies from silently entering the cluster.

Applying Policies Q102 of 200
What happens when you update an existing Kyverno policy with kubectl apply -f updated-policy.yaml?
A) Policy updates require deleting the old policy first — kubectl apply won't update existing Kyverno policies
B) The policy is updated in etcd but requires a Kyverno restart to take effect
C) The policy is updated in-place. Kyverno detects the change, re-evaluates, updates webhooks if needed, and if background: true, triggers a RE-SCAN of all matching existing resources. No restart needed — it's automatic
D) Updating a policy only affects new resources — existing resources are never re-scanned after a policy change
✅ Correct Answer: C

Policy updates are hot-reloaded — Kyverno watches its own CRDs and reacts to changes immediately. No restarts, no downtime, no manual intervention. This is a fundamental design principle: Kyverno policies are declarative, and the controller continuously reconciles desired state (your policy) with actual state (webhooks, background scans).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Policy Update — What Happens When You kubectl apply
  $ kubectl apply -f updated-policy.yaml
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  t=0s    Policy CR updated in etcd                        │
  │  t=0.1s  Kyverno WATCH informer detects the change       │
  │  t=0.2s  Policy re-parsed: YAML → internal rules         │
  │  t=0.3s  Webhook configurations re-evaluated             │
  │          → If match rules changed: update webhooks        │
  │          → If match rules unchanged: webhooks stay as-is  │
  │  t=0.5s  Policy status updated: READY=True               │
  │  t=1s    (if background:true) Re-scan of matching        │
  │          existing resources begins                        │
  │  t=5s    PolicyReports regenerated with new results      │
  │  t=10s   All new admission requests use updated policy   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️ WHAT CHANGES REQUIRE WEBHOOK RE-REGISTRATION:
  • match block changes (different kinds/namespaces)
  • exclude block changes
  • validationFailureAction: Audit ↔ Enforce
  ⚠️ WHAT DOESN'T: validate message text, pattern details

For anihpj/jobpost, policy updates are deployed through GitOps with ArgoCD. When a PR merges, ArgoCD applies the change, and Kyverno picks it up within seconds — zero-touch policy updates with full audit trail in git.

4.2 Resource Selection — match & excludeQ103-Q112

Applying Policies Q103 of 200
What is the match block and how does it determine which resources are evaluated?
A) match is the resource SELECTOR — it defines WHICH resources the policy applies to by kind, name, namespace, labels, and annotations. Resources that don't match are SKIPPED
B) match is optional — if omitted, the policy applies to no resources
C) match selects which rules within the policy are active — it doesn't control resource targeting
D) match uses regex patterns — it doesn't support kind or namespace filtering
✅ Correct Answer: A

The match block is the aiming mechanism — if misconfigured, your policy either fires on everything or nothing.

📝 Detailed Explanation
match Block Fields
  match:
    any:
    - resources:
        kinds: [Pod]              ← WHAT type?
        names: [anihpj-api-*]     ← WHICH specific resources?
        namespaces: [anihpj]      ← WHERE?
        selector:
          matchLabels: {app: api} ← Filter by LABELS
        annotations: {team: pf}   ← Filter by ANNOTATIONS

For anihpj/jobpost, precise match blocks prevent policies from accidentally affecting system pods.

Applying Policies Q104 of 200
What is the difference between match.any and match.all?
A) any and all are interchangeable — they produce the same results
B) any selects matching resources; all selects non-matching resources
C) any is for namespaced Policies; all is for ClusterPolicies
D) any = OR logic — matches if AT LEAST ONE criterion is satisfied. all = AND logic — matches only if ALL criteria are satisfied simultaneously
✅ Correct Answer: D

Think of match.any as an OR gate and match.all as an AND gate. any is like "let in anyone who has EITHER a ticket OR a backstage pass." all is like "only let in people who have BOTH a ticket AND a photo ID AND are over 18."

📝 Detailed Explanation
match.any vs match.all — OR vs AND Logic Explained
  match.any: (Pod in anihpj) OR (Deployment in staging)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  - resources: {kinds: [Pod], namespaces: [anihpj]}       │
  │    → Any Pod in anihpj matches ✅                         │
  │  - resources: {kinds: [Deployment], namespaces: [staging]}│
  │    → Any Deployment in staging also matches ✅             │
  │  RESULT: Pods in anihpj OR Deployments in staging         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  match.all: Pod AND in namespace anihpj AND label app=api
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  - resources:                                             │
  │      kinds: [Pod]           ← MUST be a Pod              │
  │      namespaces: [anihpj]   ← MUST be in anihpj ns       │
  │      selector: {app: api}   ← MUST have this label       │
  │  RESULT: Only Pods in anihpj WITH app=api label           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WHEN TO USE EACH:
  • any: Multiple independent target groups (e.g., Pods + Deployments)
  • all: Single target with multiple required conditions
  • Most policies use any with a single criterion — it's the simpler default

For anihpj/jobpost, we use match.any for most policies like "block privileged pods in anihpj namespace" — simple single-criterion matches. We use match.all for our strictest security policies that should only fire when multiple conditions converge — like "enforce read-only root filesystem only on production Pods running as non-root with specific labels."

Applying Policies Q105 of 200
What can you target with match.resources.kinds?
A) Only Pod resources — Kyverno can only target Pods
B) Any Kubernetes resource: Pod, Deployment, StatefulSet, DaemonSet, Job, CronJob, Service, ConfigMap, Secret, Namespace, Ingress, NetworkPolicy, CRDs, and more
C) Only built-in resources — CRDs cannot be targeted
D) Resource kinds are limited to a predefined list of 10 types
✅ Correct Answer: B

Kyverno targets any Kubernetes resource — built-in types AND custom resources. A single policy can validate Pods, mutate Deployments, and generate NetworkPolicies. This universal targeting is what makes Kyverno far more powerful than pod-specific tools like Pod Security Admission.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Resource Kinds — Complete Targeting Reference
  WORKLOAD RESOURCES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kinds: [Pod]              ← Bare pods                   │
  │  kinds: [Deployment]       ← Deployments (autogen covers pods)│
  │  kinds: [StatefulSet]     ← Stateful apps with identity  │
  │  kinds: [DaemonSet]       ← One pod per node            │
  │  kinds: [Job, CronJob]    ← Batch workloads              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  INFRASTRUCTURE RESOURCES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kinds: [Namespace]        ← Validate new namespaces     │
  │  kinds: [Ingress]          ← TLS, host rules             │
  │  kinds: [Service]          ← Port restrictions           │
  │  kinds: [NetworkPolicy]    ← Policy validation           │
  │  kinds: [ConfigMap, Secret]← Data validation             │
  │  kinds: [PersistentVolumeClaim] ← Storage limits         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CUSTOM RESOURCES (CRDs):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kinds: [MyCustomResource]  ← Any CRD installed in cluster│
  │  Kyverno can validate, mutate, and generate CRDs too      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, our 13 policies target Pods, Deployments, StatefulSets, Namespaces, Ingresses, Services, and PVCs — covering every resource type in our application stack with a single policy engine.

Applying Policies Q106 of 200
How do you target specific resources by name with match.resources.names?
A) Kyverno cannot target resources by name — only by kind and namespace
B) Name targeting uses regex — simple strings are not supported
C) names: ["anihpj-db-0", "anihpj-redis-*"] — exact names or wildcard patterns. Useful for exempting specific resources from a policy
D) Name targeting requires the resource UID — metadata.name cannot be used
✅ Correct Answer: C

Name targeting is the exception mechanism built into match blocks — pinpoint specific resources by their metadata.name. When a policy should apply broadly but exempt specific named resources, use names with wildcards to carve out exceptions right in the rule.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Targeting by Name — Exact Names + Wildcard Patterns
  EXACT NAMES (match OR exclude):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  names: ["anihpj-db-0"]                                   │
  │  → Targets ONLY the specific pod named "anihpj-db-0"      │
  │  → Used in match: apply policy ONLY to this one resource   │
  │  → Used in exclude: exempt ONLY this one resource          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WILDCARD NAMES (glob-style):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  names: ["anihpj-db-*"]                                   │
  │  → anihpj-db-0, anihpj-db-1, anihpj-db-2 (entire STS)    │
  │  → names: ["debug-*"] → debug-pod, debug-job-abc123       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  COMMON EXCLUDE PATTERN:
  match: {kinds: [Pod], namespaces: [anihpj]}
  exclude: {names: ["anihpj-db-*", "debug-*"]}
  → All anihpj pods EXCEPT database and debug containers

For anihpj/jobpost, we use name targeting to exempt our PostgreSQL StatefulSet pods (anihpj-db-*) from the hostPath and privilege policies. The database legitimately needs elevated permissions for data persistence — name targeting ensures ONLY the database gets this exemption, not any other pod that happens to request hostPath.

Applying Policies Q107 of 200
How do namespace selectors work in Kyverno match blocks?
A) namespaces: ["anihpj", "staging"] restricts to specific namespaces. "*" for all. namespaceSelector with label matching for dynamic selection
B) Namespace filtering is only available in ClusterPolicies
C) Namespace selectors use regex — simple names are not supported
D) Namespace filtering requires --namespace flag on kubectl
✅ Correct Answer: A

Namespace selectors control WHERE in your cluster a policy operates. A ClusterPolicy without any namespace restriction applies to ALL namespaces — including kube-system, kyverno, and other system namespaces. Always scope your policies intentionally.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Namespace Selection — Three Approaches
  1. SPECIFIC NAMESPACES (most common):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  namespaces: ["anihpj", "staging", "production"]          │
  │  → Policy ONLY applies to these 3 namespaces              │
  │  → kube-system, kyverno, default are all EXEMPT           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  2. ALL NAMESPACES (use with CAUTION):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  namespaces: ["*"]                                        │
  │  → Policy applies to EVERY namespace                      │
  │  → ⚠️ Combined with kinds: ["*"] = cluster-wide scope     │
  │  → Always add an exclude for system namespaces!            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  3. DYNAMIC BY LABEL (most flexible):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  namespaceSelector:                                        │
  │    matchExpressions:                                       │
  │    - key: environment                                      │
  │      operator: In                                          │
  │      values: [production, staging]                         │
  │  → Auto-applies to any namespace labeled environment=prod  │
  │  → New namespaces are automatically covered if labeled     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, production policies use namespaces: ["anihpj"] to avoid interfering with system namespaces. We also use namespaceSelector to auto-apply security policies to any namespace labeled environment=production — so new production namespaces get policies automatically without manual policy updates.

Applying Policies Q108 of 200
How do you use labels and annotations to target resources in match blocks?
A) Labels and annotations cannot be used in match blocks
B) Labels are accessed via JMESPath — they cannot be selectors
C) Only labels are supported — annotations cannot be used
D) selector.matchLabels: {app: api} for labels. annotations: {team: platform} for annotations. Combine for precise targeting
✅ Correct Answer: D

Labels and annotations unlock metadata-driven policy targeting — beyond just "what kind of resource?" and "which namespace?", you can filter by ANY custom metadata your team uses. This is how you differentiate prod from staging without separate policies.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Label + Annotation Selection — Fine-Grained Targeting
  match:
    any:
    - resources:
        kinds: [Pod]
        namespaces: [anihpj]
        selector:
          matchLabels:
            app: anihpj-api        ← Label: app=anihpj-api
            tier: backend           ← Label: tier=backend
          matchExpressions:         ← Advanced label matching
          - key: environment
            operator: In
            values: [production]
        annotations:
          policy.anihpj.com/scan: "true"  ← Annotation-based opt-in

  RESULT: Only Pods in anihpj with labels app=api AND tier=backend
          AND annotation policy.anihpj.com/scan=true are matched

  USE CASES:
  • Differentiate prod vs dev: environment=production label
  • Opt-in policies: annotation policy.anihpj.com/scan=true
  • Tier-specific rules: tier=frontend vs tier=backend vs tier=database

For anihpj/jobpost, we use label selectors to apply different policies to API pods (app=api, tier=backend) vs web pods (app=web, tier=frontend) vs database pods (app=postgres, tier=database). Annotation-based targeting lets teams opt specific pods into experimental policies by setting policy.anihpj.com/scan=true.

Applying Policies Q109 of 200
What is the exclude block and how does it work?
A) exclude replaces match — you use one or the other
B) exclude REMOVES resources from the set matched by match — it's a subtract operation. "Match ALL pods, EXCLUDE debug-*"
C) exclude runs before match — it pre-filters resources
D) exclude is deprecated — use PolicyExceptions instead
✅ Correct Answer: B

exclude is the subtraction operator in Kyverno — it carves out exceptions from a broad match. Think of it like: "Invite everyone in the engineering department (match), EXCEPT the interns (exclude)." The exclude block uses the exact same syntax as match — kinds, names, namespaces, labels, annotations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
match + exclude — Subtract to Target Precisely
  STEP 1 — match grabs a broad set:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  match:                                                   │
  │    any:                                                   │
  │    - resources:                                           │
  │        kinds: [Pod]                                       │
  │        namespaces: [anihpj]                               │
  │  → RESULT SET: {api-pod, web-pod, db-pod-0, debug-shell}  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  STEP 2 — exclude removes a subset:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  exclude:                                                 │
  │    any:                                                   │
  │    - resources:                                           │
  │        names: ["debug-*"]                                 │
  │  → REMOVED: {debug-shell}                                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  FINAL SET EVALUATED BY POLICY: {api-pod, web-pod, db-pod-0}
  debug-shell is exempt — the policy never checks it.

  exclude vs PolicyExceptions:
  • exclude: INLINE in the policy — permanent, for structural exceptions
  • PolicyException: Separate CR — temporary, for operational exceptions

For anihpj/jobpost, we use exclude to exempt our PostgreSQL StatefulSet (db-pod-*) from the "no hostPath" policy — the database legitimately needs hostPath for data persistence. All other pods in the anihpj namespace are still blocked from using hostPath. This is a permanent structural exception, so it belongs in the policy's exclude block, not a PolicyException.

Applying Policies Q110 of 200
How do wildcards (*) work in Kyverno match patterns?
A) Wildcards are not supported in match blocks
B) * matches one character; ? matches any number
C) * matches any characters (glob-style). names: ["anihpj-*"] matches any resource starting with "anihpj-". namespaces: ["*"] matches all namespaces
D) Wildcards use full regex syntax — .* matches any characters
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kyverno uses glob-style wildcards — just like shell file patterns (ls *.txt). Simple, intuitive, no regex syntax to memorize. Wildcards make match blocks flexible without adding complexity.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Wildcard Patterns in Match Blocks — Complete Reference
  WILDCARD IN NAMES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  names: ["anihpj-*"]      → anihpj-api, anihpj-web, anihpj-db-0 │
  │  names: ["*-db-*"]        → anihpj-db-0, staging-db-1         │
  │  names: ["debug-*"]       → debug-pod, debug-job-abc123        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WILDCARD IN NAMESPACES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  namespaces: ["team-*"]   → team-platform, team-frontend      │
  │  namespaces: ["*"]        → ALL namespaces (use with care!)   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WILDCARD IN KINDS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kinds: ["*"]             → Pod, Deployment, Service, ALL     │
  │  kinds: ["Pod", "Deployment"] → Only these two (explicit)     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️ CAUTION: kinds: ["*"] matches EVERY resource —
  use only when you intentionally want cluster-wide scope.

For anihpj/jobpost, we use wildcards to target all application pods with names: ["anihpj-*"] — this automatically covers new pods as we scale, without needing to update match blocks every time a new deployment is added. We avoid kinds: ["*"] to prevent accidentally affecting system resources.

Applying Policies Q111 of 200
How do you create complex match blocks combining ANY + ALL?
A) Nest match.any.all — outer any (OR between groups), inner all (AND within each group). Each group under any must be fully satisfied
B) any and all cannot be combined — a match block uses one or the other
C) Complex match requires external JMESPath conditions
D) Combining any/all is only possible in ClusterPolicies
✅ Correct Answer: A

Nested any.all gives arbitrarily complex resource selection.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Nested any.all
  match:
    any:              ← OR between groups
    - all:            ← Group 1: ALL must be true
      - resources: {kinds: [Pod], namespaces: [anihpj], labels: {app: api}}
    - all:            ← Group 2: ALL must be true
      - resources: {kinds: [Deployment], namespaces: [staging], labels: {app: web}}

For anihpj/jobpost, nested any.all targets Pods AND Deployments across multiple namespaces with different label requirements.

Applying Policies Q112 of 200
How does match.subjects work — targeting by WHO triggered the request?
A) Subject matching is not supported in Kyverno
B) Subjects only work with service accounts — users are not supported
C) Subject matching uses client IP addresses
D) subjects filters by UserInfo, ServiceAccount, or Group. Requires background: false since background scans have no user identity
✅ Correct Answer: D

Subject matching adds identity-awareness to policies — different rules for different users and service accounts. This is crucial for CI/CD pipelines where automated systems need different treatment than human developers.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Subject Matching — Who Triggered the Request?
  MATCH BY SERVICE ACCOUNT:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  subjects:                                                │
  │  - kind: ServiceAccount                                   │
  │    name: ci-bot                                           │
  │    namespace: cicd                                        │
  │  → Policy only applies when the ci-bot SA makes the call │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  MATCH BY USER GROUP:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  subjects:                                                │
  │  - kind: Group                                            │
  │    name: developers                                       │
  │  → Policy only applies to users in the "developers" group│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  MATCH BY SPECIFIC USER:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  subjects:                                                │
  │  - kind: User                                             │
  │    name: admin@anihpj.com                                 │
  │  → Policy targets requests from this specific user        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️ CRITICAL: When using subjects, you MUST set:
  spec:
    background: false  ← Scans have NO user identity!

For anihpj/jobpost, we use subject matching so CI/CD service accounts face stricter policies than human developers — the ci-bot SA runs additional image verification that developers can bypass during local testing.

4.3 Common Policy SettingsQ113-Q116

Applying Policies Q113 of 200
What is validationFailureAction: Audit vs Enforce?
A) Audit and Enforce are identical — names are for documentation only
B) Audit = Log to PolicyReport but ALLOW (safe for testing). Enforce = BLOCK with 403 (production). Deploy in Audit, review for 1-2 weeks, then switch to Enforce
C) Audit blocks silently; Enforce blocks with an error message
D) Audit is for dev clusters; Enforce requires enterprise license
✅ Correct Answer: B

This is arguably the single most important setting in any Kyverno policy. validationFailureAction controls whether the policy is a watchdog (Audit — observes and reports) or a guard dog (Enforce — blocks and bites). Get this wrong and you either have no real security (all Audit) or broken deployments (premature Enforce).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Audit vs Enforce — The Safe Rollout Strategy
  PHASE 1 — Audit Mode (Safe Testing):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  validationFailureAction: Audit                           │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  kubectl apply -f non-compliant-pod.yaml            │ │
  │  │  → Kyverno checks the pod against all policies      │ │
  │  │  → VIOLATION FOUND: runAsNonRoot is false           │ │
  │  │  → PolicyReport UPDATED: fail (recorded)            │ │
  │  │  → Pod is ALLOWED to be created ✅                  │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │  Review PolicyReports for 1-2 weeks                      │
  │  Identify false positives, tune match blocks              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  PHASE 2 — Enforce Mode (Active Blocking):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  validationFailureAction: Enforce                         │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  kubectl apply -f non-compliant-pod.yaml            │ │
  │  │  → Kyverno checks the pod against all policies      │ │
  │  │  → VIOLATION FOUND: runAsNonRoot is false           │ │
  │  │  → Pod is BLOCKED with 403 Forbidden ❌             │ │
  │  │  → Error: "admission webhook 'validate.kyverno...'  │ │
  │  │           denied the request: Running as root is    │ │
  │  │           not allowed. Set runAsNonRoot: true."     │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, ALL 13 policies started in Audit mode for the first week. After analyzing PolicyReports and confirming zero false positives, we switched critical policies (block-privileged, enforce-non-root, block-hostpath) to Enforce. Our CI/CD pipeline waits for READY=True before marking a policy deployment successful.

Applying Policies Q114 of 200
When should you set background: false?
A) background: false should always be used — background scanning is deprecated
B) Set when you want the policy to apply retroactively
C) Set background: false when: (1) policy uses subjects/request.userInfo, (2) only new resources matter, (3) external API calls fail in background mode, (4) reduce API load
D) background: false disables the policy entirely
✅ Correct Answer: C

The #1 reason to set background: false is request.userInfo — background scans have NO user identity. If your policy checks "who created this resource?", the scan will always get an empty answer, potentially blocking everything or silently passing everything.

📝 Detailed Explanation
When to Set background: false — Decision Checklist
  SET background: false WHEN:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ Policy uses subjects (UserInfo, ServiceAccount)       │
  │     REASON: Scans have no user identity — checks fail     │
  │                                                           │
  │  ✅ Policy references request.userInfo in conditions      │
  │     REASON: Same as above — userInfo is empty during scan │
  │                                                           │
  │  ✅ Policy is a one-time audit check                      │
  │     REASON: No need to re-check old resources repeatedly   │
  │                                                           │
  │  ✅ Policy makes external API calls (registry, webhook)   │
  │     REASON: API calls during scan can fail or be expensive│
  │                                                           │
  │  ✅ Policy targets ephemeral resources (Jobs, CronJobs)   │
  │     REASON: These resources come and go — re-scan is noisy│
  │                                                           │
  │  ✅ You want to reduce Kubernetes API load                │
  │     REASON: Every scan = N API calls (N = matching res)   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KEEP background: true (default) WHEN:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ Structural validation (runAsNonRoot, resource limits) │
  │  ✅ Label/annotation requirements                         │
  │  ✅ Security context checks                               │
  │  → These work during both admission AND background scans  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, 1 of our 13 policies uses background: false — the one checking which CI/CD ServiceAccount deployed the resource. Without this setting, the background scan would either fail (blocking all existing resources on re-scan) or produce incorrect results — a dangerous false positive/false negative scenario.

Applying Policies Q115 of 200
What is failurePolicy: Fail vs Ignore at the policy level?
A) failurePolicy only affects audit logging — behavior is unchanged
B) Fail/Ignore are webhook-level — cannot be set per-policy
C) Ignore is recommended for production — prevents breaking deployments
D) Fail = if policy evaluation ERRORS (bad JMESPath, timeout), BLOCK the resource. Ignore = if error, ALLOW (risky). Different from validationFailureAction which handles VIOLATIONS, not ERRORS
✅ Correct Answer: D

failurePolicy and validationFailureAction sound similar but control COMPLETELY different things. Confusing them is one of the most common Kyverno mistakes — and dangerous because it can silently allow non-compliant resources.

📝 Detailed Explanation
failurePolicy vs validationFailureAction — Two Different Concepts
  SCENARIO A: Resource VIOLATES the policy (runAsRoot: false)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Controlled by: validationFailureAction                   │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  Audit   → "This pod violates the policy, but I'll  │ │
  │  │            log it and let it through"               │ │
  │  │  Enforce → "This pod violates the policy — BLOCKED" │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │  This is about POLICY VIOLATION (resource is non-compliant)│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  SCENARIO B: Policy has an EVALUATION ERROR (broken JMESPath)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Controlled by: failurePolicy                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  Fail    → "I can't evaluate this policy — BLOCK    │ │
  │  │            the resource to be safe" (default)       │ │
  │  │  Ignore  → "I can't evaluate this policy — let the  │ │
  │  │            resource through anyway" (risky!)        │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │  This is about POLICY ERROR (the policy itself is broken) │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️ WARNING: failurePolicy: Ignore means broken policies
  silently pass — the worst possible security posture.

For anihpj/jobpost, ALL policies use failurePolicy: Fail. If a policy is broken (bad JMESPath, timeout), we WANT resources blocked until the policy is fixed. Silently passing errors is how security gaps form — a broken policy that silently allows everything is worse than no policy at all.

Applying Policies Q116 of 200
What is webhookTimeoutSeconds and when should you adjust it?
A) Per-policy timeout override. Increase for slow policies (API calls, image verification). Decrease for simple patterns. Default inherited from controller flag (10s). Only needed when policies consistently time out
B) Controls background scan frequency — default 3600 seconds
C) Controller-level flag only — cannot be set per-policy
D) Should always be 30 — that's the Kubernetes maximum
✅ Correct Answer: A

This is a per-policy performance tuning knob — rarely needed but critical when a specific policy consistently times out. The default 10s is generous for simple pattern validation; adjust only after confirming the issue via Kyverno metrics (admission_review_duration_seconds).

📝 Detailed Explanation
webhookTimeoutSeconds — Performance Tuning Guide
  DEFAULT: 10 seconds (inherited from controller --webhookTimeout)

  INCREASE (15-25s) when:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ Policy has API calls in context (registry lookups)    │
  │  ✅ verifyImages checks Cosign + Rekor (network calls)    │
  │  ✅ Large ConfigMap data processing in context            │
  │  ✅ Complex JMESPath on very large resources (>10KB)      │
  │  RISK: Increasing timeout delays ALL admissions           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  DECREASE (5s) when:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ Simple pattern validation (no external calls)         │
  │  ✅ Basic label/annotation checks                         │
  │  ✅ Fast path — policy should return in <100ms            │
  │  RISK: Setting too low causes false timeout failures      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  MONITORING: Track kyverno_admission_review_duration_seconds
  before adjusting — don't guess, measure!

For anihpj/jobpost, our image verification policy uses webhookTimeoutSeconds: 20 because Cosign verification requires network calls to both the registry and Rekor transparency log. Our simple label-requirement policies use the default 10s.

✍️ Category 5: Writing Policies (32%)Q117-Q180

The Heart of the KCA Exam — Writing Real Policies

At 32% of the exam, this is the most important domain. You'll learn ALL rule types — validate, mutate, generate, verifyImages — plus preconditions, variables, JSON patches, autogen, cleanup, and CEL expressions.

5.1 Validation Rules — Pattern & DenyQ117-Q128

Writing Policies Q117 of 200
What is the anatomy of a validate rule in Kyverno?
A) A validate rule only contains a deny statement — patterns are not part of validation
B) A validate rule has: name, match, optional exclude, validate.message (user-facing error), and either validate.pattern (overlay) or validate.deny (conditions). Only ONE of pattern or deny per rule
C) validate rules are the same as mutate rules — both modify resources
D) validate rules require a separate kyverno-validate binary
✅ Correct Answer: B

The validate rule is the most common rule type — it checks resources against your requirements and either passes or blocks. Every Kyverno user writes validate rules first; they're the foundation of policy-as-code.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Validate Rule Anatomy — Annotated Breakdown
  rules:
  - name: validate-runAsNonRoot          ← Unique name per rule
    match:                                ← Resource selection
      any:
      - resources:
          kinds: [Pod]
          namespaces: [anihpj]
    exclude:                              ← Optional: remove resources
      any:
      - resources:
          names: ["debug-*"]
    validate:                             ← THE VALIDATION BLOCK
      message: "Running as root is not allowed"
      pattern:                            ← OPTION 1: pattern (overlay)
        spec:
          containers:
          - (securityContext):
              (runAsNonRoot): true
      # OR use deny instead:             ← OPTION 2: deny (conditions)
      # deny:
      #   conditions:
      #     any:
      #     - key: "{{ request.object... }}"
      #       operator: Equals
      #       value: false

  ONE RULE = ONE validate block = EITHER pattern OR deny

For anihpj/jobpost, 8 of our 13 policies use validate rules — they're the backbone of our security enforcement, checking everything from non-root users to image registries.

Writing Policies Q118 of 200
What is pattern-based validation (overlay pattern)?
A) Pattern validation uses regex to match resource fields
B) Pattern validation modifies resources to match a template
C) Pattern validation is an overlay approach — you declare the EXPECTED structure, and Kyverno compares the actual resource against it. Missing or mismatched fields fail. Think of tracing paper: overlay your expected pattern and check for gaps
D) Pattern validation requires exact match — extra fields cause failures
✅ Correct Answer: C

Pattern validation is Kyverno's signature feature — instead of writing complex conditional logic, you simply declare what the resource SHOULD look like. Kyverno checks the gaps between your pattern and the actual resource. This is far simpler than Rego (OPA) or custom webhooks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pattern Validation — Overlay Approach Explained
  YOUR PATTERN (what you expect):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                                    │
  │    containers:                                            │
  │    - securityContext:                                     │
  │        runAsNonRoot: true      ← This field MUST be true  │
  │        readOnlyRootFilesystem: true ← MUST be true        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ACTUAL RESOURCE (what the user submitted):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                                    │
  │    containers:                                            │
  │    - name: nginx                                          │
  │      image: nginx:latest                                  │
  │      securityContext:                                     │
  │        runAsNonRoot: false     ← ❌ FAIL: expected true   │
  │      # readOnlyRootFilesystem MISSING → ❌ FAIL           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  OVERLAY RESULT:
  • runAsNonRoot: true  → Actual is false → VIOLATION
  • readOnlyRootFilesystem → Field is missing → VIOLATION
  → Pod BLOCKED with message explaining both failures

For anihpj/jobpost, pattern validation is our default approach — it's intuitive, self-documenting, and catches both wrong values AND missing fields in a single declaration.

Writing Policies Q119 of 200
What are anchors: (key), +(key), =(key), -(key)?
A) Anchors are only used in deny rules
B) All anchors behave identically — symbols are cosmetic
C) Anchors are deprecated — replaced by CEL
D) (key) = value anchor (MUST exist and match). +(key) = conditional (if parent exists, check this). =(key) = global (match across ALL list elements). -(key) = existence (must NOT exist)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Anchors are the fine-tuning knobs of pattern validation — they control HOW strictly Kyverno checks each field. The parentheses are NOT cosmetic — each anchor symbol changes validation behavior significantly.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Anchor Reference Card — All Four Types
  (key)   VALUE ANCHOR — Field MUST exist AND match the value
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  (runAsNonRoot): true                                     │
  │  → runAsNonRoot MUST be present AND MUST equal true       │
  │  → Missing? FAIL. Wrong value? FAIL.                      │
  │  → DEFAULT anchor — use for required fields               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  +(key)  CONDITIONAL ANCHOR — Check ONLY if parent exists
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  +(runAsNonRoot): true                                    │
  │  → IF securityContext exists → runAsNonRoot must = true   │
  │  → IF no securityContext at all → OK, skip check          │
  │  → Use for: optional but if present must be correct       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  =(key)  GLOBAL ANCHOR — Match across ALL list elements
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  =(image): "registry.anihpj.com/*"                        │
  │  → EVERY container's image must match this pattern        │
  │  → If container 1 uses Harbor but container 2 uses Docker Hub → FAIL │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  -(key)  EXISTENCE ANCHOR — Field must NOT exist
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  -(hostPath)                                              │
  │  → hostPath volume type must NOT be present anywhere      │
  │  → If ANY volume uses hostPath → FAIL                     │
  │  → Use for: blocking dangerous fields entirely            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use (key) for mandatory security fields (runAsNonRoot, readOnlyRootFilesystem), +(key) for optional-but-must-be-correct fields (seccompProfile), =(key) to ensure ALL containers pull from our Harbor registry, and -(key) to prohibit hostPath volumes entirely across all 13 policies.

Writing Policies Q120 of 200
What is validate.deny and when use it vs validate.pattern?
A) deny blocks resources based on CONDITIONS — "if this, then block". Use when: (1) complex boolean logic needed, (2) checking dynamic context data, (3) pattern overlay can't express the rule. Deny is more flexible but harder to read; pattern is simpler but less powerful
B) deny and pattern are identical — different syntax, same behavior
C) deny is deprecated — always use pattern
D) deny only works with ClusterPolicies
✅ Correct Answer: A

deny is the conditional logic approach — use when pattern overlay can't express what you need. Deny is more flexible but requires JMESPath knowledge; pattern is simpler but less powerful for complex boolean logic.

📝 Detailed Explanation
validate.deny — Condition-Based Blocking
  DENY RULE STRUCTURE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  validate:                                                │
  │    message: "Images must come from registry.anihpj.com"  │
  │    deny:                                                  │
  │      conditions:                                          │
  │        any:                    ← OR: block if ANY true   │
  │        - key: "{{ images.containers.*.registry }}"       │
  │          operator: NotEquals                              │
  │          value: "registry.anihpj.com"                     │
  │        - key: "{{ images.containers.*.registry }}"       │
  │          operator: NotEquals                              │
  │          value: "harbor.anihpj.com"                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  SUPPORTED OPERATORS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Equals, NotEquals, GreaterThan, LessThan                │
  │  GreaterThanOrEquals, LessThanOrEquals                   │
  │  In, NotIn, StartsWith, EndsWith, Contains               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WHEN PATTERN CANNOT EXPRESS IT (use deny):
  • "Image must NOT come from registry X" (negation)
  • "Memory limit must be > request" (comparison)
  • "Tag must NOT be 'latest'" (string check)
  • "Replicas must be > 1" (numeric comparison)

For anihpj/jobpost, we use deny for our image registry check — JMESPath extracts the registry from image references for comparison against our allowlist, which pattern can't express.

Writing Policies Q121 of 200
How do you write effective, human-readable error messages?
A) Error messages are auto-generated — you cannot customize them
B) Write messages that: (1) explain WHAT is wrong, (2) explain WHY it matters, (3) tell HOW to fix it. Example: "Running as root is not allowed. Set runAsNonRoot: true. Required for PCI-DSS compliance."
C) Error messages should be technical codes — look up in documentation
D) Messages are limited to 64 characters
✅ Correct Answer: B

Good error messages reduce support tickets by 50%+. When kubectl returns a 403 because Kyverno blocked a resource, the error message is the ONLY information the developer sees. A well-written message lets them fix the issue immediately without asking the platform team.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Bad vs Good Error Messages — Real Examples
  ❌ BAD: "Validation failed"
  ❌ BAD: "Error: admission webhook denied the request"
  ❌ BAD: "Policy violation"
  → Developer has NO IDEA what's wrong or how to fix it

  ✅ GOOD: "Running as root is not allowed (runAsNonRoot must be true).
           Add securityContext.runAsNonRoot: true to each container.
           Required for PCI-DSS compliance.
           See: https://wiki.anihpj.com/kb/kyverno-policies#non-root"
  → Developer knows WHAT is wrong, WHY it matters, and HOW to fix it

  ✅ GOOD: "Image must come from registry.anihpj.com.
           Your image 'docker.io/nginx:latest' is from an untrusted source.
           Replace with: image: registry.anihpj.com/nginx:latest"
  → Shows the EXACT fix — developer can copy-paste the solution

  THE 3-PART MESSAGE FORMULA:
  1. WHAT is wrong (the specific violation)
  2. WHY it matters (compliance, security, operational reason)
  3. HOW to fix it (exact YAML, command, or link to docs)

For anihpj/jobpost, every policy message follows the 3-part formula and includes a link to our internal wiki. Since implementing this, platform team Slack questions about policy violations dropped from ~15/week to ~2/week — developers can self-serve fixes.

Writing Policies Q122 of 200
How does pattern validation work on LISTS — validating each container?
A) Patterns cannot validate lists — only scalar values
B) List validation only checks the first element
C) When a pattern encounters a list, it applies the pattern to EACH element. Every container must satisfy the pattern. If ONE fails, the entire resource is blocked. Use =(key) for same-value-across-all validation
D) List elements are validated in aggregate
✅ Correct Answer: C

When a pattern encounters a list (like containers), Kyverno iterates over every element — each container, each volume, each env var is checked independently. If ONE element fails, the ENTIRE resource is blocked. This is crucial for security: you can't have one "good" container and one "bad" container in the same pod.

📝 Detailed Explanation
List Validation — Every Element Checked Independently
  Pattern:
    containers:
    - (securityContext):
        (runAsNonRoot): true

  Pod with 3 containers — the pattern checks EACH one:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  containers:                                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  - name: api                                         │ │
  │  │    securityContext: {runAsNonRoot: true}   ✅ PASS  │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  - name: sidecar                                     │ │
  │  │    securityContext: {runAsNonRoot: true}   ✅ PASS  │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  - name: debug                                       │ │
  │  │    securityContext: {runAsNonRoot: false}  ❌ FAIL  │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                           │
  │  RESULT: POD BLOCKED! 2/3 passed but 1 failed → BLOCK     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  GLOBAL ANCHOR = for same-value-across-all:
    =(image): "registry.anihpj.com/*"
    → ALL containers must use images from our registry

  PRO TIP: Use =(key) global anchor when you need the SAME
  value across all list elements, or regular (key) to check
  each element independently.

For anihpj/jobpost, list validation ensures that EVERY container in a pod meets security requirements — including sidecar containers like Istio proxies and logging agents. A pod with a compliant API container but a privileged debug sidecar is still blocked. This prevents attackers from sneaking in malicious sidecars alongside legitimate containers.

Writing Policies Q123 of 200
How do wildcards (*) work in validation patterns?
A) * as a value = "any value acceptable" — field must exist but can be anything. "registry.com/*" as a string = matches any image starting with that prefix
B) Wildcards replace the entire field — using * removes it from validation
C) Wildcards only work in deny conditions
D) * matches one character; ** matches any number
✅ Correct Answer: A

Wildcards say "this field must exist, but I don't care what its value is." Combined with prefix strings, they enable flexible-but-constrained validation — like requiring images from a specific registry while allowing any image name within it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Wildcards in Patterns — Complete Reference
  "*" AS A VALUE (any value acceptable):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  (image): "*"                   ← Must exist, any value OK│
  │  (app): "?*"                    ← Must exist, non-empty   │
  │  labels:                         ← All labels: any values  │
  │    "*": "*"                                              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  "*" IN STRING PREFIX (constrained value):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  (image): "registry.anihpj.com/*" ← Must start with this  │
  │  → "registry.anihpj.com/nginx:v1" ✅                     │
  │  → "docker.io/nginx:v1" ❌                               │
  │  → "registry.anihpj.com/team/app:v2" ✅                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  "*" IN LABEL KEYS/VALUES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  labels:                                                  │
  │    "app.kubernetes.io/*": "*"   ← Any k8s-standard label  │
  │    "team": "?*"                  ← Must exist, non-empty   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use "registry.anihpj.com/*" to ensure all images come from our Harbor registry while allowing any team to use any image name and tag within it — flexible but secure.

Writing Policies Q124 of 200
How do nested patterns work for deeply nested YAML?
A) Kyverno can only validate one level deep
B) Nested patterns require separate sub-rules per level
C) Nested patterns use dot notation
D) Patterns naturally nest by YAML indentation — spec→containers→securityContext→capabilities is expressed as nested YAML levels. Kyverno traverses both the pattern and resource simultaneously at each level
✅ Correct Answer: D

Nesting is natural YAML — the pattern structure mirrors the resource structure. No special syntax for deep fields; just indent further. Kyverno traverses both the pattern and the resource simultaneously at each nesting level.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Nested Pattern — Deep Validation by Indentation
  pattern:
    spec:                                    ← Level 0
      containers:                             ← Level 1
      - securityContext:                      ← Level 2
          capabilities:                       ← Level 3
            drop:                             ← Level 4
            - ALL                             ← Level 5 (value)
            -(add)                            ← Level 5 (must NOT have add)
          seccompProfile:                     ← Level 3
            (type): "RuntimeDefault"          ← Level 4

  Each indentation level corresponds to a YAML nesting level.
  Kyverno walks the actual resource at each level in parallel.

  EXAMPLE: Validating /spec/containers[0]/securityContext/capabilities/drop
  → Must contain ALL, must NOT contain add entries

For anihpj/jobpost, our "drop capabilities" policy uses nested patterns 5 levels deep — validating that ALL capabilities are dropped AND no new capabilities are added, reaching into the deepest parts of the security context.

Writing Policies Q125 of 200
When should you use pattern vs deny?
A) deny is always preferred — pattern is legacy
B) Pattern preferred for structure checks — "field X must equal Y." Deny for logic pattern can't express — comparisons, string functions, dynamic values. Rule of thumb: if you can say "look like THIS," use pattern; otherwise, deny
C) Pattern and deny produce different PolicyReport results
D) Deny is faster — use for performance-critical policies
✅ Correct Answer: B

Choosing between pattern and deny is about simplicity vs flexibility. Pattern is the "show me what you expect" approach — intuitive and self-documenting. Deny is the "tell me when to block" approach — more powerful but requires JMESPath knowledge.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pattern vs Deny — Decision Guide with Examples
  USE PATTERN when you can say "the resource should look like THIS":
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ "runAsNonRoot must be true"                           │
  │  ✅ "All containers must have resource limits"            │
  │  ✅ "Labels app and team must exist"                      │
  │  ✅ "SecurityContext must be present"                     │
  │  ✅ "capabilities.drop must include ALL"                  │
  │  Pattern is declarative — you draw the shape,              │
  │  Kyverno checks if the resource fits.                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  USE DENY when you need logic pattern can't express:
