  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ "Image registry must NOT be docker.io"                │
  │  ✅ "Image tag must NOT be latest"                        │
  │  ✅ "Memory limit must be >= request"                     │
  │  ✅ "Only allow images from list of approved registries"  │
  │  ✅ "Replica count must be > 1 for production"            │
  │  Deny uses JMESPath comparisons — greater_than,           │
  │  NotEquals, starts_with, contains, etc.                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RULE OF THUMB: Start with pattern. If you find yourself
  fighting the overlay approach, switch to deny.
  ~80% of production policies use pattern for structure checks.

For anihpj/jobpost, we default to pattern for 6 of our 8 validate policies (non-root, read-only root FS, drop capabilities, labels, resource limits, PVC size). We use deny for 2 policies that need JMESPath: image registry check ("registry must NOT be docker.io") and tag check ("tag must NOT be latest").

Writing Policies Q126 of 200
How do you negate a pattern — asserting something does NOT match?
A) Patterns cannot be negated — must use deny
B) Prefix with ! to negate — !pattern
C) Use -(key) anchor for field existence negation. For value negation ("image must NOT be nginx:latest"), switch to deny rule. Pattern is inherently positive ("must match this")
D) Use notMatch block — inverse of match
✅ Correct Answer: C

Pattern is inherently positive — it says what SHOULD be present. To say what should NOT be, you have two options depending on whether you're negating field EXISTENCE or field VALUES.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pattern Negation — Two Approaches
  NEGATE FIELD EXISTENCE (pattern anchor):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  -(hostPath)                     ← hostPath must NOT exist│
  │  -(securityContext.privileged)   ← privileged must NOT be│
  │  Use -(key) when: "This field should never appear"       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  NEGATE FIELD VALUE (switch to deny):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  deny:                                                    │
  │    conditions:                                            │
  │      any:                                                 │
  │      - key: "{{ images.containers.*.registry }}"          │
  │        operator: Equals                                   │
  │        value: "docker.io"      ← Block if it IS docker.io │
  │  Use deny when: "This field CAN exist, but NOT with X"    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  DECISION GUIDE:
  • "This field must NOT be present" → -(key) anchor
  • "This field must NOT equal X" → deny with conditions

For anihpj/jobpost, we use -(hostPath) to block hostPath volumes entirely and deny rules to block images from Docker Hub while allowing images from other untrusted registries to be checked separately.

Writing Policies Q127 of 200
What are common pattern mistakes and how do you debug them?
A) (1) Missing anchor parentheses — key: value vs (key): value (forgotten parens = field IGNORED). (2) Wrong indentation. (3) Over-restrictive patterns. (4) Forgetting patterns check EXISTENCE — missing fields fail. Debug with kyverno apply
B) Mistakes are caught at kubectl apply — no separate debugging needed
C) Only debugging tool is kubectl logs on Kyverno pod
D) Pattern mistakes only affect PolicyReports — never block resources
✅ Correct Answer: A

The #1 mistake: forgetting anchor parentheses. runAsNonRoot: true in a pattern does NOTHING — it must be (runAsNonRoot): true with parentheses. This is the most common error for new Kyverno users and the hardest to debug because the policy deploys without error but silently ignores your validation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Common Pattern Mistakes — Debugging Guide
  MISTAKE #1: Missing anchor parentheses
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ runAsNonRoot: true       ← Not anchored → IGNORED!   │
  │  ✅ (runAsNonRoot): true     ← Anchored → ENFORCED!      │
  │  DEBUG: kyverno apply policy.yaml -r pod.yaml            │
  │  → If a field should block but passes, check anchors     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  MISTAKE #2: Wrong indentation
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ spec:                                                 │
  │       containers:          ← Wrong level (under spec)    │
  │         - securityContext: ← Wrong level                 │
  │  ✅ spec:                                                 │
  │       containers:          ← Direct child of spec        │
  │       - securityContext:   ← Direct child of list item   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  MISTAKE #3: Using =() instead of ()
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  =() is the GLOBAL anchor — applies to ALL list elements │
  │  () is the VALUE anchor — applies per element            │
  │  Using the wrong one changes validation scope             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, our policy development checklist includes: (1) kyverno apply against compliant AND non-compliant resources, (2) verify anchors are correct, (3) check indentation with a YAML linter, (4) confirm both pass AND fail cases produce expected results.

Writing Policies Q128 of 200
Walk through creating your first validate policy — "require labels on all pods."
A) Must use Kyverno policy generator wizard
B) (1) Kind: ClusterPolicy. (2) validationFailureAction: Audit. (3) Match: kinds: [Pod]. (4) Pattern: (labels).(app): "?*". (5) Message. (6) Test: kyverno apply. (7) Deploy: kubectl apply
C) Label policies must use deny rules
D) "Require labels" is built into Kyverno — just enable a flag
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Hello World of Kyverno — simple enough to learn in 2 minutes, useful in production.

📝 Detailed Explanation
First Policy — Require Labels
  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
  kind: ClusterPolicy
  metadata: {name: require-pod-labels}
  spec:
    validationFailureAction: Audit
    rules:
    - name: check-labels
      match: {any: [{resources: {kinds: [Pod]}}]}
      validate:
        message: "Pods must have 'app' and 'team' labels"
        pattern:
          metadata:
            labels:
              (app): "?*"
              (team): "?*"

For anihpj/jobpost, every new platform engineer writes this policy as their first Kyverno exercise.

5.2 Preconditions — Guard Rules Before ValidationQ129-Q132

Writing Policies Q129 of 200
What are preconditions and how do they differ from match?
A) Preconditions are alternative syntax for match — same purpose
B) Preconditions only available in ClusterPolicies
C) match selects by METADATA (kind, namespace, labels). preconditions evaluate CONTENT (field values, computed data) BEFORE the rule runs. If preconditions fail, the rule is SKIPPED — a content-aware gate
D) Preconditions run AFTER validation — they filter PolicyReport results
✅ Correct Answer: C

Preconditions are content-aware gates — match says "is this a Pod?", precondition says "does this Pod's image come from our registry?" Match uses metadata (kind, namespace, labels); preconditions evaluate actual field values using JMESPath.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Preconditions vs Match — Two Different Filters
  MATCH (metadata filter — runs FIRST):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  "Is this a Pod in the anihpj namespace?"                │
  │  → Based on: kind, name, namespace, labels, annotations  │
  │  → Selects WHICH resources to evaluate                   │
  │  → If no match → Resource IGNORED (never evaluated)      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  PRECONDITION (content filter — runs SECOND):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  "Does this Pod's image start with registry.anihpj.com?" │
  │  → Based on: spec fields, JMESPath expressions           │
  │  → Gates WHETHER the rule executes for matched resources │
  │  → If precondition fails → Rule SKIPPED (may run others) │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  EXAMPLE: Stricter rules for production images
  match: Pods in anihpj namespace
  precondition: image starts with registry.anihpj.com
  → Rule only validates production images; dev images skip

For anihpj/jobpost, we use preconditions to apply stricter rules only to production images while being more lenient with development images — the same policy adapts based on content.

Writing Policies Q130 of 200
What is precondition.any vs precondition.all?
A) any and all are identical — naming is cosmetic
B) any for namespaced Policies; all for ClusterPolicies
C) any skips if ANY fail; all skips if ALL fail
D) any = OR — passes if AT LEAST ONE condition true. all = AND — passes only if ALL conditions true. Same logic as match.any/all, operating on content instead of metadata
✅ Correct Answer: D

Same OR/AND logic as match.any vs match.all, but operating on resource CONTENT instead of metadata. Preconditions are content-aware gates — they check field values, not just labels and names.

📝 Detailed Explanation
precondition.any vs precondition.all — OR vs AND Gating
  precondition.any: Rule runs if image from ECR OR Harbor
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  preconditions:                                           │
  │    any:                                                   │
  │    - key: "{{ images.containers.*.registry }}"            │
  │      operator: Equals                                     │
  │      value: "ecr.aws.com"                                 │
  │    - key: "{{ images.containers.*.registry }}"            │
  │      operator: Equals                                     │
  │      value: "harbor.anihpj.com"                           │
  │  → Rule runs if image is from ECR OR Harbor               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  precondition.all: Rule runs ONLY if image from Harbor AND not latest
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  preconditions:                                           │
  │    all:                                                   │
  │    - key: "{{ images.containers.*.registry }}"            │
  │      operator: Equals                                     │
  │      value: "harbor.anihpj.com"                           │
  │    - key: "{{ images.containers.*.tag }}"                 │
  │      operator: NotEquals                                  │
  │      value: "latest"                                      │
  │  → Rule runs only if BOTH conditions are true             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WHEN TO USE EACH:
  • any: Multiple acceptable scenarios (any trusted registry)
  • all: Multiple REQUIRED conditions (registry AND tag AND digest)

For anihpj/jobpost, we use precondition.all for our strictest policies — the rule only fires when both "image is from Harbor" AND "tag is not latest" are simultaneously true. Our precondition.any policies accept images from multiple trusted registries (Harbor for internal, ECR for third-party).

Writing Policies Q131 of 200
How does precondition differ from match — when should you use each?
A) match selects resources by METADATA (kind, namespace, labels, name) — "IS this a Pod in anihpj?" precondition evaluates resource CONTENT (field values, JMESPath results) — "Does this Pod's image start with registry.anihpj.com?" Match runs FIRST (resource selection), precondition runs SECOND (content gating). If match fails, the resource is never evaluated. If precondition fails, the rule is SKIPPED for that resource
B) precondition is just an alias for match — they are interchangeable
C) match runs after precondition — precondition filters first
D) precondition replaces match — you cannot use both in the same rule
✅ Correct Answer: A

Match says WHO — precondition says WHEN. Match is the door bouncer checking IDs; precondition is the metal detector checking what you're carrying.

📝 Detailed Explanation
match vs precondition — Two-Stage Filtering
  API Request → MATCH (metadata check)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Is this a Pod? Is it in namespace anihpj?           │
  │  Has label app=api?                                   │
  │  → NO: Resource IGNORED (never evaluated)             │
  │  → YES: Continue to preconditions                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                        ↓
  PRECONDITION (content check)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Does image start with registry.anihpj.com?           │
  │  Does it have at least 2 replicas?                    │
  │  → NO: Rule SKIPPED (but other rules may still run)   │
  │  → YES: Rule executed (validate/mutate/generate)       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use match to target "Pods in anihpj namespace" and preconditions to apply stricter rules only when the image is tagged as :latest — a dangerous tag that needs extra validation.

Writing Policies Q132 of 200
How do you write complex preconditions using JMESPath to check nested or dynamic fields?
A) JMESPath cannot be used in preconditions — only simple key=value checks are supported
B) Preconditions with JMESPath use regex patterns — JMESPath is not a supported expression language
C) Use JMESPath in the key field of preconditions: key: "{{ request.object.spec.containers[?name=='api'].securityContext.privileged | [0] || `false` }}" — this extracts the privileged field from the container named "api". JMESPath enables checking nested paths, array filtering, and computed values that simple field references can't reach
D) Complex preconditions require a separate ConfigMap with Lua scripts — YAML alone cannot express them
✅ Correct Answer: C

JMESPath in preconditions unlocks surgical precision — check the Nth container, the 3rd volume mount, or any deeply nested field.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Complex Precondition with JMESPath
  preconditions:
    all:
    - key: "{{ request.object.spec.containers[?name=='api'].securityContext.privileged | [0] || `false` }}"
      operator: Equals
      value: false
    - key: "{{ request.object.metadata.labels.tier || 'none' }}"
      operator: NotEquals
      value: none

  ← Rule only runs if: container "api" is NOT privileged AND pod HAS a tier label

For anihpj/jobpost, we use JMESPath preconditions to check that our API container specifically (not just any container) is non-privileged — identifying the container by name rather than position.

5.3 Background Scans — Checking Existing ResourcesQ133-Q136

Writing Policies Q133 of 200
What is a Kyverno background scan, and why does it matter for existing resources?
A) Background scan is a Kubernetes feature — Kyverno just reads its output
B) A background scan is Kyverno periodically re-evaluating ALL EXISTING resources against ALL active policies — not just new ones arriving through admission. Without it, resources created BEFORE a policy was deployed would NEVER be checked. Think of it like a security audit that sweeps the entire building, not just the front door
C) Background scan only checks resources in kube-system — it's a system-level audit
D) Background scan is a one-time operation that runs when Kyverno starts up — it doesn't repeat
✅ Correct Answer: B

Background scans are the retroactive enforcement mechanism — policies only apply to NEW resources by default. Scans catch the old ones.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Background Scan vs Admission — Two Paths
  ADMISSION PATH (new resources):
  kubectl apply → API Server → Kyverno webhook → Policy check → Allow/Deny

  BACKGROUND SCAN PATH (existing resources):
  Kyverno Background Controller scans cluster every N minutes
  → Finds ALL matching resources (even from months ago)
  → Re-evaluates against current policy versions
  → Updates PolicyReports with pass/fail results
  → Does NOT delete or modify — just reports compliance status

For anihpj/jobpost, when we deploy a new "require labels" policy, the background scan immediately checks all 50+ existing pods and tells us which ones are missing labels — so we can fix them proactively.

Writing Policies Q134 of 200
When should you set background: false — what are the specific scenarios?
A) Set background: false to disable Kyverno entirely — the policy won't run at all
B) Set background: false only for policies in Audit mode — Enforce policies must have it on
C) Set background: false to speed up Kyverno — background scans are always unnecessary overhead
D) Set background: false when: (1) Policy uses subjects or request.userInfo (scans have no user identity — they would fail or give wrong results), (2) Policy only matters for NEW resources going forward (ephemeral Jobs), (3) Policy makes external API calls that fail in background mode, (4) You want to reduce Kubernetes API load by skipping scans for specific policies
✅ Correct Answer: D

The #1 reason: request.userInfo doesn't exist during background scans. If your policy checks "who made this request?", background scans break.

📝 Detailed Explanation
When background: false is Required
  ✅ Policy references request.userInfo or subjects → MUST disable
  ✅ Policy is a one-time audit (not ongoing enforcement)
  ✅ Policy makes API calls to external services
  ✅ Policy targets ephemeral resources (Jobs, Pods that restart often)
  ✅ You want to reduce API server load from scanning
  ❌ Policy validates structural fields (runAsNonRoot, resource limits) → keep ON

For anihpj/jobpost, 1 of our 13 policies uses background: false — the one checking which CI/CD ServiceAccount deployed the resource. Scans have no SA context, so the check would always fail or give incorrect results.

Writing Policies Q135 of 200
How do you read background scan results — what are BackgroundScanReports?
A) BackgroundScanReport is a namespaced CR that shows per-policy scan results for existing resources. Use kubectl get backgroundscanreport -n anihpj to list them. Each report shows: policy name, rule name, resource name/kind, result (pass/fail/warn/error/skip), and a timestamp of when the scan ran. BackgroundScanReports are separate from admission PolicyReports — they specifically track the BACKGROUND scan path, not live admission
B) Background scan results are in the Kyverno pod logs — there's no CR for them
C) Background scans write to the same PolicyReport as admission — you can't tell them apart
D) BackgroundScanReport is a ClusterPolicyReport — it's cluster-wide, not namespaced
✅ Correct Answer: A

BackgroundScanReports are your compliance dashboard for existing resources — separate from admission reports to help you distinguish "blocked at the door" vs "already inside but non-compliant."

📝 Detailed Explanation
Reading BackgroundScanReports
  $ kubectl get backgroundscanreport -n anihpj
  NAME                              POLICY              RULE          RESULT   AGE
  cpol-require-labels-7d8f9         require-labels      check-labels   fail    2m
  cpol-block-privileged-a3b2c       block-privileged    validate-priv  pass    2m

  $ kubectl get backgroundscanreport cpol-require-labels-7d8f9 -o yaml
  # Shows: which resource failed, which field, scan timestamp

For anihpj/jobpost, we monitor BackgroundScanReports to find resources that were deployed before a policy existed — then fix them proactively before switching the policy from Audit to Enforce.

Writing Policies Q136 of 200
What are the performance considerations for background scans, and how can you limit their scope?
A) Background scans have zero performance impact — they run on dedicated hardware
B) Background scans generate Kubernetes API load proportional to (number of policies) × (matching resources). To limit scope: use narrow match blocks (specific kinds/namespaces, not "*"), set background: false for policies that don't need retroactive scanning, configure --backgroundScanWorkers and --backgroundScanInterval controller flags, and use config.resourceFilters to skip high-volume resources like events or leases
C) Performance is managed automatically — Kyverno self-tunes based on cluster size
D) Background scans only run at night — performance during business hours is unaffected
✅ Correct Answer: B

Background scans are proportional to your cluster size and policy count. A ClusterPolicy matching kinds: ["*"] scans EVERY resource — potentially thousands per cycle.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Background Scan Performance Tuning
  TUNING KNOBS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  match specificity:  kinds: [Pod] instead of kinds: ["*"]    │
  │  namespace filtering: namespaces: [anihpj] not ["*"]          │
  │  background: false    skip scan for userInfo-dependent policies│
  │  --backgroundScanWorkers    default: 2, increase for large cls │
  │  --backgroundScanInterval   default: 1h, increase to reduce    │
  │  config.resourceFilters     skip events, leases, endpoints     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  IMPACT ESTIMATE:
  10 policies × 500 resources = 5,000 evaluations per scan cycle
  Be SPECIFIC in match blocks — every "*" multiplies the cost.

For anihpj/jobpost with ~50 pods, background scan overhead is negligible. But we still use narrow match blocks as a best practice and exclude kube-system resources.

5.4 Mutation Rules — Automatically Fix ResourcesQ137-Q146

Writing Policies Q137 of 200
What is a mutate rule in Kyverno, and where does it sit in the admission flow?
A) Mutate rules delete non-compliant resources and recreate them with correct settings
B) Mutate rules run AFTER validation — they fix resources that failed validation
C) A mutate rule automatically modifies incoming resources BEFORE they're persisted to etcd. It runs FIRST in the admission flow (mutate → validate), so mutated fields are then checked by validate rules. Think of it like an auto-correct feature — it fixes minor issues without blocking the user. Common uses: add default labels, inject sidecars, set resource limits, rewrite image registries
D) Mutate rules are read-only — they suggest changes but don't actually modify resources
✅ Correct Answer: C

Mutation is Kyverno's "fix it for you" mode. Instead of blocking and saying "add a label!", it just adds the label automatically.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Admission Flow — Mutate Runs FIRST
  kubectl apply → API Server
                        ↓
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  MUTATE phase (Kyverno mutating webhook) │
  │  → Add default labels                    │
  │  → Inject sidecar container              │
  │  → Rewrite image registry                │
  │  → Set default resource limits           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────┘
                        ↓
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VALIDATE phase (Kyverno validating)      │
  │  → Check the MUTATED version             │
  │  → Block if still non-compliant           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────┘
                        ↓
                      etcd

For anihpj/jobpost, our mutate policy automatically adds the team=platform label to every pod — developers don't need to remember it, and the validate policy then confirms it's present.

Writing Policies Q138 of 200
What is patchesStrategicMerge and when should you use it for mutations?
A) patchesStrategicMerge overlays a partial YAML onto the resource using Kubernetes' strategic merge patch logic — it's the SIMPLER mutation approach. You declare what you want ADDED or OVERLAID, and Kyverno merges it. Best for: adding labels/annotations, adding containers/env vars to existing lists, overlaying entire sub-structures like securityContext. Lists with merge keys (like containers with "name") are intelligently merged — matching items are updated, new items are added
B) patchesStrategicMerge replaces the entire resource with your patch — it's a full replacement
C) patchesStrategicMerge only works with ConfigMaps — it cannot mutate other resource types
D) Strategic merge is deprecated — only JSON Patch should be used for mutations
✅ Correct Answer: A

Strategic merge is the beginner-friendly mutation — just write what you want added, Kyverno handles the merging logic.

📝 Detailed Explanation
patchesStrategicMerge — Adding Default Labels
  mutate:
    patchesStrategicMerge:
      metadata:
        labels:
          +(team): platform
          +(environment): "{{ request.namespace }}"
      spec:
        containers:
        - name: "*"           ← Apply to ALL containers
          resources:
            requests:
              memory: "128Mi"
              cpu: "100m"

For anihpj/jobpost, we use patchesStrategicMerge to add team and environment labels to every pod, plus default resource requests for all containers — simple overlays that don't need JSON Patch precision.

Writing Policies Q139 of 200
What is patchesJson6902 and how does it differ from strategic merge?
A) patchesJson6902 is the same as strategic merge but with JSON syntax
B) patchesJson6902 is only for removing fields — it cannot add or modify
C) JSON Patch is deprecated in favor of strategic merge
D) patchesJson6902 uses RFC 6902 JSON Patch operations — add, replace, remove, copy, move, test — targeting specific JSON paths like /spec/containers/0/image. It's MORE PRECISE than strategic merge — you specify exact paths and operations. Use it when you need: remove a field entirely, change a specific value at a known path, conditional operations, or path expressions that strategic merge can't express
✅ Correct Answer: D

JSON Patch is the surgical scalpel — strategic merge is the overlay brush. JSON Patch for precision, strategic merge for simplicity.

📝 Detailed Explanation
JSON Patch — RFC 6902 Operations
  mutate:
    patchesJson6902: |
      - path: "/spec/containers/0/image"
        op: replace
        value: "registry.anihpj.com/nginx:1.25"
      - path: "/metadata/labels/team"
        op: add
        value: "platform"
      - path: "/spec/containers/0/securityContext/privileged"
        op: remove

  # "replace first container's image, add team label, remove privileged"

For anihpj/jobpost, we use JSON Patch to rewrite image registries — it needs to target the exact image field path in each container, which strategic merge can't express as cleanly.

Writing Policies Q140 of 200
Strategic Merge Patch vs JSON Patch — which mutation approach should you use and when?
A) Always use JSON Patch — strategic merge is legacy and less capable
B) Strategic merge (patchesStrategicMerge) for: adding labels/annotations, overlaying securityContext, adding containers with merge-by-name. JSON Patch (patchesJson6902) for: removing fields, replacing specific array indices, precise path targeting, conditional operations. Rule of thumb: if you're ADDING or OVERLAYING structured data → strategic merge. If you're REMOVING, REPLACING specific values, or need exact paths → JSON Patch
C) Both produce the same result — use whichever you prefer syntactically
D) Strategic merge for namespaced Policies; JSON Patch for ClusterPolicies
✅ Correct Answer: B

Choose based on the operation you need, not personal preference. Adding = strategic merge. Removing = JSON Patch. Replacing = either, but JSON Patch is explicit.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Strategic Merge vs JSON Patch — Decision Matrix
  OPERATION                STRATEGIC MERGE    JSON PATCH
  ─────────                ───────────────    ──────────
  Add labels/annotations   ✅ Best choice     ✅ Works
  Add container to list    ✅ By name merge    ✅ By index
  Overlay securityContext  ✅ Simple overlay   ✅ Works
  Remove a field           ❌ Cannot           ✅ Best choice
  Replace value at path    ✅ If merge-aware   ✅ Best choice
  Add to numeric array     ❌ Difficult        ✅ By index
  Conditional mutate       ❌ Not supported    ✅ With test op

For anihpj/jobpost, we use strategic merge for 3 label/resource policies and JSON Patch for 2 image-registry and capability-removal policies — choosing the right tool for each job.

Writing Policies Q141 of 200
What are the most common mutation patterns used in production Kyverno policies?
A) Mutation is rarely used in production — most teams only use validate rules
B) The only common mutation is adding labels — everything else is too complex for mutation
C) (1) Add default labels/annotations — team, environment, cost-center. (2) Set default resource limits — CPU/memory requests and limits for all containers. (3) Inject sidecar containers — service mesh proxies, logging agents. (4) Rewrite image registries — add registry prefix, replace docker.io with internal registry. (5) Add security defaults — runAsNonRoot, drop capabilities, readOnlyRootFilesystem. (6) Add environment variables — inject POD_NAME, NAMESPACE via downward API
D) Mutation is limited to metadata fields — spec mutations are not supported
✅ Correct Answer: C

Mutation is the developer experience layer — it sets sensible defaults so developers don't need to know every security requirement.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Top 6 Mutation Patterns
  1. Default labels:   team=platform, cost-center=eng
  2. Resource defaults: cpu=100m, memory=128Mi (if not set)
  3. Sidecar injection: Istio proxy, Fluentd log collector
  4. Registry rewrite:  docker.io/nginx → registry.anihpj.com/nginx
  5. Security defaults: runAsNonRoot:true, drop ALL capabilities
  6. Env injection:     POD_NAME, POD_NAMESPACE via fieldRef

For anihpj/jobpost, our 3 mutate policies cover patterns 1, 4, and 5 — labels, image registry, and security defaults. Developers focus on app logic; Kyverno handles compliance.

Writing Policies Q142 of 200
How do you mutate image registries — replacing docker.io with your internal registry?
A) Use a mutate rule with JMESPath to conditionally rewrite the image field: check if the image doesn't already contain your registry prefix, then use patchesJson6902 to replace the image path with registry.anihpj.com/{{images.containers.*.name}}@{{images.containers.*.digest}}. This preserves the image name and tag while changing the registry host. Works on ALL containers automatically via foreach
B) Image registry mutation requires a custom admission webhook — Kyverno cannot modify image references
C) Simply add the registry prefix in the Dockerfile — no Kyverno policy needed
D) Use a validate.deny rule — it blocks non-compliant images instead of rewriting them
✅ Correct Answer: A

Image registry mutation is one of Kyverno's most valuable production features — developers use familiar image names (nginx:latest), and Kyverno transparently redirects to your internal registry. This eliminates the need for every developer to know registry paths and ensures all images go through your security pipeline.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Image Registry Mutation — Transparent Rewriting
  WHAT THE DEVELOPER WRITES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  containers:                                              │
  │  - name: api                                             │
  │    image: nginx:latest                                    │
  │  - name: redis                                           │
  │    image: redis:7-alpine                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WHAT KYVERNO MUTATES TO (transparently):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  containers:                                              │
  │  - name: api                                             │
  │    image: registry.anihpj.com/nginx:latest               │
  │  - name: redis                                           │
  │    image: registry.anihpj.com/redis:7-alpine             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  MUTATION APPROACH (forEach + JMESPath):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  mutate:                                                  │
  │    foreach:                                               │
  │    - list: request.object.spec.containers                 │
  │      preconditions:                                       │
  │        all:                                               │
  │        - key: "{{ images.containers."{{element.name}}".registry }}"
  │          operator: NotIn                                  │
  │          value: ["registry.anihpj.com"]                   │
  │      patchesJson6902: |                                   │
  │        - path: /spec/containers/{{elementIndex}}/image    │
  │          op: replace                                      │
  │          value: registry.anihpj.com/{{element.image}}     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, our image registry mutation ensures ALL images are pulled through Harbor — enabling image caching (faster pulls), vulnerability scanning (security), and license compliance checks on every deployed image.

Writing Policies Q143 of 200
How do you create conditional mutations — only mutate when certain conditions are met?
A) Conditional mutations are not supported — mutations always apply to all matched resources
B) Use preconditions in the mutate rule — the mutation only executes if preconditions pass. Example: "Only add default resource limits if the container doesn't already have them." Combine with JMESPath to check the current state: preconditions.all[].key: "{{ request.object.spec.containers[0].resources.requests.cpu || ''}}" operator: Equals value: "" — only mutate if CPU request is empty
C) Conditional mutations use if/else YAML keywords — not preconditions
D) Conditions are set via annotations on the policy — not within the rule itself
✅ Correct Answer: B

Preconditions in mutate rules create smart defaults — "only add limits if they're missing," not "overwrite existing limits."

📝 Detailed Explanation
Conditional Mutation Pattern
  rules:
  - name: default-resources
    match: {any: [{resources: {kinds: [Pod]}}]}
    preconditions:
      any:
      - key: "{{ request.object.spec.containers[0].resources.requests.cpu || ''}}"
        operator: Equals
        value: ""              ← Only if no CPU request set
    mutate:
      patchesStrategicMerge:
        spec:
          containers:
          - name: "*"
            resources:
              requests:
                cpu: "100m"
                memory: "128Mi"

For anihpj/jobpost, our resource defaults policy uses conditional mutation — it only adds 100m CPU / 128Mi RAM if the developer forgot to set any limits, never overwriting intentional values.

Writing Policies Q144 of 200
How do you mutate lists and arrays — like adding environment variables or volumes to containers?
A) Kyverno cannot mutate list fields — only scalar values are modifiable
B) List mutation replaces the entire list — you must specify ALL elements
C) With patchesStrategicMerge, lists with merge keys (containers by name, volumes by name, env vars by name) are intelligently merged — matching items are updated, new items are appended. With patchesJson6902, use /spec/containers/0/env/- (the - means "append to end of array"). Both approaches work; strategic merge is simpler for named lists
D) List mutation requires a separate generate rule — mutate rules can't modify arrays
✅ Correct Answer: C

List mutation is surprisingly common — adding env vars, sidecar containers, volume mounts. Kyverno handles it gracefully.

📝 Detailed Explanation
List Mutation — Adding Env Vars
  # Strategic Merge: Add env var to all containers
  mutate:
    patchesStrategicMerge:
      spec:
        containers:
        - name: "*"
          env:
          - name: K8S_NAMESPACE
            valueFrom:
              fieldRef:
                fieldPath: metadata.namespace

  # JSON Patch: Append env var to first container
  mutate:
    patchesJson6902: |
      - path: "/spec/containers/0/env/-"
        op: add
        value: {"name": "K8S_NAMESPACE", "valueFrom": {"fieldRef": {"fieldPath": "metadata.namespace"}}}

For anihpj/jobpost, we use strategic merge to inject POD_NAME and POD_NAMESPACE env vars into every container — developers get these automatically without any configuration.

Writing Policies Q145 of 200
What happens when MULTIPLE mutate policies try to modify the same field?
A) Only the first policy executes — subsequent mutations are silently ignored
B) Mutations are applied in alphabetical order by policy name
C) Conflicting mutations cause an error — the resource is blocked
D) Mutations are applied in alphabetical order by policy name, and each subsequent mutation sees the result of all previous ones. The LAST mutation to touch a field WINS. This means policy naming is CRITICAL for mutation ordering. If policy "a-add-labels" sets team=unknown and policy "z-set-team" sets team=platform, the final value is team=platform (z runs last). Use naming conventions (00-*, 10-*, 20-*) to control order explicitly
✅ Correct Answer: D

Mutation ordering is alphabetical by policy name — the simplest but most overlooked aspect of mutation design. Names matter.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Mutation Ordering — Policy Names Control Execution
  Policies execute alphabetically:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  00-add-default-labels    → team=unknown (set first)    │
  │  10-add-resource-limits   → cpu=100m                     │
  │  20-set-team-label        → team=platform (OVERWRITES!)  │
  │  30-inject-sidecar        → adds Istio proxy             │
  │  90-rewrite-registry      → rewrites image path          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Final result: team=platform (from 20-*), not team=unknown

For anihpj/jobpost, we use numbered prefixes (01-*, 02-*, etc.) on all mutate policies — ensuring labels are added first, then security defaults, then registry rewrites, in a deliberate order.

Writing Policies Q146 of 200
How do you use variables in mutations to create dynamic, context-aware patches?
A) Use Kyverno variables in mutation values: value: "{{ request.namespace }}" to inject the target namespace, value: "{{ images.containers.*.name }}" for image names, value: "{{ @ }}" for the entire resource. Variables make mutations dynamic — the same policy produces different results for different resources. Combine with JMESPath for complex transformations: "{{ divide(sum(request.object.spec.containers[].resources.requests.memory), '1048576') }}" to convert bytes to MiB
B) Variables are only for validate rules — mutations must use static values
C) Mutation variables use Go templates ({{.Namespace}}) — not Kyverno variables
D) Variables in mutations only work with ConfigMap data — not resource fields
✅ Correct Answer: A

Variables make mutations dynamic — the same policy adapts to each resource, injecting namespace-specific labels, image-aware annotations, and computed values.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Variables in Mutations
  # Add namespace as a label
  +(namespace): "{{ request.namespace }}"

  # Add image digest as annotation
  +(image-digest): "{{ images.containers.*.digest }}"

  # Convert memory to MiB
  +(memory-mib): "{{ divide(sum(request.object.spec.containers[].resources.requests.memory || `0`), '1048576') }}"

For anihpj/jobpost, we use variables to inject the namespace name as a label and the git commit SHA from ConfigMap context — giving every pod traceable deployment metadata.

5.5 Generation Rules — Automatically Create ResourcesQ147-Q154

Writing Policies Q147 of 200
What is a generate rule in Kyverno, and what problem does it solve?
A) Generate rules create random test data — they're for development only
B) A generate rule automatically creates NEW Kubernetes resources when triggered by an event. Unlike mutate (which modifies existing resources), generate CREATES resources that didn't exist before. Classic use case: "When a new Namespace is created, automatically create a NetworkPolicy, ResourceQuota, and default ConfigMap inside it." Generate rules are the ONLY rule type that creates NEW resources — they're like auto-provisioning triggers
C) Generate rules produce reports — they don't create actual Kubernetes resources
D) Generate rules clone existing resources — they cannot create new resources from templates
✅ Correct Answer: B

Generate rules are Kyverno's auto-provisioning engine — turn "every namespace needs X, Y, Z" from a manual checklist into automatic enforcement.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Generate Rule — Auto-Provision on Namespace Creation
  Developer creates: kubectl create namespace team-anihpj
                              ↓
  Kyverno WATCH detects new Namespace
                              ↓
  Generate rule fires:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Creates NetworkPolicy (default-deny)                │
  │  Creates ResourceQuota (max 10 pods, 50Gi storage)   │
  │  Creates ConfigMap (default settings)                │
  │  Creates RoleBinding (default viewer role)           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, our generate rule auto-creates a NetworkPolicy in every new namespace — ensuring no namespace is accidentally left without network isolation.

Writing Policies Q148 of 200
What is the difference between generate.clone and generate.data?
A) clone copies from etcd; data copies from a file on disk
B) clone and data are different names for the same operation
C) clone copies an EXISTING resource from a source namespace (e.g., "copy the golden ConfigMap from namespace 'default' into every new namespace"). data creates a resource from an INLINE definition in the policy itself (e.g., "create this specific NetworkPolicy YAML in every new namespace"). Clone is for reusing existing templates; data is for self-contained definitions
D) clone is for namespaced resources; data is for cluster-wide resources
✅ Correct Answer: C

Clone = copy-paste an existing "golden" resource. Data = write inline in the policy. Each has its use case.

📝 Detailed Explanation
clone vs data
  CLONE (copy from source):
  generate:
    clone:
      namespace: default            ← Source namespace
      name: golden-network-policy   ← Source resource name

  DATA (inline definition):
  generate:
    data:
      apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
      kind: NetworkPolicy
      metadata: {name: default-deny}
      spec:
        podSelector: {}
        policyTypes: [Ingress, Egress]

For anihpj/jobpost, we use clone for our "golden ConfigMap" (maintained once, propagated everywhere) and data for simpler resources like NetworkPolicies that are compact enough to define inline.

Writing Policies Q149 of 200
What does synchronize: true do in a generate rule?
A) synchronize: true enables background scanning — unrelated to generation
B) synchronize: true creates one copy per replica — used for StatefulSets
C) synchronize: true syncs the policy across multiple Kyverno instances for HA
D) synchronize: true (default) means Kyverno keeps the generated resource IN SYNC with the source. If the source (clone) or policy definition (data) changes, Kyverno UPDATES the generated resource. If the generated resource is deleted, Kyverno RECREATES it. synchronize: false means "create once and never touch again" — the generated resource is a one-time copy that can diverge
✅ Correct Answer: D

synchronize is the "keep in sync" toggle — on by default because most teams want generated resources to stay updated.

📝 Detailed Explanation
synchronize: true vs false
  synchronize: true (default):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Source ConfigMap changes → Generated copy updates│
  │  Generated resource deleted → Kyverno recreates   │
  │  Policy updated → All generated resources update  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  synchronize: false:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Resource created ONCE at trigger time            │
  │  Source changes are IGNORED                       │
  │  If deleted manually → NOT recreated              │
  │  Use for: "seed" data that teams customize locally │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use synchronize: true for NetworkPolicies (must stay compliant) and synchronize: false for seed ConfigMaps (teams can customize their own copies after initial creation).

Writing Policies Q150 of 200
What is generateExisting: true and when do you need it?
A) By default, generate rules only fire for NEW resources (e.g., newly created namespaces). generateExisting: true tells Kyverno to also process resources that ALREADY EXIST when the policy is deployed. Without it, if you deploy a "generate NetworkPolicy for every namespace" policy, namespaces created BEFORE the policy existed would NOT get a NetworkPolicy. With generateExisting: true, Kyverno retroactively generates resources for all existing matching resources
B) generateExisting makes generated resources permanent — they can't be deleted
C) generateExisting targets only resources older than 30 days
D) generateExisting: true is the default — generate rules always process existing resources
✅ Correct Answer: A

generateExisting is the retroactive switch — without it, generate rules only help future resources. With it, they clean up the past too.

📝 Detailed Explanation
generateExisting — Retroactive Generation
  Deploy "auto-NetworkPolicy" generate policy:
  t=0:   Policy active, but existing namespaces UNCHANGED
  t=0:   Set generateExisting: true
  t=1:   Kyverno processes ALL existing namespaces
  t=2:   NetworkPolicy created in namespace1, namespace2, ... namespace50

  ⚠️ Caution: generateExisting can create many resources at once!
  Always review the scope before enabling it on a large cluster.

For anihpj/jobpost, when we first deployed our "auto-NetworkPolicy" policy, we used generateExisting: true to backfill all 8 existing namespaces with default-deny policies — then left it on for future namespaces.

Writing Policies Q151 of 200
What are the most common real-world generate rule scenarios?
A) Generate rules are theoretical — few teams use them in production
B) (1) Auto-create NetworkPolicy in every new namespace for default-deny. (2) Auto-create ResourceQuota to prevent resource hogging. (3) Propagate ConfigMaps/Secrets — copy a golden ConfigMap into every namespace. (4) Auto-create RBAC — RoleBinding for default ServiceAccount. (5) Auto-create PodDisruptionBudget for every Deployment. (6) Auto-create ServiceMonitors for Prometheus scraping
C) Generate rules are only for creating Pods — they can't create other resource types
D) The only valid use case is creating ConfigMaps — other resources must be created manually
✅ Correct Answer: B

Generate rules are the ultimate "set and forget" tool — define once, automatically provisioned everywhere, forever.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Top 6 Generate Patterns
  1. NetworkPolicy:   Default-deny in every namespace
  2. ResourceQuota:   Prevent resource hogging per namespace
  3. ConfigMap/Secret: Propagate golden configs everywhere
  4. RBAC:            Default RoleBindings for new namespaces
  5. PDB:             Auto-create PodDisruptionBudget for Deployments
  6. ServiceMonitor:  Auto-configure Prometheus monitoring

For anihpj/jobpost, our 2 generate policies cover patterns 1 and 3 — NetworkPolicy auto-creation and golden ConfigMap propagation to all 8 namespaces.

Writing Policies Q152 of 200
How do you add conditions to generate rules — only generating for specific triggers?
A) Generate rules cannot be conditional — they always fire on every match
B) Conditions use if/else YAML syntax within the generate block
C) Use match with label selectors or exclude blocks to control WHICH triggers activate the generate rule. Example: "Only generate NetworkPolicy for namespaces labeled environment: production" by adding a namespaceSelector in the match block. Also use preconditions within the rule for content-based gating — "only generate if the namespace annotation team exists"
D) Conditional generation uses a separate Condition CRD — not inline in the policy
✅ Correct Answer: C

Match + exclude + preconditions = precise generation targeting. Not every namespace needs every generated resource.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Conditional Generate — Only for Production Namespaces
  rules:
  - name: generate-np-for-production
    match:
      any:
      - resources:
          kinds: [Namespace]
          selector:
            matchLabels:
              environment: production   ← Only production namespaces
    exclude:
      any:
      - resources:
          names: [kube-system, kyverno]  ← Never these
    generate:
      data:
        kind: NetworkPolicy
        ...

For anihpj/jobpost, we use label-based generation — namespaces labeled environment=production get strict NetworkPolicies, while dev namespaces get more permissive ones. The label controls which policy applies.

Writing Policies Q153 of 200
What happens when a generate rule FAILS — and how do you diagnose it?
A) Generate failures silently succeed — there's no error reporting
B) Generate failures block the triggering resource — the namespace creation also fails
C) Generate failures are logged only in the API server audit log — not in Kyverno
D) If a generate rule fails (e.g., clone source doesn't exist, RBAC insufficient, resource already exists), Kyverno creates an UpdateRequest in ERROR state. Debug with: kubectl get updaterequests -A to find failed generations, then kubectl describe ur <name> for the error message. Common causes: missing source for clone, Kyverno lacks RBAC to create the target resource, or the generated resource YAML is invalid
✅ Correct Answer: D

UpdateRequests are the debug trail for generate failures — every attempt (success or failure) leaves a record.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Debugging Generate Failures
  $ kubectl get updaterequests -A
  NAMESPACE  NAME                      POLICY              STATUS
  anihpj     ur-generate-np-7d8f9      auto-networkpolicy  Completed
  anihpj     ur-generate-rq-a3b2c      auto-resourcequota  Error

  $ kubectl describe ur ur-generate-rq-a3b2c
  Status:
    State:  Failed
    Message: "failed to create ResourceQuota: namespaces "default"
             is forbidden: cannot set blockOwnerDeletion..."

For anihpj/jobpost, we monitor UpdateRequests in our CI/CD pipeline — any ERROR-state UpdateRequest triggers an alert so we can fix RBAC or source resources before it affects production.

Writing Policies Q154 of 200
What happens when a GENERATED resource is itself subject to Kyverno policies?
A) Generated resources ARE subject to other Kyverno policies — including validate, mutate, and even other generate rules. This creates a cascade: Generate creates a NetworkPolicy → Validate checks it has required labels → Mutate adds missing labels if needed. This is powerful but requires careful design to avoid infinite loops. Kyverno prevents infinite generate loops by tracking which resources were already processed
B) Generated resources bypass all policies — they're created directly in etcd
C) Generated resources only go through validate rules — they skip mutate rules
D) Generated resources are marked with an annotation that exempts them from all policies
✅ Correct Answer: A

Generated resources go through the full admission pipeline — they're created by Kyverno acting as a Kubernetes client, so they pass through admission like any other API request.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Generate + Downstream Validation Cascade
  Namespace created → generate rule fires
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Generate: creates NetworkPolicy                 │
  │     ↓ (created by Kyverno client)                │
  │  Mutate webhook: adds required labels (if any)   │
  │     ↓                                            │
  │  Validate webhook: checks the NetworkPolicy      │
  │     ↓                                            │
  │  NetworkPolicy persisted in etcd                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️ Kyverno tracks generation state to prevent loops

For anihpj/jobpost, our generated NetworkPolicies pass through our label-requirement validate policy and get the team=platform label from our mutate policy — the full pipeline applies, ensuring every generated resource meets the same standards as manually created ones.

5.6 VerifyImage Rules — Supply Chain SecurityQ155-Q162

Writing Policies Q155 of 200
What is verifyImages and how does it protect your supply chain?
A) verifyImages checks that image tags follow semantic versioning — it's a naming convention validator
B) verifyImages cryptographically VERIFIES that container images are signed by a trusted authority BEFORE they run. It uses Cosign/Sigstore to check digital signatures, attestations, and certificates. The flow: Developer builds image → Cosign signs with private key → Image pushed to registry → Kyverno checks the signature against the public key → If valid, allow; if unsigned or invalid, block. This prevents supply chain attacks where an attacker replaces a legitimate image with a malicious one
C) verifyImages scans images for CVEs — it's a vulnerability scanner, not a signature verifier
D) verifyImages checks image size — it blocks images larger than a configured limit
✅ Correct Answer: B

verifyImages is Kyverno's supply chain gatekeeper — it answers "Do I trust this image?" using cryptographic proof, not just naming conventions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Image Verification — The Supply Chain Flow
  Developer → Build image → Cosign sign (private key)
     ↓
  Push to registry: registry.anihpj.com/anihpj-api:v1.2.3
     ↓
  kubectl apply (or CI/CD deploys pod using this image)
     ↓
  Kyverno verifyImages rule:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. Fetch image signature from registry           │
  │  2. Verify signature against trusted public key   │
  │  3. Check attestations (SLSA, SBOM) if required   │
  │  4. VALID → Allow deployment                      │
  │     INVALID → BLOCK: "image verification failed"  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, every production image is signed with Cosign. Our verifyImages policy ensures only signed images from our CI pipeline can run in production — preventing supply chain attacks.

Writing Policies Q156 of 200
What attestor types does Kyverno support for verifyImages — keys, certificates, and keyless?
A) Kyverno only supports static public key verification — no other attestor types
B) Attestor types are: passwords, tokens, and API keys — Kyverno uses basic authentication
C) Kyverno supports: (1) Keys — direct public key (Cosign-generated key pair), simplest setup. (2) Certificates — x509 certs from a CA, useful for enterprise PKI. (3) Keyless (OIDC) — Sigstore's Fulcio CA issues short-lived certs based on OIDC identity (e.g., "this image was built by GitHub Actions workflow X in repo Y"), no key management needed. (4) Attestations — verify SLSA provenance, SBOMs, vulnerability scan results attached to the image
D) Attestor types change with each Kyverno version — there's no stable set
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kyverno supports four attestor types — from simple key pairs to enterprise PKI to keyless OIDC. Choose based on your operational maturity.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Attestor Types Comparison
  TYPE          SETUP COMPLEXITY   KEY MANAGEMENT    BEST FOR
  ────          ────────────────   ──────────────    ───────
  Keys          ★☆☆ (simple)       Manual            Small teams, getting started
  Certificates  ★★☆ (moderate)     CA-managed        Enterprise with existing PKI
  Keyless/OIDC  ★★☆ (moderate)     NONE (auto!)      CI/CD pipelines, GitHub Actions
  Attestations  ★★★ (advanced)    Depends on type   SLSA compliance, audit-ready

For anihpj/jobpost, we started with static keys (simple), then migrated to keyless signing via GitHub Actions OIDC — no key rotation, no secrets to manage, and every deployment is cryptographically tied to a specific git commit.

Writing Policies Q157 of 200
What is Cosign and how does it work with Kyverno for image signing?
A) Cosign is a Sigstore tool that signs container images and stores signatures in the OCI registry alongside the image. Workflow: (1) cosign generate-key-pair creates cosign.key (private) and cosign.pub (public). (2) cosign sign --key cosign.key registry.anihpj.com/anihpj-api:v1.2.3 signs the image — the signature is pushed to the registry. (3) Kyverno's verifyImages rule references cosign.pub and checks the signature at admission time. Works with any OCI-compliant registry (Docker Hub, ECR, GCR, Harbor)
B) Cosign is a Kubernetes operator that replaces Kyverno — they don't work together
C) Cosign signs Kubernetes manifests, not container images
D) Cosign requires a separate Sigstore infrastructure — it cannot work with standard registries
✅ Correct Answer: A

Cosign is the signing tool; Kyverno is the verification engine. Together they create a complete supply chain security chain.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cosign + Kyverno — End to End
  # 1. Generate keys (once)
  $ cosign generate-key-pair
  → cosign.key (PRIVATE — keep secret!)
  → cosign.pub  (PUBLIC — give to Kyverno)

  # 2. Sign image (in CI/CD)
  $ cosign sign --key cosign.key registry.anihpj.com/anihpj-api:v1.2.3

  # 3. Kyverno policy references the public key
  verifyImages:
  - imageReferences: ["registry.anihpj.com/*"]
    attestors:
    - entries:
      - keys:
          publicKeys: |
            -----BEGIN PUBLIC KEY-----
            MFkwEwYHKoZIzj0CAQYIKoZIzj0DAQc...
            -----END PUBLIC KEY-----

For anihpj/jobpost, every CI build runs cosign sign before pushing to Harbor. Kyverno verifies all anihpj images against our public key — if an attacker bypasses CI and pushes directly, the image has no signature and is blocked.

Writing Policies Q158 of 200
What is keyless signing with Sigstore (Fulcio + Rekor), and why is it better than static keys?
A) Keyless signing means images are unsigned — it's not a real security mechanism
B) Keyless signing uses passwords instead of keys — it's less secure but simpler
C) Keyless signing stores the private key in a cloud KMS — it's "keyless" because you don't manage the key locally
D) Sigstore keyless signing uses OIDC identity instead of long-lived keys. Fulcio (CA) issues a SHORT-LIVED certificate (valid ~10 minutes) bound to your OIDC identity (e.g., "GitHub Actions workflow X in repo Y at commit Z"). Rekor (transparency log) records the signature publicly — making it auditable and tamper-evident. Benefits: NO key generation, NO key storage, NO key rotation, signatures expire naturally, and every signature is traceable to a specific build identity
✅ Correct Answer: D

Keyless signing is the modern approach — no keys to lose, steal, or rotate. Your identity IS your key.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Keyless Signing — Fulcio + Rekor Flow
  GitHub Actions CI:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. Build image                                  │
  │  2. Cosign requests short-lived cert from Fulcio  │
  │     → "I'm repo:Anihs-Canvas/anihpj, workflow:ci" │
  │  3. Fulcio verifies OIDC token, issues cert (10m) │
  │  4. Cosign signs image with ephemeral key         │
  │  5. Signature + cert recorded in Rekor log        │
  │  6. Image pushed to registry with signature       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Kyverno verifyImages (keyless mode):
  → Dereferences OIDC issuer + identity
  → Verifies against Fulcio's root CA
  → Checks Rekor log for transparency proof

For anihpj/jobpost, migrating from static keys to keyless signing eliminated key rotation headaches and gave us a complete audit trail — every deployed image is traceable to a specific GitHub Actions run and git commit.

Writing Policies Q159 of 200
How does Kyverno verify image attestations — SLSA provenance, SBOMs, vulnerability scans?
A) Kyverno cannot verify attestations — it only checks image signatures
B) Attestations are signed metadata attached to images. Kyverno can verify them using attestations in the verifyImages rule. Types include: (1) SLSA provenance — proves HOW and WHERE the image was built (builder, source repo, commit SHA). (2) SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) — lists all dependencies. (3) Vulnerability scan results — proves the image passed a security scan. Kyverno checks the attestation's predicate type and optionally predicate data using CEL or conditions
C) Attestations are embedded in the Dockerfile — Kyverno reads them from the image layers
D) Attestation verification requires a separate Kyverno plugin — it's not in the core product
✅ Correct Answer: B

Attestations add layers of trust beyond just "this image is signed" — they answer "how was it built?", "what's in it?", and "has it been scanned?"

📝 Detailed Explanation
Attestation Verification in Kyverno
  verifyImages:
  - imageReferences: ["registry.anihpj.com/*"]
    attestors:
    - entries:
      - keyless: {...}
    attestations:
    - predicateType: "https://slsa.dev/provenance/v1"
      conditions:
        all:
        - key: "{{ builder.id }}"
          operator: Equals
          value: "https://github.com/slsa-framework/slsa-github-generator"
    - predicateType: "https://spdx.dev/Document"
      conditions: [...]

For anihpj/jobpost, we verify SLSA Level 2 provenance attestations — ensuring every production image was built by our trusted GitHub Actions workflow, not an impostor or a compromised developer workstation.

Writing Policies Q160 of 200
How does image digest mutation work — replacing mutable tags with immutable digests?
A) Digest mutation is a kubectl feature — Kyverno is not involved
B) Digest mutation requires rebuilding the image with a new tag — it can't be done at admission time
C) Kyverno can replace mutable image tags with immutable digests via a mutate rule: image: nginx:latest → image: nginx@sha256:abc123.... This is done by setting mutateDigest: true in the verifyImages rule, which resolves the tag to its current digest at admission time and patches the pod spec. Benefits: (1) Tags are mutable (latest changes over time), digests are immutable. (2) Prevents "time-of-check vs time-of-use" attacks. (3) Ensures every node runs the exact same image bits. (4) Enables reproducible deployments
D) Digest mutation only works with Docker Hub — private registries don't support it
✅ Correct Answer: C

Digest mutation is the ultimate image integrity guarantee — even if someone retags a malicious image as v1.2.3, the digest won't match.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Tag → Digest Mutation
  Developer writes:  image: anihpj-api:v1.2.3
  Kyverno mutates:   image: anihpj-api@sha256:7d8f9a3b2c1e4f5...

  verifyImages:
  - imageReferences: ["registry.anihpj.com/*"]
    mutateDigest: true        ← Resolves tag → digest
    required: true
    verifyDigest: true        ← Also verifies the digest is signed

  # Result: Pod spec now uses digest, not mutable tag.
  # If someone retags a different image as "v1.2.3", the digest
  # won't match what Kyverno previously resolved → blocked!

For anihpj/jobpost, digest mutation ensures that even if our CI retags images, every deployment uses the exact SHA256 digest — identical bits on every node, fully reproducible, and immune to tag confusion attacks.

Writing Policies Q161 of 200
How do you block images from untrusted registries using Kyverno — preventing pulls from Docker Hub, unknown registries?
A) Kyverno cannot restrict image registries — that's a container runtime configuration
B) Use a validate.deny rule with JMESPath to check the image registry: extract {{ images.containers.*.registry }} and compare against an allowlist of trusted registries. Combine with verifyImages for cryptographic proof. Block any image whose registry does NOT match your allowed list. This prevents supply chain attacks where images are pulled from malicious registries
C) Image registry restrictions use the Kubernetes ImagePolicyWebhook — Kyverno delegates to that
D) Registry blocking is a Helm chart setting — you configure it during Kyverno installation
✅ Correct Answer: B

Registry allowlisting combines deny rules for pattern matching with verifyImages for cryptographic proof. The deny rule acts as a first-pass filter; verifyImages provides the cryptographic guarantee for allowed registries.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Blocking Untrusted Registries — Two-Layer Defense
  LAYER 1 — Deny Rule (pattern match):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  validate:                                                │
  │    message: "Images must come from approved registries"   │
  │    deny:                                                  │
  │      conditions:                                          │
  │        all:                                               │
  │        - key: "{{ images.containers.*.registry }}"        │
  │          operator: NotIn                                  │
  │          value: ["registry.anihpj.com","harbor.anihpj"]   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  LAYER 2 — verifyImages (cryptographic):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  verifyImages:                                            │
  │  - imageReferences: ["registry.anihpj.com/*"]             │
  │    attestors:                                             │
  │    - entries:                                             │
  │      - keys: {publicKeys: "..."}                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  → Result: Image must be FROM allowed registry AND be SIGNED

For anihpj/jobpost, we allowlist two registries — Harbor for internal images and ECR for trusted third-party images. Everything else (Docker Hub, Quay, unknown registries) is blocked. Combined with Cosign verification, we prevent both untrusted sources AND tampered images.

Writing Policies Q162 of 200
Describe the complete end-to-end anihpj supply chain: Build → Sign → Attest → Verify in Kyverno.
A) Supply chain security is handled by the CI/CD system — Kyverno is not involved
B) Kyverno verifies images at build time — it signs images before they're pushed to the registry
C) The supply chain is: Build → Push → Scan — Kyverno only does the scanning step
D) (1) Build: GitHub Actions builds the Docker image. (2) Sign: Cosign signs with keyless OIDC, Fulcio issues ephemeral cert. (3) Attest: SLSA provenance + SBOM attestations attached to image. (4) Push: Image + signature + attestations pushed to Harbor. (5) Verify: Kyverno's verifyImages rule checks signature against Fulcio CA, validates attestation predicate types, and blocks if ANY check fails
✅ Correct Answer: D

This is the complete SLSA-compliant supply chain — every step is automated, every artifact is cryptographically verified, and Kyverno is the final gatekeeper at deployment time.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj End-to-End Supply Chain
  GitHub Actions CI/CD Pipeline:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. BUILD:  docker build -t registry.anihpj.com/api:v2 │
  │  2. SIGN:   cosign sign (keyless OIDC → Fulcio cert)   │
  │  3. ATTEST: cosign attest —predicate=slsa-provenance   │
  │  4. PUSH:   docker push + signature + attestations     │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           ↓
  Harbor Registry:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj-api:v2                                          │
  │  └── sha256:abc123...  (image digest)                   │
  │  └── .sig              (Cosign signature)               │
  │  └── .att              (SLSA provenance attestation)    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           ↓
  Kyverno Admission (kubectl apply):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  verifyImages:                                          │
  │    → Check signature against Fulcio root CA             │
  │    → Verify OIDC identity: repo=Anihs-Canvas/anihpj    │
  │    → Validate SLSA predicate: builder=GitHub Actions   │
  │    → ALL PASS → Image allowed ✅                        │
  │    → ANY FAIL → Deployment blocked ❌                   │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, this pipeline means every production deployment is cryptographically traceable to a specific GitHub commit, built by an authorized workflow, and verified at admission time — satisfying SOC2 and SLSA Level 2 compliance requirements.

5.7 Variables & API Calls in PoliciesQ163-Q168

Writing Policies Q163 of 200
What are Kyverno variables and how do they make policies dynamic?
A) Variables are dynamic placeholders that resolve at policy evaluation time: {{ request.object }} (the resource being evaluated), {{ @ }} (alias for the entire matched element), {{ images.containers.*.name }} (image data), {{ element }} (current item in foreach loops). Variables make policies context-aware — the same policy adapts to each resource's actual content. Instead of hardcoding values, you reference the resource's live data
B) Variables use Go template syntax {{.VariableName}} — not Kyverno's own syntax
C) Variables are only available in validate rules — mutate and generate rules cannot use them
D) Variables are defined in a separate ConfigMap — they're not inline in the policy
✅ Correct Answer: A

Variables are the "live data" injection mechanism — instead of writing "check that namespace equals anihpj", you write "check that namespace equals {{ request.namespace }}" and the policy works for ALL namespaces.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Variable Types — Quick Reference
  BUILT-IN VARIABLES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  {{ request.object }}         The full resource YAML     │
  │  {{ request.object.metadata.name }}  Resource name       │
  │  {{ request.namespace }}      Target namespace           │
  │  {{ request.operation }}      CREATE / UPDATE / DELETE   │
  │  {{ @ }}                      Current element in foreach │
  │  {{ element }}                Alias for current element  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  IMAGE VARIABLES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  {{ images.containers.*.name }}     Image name           │
  │  {{ images.containers.*.registry }} Image registry       │
  │  {{ images.containers.*.tag }}       Image tag           │
  │  {{ images.containers.*.digest }}    SHA256 digest       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Example — Dynamic label injection:
    +(namespace): "{{ request.namespace }}"
    → Pod in "anihpj" gets label namespace=anihpj
    → Pod in "staging" gets label namespace=staging

For anihpj/jobpost, variables let us write ONE policy that works across all 8 namespaces — the namespace name, image references, and pod metadata are all resolved dynamically at admission time.

Writing Policies Q164 of 200
What is request.object and how do you use it to access the resource being evaluated?
A) request.object returns the Kubernetes client-go object — it's a programming API
B) request.object only contains the resource's metadata — spec fields are not accessible
C) request.object contains the ENTIRE resource manifest as received by the API server — the full YAML/JSON of the Pod, Deployment, or any resource being evaluated. Access specific fields with JMESPath: request.object.spec.containers[0].image, request.object.metadata.labels.app. This is the most commonly used variable — it gives you access to every field in the resource for validation, mutation, or precondition checks
D) request.object is a ConfigMap reference — it maps to external data, not the resource itself
✅ Correct Answer: C

request.object is your window into the resource — every field the user submitted in their kubectl apply is accessible through this variable.

📝 Detailed Explanation
request.object — Common Field Access Patterns
  # Metadata fields
  {{ request.object.metadata.name }}
  {{ request.object.metadata.namespace }}
  {{ request.object.metadata.labels.app }}
  {{ request.object.metadata.annotations.team }}

  # Spec fields
  {{ request.object.spec.containers[0].image }}
  {{ request.object.spec.containers[0].securityContext.runAsNonRoot }}
  {{ request.object.spec.containers[0].resources.requests.cpu }}

  # Advanced JMESPath
  {{ request.object.spec.containers[?name=='api'].image | [0] }}
  → "Find the container named 'api' and return its image"

For anihpj/jobpost, we use request.object extensively — checking container images, validating label presence, verifying security contexts, and computing resource limits dynamically based on the actual pod spec.

Writing Policies Q165 of 200
How do context variables work — ConfigMap data and API server calls in Kyverno policies?
A) Context variables use environment variables — they're set in the Kyverno pod spec
B) context imports EXTERNAL data into policy evaluation. Two main sources: (1) ConfigMap — read key-value data from a ConfigMap: context[].configMap.name: "allowed-registries". (2) API Call — make an HTTP request to an external service and use the response: context[].apiCall.url: "https://vulnerability-scanner/api/scan". Context data is available as {{ context-name.key }} in conditions and mutations
C) Context variables are only for audit logs — they don't affect policy decisions
D) Context data requires a Kyverno enterprise license — it's not in the open-source version
✅ Correct Answer: B

Context variables bring outside data into policy decisions — your policies can reference ConfigMaps, call external APIs, and make decisions based on data that isn't in the resource itself.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Context Variables — ConfigMap + API Call
  CONFIGMAP CONTEXT:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  context:                                                 │
  │  - name: registries                                       │
  │    configMap:                                             │
  │      name: allowed-registries                             │
  │      namespace: kyverno                                   │
  │                                                           │
  │  Usage: {{ registries.data.production }}                  │
  │  → Returns the value from ConfigMap's "production" key    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  API CALL CONTEXT:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  context:                                                 │
  │  - name: vuln-scan                                        │
  │    apiCall:                                               │
  │      url: "https://scanner.anihpj.com/api/v1/scan"        │
  │      headers:                                             │
  │      - key: Authorization                                 │
  │        value: "Bearer {{ serviceAccountToken }}"          │
  │                                                           │
  │  Usage: {{ vuln-scan.result.critical }}                   │
  │  → Returns the API response (JSON parsed automatically)   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use ConfigMap context for the allowed-registries list — updating the ConfigMap instantly updates ALL policies without modifying any policy YAML. Our vulnerability scanner API call context is used in pre-production namespaces to block pods with critical CVEs.

Writing Policies Q166 of 200
How do you use JMESPath within variables to filter, transform, and compute data?
A) JMESPath is a separate CLI tool — it cannot be used inside policy variables
B) JMESPath in variables only supports simple field access — no filtering or transformation
C) JMESPath transformations use Lua scripts — not JMESPath syntax directly
D) JMESPath expressions can be embedded directly in variables: {{ request.object.spec.containers[?starts_with(image, 'registry.anihpj.com/')].image | [0] || 'none' }} — this filters containers by image prefix, returns the first match, or defaults to 'none'. JMESPath supports filtering [?expr], projections [*].field, pipes |, and functions like contains(), starts_with(), length(), join(), sum()
✅ Correct Answer: D

JMESPath inside variables is Kyverno's data transformation engine — it turns raw resource data into actionable policy decisions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
JMESPath in Variables — Common Patterns
  FILTER: Find containers with specific attributes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  {{ request.object.spec.containers[?securityContext.privileged == `true`].name }}
  │  → Returns names of ALL privileged containers              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  PROJECT: Extract all values of a field across a list
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  {{ request.object.spec.containers[*].image }}
  │  → Returns [nginx:latest, redis:7, istio-proxy:1.19]     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  COMPUTE: Aggregate values with functions
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  {{ sum(request.object.spec.containers[*].resources.requests.memory) }}
  │  → Returns total memory requested across all containers   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  DEFAULT: Provide fallback values
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  {{ request.object.metadata.labels.team || 'unknown' }}   │
  │  → Returns label value, or 'unknown' if label is missing  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use JMESPath in variables to compute total resource requests across all containers, find all containers using privileged mode, and extract image registries for comparison against our allowlist — all without writing any external code.

Writing Policies Q167 of 200
How do you make external API calls from Kyverno context — authenticating and parsing responses?
A) Configure an apiCall in context with URL, HTTP method, headers (including serviceAccountToken for auth), and optionally a JMESPath expression to parse the JSON response. Kyverno makes the HTTP call, parses the JSON response, and stores the result in a context variable. Example: Call a vulnerability scanner API, extract the critical CVE count with jmesPath: "result.summary.critical", then use {{ scan-result.critical }} in conditions
B) API calls use shell scripts executed by Kyverno — they're not native HTTP calls
C) External API calls are only supported in enterprise Kyverno — not in the open-source version
D) API call responses are limited to 256 characters — complex responses are truncated
✅ Correct Answer: A

API calls from context make Kyverno a policy decision hub — it can query external systems (scanners, CMDBs, approval systems) and incorporate their responses into admission decisions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
External API Call — Vulnerability Scanner Example
  context:
  - name: scan-results
    apiCall:
      url: "https://scanner.anihpj.com/api/v1/scan"
      method: POST
      data:
      - key: image
        value: "{{ images.containers.*.reference }}"
      headers:
      - key: Authorization
        value: "Bearer {{ serviceAccountToken }}"
      jmesPath: "result.summary"  ← Parse JSON response
