  # Now use the parsed response in conditions:
  validate:
    deny:
      conditions:
        any:
        - key: "{{ scan-results.critical }}"
          operator: GreaterThan
          value: 0
    message: "Image has critical vulnerabilities — blocked"

  ⚠️ TIP: Add timeouts and error handling — failed API calls
  should NOT silently allow resources through.

For anihpj/jobpost, our vulnerability scanner API call context blocks deployments if the image has critical CVEs. The scanner runs independently; Kyverno queries it at admission time and enforces the policy based on live scan results.

Writing Policies Q168 of 200
How do you share variables across multiple rules in the same policy?
A) Variables are rule-scoped — they cannot be shared between rules in the same policy
B) Use global variables defined in the policy metadata — they're available to all rules
C) Context variables defined at the rule level are scoped to THAT rule. To share data across rules, define context at the spec level (policy-global) — it's available to ALL rules in the policy. Each rule can also define its own rule-level context. Use context[].name at spec level for shared data and per-rule context for rule-specific data
D) Variables are shared via annotations — you annotate the policy with the variable value
✅ Correct Answer: C

Context scope is the key — spec-level context is shared across all rules; rule-level context is private to that rule. Choose the right scope for your data.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Context Scoping — Policy-Level vs Rule-Level
  SPEC-LEVEL (shared by all rules):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                                    │
  │    rules:                                                 │
  │    - name: validate-registry                              │
  │      context:              ← Rule-level (private)         │
  │        - name: rule-data   ← Only visible to THIS rule    │
  │      match: {...}                                         │
  │      validate: {...}                                      │
  │    - name: mutate-labels                                  │
  │      context:              ← Rule-level (private)         │
  │        - name: other-data  ← Only visible to THIS rule    │
  │      mutate: {...}                                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RULE-LEVEL context → Only accessible within that rule

For anihpj/jobpost, our multi-rule policies define ConfigMap context at the spec level (shared across all rules) so that all rules in the policy reference the same allowed-registries list without duplicating the context definition.

5.8 JSON Patches — RFC 6902 OperationsQ169-Q172

Writing Policies Q169 of 200
What are JSON Patches (RFC 6902) and what operations do they support?
A) JSON Patches are Kubernetes-specific — they only work with ConfigMaps and Secrets
B) JSON Patch is an IETF standard (RFC 6902) for describing partial modifications to a JSON document. Six operations: add (insert a value at a path), replace (change an existing value), remove (delete a value), copy (duplicate from one path to another), move (relocate a value), test (assert a value equals expected — used as a precondition). In Kyverno, JSON Patches are used in mutate rules via patchesJson6902
C) Kyverno supports only add, replace, and remove — copy, move, and test are not available
D) JSON Patches are an alternative to YAML — they replace strategic merge entirely
✅ Correct Answer: B

JSON Patch is the surgical tool for precise mutations — when strategic merge is too blunt, JSON Patch lets you target exact JSON paths with specific operations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RFC 6902 — All Six Operations
  OPERATION    PATH SYNTAX              EFFECT
  ─────────    ────────────             ──────
  add          /spec/containers/-       Append to array (new container)
  replace      /spec/containers/0/image Change first container's image
  remove       /spec/containers/0/securityContext/privileged  Delete field
  copy         /metadata/labels/app → /metadata/labels/old-app  Duplicate
  move         /spec/containers/0  → /spec/initContainers/-     Relocate
  test         /metadata/name == "my-pod"  Assert (fail if wrong)

  Kyverno patchesJson6902 example:
  mutate:
    patchesJson6902: |
      - path: "/spec/containers/0/image"
        op: replace
        value: "registry.anihpj.com/nginx:1.25"

For anihpj/jobpost, we use JSON Patch for operations that strategic merge can't handle — removing privileged flags (remove op), replacing specific container images (replace op), and testing preconditions before mutating (test op).

Writing Policies Q170 of 200
How do you use JSON Patches in mutate rules for precise field manipulation?
A) Use patchesJson6902 with a YAML string containing an array of JSON Patch operations. Each operation specifies path (JSON pointer like /spec/containers/0/image), op (add/replace/remove/copy/move/test), and value (the new data). Paths use JSON Pointer syntax: / separates levels, 0 is first array element, - appends to end. The patches are applied sequentially — each patch sees the result of previous patches
B) JSON Patches replace the entire resource — you must specify the full desired state
C) patchesJson6902 uses dot notation (spec.containers.0.image) — not JSON Pointer slash syntax
D) JSON Patch in mutate rules requires a separate JSON file — inline YAML is not supported
✅ Correct Answer: A

JSON Patches are applied sequentially — each operation builds on the result of previous ones. This enables multi-step mutations that transform resources in stages.

📝 Detailed Explanation
patchesJson6902 — Multi-Step Mutation
  mutate:
    patchesJson6902: |
      - path: "/metadata/labels/team"
        op: add
        value: "platform"
      - path: "/spec/containers/0/image"
        op: replace
        value: "registry.anihpj.com/api:v2"
      - path: "/spec/containers/0/securityContext/privileged"
        op: remove
      - path: "/spec/containers/0/env/-"
        op: add
        value: {"name": "NAMESPACE", "value": "anihpj"}

  SEQUENTIAL APPLICATION:
  1. Add label team=platform
  2. Replace first container's image
  3. Remove privileged flag (patch 3 sees result of 1+2)
  4. Append NAMESPACE env var (patch 4 sees result of 1+2+3)

For anihpj/jobpost, our image-registry mutation uses a multi-step JSON Patch: first add the registry label, then replace the image path, then remove any privileged flag — three operations applied sequentially to transform the pod spec.

Writing Policies Q171 of 200
What are advanced JSON Patch techniques — conditional operations and nested paths?
A) JSON Patch does not support conditional operations — all patches are unconditional
B) Conditional patches use if/else JSON syntax — Kyverno supports JSON Logic natively
C) Use the test operation as a PRECONDITION — if the test fails, subsequent patches in the same rule are SKIPPED. Combine with nested paths: /spec/containers/0/resources/requests/cpu for deep fields, and array indices with filters for dynamic targeting. Advanced technique: use JMESPath in preconditions to gate the entire mutate rule, then JSON Patch for the precise operation
D) Advanced JSON Patch requires a Kyverno plugin — native patches are limited to simple paths
✅ Correct Answer: C

The test operation is JSON Patch's conditional gate — it says "only apply the following patches if this assertion is true." Combined with JMESPath preconditions, you get powerful conditional mutation logic.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Conditional JSON Patch with test Operation
  # Only replace the image if it currently uses "latest" tag
  patchesJson6902: |
    - path: "/spec/containers/0/image"
      op: test
      value: "nginx:latest"          ← Is this the current value?
    - path: "/spec/containers/0/image"
      op: replace
      value: "registry.anihpj.com/nginx:1.25"

  # If test FAILS (image is not nginx:latest) → ALL subsequent
  # patches in this rule are SKIPPED. The resource is unchanged.

  NESTED PATHS:
    /spec/containers/0/resources/limits/cpu
    /metadata/annotations/policy.anihpj.com~1scan
    ↑ Use ~1 to escape / in JSON Pointer keys

For anihpj/jobpost, we use test operations to only mutate images tagged :latest — images with specific version tags are left untouched. This prevents accidental downgrades while still enforcing registry rewriting for untagged images.

Writing Policies Q172 of 200
How do you test and validate JSON Patches before deploying them?
A) JSON Patches cannot be tested — you must deploy and observe the results
B) Use kyverno apply with the --mutate flag to see the mutated output BEFORE deploying. Compare kubectl get (original) vs kyverno apply --mutate (mutated). Validate JSON Pointer paths with kyverno jp: kyverno jp -q "spec.containers[0].image" pod.yaml — if the path is wrong, jp returns null. Test operations can be validated by running the policy against known-good and known-bad resources
C) JSON Patch testing uses the Kyverno dashboard — CLI tools don't support it
D) Patches are validated at kubectl apply time — the API server checks JSON Pointer syntax
✅ Correct Answer: B

Always test patches locally before deploying — a wrong JSON Pointer path silently does nothing, which can be worse than blocking because your mutation silently fails to apply.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Testing JSON Patches — Workflow
  STEP 1: Verify the path exists
  $ kyverno jp -q "spec.containers[0].image" pod.yaml
  "nginx:latest"
  → Path is correct ✅

  $ kyverno jp -q "spec.container[0].image" pod.yaml
  null
  → Path is wrong (container vs containers) ❌

  STEP 2: Test the full mutation
  $ kyverno apply policy.yaml -r pod.yaml --mutate
  ---
  # Mutated resource (after patches applied)
  spec:
    containers:
    - image: registry.anihpj.com/nginx:1.25  ← changed!
      name: nginx

  STEP 3: Compare before/after
  $ diff <(kubectl get pod original -o yaml) mutated-pod.yaml

For anihpj/jobpost, every new JSON Patch goes through this 3-step validation: verify the path with jp, test the mutation with kyverno apply --mutate, and diff the result against expectations. Only then is the policy deployed to the cluster.

5.9 Autogen Rules — Automatic Pod Controller PoliciesQ173-Q176

Writing Policies Q173 of 200
What is Kyverno autogen and why does it eliminate an entire class of policy gaps?
A) Autogen is a CLI command that auto-generates policy YAML from natural language descriptions
B) Autogen automatically creates new policies based on cluster usage patterns — an AI feature
C) Autogen generates test cases for policies — it's a testing helper, not a policy feature
D) Autogen automatically generates equivalent rules for Pod controllers (Deployment, StatefulSet, DaemonSet, Job, CronJob) when your policy targets Pods. Without autogen, a policy matching kinds: [Pod] would ONLY check bare Pods — Deployments and StatefulSets would bypass the policy entirely. Autogen extracts the PodTemplate from each controller and generates rules that validate the embedded pod spec
✅ Correct Answer: D

Without autogen, 99% of your pods would bypass your policies — because most pods are created by Deployments, not as bare Pods. Autogen closes this gap automatically.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Why Autogen Is Critical — The Hidden Gap
  WITHOUT AUTOGEN (the gap):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Policy matches: kinds: [Pod]                             │
  │                                                           │
  │  kubectl apply -f bare-pod.yaml         → Checked ✅     │
  │  kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml       → NOT checked ❌ │
  │  kubectl apply -f statefulset.yaml      → NOT checked ❌ │
  │  kubectl apply -f daemonset.yaml        → NOT checked ❌ │
  │                                                           │
  │  ⚠️ 95% of pods are created via controllers — your        │
  │  policy only catches the 5% of bare pods!                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WITH AUTOGEN (default, gap closed):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Policy matches: kinds: [Pod] (autogen enabled)           │
  │                                                           │
  │  Autogen GENERATES equivalent rules for:                  │
  │  → Deployment      (extracts spec.template)               │
  │  → StatefulSet     (extracts spec.template)               │
  │  → DaemonSet       (extracts spec.template)               │
  │  → Job             (extracts spec.template)               │
  │  → CronJob         (extracts spec.jobTemplate.spec... )  │
  │                                                           │
  │  Result: ALL pod-creating resources are checked ✅         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, all 13 policies target Pods and rely on autogen to cover our Deployments (api, web, redis, celery) and StatefulSet (postgres). Without autogen, we'd need 65+ separate rules — one for each controller type.

Writing Policies Q174 of 200
Which Pod controllers does autogen cover, and how does it handle each one?
A) Autogen covers Deployment, StatefulSet, DaemonSet, Job, and CronJob. For Deployment/StatefulSet/DaemonSet/Job: extracts spec.template (the PodTemplate) and applies the pod rules to it. For CronJob: extracts spec.jobTemplate.spec.template (nested two levels deep). Each generated rule targets the controller kind with the pod rules applied to the embedded PodTemplate — the same validation that runs on bare Pods
B) Autogen covers ALL resource types — not just Pod controllers
C) Autogen only covers Deployment and StatefulSet — DaemonSet, Job, CronJob require manual rules
D) Autogen creates separate Policy resources — one for each controller type, maintaining them independently
✅ Correct Answer: A

Autogen covers the big five Pod controllers — the same set that most Kubernetes workloads use in production.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Autogen Controller Coverage
  CONTROLLER       POD TEMPLATE PATH              AUTOGEN?
  ──────────       ─────────────────              ───────
  Deployment       spec.template                  ✅
  StatefulSet      spec.template                  ✅
  DaemonSet        spec.template                  ✅
  Job              spec.template                  ✅
  CronJob          spec.jobTemplate.spec.template ✅
  ReplicaSet       spec.template                  ❌ (not autogen)
  ReplicationController  spec.template            ❌ (not autogen)

  DEPLOYMENT EXAMPLE:
  Original pod rule checks: spec.containers[].securityContext
  Autogen rule checks: spec.template.spec.containers[].securityContext
  → Same validation, different path into the resource

For anihpj/jobpost, our Deployments (api, web, redis, celery) and StatefulSet (postgres) are all covered by autogen. We also have a CronJob for database backups — autogen ensures it follows the same security policies as our long-running workloads.

Writing Policies Q175 of 200
How and when should you disable autogen for a specific policy?
A) Autogen cannot be disabled — it's a core Kyverno feature that always runs
B) Disable autogen with a Helm chart value — it's a cluster-wide setting, not per-policy
C) Set the annotation pod-policies.kyverno.io/autogen-controllers: "none" on the policy. This disables autogen for THAT specific policy. Use this when: (1) your policy intentionally only targets bare Pods, (2) the policy uses fields that don't exist in PodTemplates, (3) you want to manually write separate rules for each controller type for fine-grained control
D) Autogen is disabled by setting background: false — they're the same mechanism
✅ Correct Answer: C

The annotation pod-policies.kyverno.io/autogen-controllers: "none" is the per-policy autogen kill switch — simple, explicit, and scoped to exactly the policy that needs it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Disabling Autogen — Per-Policy Annotation
  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
  kind: ClusterPolicy
  metadata:
    name: validate-bare-pods-only
    annotations:
      pod-policies.kyverno.io/autogen-controllers: "none"
  spec:
    rules:
    - name: check-bare-pod
      match:
        any:
        - resources:
            kinds: [Pod]
      validate:
        message: "Bare pods must have explicit restart policy"
        pattern:
          spec:
            (restartPolicy): "Never"

  ↑ This policy ONLY applies to bare Pods — not Deployments, etc.

For anihpj/jobpost, one policy uses autogen-controllers: "none" — it validates bare Pods for debugging purposes and should not apply to production Deployments. All other 12 policies use default autogen behavior.

Writing Policies Q176 of 200
What autogen behaviors should you be aware of with complex patterns — what does and doesn't auto-generate?
A) Autogen always generates perfect equivalents — there are no edge cases to worry about
B) Autogen works at the rule level — if a rule uses kinds: [Pod], it generates controller equivalents. BUT: (1) Rules using request.object variables that reference Pod-specific paths may not map cleanly to controller paths. (2) Preconditions with JMESPath referencing pod fields need review. (3) mutate rules that patch /spec/containers work differently on controller PodTemplates. (4) exclude blocks are also auto-generated — verify the exclusions still make sense for controllers
C) Autogen only works with validate rules — mutate and generate rules are excluded
D) Autogen automatically fixes path mismatches — no manual review is needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

Autogen is powerful but not magic — always review generated rules, especially for complex patterns with JMESPath or nested field references.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Autogen Edge Cases — What to Watch For
  SCENARIOS REQUIRING MANUAL REVIEW:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ⚠️ METADATA: Pod metadata.labels ≠ Deployment            │
  │     spec.template.metadata.labels                         │
  │     → Autogen handles this correctly ✅                   │
  │                                                           │
  │  ⚠️ EXCLUDE PATTERNS: Excluding specific Pod names in    │
  │     autogen'd rules — controller pods have different names│
  │     → Review exclude blocks for controller applicability  │
  │                                                           │
  │  ⚠️ JMESPATH: request.object.spec.containers works for   │
  │     Pods but autogen maps to request.object.spec.template │
  │     .spec.containers — JMESPath may need adjustment       │
  │     → Test with kyverno apply against a Deployment        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we review autogen behavior for every policy during our PR process — running kyverno apply against Deployments and StatefulSets to ensure the generated rules work as expected before merging policy changes.

5.10 Cleanup Policies — TTL-Based Resource DeletionQ177-Q178

Writing Policies Q177 of 200
What is a cleanup policy and what problem does it solve?
A) A cleanup policy automatically DELETES resources that match specified criteria — typically based on age (TTL). Example: "Delete PolicyReports older than 30 days." Unlike generate (which CREATES resources), cleanup REMOVES stale, unused, or expired resources. This prevents cluster bloat — PolicyReports, completed Jobs, old ReplicaSets accumulate indefinitely without cleanup. Kyverno's Cleanup Controller periodically evaluates cleanup policies and removes matching resources
B) Cleanup policies remove malware from container images — they scan and clean at build time
C) Cleanup policies delete Kyverno policies that haven't been used — it's a policy lifecycle feature
D) Cleanup is a manual kubectl operation — Kyverno doesn't support automated cleanup
✅ Correct Answer: A

Cleanup is Kyverno's automated garbage collector — it prevents your cluster from becoming a landfill of old reports, completed jobs, and stale resources.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cleanup Policy — TTL-Based Deletion
  CLEANUP POLICY EXAMPLE (Delete PolicyReports > 30 days):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v2alpha1                          │
  │  kind: CleanupPolicy                                      │
  │  metadata:                                                │
  │    name: cleanup-old-reports                              │
  │  spec:                                                    │
  │    match:                                                 │
  │      any:                                                 │
  │      - resources:                                         │
  │          kinds: [PolicyReport]                            │
  │    conditions:                                            │
  │      all:                                                 │
  │      - key: "{{ target.metadata.creationTimestamp }}"     │
  │        operator: LessThan                                 │
  │        value: "{{ timeSince('720h') }}"   ← 30 days ago  │
  │    schedule: "0 2 * * *"                  ← Daily at 2AM  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RESULT: Every night at 2AM, PolicyReports older than 30 days
  are automatically deleted — no manual kubectl delete needed.

For anihpj/jobpost, our cleanup policy deletes PolicyReports older than 30 days and completed Jobs older than 7 days — keeping our cluster clean and our kubectl get output manageable without manual housekeeping.

Writing Policies Q178 of 200
How does cleanup schedule syntax work — cron-based scheduling for resource deletion?
A) Cleanup schedules use a simple interval in seconds — not cron syntax
B) Cleanup runs continuously — there's no scheduling, it deletes resources immediately
C) Scheduling is configured via the Kyverno ConfigMap — not per-policy
D) The schedule field uses standard cron syntax: "* * * * *" (minute, hour, day-of-month, month, day-of-week). Examples: "0 2 * * *" = daily at 2AM, "0 */6 * * *" = every 6 hours, "30 3 * * 0" = Sundays at 3:30AM. The Cleanup Controller checks the schedule and runs the cleanup when triggered. Each cleanup policy can have its own independent schedule
✅ Correct Answer: D

Cron scheduling gives you precise control over when cleanup runs — schedule it during off-peak hours to avoid impacting cluster performance.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cleanup Cron Schedule — Examples
  CRON FORMAT: minute hour day-of-month month day-of-week

  SCHEDULE           RUNS AT
  ────────           ───────
  "0 2 * * *"        Daily at 2:00 AM (off-peak cleanup)
  "0 */6 * * *"      Every 6 hours (0:00, 6:00, 12:00, 18:00)
  "30 3 * * 0"       Sundays at 3:30 AM (weekly cleanup)
  "0 0 1 * *"        1st day of every month at midnight
  "*/30 * * * *"     Every 30 minutes (aggressive cleanup)

  TYPICAL ANIHPJ SETUP:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PolicyReports:   "0 2 * * *"   Daily at 2AM             │
  │  Old Jobs:        "0 4 * * 0"   Sundays at 4AM           │
  │  Stale ReplicaSets: "0 3 * * 6" Saturdays at 3AM         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we schedule cleanup during low-traffic hours (2-4 AM) to minimize API server load. PolicyReports clean daily, completed Jobs clean weekly, and old ReplicaSets clean on weekends.

5.11 Common Expression Language (CEL)Q179-Q180

Writing Policies Q179 of 200
What is CEL (Common Expression Language) in Kyverno, and what makes it different?
A) CEL is a YAML templating language — it replaces Helm for Kyverno policy templating
B) CEL is Kyverno's custom scripting language — it's unique to Kyverno and not used elsewhere
C) CEL (Common Expression Language) is a Google-developed, lightweight expression language used across Kubernetes (admission policies, CRD validation, Kubernetes API). Kyverno 1.11+ supports CEL expressions in validate rules via cel.expressions. CEL provides: type-safe operations, arithmetic (+ - * /), comparisons (> < == !=), logical operators (&& || !), and built-in macros like has(), size(), contains(). Unlike JMESPath (optimized for data extraction), CEL is optimized for CONDITIONAL LOGIC
D) CEL replaces all existing Kyverno validation — pattern and deny rules are deprecated
✅ Correct Answer: C

CEL is the newest addition to Kyverno's validation toolkit — it brings Kubernetes-native expression power for complex conditional validations that go beyond what pattern and deny can express.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CEL in Kyverno — Syntax and Examples
  VALIDATE WITH CEL (Kyverno 1.11+):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  rules:                                                   │
  │  - name: cel-example                                      │
  │    match: {any: [{resources: {kinds: [Pod]}}]}            │
  │    validate:                                              │
  │      cel:                                                 │
  │        expressions:                                       │
  │        - expression: "object.spec.containers.all(c,       │
  │            has(c.securityContext) &&                    │
  │            c.securityContext.runAsNonRoot == true)"       │
  │          message: "All containers must run as non-root"   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CEL BUILT-INS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  has(field)              Check if field exists            │
  │  size(list)              Count elements in a list         │
  │  contains(str, substr)   Check string contains substring  │
  │  matches(str, regex)     Regex pattern matching           │
  │  object.spec.containers  Full resource access             │
  │  oldObject               Previous state (for UPDATE ops)  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we're evaluating CEL for our most complex validation rules — particularly those requiring arithmetic comparisons (memory limit >= 2x request) and all()/any() container checks that are verbose in JMESPath.

Writing Policies Q180 of 200
CEL vs JMESPath — when should you use each in Kyverno policies?
A) JMESPath: Use for DATA EXTRACTION — pulling values from nested fields, filtering arrays, transforming data. Best in preconditions, context variables, and deny rule conditions. CEL: Use for COMPLEX VALIDATION LOGIC — type-safe comparisons, arithmetic, all()/any() on collections, cross-field validation (e.g., memory.limit >= memory.request * 2). CEL excels at boolean logic; JMESPath excels at path navigation. They're complementary — not replacements
B) CEL replaces JMESPath entirely — JMESPath will be deprecated in future Kyverno versions
C) JMESPath is for mutate rules; CEL is for validate rules — they serve different rule types exclusively
D) CEL is for beginners; JMESPath is for advanced users — they're difficulty-tiered
✅ Correct Answer: A

JMESPath and CEL are complementary tools — like a screwdriver and a wrench. Use the right tool for each job; they work together in the same policy.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CEL vs JMESPath — When to Use Each
  JMESPath — BEST FOR:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ Extracting nested values from deep paths              │
  │  ✅ Filtering arrays: containers[?name=='api']           │
  │  ✅ Projections: containers[*].image                     │
  │  ✅ String manipulation: join(), contains()              │
  │  ✅ Use in: preconditions, context variables, deny rules  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CEL — BEST FOR:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ Boolean logic: has() && size() > 0                   │
  │  ✅ Arithmetic: memory.limit >= memory.request * 2        │
  │  ✅ Collection operations: containers.all(c, ...)         │
  │  ✅ Cross-field validation: limit > request               │
  │  ✅ Type-safe comparisons with clear error messages       │
  │  ✅ Use in: cel.expressions in validate rules (1.11+)     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  TOGETHER: Use JMESPath in preconditions to extract data,
  then CEL in validate to make the boolean decision.

For anihpj/jobpost, we use JMESPath for all existing policy conditions (mature, well-understood) and are gradually adopting CEL for new policies requiring arithmetic comparisons — specifically our resource quota enforcement policies that need to verify limits are proportional to requests.

Managing Kyverno After Deployment — Reports, Exceptions & Metrics

This category is about the ongoing management of Kyverno — reading compliance reports to understand your security posture, handling policy exceptions for special cases, and monitoring Kyverno's health and performance. Think of it as the security operations center dashboard — after the policies are deployed, this is how you know they're working.

6.1 Policy Reports — Your Compliance DashboardQ181-Q190

Policy Mgmt Q181 of 200
What are PolicyReports in Kyverno — the "report card" for every resource?
A) PolicyReports are Kubernetes Events — they appear in kubectl describe output
B) PolicyReports are log entries in the Kyverno pod — viewable via kubectl logs
C) PolicyReports are Custom Resources that record the compliance status of EVERY resource against EVERY policy. Each report entry shows: which policy was evaluated, which rule, the resource name, and the result (pass/fail/warn/error/skip). Think of it like a school report card — every resource gets a grade from every policy. Use kubectl get policyreport -n anihpj to list them
D) PolicyReports are generated by kubectl — Kyverno only provides the raw data through an API
✅ Correct Answer: C

PolicyReport Structure — The Compliance Report Card

What is the difference between PolicyReport and ClusterPolicyReport?
A) PolicyReport is NAMESPACED — reports on resources within a specific namespace. ClusterPolicyReport is CLUSTER-WIDE — reports on cluster-scoped resources (Namespaces, Nodes, ClusterRoles) or aggregates results across all namespaces. Both contain the same result structure (pass/fail/warn/error/skip). Use kubectl get policyreport -n anihpj vs kubectl get clusterpolicyreport
B) PolicyReport is for audit results; ClusterPolicyReport is for enforce results
C) PolicyReport is the old name; ClusterPolicyReport is the new replacement in Kyverno 1.11+
D) PolicyReport covers pods; ClusterPolicyReport covers everything else
✅ Correct Answer: A

Same naming convention as Policy vs ClusterPolicy — namespaced reports for namespaced resources, cluster reports for cluster-wide visibility.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PolicyReport vs ClusterPolicyReport
  PolicyReport (namespaced):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get policyreport -n anihpj                     │
  │  → Shows results for resources IN the anihpj namespace   │
  │  → Each namespace has its own PolicyReport per policy     │
  │  → Pod, Deployment, Service, ConfigMap in that namespace  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ClusterPolicyReport (cluster-wide):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get clusterpolicyreport                        │
  │  → Shows results for CLUSTER-SCOPED resources            │
  │  → Namespaces, Nodes, ClusterRoles, PersistentVolumes    │
  │  → NOT scoped to any single namespace                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use PolicyReports in the anihpj namespace to check pod compliance and ClusterPolicyReports to verify our Namespace-level policies (like auto-generated NetworkPolicies).

Policy Mgmt Q184 of 200
What do the result types pass, fail, and warn mean in PolicyReports?
A) pass = resource approved, fail = resource deleted, warn = resource flagged for manual review
B) pass = policy enabled, fail = policy disabled, warn = policy misconfigured
C) Only pass and fail exist — warn is not a valid result type in Kyverno
D) pass = Resource fully complies with the policy rule. fail = Resource VIOLATED the rule AND was blocked (Enforce mode) — the resource was NOT created. warn = Resource WOULD violate the rule but was ALLOWED (Audit mode) — the resource was created but flagged for review. fail = blocked; warn = allowed but flagged
✅ Correct Answer: D

The difference between fail and warn is blocking vs reporting — fail means Kyverno stopped the resource; warn means Kyverno let it through but wants you to know.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Result Types — What Each Means
  pass: ✅ Resource COMPLIES — everything is correct
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Policy: require-non-root                                 │
  │  Rule: check-runAsNonRoot                                 │
  │  Pod: anihpj-api-7d8f9 → runAsNonRoot: true → PASS       │
  │  Action: None needed — resource is compliant              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  fail: ❌ Resource VIOLATED and was BLOCKED (Enforce mode)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Policy: require-non-root (Enforce)                       │
  │  Pod: debug-shell → runAsNonRoot: false → FAIL + BLOCKED │
  │  Action: kubectl returned 403 — resource NOT created     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  warn: ⚠️ Resource WOULD violate but was ALLOWED (Audit mode)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Policy: require-non-root (Audit)                         │
  │  Pod: debug-shell → runAsNonRoot: false → WARN + ALLOWED │
  │  Action: Resource was created — but flagged in report     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we monitor warn results during the Audit phase — they tell us which resources would break when we switch to Enforce, so we can fix them proactively.

Policy Mgmt Q185 of 200
What do the error and skip result types mean in PolicyReports?
A) error = resource had a typo in its name; skip = resource was too small to check
B) error = The POLICY ITSELF encountered an error during evaluation — broken JMESPath, API call timeout, invalid variable reference. This is a POLICY problem, not a resource problem. skip = The rule did NOT apply to this resource — either the match block didn't select it, or a precondition failed. Skipped resources are intentionally not evaluated
C) error = resource was blocked; skip = resource was allowed — same as fail/pass
D) error and skip only appear in debug mode — they're not in normal PolicyReports
✅ Correct Answer: B

error = broken policy (fix the policy). skip = policy doesn't apply (by design). Understanding this distinction is critical for debugging.

📝 Detailed Explanation
error vs skip — When They Appear
  error — POLICY problem (investigate immediately):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Causes: JMESPath syntax error, undefined variable,      │
  │          API call timeout, missing ConfigMap context      │
  │  Action: Fix the POLICY — not the resource                │
  │          kubectl describe policyreport → check message    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  skip — Expected behavior (resource not targeted):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Causes: Match block didn't select this resource kind,   │
  │          Precondition failed, Resource in excluded ns     │
  │  Action: Usually NONE — this is by design                │
  │          Review if you EXPECTED the resource to be checked│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we alert on ANY error results — they indicate a broken policy that needs immediate attention. Skip results are expected and not monitored.

How do you filter and query PolicyReports for specific information?

Common filters: show only FAIL results, count violations per policy, list non-compliant resources, find all errors. PolicyReports are standard CRDs — any Kubernetes tool works

✅ Correct Answer: C

📝 Detailed Explanation
Filtering PolicyReports — Common Queries
  SHOW ONLY FAIL RESULTS:
  $ kubectl get polr -n anihpj -o json | jq '.results[] | select(.result=="fail")'

  COUNT VIOLATIONS PER POLICY:
  $ kubectl get polr -n anihpj -o json | jq '[.results[] | select(.result=="fail")] | group_by(.policy) | map({policy: .[0].policy, count: length})'

  FIND NON-COMPLIANT RESOURCES:
  $ kubectl get polr -n anihpj -o json | jq '.results[] | select(.result=="fail" or .result=="warn") | .resources[].name'

  FIND ALL ERRORS (broken policies):
  $ kubectl get polr -A -o json | jq '.items[].results[] | select(.result=="error")'

For anihpj/jobpost, our CI/CD pipeline runs these jq queries after each deployment and posts the results to a Slack channel — giving developers immediate visibility into policy violations.

Policy Mgmt Q189 of 200
What is the difference between background scan reports and admission reports?
A) Background scans and admission produce identical reports — they're indistinguishable
B) Admission reports are generated at admission time when a NEW resource is created/updated via the API. Background scan reports are generated when Kyverno periodically re-scans EXISTING resources. The same PolicyReport CR stores results from both sources — the source is identified by the scored field and timestamps. Admission reports are real-time; background reports are periodic snapshots
C) Background scan reports use a different CRD (BackgroundScanReport) — they're not in PolicyReports
D) Admission reports are the only source — background scans don't generate reports
✅ Correct Answer: B

Both admission and background results go into the same PolicyReport — the difference is WHEN they were evaluated and WHAT triggered them.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Admission vs Background — Two Paths, Same Report
  ADMISSION PATH (real-time, on kubectl apply):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply → API Server → Kyverno webhook             │
  │  → Policy evaluated IMMEDIATELY                           │
  │  → Result written to PolicyReport (scored: true)          │
  │  → Resource either created (pass/warn) or blocked (fail)  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  BACKGROUND PATH (periodic, on existing resources):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Kyverno Background Controller scans cluster              │
  │  → Policy evaluated on EXISTING resources                 │
  │  → Result written to SAME PolicyReport (scored: true)     │
  │  → Resource already exists — no blocking                  │
  │  → Identify: timestamp is from scan time, not admission   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use admission reports for immediate violation alerts and background reports for weekly compliance audits — both from the same PolicyReport CRs.

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

6.2 PolicyExceptions — Handling Special CasesQ191-Q196

Policy Mgmt Q191 of 200
What are PolicyExceptions and when should you use them?
A) PolicyExceptions are errors in policy YAML — they should be fixed, not created
B) PolicyExceptions disable Kyverno entirely for specific namespaces — a kill switch
C) PolicyExceptions modify policies — they're like patches that change policy behavior permanently
D) A PolicyException is a separate CRD that EXEMPTS specific resources from specific policy rules — without modifying the policy itself. Example: "Pod anihpj-db-0 is EXEMPT from the block-hostpath policy." Use for OPERATIONAL exceptions (temporary, specific, reviewed). Unlike exclude blocks (permanent, structural), PolicyExceptions are designed for TEMPORARY, AUDITED exceptions that should be regularly reviewed and removed when no longer needed
✅ Correct Answer: D

PolicyExceptions are the "approved exception" mechanism — they say "we know this resource violates the policy, and we're OK with it... FOR NOW." Unlike exclude (permanent), exceptions should be reviewed and expire.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PolicyException — When and Why
  POLICYEXCEPTION CR:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v2                                 │
  │  kind: PolicyException                                     │
  │  metadata:                                                 │
  │    name: db-needs-hostpath                                 │
  │    namespace: anihpj                                       │
  │  spec:                                                     │
  │    exceptions:                                             │
  │    - policyName: block-hostpath                            │
  │      ruleNames: [validate-hostpath]                        │
  │    match:                                                  │
  │      any:                                                  │
  │      - resources:                                          │
  │          names: [anihpj-db-0]                              │
  │  → Pod "anihpj-db-0" is EXEMPT from "block-hostpath"       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WHEN TO USE PolicyException vs exclude:
  • Exception: Temporary, operational, needs review → PolicyException
  • exclude:   Permanent, structural, by design → inline exclude block

For anihpj/jobpost, we use PolicyExceptions when the database team needs to test a new storage driver that requires hostPath for a week — the exception is created, reviewed after 7 days, and either removed or converted to a permanent exclude after architecture review.

Policy Mgmt Q192 of 200
How do you create a PolicyException — matching policy name, rule name, and resource?
A) Create a PolicyException CR in the SAME namespace as the target resource. Specify spec.exceptions[].policyName (the policy to exempt from), ruleNames (specific rules within that policy — or "*" for all rules), and match (which resources get the exemption). The exception takes effect immediately — Kyverno watches for PolicyException CRs and excludes matching resources from the specified policy rules
B) PolicyExceptions are annotations on the resource — not a separate CRD
C) PolicyExceptions are created in the kyverno namespace — not the resource's namespace
D) PolicyExceptions require Kyverno controller restart to take effect
✅ Correct Answer: A

PolicyExceptions are immediate and hot-reloaded — create the CR, and Kyverno starts honoring the exception within seconds.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Creating a PolicyException — Step by Step
  $ kubectl apply -f - <

For anihpj/jobpost, PolicyExceptions are created via a self-service workflow — developers submit a PR with the exception YAML, the platform team approves it, and it's auto-applied by ArgoCD. All exceptions are tracked in git.

B) PolicyExceptions are cluster-wide — they affect all namespaces simultaneously

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                                    │

  │    - policyName: block-hostpath                           │
  │      ruleNames: ["*"]                ← All rules         │

  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, our database StatefulSet has a single PolicyException covering all DB pods from 3 policies — hostPath, non-root, and capabilities. This is cleaner than 3 separate exception CRs and easier to review during weekly audits.

Policy Mgmt Q194 of 200
What are PolicyException best practices for production environments?
A) Create exceptions freely — they're low-risk and don't need review
B) (1) Git-track all exceptions — store PolicyException YAMLs in version control. (2) Require justification — annotate each exception with reason, approver, and ticket number. (3) Set expiration — review exceptions monthly; remove those no longer needed. (4) Use narrow scope — exempt specific resources, not entire namespaces. (5) Monitor exception count — if exceptions grow, the policies may be too strict or wrong. (6) Prefer exclude for permanent exceptions
C) PolicyExceptions should be broad (namespace-wide) to minimize the number of CRs to manage
D) Exceptions never need review — they're part of the policy and should be permanent
✅ Correct Answer: B

PolicyExceptions are a necessary evil — manage them with the same rigor as production IAM roles. They're temporary security holes; track them accordingly.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PolicyException Best Practices Checklist
  ☑️ Git-tracked: Every exception in version control
  ☑️ Annotated:   metadata.annotations:
                   policy.anihpj.com/reason: "DB needs hostPath"
                   policy.anihpj.com/approver: "platform-team"
                   policy.anihpj.com/ticket: "JIRA-4567"
                   policy.anihpj.com/expires: "2024-03-01"
  ☑️ Narrow scope: names: [anihpj-db-0] NOT namespaces: ["*"]
  ☑️ Monthly review: Script checks annotations.expires field
  ☑️ Monitoring:     Alert if exception count > 10 per namespace
  ☑️ Convert or remove: Permanent exceptions → exclude block

For anihpj/jobpost, every PolicyException requires a Jira ticket, team lead approval, and an expiration date. A cronjob deletes expired exceptions automatically and notifies the platform team.

Policy Mgmt Q195 of 200
How do you manage the PolicyException lifecycle — expiration, review, and cleanup?
A) PolicyExceptions never expire — they persist until manually deleted
B) Exceptions auto-expire after 30 days — Kyverno handles this automatically
C) Implement a lifecycle management process: (1) Annotate each exception with metadata (reason, approver, ticket, target removal date). (2) Run a weekly cronjob that lists exceptions and flags those past their review date. (3) Use Kyverno's own cleanup policy to auto-delete expired exceptions (meta-exception management!). (4) Maintain a dashboard showing exception count trend — if it's growing, investigate why. (5) Quarterly architecture review: convert recurring exceptions to permanent exclude blocks or redesign to eliminate the need
D) Exception lifecycle management requires a third-party tool — Kyverno has no native support
✅ Correct Answer: C

Use Kyverno to manage Kyverno — a cleanup policy that auto-deletes expired PolicyExceptions is deliciously meta.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Exception Lifecycle Automation
  WEEKLY REVIEW CRONJOB:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Find exceptions past their review date                  │
  │  $ kubectl get policyexception -A -o json | jq '.items[] |│
  │    select(.metadata.annotations."policy.anihpj.com/expires"│
  │    < now) | .metadata.name'                                │
  │  → Output: List of exceptions needing review               │
  │  → Action: Extend or delete each one                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  AUTO-CLEANUP (Kyverno cleanup policy):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v2alpha1                           │
  │  kind: CleanupPolicy                                       │
  │  metadata:                                                 │
  │    name: cleanup-expired-exceptions                        │
  │  spec:                                                     │
  │    match:                                                  │
  │      any:                                                  │
  │      - resources:                                          │
  │          kinds: [PolicyException]                          │
  │    conditions:                                             │
  │      all:                                                  │
  │      - key: "{{ target.metadata.annotations.'policy...' }}"│
  │        operator: LessThan                                  │
  │        value: "{{ timeSince('0s') }}"  ← Expired          │
  │    schedule: "0 3 * * 0"  ← Sundays at 3AM                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, our exception lifecycle is fully automated — Slack notifications for expiring exceptions, weekly review reports, and Kyverno self-cleans expired exceptions. We maintain a <3% exception rate across all policies.

Policy Mgmt Q196 of 200
When should you use a PolicyException vs an exclude block in match?
A) PolicyException: TEMPORARY, OPERATIONAL — "we need to run this debug pod with hostPath for this week's investigation." Requires review, has an expiration, tracked in git as a separate CR. exclude block: PERMANENT, STRUCTURAL — "PostgreSQL StatefulSet will ALWAYS need hostPath for data persistence." Part of the policy definition, reviewed with the policy, no expiration needed. Rule: if the exception will exist forever, use exclude. If it's temporary or uncertain, use PolicyException
B) PolicyExceptions are the modern replacement for exclude — always use them
C) exclude blocks are deprecated — they'll be removed in future Kyverno versions
D) There's no difference — they're two syntaxes for the same behavior
✅ Correct Answer: A

The decision is simple: permanent → exclude. Temporary → PolicyException.

📝 Detailed Explanation
exclude vs PolicyException — Decision Flow
  Is this exception PERMANENT?
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  YES → Use exclude block in the policy                    │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  Example: Database always needs hostPath             │ │
  │  │  Impact: Part of policy YAML, reviewed with policy   │ │
  │  │  Lifecycle: Exists as long as the policy does        │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                           │
  │  NO (temporary) → Use PolicyException                    │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  Example: Debug pod needs privilege for 1 week       │ │
  │  │  Impact: Separate CR, tracked in git, has expiry     │ │
  │  │  Lifecycle: Created → Reviewed → Removed              │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, 3 permanent exceptions live in exclude blocks (database hostPath, Redis capabilities, monitoring agent privileges). All other exceptions use PolicyExceptions with a 30-day maximum lifetime — forcing regular re-evaluation.

6.3 Kyverno Metrics — Monitoring & ObservabilityQ197-Q200

Policy Mgmt Q197 of 200
What metrics does Kyverno expose, and how do you access them?
A) Kyverno metrics are accessible only through the Kyverno dashboard — no Prometheus endpoint
B) Kyverno exposes a Prometheus /metrics endpoint on port 8000. Key metrics: kyverno_policy_results_total (policy evaluation counts by result type), kyverno_admission_requests_total (admission requests processed), kyverno_admission_review_duration_seconds (latency histogram), kyverno_controller_* (controller-level metrics). Configure Prometheus ServiceMonitor or PodMonitor to scrape the endpoint
C) Metrics are logged to stdout — you parse logs with fluentd, not Prometheus
D) Kyverno metrics require a separate metrics-server deployment — they're not built-in
✅ Correct Answer: B

Kyverno metrics follow the standard Prometheus pattern — a /metrics endpoint on the metrics port, scraped by Prometheus, visualized in Grafana.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kyverno Metrics Architecture
  PROMETHEUS SCRAPE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Kyverno Pod (port 8000)                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  /metrics  → Prometheus-format metrics               │ │
  │  │                                                      │ │
  │  │  kyverno_policy_results_total{policy="require-labels"│ │
  │  │    ,result="pass"} 150                                │ │
  │  │  kyverno_policy_results_total{policy="require-labels"│ │
  │  │    ,result="fail"} 3                                  │ │
  │  │  kyverno_admission_requests_total 5000               │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, Prometheus scrapes Kyverno metrics every 30 seconds. We built a Grafana dashboard showing policy pass/fail rates, admission latency, and violation trends over time.

Policy Mgmt Q198 of 200
What do kyverno_policy_results_total and kyverno_admission_requests_total tell you?
A) policy_results_total counts policy YAML files; admission_requests_total counts kubectl commands
B) These are internal debugging metrics — not useful for monitoring
C) policy_results_total tracks policy versions; admission_requests_total tracks API server health
D) kyverno_policy_results_total = how many resources passed/failed/warned/errored per policy — the COMPLIANCE METER. kyverno_admission_requests_total = how many admission requests Kyverno processed — the THROUGHPUT METER. Together they tell you: "How effective are my policies?" (results) and "How busy is Kyverno?" (throughput). Alert on spikes in fail/error; monitor pass/warn trends
✅ Correct Answer: D

These two metrics are your Kyverno health dashboard — compliance rate and throughput. Together they give you a complete picture of policy enforcement.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Key Metrics — What to Monitor
  COMPLIANCE METER — kyverno_policy_results_total:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pass rate:   pass / (pass + fail + warn) × 100          │
  │  Violation rate: (fail + warn) / total × 100             │
  │  Error rate:  error / total × 100  ← Alert if > 0!       │
  │                                                           │
  │  GRAFANA ALERT: kyverno_policy_results_total{result="err"}│
  │  > 0 → A policy is broken — investigate immediately       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  THROUGHPUT METER — kyverno_admission_requests_total:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Requests/sec: rate(kyverno_admission_requests_total[5m])│
  │  → Is Kyverno keeping up with cluster activity?          │
  │  → Spike in requests without spike in latency = healthy  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we alert if error rate > 0 (broken policy), fail rate spikes suddenly (possible attack or misconfiguration), or pass rate drops below 95% (compliance drift).

Policy Mgmt Q199 of 200
What does kyverno_admission_review_duration_seconds measure, and why does latency matter?
A) This metric measures how long kubectl commands take — not Kyverno's processing time
B) It's a histogram measuring how long Kyverno takes to evaluate each admission request. Matters because: (1) Kyverno is IN the critical path of every kubectl apply — slow Kyverno = slow deployments. (2) The API server has a timeout (default 10s) — if Kyverno exceeds it, the request fails. (3) Complex policies (image verification, API calls) increase latency. Monitor P50/P95/P99; alert if P99 > 5s. Tune with webhookTimeoutSeconds or optimize slow policies
C) This metric tracks how long background scans take — not admission latency
D) Admission latency is always under 100ms — monitoring it is unnecessary
✅ Correct Answer: B

Kyverno latency is deployment latency — every millisecond adds to the time it takes to deploy a pod. Slow policies impact developer velocity.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Admission Latency — The Critical Path
  DEPLOYMENT TIMELINE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply → API Server (1ms)                         │
  │               → AuthN/Z (5ms)                             │
  │               → Kyverno MUTATE (15ms) ← measured          │
  │               → Kyverno VALIDATE (20ms) ← measured        │
  │               → etcd write (5ms)                          │
  │  Total: ~46ms                                             │
  │                                                           │
  │  IF Kyverno P99 = 5s:                                     │
  │  → 1 in 100 deployments takes 5+ SECONDS                  │
  │  → Risk of hitting API server 10s timeout                 │
  │  → ACTION: Profile slow policies, optimize JMESPath,      │
  │    increase webhookTimeoutSeconds for complex policies    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  PROMETHEUS QUERY:
  histogram_quantile(0.99,
    kyverno_admission_review_duration_seconds_bucket)

For anihpj/jobpost, our P99 admission latency is 200ms — well within safe limits. We alert if P99 exceeds 1s, which usually indicates a newly deployed policy with an expensive JMESPath expression or API call.

Policy Mgmt Q200 of 200
How do you build a Kyverno Grafana dashboard — what metrics should you track and what alerts should you set?
A) Kyverno doesn't support Grafana — use the built-in Kyverno dashboard only
B) A Grafana dashboard only needs one panel showing total policies — metrics are not useful beyond that
C) Build a 4-panel dashboard: (1) Compliance overview — pass/fail/warn/error rates per policy (stacked bar chart). (2) Admission throughput — requests/sec, latency P50/P95/P99 (time series). (3) Violation trend — fail+warn count over time (line chart, alert if spike). (4) Policy errors — error count per policy (table, alert if > 0). ALERTS: error > 0 → broken policy, P99 latency > 5s → performance issue, fail spike > 3x baseline → possible attack
D) Grafana dashboards are auto-generated by Kyverno — no manual configuration needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

A well-designed Grafana dashboard turns Kyverno metrics from raw numbers into actionable intelligence — you can see policy effectiveness at a glance.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Grafana Dashboard — 4-Panel Layout
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ☸️ KYVERNO POLICY DASHBOARD                    [anihpj]  │
  ├────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┤
  │  COMPLIANCE OVERVIEW       │  ADMISSION THROUGHPUT         │
  │  ████████████ Pass: 95%   │  ─── Requests/sec            │
  │  ██ Warn: 3%              │  ─ · ─ P50 Latency (50ms)    │
  │  █ Fail: 1%               │  ─ ─ ─ P99 Latency (200ms)   │
  │  · Error: 0%              │                               │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │  VIOLATION TREND (30d)     │  POLICY ERRORS (current)      │
  │      ╱╲                    │  Policy           Errors      │
  │     ╱  ╲   ╱╲             │  require-labels   0           │
  │  ╱╱      ╲╱  ╲            │  block-privileged 0           │
  │  ───────────────────       │  verify-api-image 0           │
  ├────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┤
  │  ALERTS: P99>1s=WARN  Error>0=CRIT  FailSpike>3x=WARN    │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, our Kyverno Grafana dashboard is displayed on the platform team's wall monitor — we can see policy health at a glance. Alerts go to Slack and PagerDuty: error > 0 = critical page, latency spike = warning, compliance drop = email to security team.
